FOREWORD 

"Small business has made little impact in the export markets of the world. "Oversea trade increases steadily as a result of improved shipping facilities, increased purchasing power abroad, and fewer tariff and quota barriers; but U.S. small business has barely scratched the surface of available opportunities." 

With those words, the Senate Small Business Committee began its report, earlier this year, on "Small Business Exports and the 

World Market," Senate Report 89, 87th Congress, 1st session. The report recognized, of course, that many individual small firms have long and successful backgrounds in international business; but the conclusion remained that many more are overlooking profitable markets, which they have the capacity to serve, in their continuing decisions to keep their operations wholly domestic.

The U.S. deficit in international payments has focused increasing attention of Government and alert business management alike on the needs and opportunities in world trade. The Department of 

Commerce, the Small Business Administration and various private groups have sponsored a number of special university courses as well as shorter conferences and seminars for businessmen interested in expanding or initiating oversea operations. This committee has examined the problem in hearings held last year and the report mentioned above.

One of the most interesting and helpful of recently offered shorter courses on the subject was a two-and-a-half-day seminar in New 

York entitled "Going Abroad: The Profit Opportunities of Inter national Business for the Smaller Company," which was sponsored by the International Management Division of the American Manage ment Association, Inc., at its headquarters in the Hotel Astor. Senator Jacob K. Javits, a member of the Senate Small Business Committee, delivered one of the opening lectures. 

Because the AMA's seminar offered an introduction to numerous aspects of international business techniques and opportunities within a relatively brief compass of words and time, I asked and received permission from the international management division to have the committee's reporters transcribe the proceedings for use of its members and professional staff, and possible dissemination to a larger audience. A review of the transcript has persuaded me that this seminar should be made available to all small businessmen interested in oversea business. Accordingly, it is a great pleasure to be able to present it to the Senate and the public in this committee print, which is published with the permission and cooperation of the Amer ican Management Association. 

On behalf of the committee, I wish to compliment the International Management Division of AMA for its fine work in arranging this seminar and to thank it for its generosity in permitting publication. 

JOHN SPARKMAN, Chairman, Select Committee on Small Business, U.S. Senate.

AMERICAN MANAGEMENT ASSOCIATION SEMINAR GOING ABROAD: THE PROFIT OPPORTUNITIES OF INTERNATIONAL BUSINESS FOR THE SMALLER COMPANY NEW YORK, N.Y., Monday, June 26, 1961. 

The seminar, "Going Abroad: The Profit Opportunities of Inter national Business for the Smaller Company," sponsored by the 

International Management Division of the American Management 

Association, was convened at the headquarters of the association, Hotel Astor, 1515 Broadway, New York, N.Y., at 9:30 a.m. on June 

26, 1961.

The names, company affiliations and addresses of the chairman, cochairmen and speakers at the seminar will be found in the table of contents at the beginning of this volume. 

A list of the names, company affiliations and addresses of the persons registered for the seminar will be found in appendix I, p. 211. 

The general schedule and outline of the seminar is as follows: 

INTERNATIONAL Management DIVISION BRIEFING SESSION No. 10305-01-GOING ABROAD: THE PROFIT OPPORTUNITIES OF INTERNATIONAL BUSINESS FOR THE SMALLER COMPANY 

Schedule 

Monday, June 26, 1961, 9:30 a.m. to 12:30 p.m.

THE BACKGROUND 

Jacob K. Javits, U.S. Senator, member, Select Committee on Small Business,

Washington, D.C. 

I. The National Export Policy Act of 1961 

Legislation has been introduced in the current session of Congress to aid smaller companies in developing export markets and to strengthen export credit guarantees. This speaker will outline the key features of the legislation, show how broader sources of help are being made available to the smaller company. 

Robert C. Hood, President, Ansul Chemical Co., Marionette, Wis.

II. Going abroad-The profit opportunities of international business for the smaller company

In many respects, the smaller company has many natural advantages in going abroad. This speaker will outline how you can put your advantages to work for you, suggest how foreign business can affect your "break even" point and your profit picture, check some of the principal factors about your company you should have in mind in deciding which alternate route is best for you at your present stage of development. 

Luncheon, 12: 30 p.m. to 1:30 p.m.

Monday, 1:30 p.m. to 5 p.m. 

Stephen M. Lucas, general sales manager, International Division, American 

Sterilizer Corp., Erie, Pa.

III. Market research—The dimensions of oversea markets and smaller company top management decisionmaking

Although there are outside sources of help, top management of the company going abroad must take an active part in researching its markets. This speaker will help you to develop a do-it-yourself checklist of what you will want to 1

2 

AMERICAN MANAGEMENT ASSOCIATION SEMINAR 

know about yourself, your products, and your market possibilities before going abroad.

THE ALTERNATIVES 

Richard G.

Lurie, vice president, American Exporter Publications, and publisher of American Exporter and Automotive World, New York City, and Harold E. 

Mix, general credit manager, S. B. Penick & Co., New York City

IV. Exporting-The universal route to oversea profits for the smaller company 

Exporting has been the usual way of going into foreign markets. For some companies, exporting may be the only practical means of capitalizing on foreign profit opportunities. This speaker will suggest some of the current problem areas in exporting, and develop criteria that will help you decide whether exporting is the most feasible method for you. He will also discuss some of the current problems and prospects in credit extension and collection. 

John H. Breck, Jr., executive vice president, John H. Breck, Inc., Springfield, Mass.

V. Licensing Criteria for licensing, selecting the licensee and terms and conditions of the agreement

Licensing is increasingly popular as a practical method for the smaller company going abroad. But licensing is not desirable for all companies. This speaker will outline the advantages and disadvantages of licensing, explain how to go about selecting the licensee, suggest “must” provisions of the license agreement and some typical optional provisions. 

Tuesday, June 27, 1961, 9 a.m. to 12:30 p.m.

THE ALTERNATIVES continued 

Norman Redlich, president, Lafayette Brass Manufacturing Co., Inc.,

VI. Contract manufacturing New York City 

For some smaller companies, contract manufacturing is a practical route to an improved profit picture. Requiring a modest capital expenditure, contract manufacturing provides the smaller company with a feasible method of doing business abroad and, in some cases, can be useful in domestic markets in highly competitive situations. This speaker will outline the pros and cons of contract manufacturing, suggest problem areas you should check out before making a decision. 

J. R. Swihart, vice president, international operations, the Diversey Corp., Chicago, Ill. 

VII. Manufacturing abroad: the case for the joint venture

Many smaller companies reject licensing as a route, and decide to invest abroad. Aside from the obvious advantage of becoming more of a “citizen” in the foreign country, the joint venture offers many advantages in terms of knowledge of the market, customs, and practices in the foreign country. Financial and tax advantages, and a smaller capital outlay are other prime factors for those who decide to manufacture abroad. This speaker will suggest some of the major criteria you will want to have in mind before entering into a joint venture-suggest how to go about selecting your oversea partner-explain some of the pitfalls that may develop. 

Kermit Fischer, president, Fischer & Porter Co., Warminster, Pa.

VIII. Manufacturing abroad: The case for the wholly owned subsidiary 

Although a larger capital outlay is usually called for, there are situations where the wholly owned subsidiary may be the best feasible route to maximum profits. This speaker will suggest the conditions under which the wholly owned subsidiary must be considered, including the U.S. and foreign country tax rates. 

Luncheon, 12:30 p.m. to 1:30 p.m.

THE ALTERNATIVES COncluded 

William D. Vaughn, president, Ooverseas Management Services, Inc.,

New York City IX. The holding company 

This speaker will suggest some of the reasons for using a foreign based corporation for your oversea business-check factors you must have in mind in selecting a particular foreign country-outline business advantages and disadvantages inherent in the oversea holding company. He will also discuss the relative merits of various "tax haven" countries-bring you up to date on current 

Washington thinking on tax havens.

SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS WHICH WILL INFLUENCE THE ROUTE YOU TAKE 

Michael A. Luckey, representative, International Division, Chemical Bank New 

York Trust Co., New York City, and Walter A. Slowinski, resident partner, Baker, McKenzie & Hightower, Washington, D.C. 

X. Finance and taxation-major factors in deciding your route-a panel 

Exporting, licensing, contract manufacturing, joint venture, wholly owned subsidiary, and/or holding company? While the political climate may tip the scales in favor of a particular vehicle, finance and taxation are prime factors in decisionmaking. This panel will analyze the pros and cons of each vehicle in terms of the long-range objectives of your company, and outline sources of financial assistance open to the smaller company and key tax considerations you must consider. 

S. Delvalle Goldsmith, senior partner, Langner, Parry, Card & Langner, New 

York City, and Francis J. Sullivan, partner, Liddy, Sullivan, Hart, Daniels &

Stemple, New York City XI. Special problems of patents and trademarks—a panel 

In many companies, one of the knottiest problems in going abroad is the exploitation and protection of industrial property rights, including patents and trademarks. These speakers will suggest how you can go about selecting patent and trademark counsel at home or abroad, outline the basic considerations for filing patents and registering trademarks and other key steps in securing the company's position abroad-check pitfalls to be avoided. 

Wednesday, June 28, 1961, 9 a.m. to 12 m.

SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS 

Frank S. Wilson, president, International Division, the Gabriel Co., Cleveland, Ohio 

XII. Special problems in marketing when selling abroad

Customary benchmarks do not always apply in oversea marketing situations. 

This is especially true when you are entering a new market for the first time or introducing a new product. Advertising and budget techniques, exclusive distributorships, participation in trade shows, and trade associations should be examined in the light of foreign approaches. This speaker will suggest marketing problems which will call for a solution different from those applied in the United States, outline how other U.S. companies have met problems. 

John C. Borton, Acting Director, Bureau of Foreign Commerce, U.S. Depart ment of Commerce, Washington, D.C.

XIII. Current Federal action of special interest to the smaller company 

A. The U.S. Department of Commerce.-The Bureau of Foreign Commerce is stepping up its efforts to assist American business in broadening its markets. 

This speaker will give an up-to-the-minute briefing on many services that the Bureau of Foreign Commerce is prepared to deliver, show how the smaller company can tap little known sources of help.

Charles E. Houston, economist, Export-Import Bank, Washington, D.C., and 

Charles M. Meriwether, member of the Board of Directors, Export-Import 

Bank, Washington, D.C.

B. Export-Import Bank.-The Export-Import Bank has recently completed a review of its operations with expanding foreign markets in mind. This speaker will outline the basic assistance available through the Bank, suggest trends of current thinking on expanding American business opportunities abroad through facilities of the Bank.

XIV. Briefing session summary 

Mr. Frederick E. Pamp, Jr., manager of the International Management Division of the American Management Association, opened the seminar with the following remarks:

SEMINAR ON INTERNATIONAL BUSINESS OPENING REMARKS OF FREDERICK E. PAMP, JR., MANAGER OF THE INTER- NATIONAL Management DIVISION OF THE AMERICAN MANAGEMENT ASSOCIATION, NEW YORK, N.Y. 

Mr. PAMP. Well, gentlemen, we haven't had the pleasure of receiving Mr. Javits yet, but we trust that the U.S. Senate will come through in good style, as usual.

Meanwhile, we don't like to change our AMA habit, fetish almost, of starting on time and stopping on time. So let us get the formalities out of the way, get you used to the baseball field, so we can give our speakers a chance to tell you what they mean to say. 

I am Fred Pamp, division manager for the International Division of AMA. 

I see some of you here have been to AMA before, but in many cases you have not, and let me summarize our nature and mission as quickly as I can. 

AMA is a nonprofit membership association of some 32,000 members dedicated to the idea that managers can and should train each other, help each other, educate each other. We serve as a clearinghouse for management knowledge. We don't claim to be a source of that knowledge in any large way, but management is a discipline and a science. 

Insofar as it is a science it is really happening on the job every day, and the men doing that job are the best ones to help others. Thousands of American Management Association members have found over the years that for every 1 item they share they get back 15 or 20. 

Now this seminar is in the tradition of what we at AMA call orientations. We try to get together those men who have had outstanding experience in some central line of management practice, and have them tell their story. But in the process we like to mix also the experience and knowledge of the people sitting where you are today. 

So the first thing I want to emphasize is that the atmosphere of this seminar is free and open and all of us are looking for answers, including the men on this platform. If you have something to offer from your experience or even some new way of putting a question which might be revealing and helpful, please don't hesitate to do it, to add your part to it. 

The leading phenomenon of American business today, I suppose and I can say this with some authority-is the one central fact which can be put in two different ways. The negative is "foreign competition." The positive side of it is "the world market." One way it's

a problem; the other an opportunity. Stating it these two ways, I think, brings out the point.

The world market is the positive opportunity for companies of every size, of every type. Because the ways of meeting the challenge of that world market are so many, so varied, and so much need to be custom-built to the company and its product, we thought that a briefing session like this was particularly useful at this time. 

Many of the big companies know-or think they know-pretty much what they are doing, where they are going, how they are going to move. Many of them have the resources even to make up for a late start, previous mistakes, and previous neglect.

The small company frequently feels in a different situation. What we are trying to get across here is that there are a variety of ways in which your company, no matter how small or how arcane you might think its business, has a chance to step into this wider arena and make itself felt. So during these 22 day I hope you will be working up for yourselves and in your own minds the ways in which you can take a step-by-step approach to developing your place in this world market. 

I think you will find first of all that there definitely is a place for small companies in the world market that is here and coming. 

In the second place, the job of getting into it is not as hopelessly complicated as it seems. 

The International Division of AMA is almost the youngest of the divisions of AMA. We are only 4 years old. There are already over 

5,000 executives who have come to the seminars organized by this division. We have tried and in some cases succeeded, I think, in embodying some of the knowledge that has been put out in these seminars in some of our publications. You will see them in our catalog. 

But I do want to mention two that are coming.

Bob Theobald, a fine young economist, is working on a handbook for investment for which we have canvassed and polled all the countries of the western world and neutral world, and asked their statements on their policy on American exports and American investment in their country. Therefore, we have definite policy statements about the countries' attitudes, but we also have specific examples of what companies have done, and we have tabulated the basic statistical data that a company needs to know to begin to make up its mind about operating in that country.

Secondly, let me call your attention to an insert in your books. In 

April we conducted a survey of 700 smaller companies in the United States about what they were doing in foreign operations and what their thoughts and their desires were on it. You will find it in your notebook in the section after the speakers' outlines.¹ 

You may be interested in seeing what the rest of the group of managers think about this problem. 

I think the main thing that we want to say here is that it is not just policy or because everybody else is doing it that one should be thinking about foreign operations. It is also defending the future of your company. But, more important, with the somewhat increased risk, maybe the increased competition of doing business abroad, you 

1 See Appendix II, p. 212.

will find that the profit elements for many companies are far beyond what they are in domestic operation.

I want to turn the platform over now to Tom Cogan, assistant division manager, who has had charge of making up this program. 

OPENING REMARKS OF THOMAS J. COGAN, ASSISTANT DIVISION MAN- AGER, AMERICAN MANAGEMENT ASSOCIATION, NEW YORK, N.Y. 

Mr. COGAN. Good morning, gentlemen. I am glad to see you here.

Your presence here represents, I believe, a significant milestone in the history of small business. It has been estimated that only 12,000 of the 300,000 manufacturing companies in the United States are doing foreign business. You don't have to do much research to learn that most large U.S. firms are included within the 12,000 already doing business abroad. Profit opportunities abroad today, then, are open primarily to smaller U.S. companies just getting into foreign business. 

Why aren't more smaller U.S. companies doing business abroad?

Fred Pamp has already suggested two reasons: first, a feeling that foreign business is extremely complicated, and secondly, that it is harder to compete with larger companies in foreign business. I hope what you will learn here will convince you that neither is necessarily so.

Perhaps of equal importance is the fact that many closely held smaller companies since World War II have found the domestic U.S. market sufficiently attractive to absorb their full attention. But there are many forces at work affecting this picture, and there is sufficient evidence in to draw some tentative conclusions.

First, profit margins on foreign business are generally better than on domestic business. The increasing number of smaller companies reporting business abroad during the last few years lends weight to this fact. A second factor is this: Many companies which experienced a downturn in domestic business in 1957-58 and again last year found their foreign sales held up well, and in some cases improved. 

We don't have to tell you how beneficial this can be to your manufacturing and inventory management-your overall profit picture. 

A third factor is the rapid rate of growth of several oversea markets-the European Common Market-to cite but one example. Purchasing power is up and better standards of living are commonplace abroad.

Still a fourth factor is your industry situation. For example, if you are a supplier to a major industry, such as the automotive industry, you may find your industry paving your way for you into foreign markets. Your industry prefers to buy from you wherever they are assembling, if you are organized to deliver the goods. 

There is a fifth factor which holds even more promise for the smaller company abroad than at home. We all know that many smaller U.S. companies compete successfully in our domestic market with larger companies, because the smaller company can engineer the specialty refrigeration equipment, or neon tube, or what have you. This is an advantage the smaller company has especially in oversea business where the customer's needs cannot easily be satisfied by the volume, standardized product line of the larger company.

Finally, we can concede, I think, that we do not have a corner on every bit of technology. Oversea business can and does result in manufacture in the United States, under a licensing agreement, of a foreign product which can give you a competitive edge here at home and add to your overall profit picture. 

Indeed, there are defensive reasons too, as Fred Pamp has suggested, for oversea business. But the incentives are so compelling that we won't take the time to delve into them. 

For these reasons, it gives AMA considerable satisfaction to sponsor this first in a series of seminars intended to guide smaller companies in their search for oversea profit opportunities. In this effort we are fortunate to have the invaluable assistance of several top management people from smaller companies such as your own plus several specialists on technical matters like taxes and finance and patents and trademarks.

As you listen to the presentations keep in mind how you can adapt their experience to your own situation, don't hesitate to ask questions, and by all means share your own experience with us. The major reward for our leaders and speakers is to learn something from you in this experience sharing process of an AMA seminar. 

So, it gives me great pleasure to introduce Mr. John Breck, cochairman of our seminar, who will in turn introduce Mr. Bob Hood, our seminar chairman.

Mr. Breck is the executive vice president of John H. Breck of 

Springfield, Mass., and is a graduate of the American International 

College and the University of Pennsylvania. He is a member of the 

Brand Names Foundation, and is active on its board of directors. 

All I can tell you is that Mr. Breck, among many other fine people, is a good friend of AMA, and we certainly thank him and Mr. Hood and Mr. Wilson and all the others who will be here to come to help share their experience with you, and we hope that you, too, will share your experience with us. 

Thank you very much. 2 

OPENING REMARKS OF JOHN H. BRECK, JR., SEMINAR COCHAIRMAN 

Mr. BRECK. When Fred Pamp spoke a few words to us, he indicated that in his opinion the international business is not hopelessly complicated. Well, I would agree with him that it isn't hopelessly complicated. It is complicated. And I am sure that all of you people who have had some experience in international business will agree with me. 

However, there is a sufficient incentive to solve these complications, and we are going to hear our speaker, Bob Hood, discuss going abroad, the unmined profit area for the smaller company. Bob Hood, who is chairman of our discussion, is president of the Ansul Chemical Co., at Marinette, Wis., and he attended the University of Wisconsin. Bob

GOING ABROAD: THE PROFIT OPPORTUNITIES OF INTERNATIONAL BUSINESS FOR THE SMALLER COMPANY LECTURE BY ROBERT C. HOOD, SEMINAR CHAIRMAN 

Mr. HOOD. Thank you. Good morning. It is my job here to get us started on an exploration of American industry's own particular "new frontier”-international marketing. It is very much a reality 

I am sure, as you look at your program, you will realize that we have with us many talented and interesting speakers. However, I would like to start this out with some general observations. to us. 

First, how do I as a president perceive this problem? What are some of the problems that our company has been involved in? I would like to share with you some of these problems and perhaps discuss some of the things we have learned. 

I hasten to point out that this is a growing area, and, as most American businessmen are finding these days, there are more "experts" in this area than almost any other. We must constantly judge how many of these experts to use and what advise to use. Although we must make decisions we must try to remain flexible. So keeping some of this in mind, let us take a look at some of the things that we have encountered.

First of all, it seems apparent to me that the most important thing is the market itself. In other words, we must carefully evaluate the 

3 Robert C. Hood, chairman, is president of the Ansul Chemical Co. at Marinette, Wis. 

He attended the University of Wisconsin.

Mr. Hood joined the Ansul Chemical Co. in 1939 and worked successively as accountant, sales correspondent, founder and editor of the company newspaper, advertising manager, secretary, and vice president. He was elected president in 1949 at the age of 32. As an official Government representative Mr. Hood has traveled widely to study and instruct in the field of organizational practice. In 1954 he was a member of a special training mission to Austria sponsored by the Foreign Operations Administration of the State Department, and in 1956 was a delegate to the International Human Relations Conference in Rome.

Mr. Hood has spoken at a number of development conferences at Wayne University, Cornell, New York University, Oklahoma A. & M., the University of Wisconsin-and before business groups including the U.S. Chamber of Commerce, the Society for Advancement of Management, and AMA. He is the author of "The Art of Managing," a chapter in "Top Management Handbook," published by McGraw-Hill, and of the forthcoming 

"Business Organization as a Cross Product of Its Purposes and of Its Environment, written for the University of Michigan's Foundation for Research on Human Behavior. 

He has served as a director of the Young Presidents' Organization and has been chairman of the Advisory Committee of the National Training Laboratories. He has also served on the board of the Business Relations Committee of the Chamber of Commerce of the United States. Mr. Hood is active in a number of civic and welfare groups both nationally and in his hometown of Marinette, Wis.

market we are considering. This seems to me the rationale of why we are doing business anywhere, whether it be abroad or in Texas or any other place. If we are interested in going abroad, what then are the markets abroad and where are the markets?

Because our companies are generally smaller, we must make an especially critical review of our products. I suppose that if we entertain any interest in going abroad we must have some feeling that these products are marketable abroad-whether through export, through licensing or through actual manufacturing.

One of the things that we have learned is that you can get things manufactured almost any place in the world. To make a broad statement, the crux of our problem is not manufacturing the product. It is distributing the product. It is nothing more than we have learned here in the U.S. market. Time and again when we have looked into a country we have noted that our technical men seem to find out rather readily how we can get things manufactured. But it is in the nevernever land of distribution that we find our real challenge. 

Who knows the market? What are the markets?

Gaining information on the international market is much more difficult than it is for the U.S. market. Most of our companies, being small, do not have readily available adequate data on markets for our products. So we find a little here and a little there, and more or less conduct our own market surveys as we go along. This, then, creates even more of a risk and even more of a problem as we try to decide how to distribute, and how to identify the markets and the potential markets.

When we contemplate going abroad, we must look at the present status of our products in the United States. Certainly, this will be one of the reasons that has led to our interest in international marketing. In our own company we find that some of our product lines have fairly well saturated the U.S. market. However, the technical knowhow we have developed is a very significant factor and a very marketable commodity indeed.

It certainly seems that in some areas abroad, taking this technical know-how and applying it to a new market creates an important opportunity for us. Most of us have started in this area by exporting. 

It starts with people writing in and asking about our products. We send price lists and product data and then gradually we develop an export department. This export department begins to make contacts in various countries around the world, and as these foreign contacts come to see us, we develop close relationships. 

However, now, we are going to see them. As our export operation grows we begin to run into new problems with our distributors-problems of higher tariff walls or various other national concerns. At this point we become aware of the need to rather scrupulously examine the market and its potential. We consider the possibility of licensing. 

We wonder if this area warrants a partnership arrangement, or do we require a manufacturer. There seems to be a logical development if you look on this in the continuum of time. We are at various development stages in different countries at the same time. Often we find ourselves manufacturing in one country and in partnership with somebody in another. Possibly we are still exporting or at any one of a number of stages in the refinement of marketing abroad.

It seems important, from our experience, that you do not slide into this without full awareness of what is happening. You must as a corporate group develop a point of view as to what you want to do in this area. You must have something in mind, even if it is very general.

I am talking about policy. I have made a statement in our company repeatedly, and I have no reason to change it at this time. I have said that if we as a company are not an international company in the next 10 years, our chances of growth are materially reduced and our survival is jeopardized.

Now that is a nice flowing statement; but to clothe these bones is a long, hard job. I started by saying that markets are the key. As a businessman I must look to every market, but I feel now that I must look especially hard at the markets of the free world. We no longer do business in the isolated confines of the United States. I feel that we have a real challenge ahead of us as management, just to find out whether we should be abroad or whether we shouldn't.

There are some terms that bother me a little. The term "abroad," as an example, and the term "foreign." If you adopt the point of view that you are an international company, nothing is "foreign" or "alien" or "abroad"-the world is your market and you must learn how to deal in a larger marketplace and be an economic and social part of it.

We approach this with what appears to be a frightening lack of experience. However one of the things we found that has been valuable has been our experience in the U.S. market. If a company distributes in more than its local geographical area in other words, throughout the United States-then it has been dealing in a fairly large marketplace.

Now this is an advantage when you go abroad because you have been in as large a common market as the world has known, and the know-how you have gained is invaluable. Obviously, you are not going to duplicate abroad the ways in which you have approached the market of the United States. But it does give you a "having been there" advantage that many of the people of Europe or Latin America or Japan do not have. With the opening up of the Common Market in Europe and the Outer Seven, this is one of the positive things that we can use to advantage.

The similarities are not as evident because the market is not as "common" as the term indicates. Standardization of products is a long way from reality, but there is beginning to be a great deal of interchange. The aspects of marketing and the problems that we have already worked through here in the United States are going to be very helpful to us in Europe, I am sure. 

Another aspect of the problem that seems important to me is the balancing between the specific and the general. Let me explain. We must ask, "What are the general factors that must be considered in this market?" Then, "What are the specific ones that are peculiar to our particular product or company?" To put it another way, we must examine the general free market areas horizontally as thoroughly as we examine the product and its potential vertically. Then we must relate these considerations and balance them.

There is plenty of advice on this, but you have to digest it carefully. 

It seems to me that in a small company this responsibility falls rather firmly on the shoulders of the president. This is an area that he cannot delegate very well because of its importance to the total organization and because of the timing. He must get involved himself. 

I learned from bumps on my head, having tried to delegate this in connection with an operation we have in Mexico. It didn't work out at all. Just a couple of months ago we finally got out from under various and sundry relationships and back to the spot we should have started on. We lost about 2 years, but perhaps we learn through these trials.

You cannot delegate the early stages of investigation into foreign operations.

I mentioned earlier that this is a rather fruitful area for consultants of all shapes and sizes. They are all around us and each has his own particular way of going about doing it. It seems to me we can't overlook them, and it is wise to constantly check with them. But again I remind you that this is an area where you must assume responsibility. 

In choosing the best course of action, you must listen to as many people as you can without getting confused, and then actually go and see for yourself. There is nothing like physically being there, walking through these places, and developing a feeling about the market. 

This is a time-consuming executive requirement in my opinion. 

It is a new area that very few of us have had any training in; and yet it is not an area where you can get a vice president in charge of international operations, and say, "Now you take care of this end and we will be behind you. You come in with your plans and explain them to us. 

We have found that until we got an active group on our board of directors involved, we were just treading water. We now have three members of a board of seven and myself who are not only actively involved in our American operation but are examining it and relating it carefully to the challenge of international marketing. We are willing to spend the required traveling, discussion and negotiation time. 

I can't speak too highly of the need for planning. It is harder to plan in this area because there are fewer models. And you have to begin with some general policy statements as they relate to the marketing areas.

Let me give you an example. About a year and a half ago, after a good deal of investigation, we decided to try to set up in England with a particular firm that acts as a distributor. We wanted to manufacture for them in England and thereby learn how to manufacture abroad more effectively.

In Holland we were going to do it the other way around. We were going to try to take over and learn the selling end because they were stronger in manufacturing. So we had a general policy. As yet we haven't completed these moves, although this is a year and a half later. 

After further investigation we went over and tested these hypoth- This brought us back to some of the areas of market research. 

In the United States if we are coming out with a product, we at least like to have some data to go by. There are many firms abroad that do marketing work, vague as it may be. So we started by having a market research job done for us in the European market. eses. 72811-61-2

This was of very real help to us in one of our product areas-fireextinguishing equipment. They tabulated all the various people that manufactured these products in the various countries, contacted them personally and got acquainted with them. Then we followed up by actually going and talking to these people. 

After about a year of this, we began to know the people in the marketplace. 

It was out of this that we came up with the tentative policy that I mentioned to you as it relates to the Outer Seven and the Inner Six. 

In starting with a broad idea and gradually refining it, I can't overstress the fact that this is going to take top talent and top time. 

This is not, in my opinion, just another market. This is an area of business that we know very little about. When you consider manufacturing abroad, this is going to mean the involvement of directors. As you know, the problem of return on investment of stockholders' money is vitally important and must be safeguarded. I believe it is necessary to get more from your directors than mere approval-you must get genuine involvement.

One of the areas we discovered which I think is most significant is what we can refer to as the cultural area. This is extremely important abroad.

Let me elaborate a little. Let us assume that you are starting a new operation here in the United States with another company. Perhaps you are building a plant in California or some other place than where you are now or maybe you are negotiating with somebody to do this. You take for granted all the cutural factors that we live with in the United States. In any of the foreign countries which we go into, the cultural factors are different. This is something, it seems, that the American businessman has learned very slowly. He has felt that his techniques have been satisfactory and that his know-how has been sufficient to carry him. It seems to me rather clear that starting sometime in the late fifties a good many things changed. We are not now the only major manufacturer in the world. We must now share the world market with other countries who can manufacture and sell equally as well as we do. 

For this reason the cultural characteristics of these other countries are important factors that we must understand. 

I am firmly convinced that this is an area where all of us must spend more time than we would normally think necessary. We must do this because the study of the cultures of other countries is so new and unfamiliar to most of us.

In the last year and a half we have had an interesting association with one of our consultants in this international area. This gentleman is an anthropologist and he had been writing a good deal about the mistakes American businessmen were making abroad. I said all right, let's stop writing about it and let's start doing something about it. And, so, this gentleman worked with us part time in this investigating area. He was a great help to us. 

Let me give you an example. Two years ago we spent 2 or 3 weeks in Japan negotiating with 13 Japanese businessmen. One of the tricks of the trade, I guess you would call it, or cultural realities that the Japanese have learned about us, is that we are always in a hurry. 

So they put it to use. They say, "Let us find when you are going

to go home and we can just stall until you are about ready to go. 

Then we will begin seriously to negotiate at that time and this will give us a psychological edge. 99 So what did we do? Well, at least we were forearmed. When we got there they said, "When are you going home?" We said, "We don't know." They said, "What do you mean you don't know? When are your return tickets?"

We said, "We don't have any. We didn't come halfway around the world to do something of this nature in a hurry. We are going to stay here until we get the job done." 

Well, this was the first inning. We went along with this anthropologist who said, "Pay attention to the cultural aspects." We did and it opened a whole new world to us. 

On Thursday we said, "We don't seem to be getting anywhere.

Let us break off for the weekend. We would like to go to Kyoto and a few other places and see some of your wonderful country. وو They were then on the defensive, and they thought we were liable to go home before they got anything.

This is a small item but it is an example that shows what an important thing time can be to your negotiations.

Americans are not accustomed to using time in this manner. 

I mentioned earlier the problem of risk. We can't afford to take too many risks, as you know, and yet it seems to us that we must learn to understand the risks in this new area of international marketing. 

This is why I mentioned the necessity of involving key responsible people in your company.

Let us begin to look at this concept of going abroad as a market development problem. In our planning we did quite a careful job of looking at and identifying the markets of the world. We attempted to learn where they stood in the continuum of development as they related to our products.

Now, if it were a raw material product, that would be one thing. 

If it needed a certain degree of development, it would be another. 

We found that the industrialized, mature countries were the ones in which we should be primarily interested. We felt it unwise to go into completely undeveloped countries or ones which were not expected to arrive at some point of economic maturity for another 5 or 

10 years. These countries are farther down our list.

Looking at the free markets, we analyzed it roughly in this manner. 

This gave us a point of view as to emphasis and timing. It gave us a plan as to when to get in these markets and when to be working on them.

It takes a great deal of time to get started on this, and again I would like to stress the problem of timing. I think it is evident now that if one is going to get into the Common Market or into that general area, time is running out. We may have 5 years-we may have less time. And, so, if you have not really considered a move it is getting late in the day. Even after you've done a lot of work, it takes some time before you can put all of these things together. 

Timing in the various markets, identity of where the markets are, and where the market is in this development stage, are, it seems to me, vitally important.

I would like to stress another thing we found most useful.

This is what we call the team approach. In your investigations of the international markets you are not only in a strange and foreign cultural environment but you are generally communicating in other languages. You are either speaking French or Japanese or Spanish or Portuguese these days, and you face a natural barrier unless some of you speak the language. Even if you do, you still have this problem to an extent.

I come back to the kind of team we have used in our investigative efforts. We have relied on a four-man group, working interchangeably. We have made a general policy of never sending one man alone. 

We feel that his interpretation of the situation might not be the only one at the time; and we interchange people on the teams we send to each country. Therefore when we finally sit down to negotiate we have four men who have had many exposures to the situation. 

It is not only exposure to the business situation that I am referring to. It is exposure to the people and the cultures--and that takes time. Frequently some of the people that you depend on and that are very capable in the United States are not so adroit abroad. 

I mentioned earlier the gamut of international marketing-all the way from taking our product and exporting it to manufacturing abroad. I'd like to talk just a little more about certain aspects of this.

One of the things we found out in our investigations has been the advantage of what we could call partnerships in the various countries. 

Because of our size, we think carefully about invading a market alone. We try to get a parner in a particular area and develop a 

50-50 relationship with him so that he has his interest and we have ours. This idea seems to be growing in acceptance, because neither one side nor the other dominates and dictates. 

In other words, you don't have to come into a foreign country and start your own operation and employ people. In a partnership you share in the economy and the culture. Then, in case of expropriation or any of the other problems which might arise, you have a Ĵocal partner who is vitally interested and in a position to act. From a small company's standpoint, it seems to me that foreign partners offer a real advantage, and I urge you to at least investigate it. 

I am talking about partnerships beyond the license idea.

The relationship might well include licensing, but basically it means full partnership in this particular market.

Well, it seems to me that I have talked a good deal about our experience, and perhaps now I might direct myself to some of your questions. Perhaps you have some at this time which might be more pertinent than my rambling along in some of these areas. 

Before I start this however, I would like to call your attention to a book called "It Is Your Business." I know some of you may have seen it. It was written by John Harriman who is the financial editor, I think, or the financial columnist of the Boston Globe. This was brought to my attention by our treasurer. It has a wealth of interesting facts brought together with perspective and insight. I found it extremely valuable, and I bought enough copies to give to all our general managers and to all our directors. Many times we are dealing with problems minutely and we fail to get a total view on what we are trying to accomplish.

Mr. LAMBERT. Who is the publisher?

Mr. HOOD. Houghton Mifflin & Co., Boston. 

Mr. LAMBERT. What is the title? 

Mr. HOOD. "It Is Your Business.' 

"?

I don't know where that fits in, but it gives a perspective view of American business, what is really happening abroad, and what this means to you.

THE NATIONAL EXPORT POLICY ACT OF 1961 LECTURE OF HON. JACOB K. JAVITS, SENIOR U.S. SENATOR FROM THE STATE OF NEW YORK 

Senator JAVITS. Thank you very much. 

Gentlemen, I am glad to be with you this morning. 

I thank you, Mr. Hood, for introducing me so promptly because I have what in Washington are called floor problems. I have to get right back to Washington because we have a social security bill on the 

Senate floor.

Gentlemen, I am deeply concerned with this subject because I believe that, interestingly for businessmen, you stand at the crossroads of something that can be of inestimable value to our country as well as to your businesses. I think it is essential for American business to move actively and aggressively into the export field. 

I think it is also essential to our Nation to materially expand export trade now, with all that that implies. This not only goes for us but it also goes for our allies with whom we must have a great deal to do on this subject.

I would like first to congratulate the American Management Association, whose elaborate plant here I never saw, though this is my home town, on staging this kind of briefing session at this time for this particular purpose, because I think it is a rare opportunity and is rare- 

Îy as felicitious as it is right now.

You have heard about our discussions on the floor of the Senate and Congress generally on foreign aid. These will be succeeded, I assure you, by discussions on foreign trade. The Reciprocal Trade Agreement Act comes up for renewal next year. It will face a terrific fight, and yet it is essential to our country. 

The reason it will face a fight is that exports imply imports, and imports hurt people; at least some think they do, and hence, you have political pressures developing to eliminate imports in one line or another because they hurt a particular local-interest industry or a particular community. And, yet, if you eliminate imports of one kind or another, you lessen the capability of other nations to accept your

exports, and you invite retaliation, and, what is even worse, you invite retaliation by the Soviet bloc, which is a very serious threat in this whole field.

Now I am not without knowledge in this field. I am not only a Senator, but I am also chairman of the Economic Committee of the NATO Parliamentarians. This is my third year, and, so, on a number of occasions in every year I have gotten from experts a thorough education on what happens in trade all over the world. In addition, I serve on the Senate Small Business Committee, on the Senate Banking and Currency Committee, and on the Joint Economic Committee, and I have dealt with this subject all my life. I used to be chairman of the Economic Policy Subcommittee of the House of Representatives. 

I say that to you because I hope you will not dismiss what I say as just being the recitation of a few facts that somebody wrote down for me and let it go at that. I assure you that what I tell you is not based upon any such superficial approach. 

Now, I believe it absolutely essential to our Nation that we double our exports in the next 10 years from $20 billion a year to $40 billion a year. Now, we doubled our exports between 1950 and 1960. So we showed that it can be done. They went from $10 billion to $20 billion. Some of that was price inflation, but a good deal of it was actual increase in goods.

Between 1959 and 1960 our exports to the six member nations of the European Economic Community increased by more than $1 billion, going up an enormous percentage rate verging on close to 50 percent. The significance of that is that it shows what can be done when you begin to get integrated markets. And when people begin to rationalize the problems created by their own boundaries and let us not forget, and I heard what Mr. Hood said about timing-let us not forget that you are having the beginning of that in the Americas. 

There are now two common market areas which are develmoping, one in Central America and one in South America, and I think the time to look into them is now and not when they are actually formed and organized as the European countries have organized under their own treaty.

Now why is it essential that we get into the export field in a much bigger way? I will give my reasons for that because I found that businessmen derive great satisfaction in feeling that what they are doing, in quite a selfish business sense, is nonetheless in the very deep interest of the United States.

I notice the gentleman here from one of the banks. Probably there are a few other bank people here. Well, I know the banks very well, and I talk with their people many, many times, and the concept of business in the public interest is a very strong one among banks and bankers, who more and more feel themselves interested. So I lay certain considerations in the public domain before you this morning as an inducing cause for doubly hard work, doubly intelligent work and redoubled interest on your part, because your interest should be not only business but patriotism.

The first reason is that we are facing terrific competition from the Communists. They are moving especially into the less developed 

Between 1954 and 1959 the Soviet bloc increased their trade with the less developed areas by 165 percent, from $860 million to

$2.3 billion in roughly 6 years. And they expect even greater gains for 1960 although final statistics are not yet available.

In that 6-year period the ratio of U.S. trade over the Soviet bloc with these same nations was reduced from 9 to 1—that is, we did, as compared with the Soviets, 9 times as much trade with these 41 less developed nations to a ratio of 4 to 1. And the Soviet bloc is primed to draw these countries into economic dependence. 

The Soviet bloc has tremendous potentials in this area. It has tremendous potentials for economic warfare. It has already caused us considerable trouble in a few commodities-flax, tin, bauxite, and now residual fuel oil. Almost at will it can seriously disrupt free world markets by dumping material at a cut price.

Now this is a very serious danger and just indicates that the Soviets can and will play an increasing role in world trade. 

When Khrushchev said "I will bury you," he meant I will bury you economically, and he confidently believes he can do this just because of this power. Hence, this is one of the main reasons why more and more we have to be dedicated to the world as well as to our own country in terms of trade.

Second, we face a serious imbalance in our international payments. 

Just last year we were losing at the rate of $3.5 billion to $4 billion a year. Even we can't stand that. Hence we had to reverse the trend. 

Now the policies of the Government and business did reverse the trend very materially, and we now have an easily manageable basic deficit of less than a billion dollars a year as the difference between our outgo and income in terms of dollars. But it is generally believed that this relative stability is temporary, and the reason it is believed it is temporary is that our export surplus is not big enough to make it permanent. Our export surplus range in the area of $5 billion is now very heavily attributable to jet aircraft and cotton, and these are considered special situations. So everybody can expect and anybody who read the First National City Bank newsletter the other day-not to place you in competition-saw that they confidently anticipated that we will have a balance-of-payments crisis again in the fall of this year. 

The only way that can be dealt with on any permanent basis is by a material increase in our exports, and let us remember that the world hungers for American products. If we had the salesmanship and the credit medium-and I will come to that in a minute-by which to get them out, the world is starving for the very things we make, providing we can get them on the proper credit terms and we can sell them and put them to use in all these countries.

It is unbelievable that the U.S. steel production should be at a 70 percent rate in a world which is thirsting for the very steel which is not being produced and which absolutely needs it if it is to stay free and not go Communist.

So point No. 2 is the serious imbalance in our international payments. Point 3 is the lessening effect of foreign trade on economic recessions in the United States. It has been demonstrated time and again that foreign trade often holds up at a time when we have a domestic recession, and this is an extremely valuable anchor to windward especially where you are dealing, as I say, with a world which is so starved for the very thing we make. And I am not going to bemuse you again with all the detailed points. You know them very well.

The question is for how long can we extend credit, and will the free world as a whole follow through in the less developed areas until these areas do come through based on their development plans, because based on the facts that they have people and resources, they are bound to come through ultimately if we persevere enough. Even the British say with their crazy ground nut scheme, about which many of you know, in which they dropped a neat $160 million or $170 million, that if they had been able to go through with it, if they had the capital and were willing to go through with it, it would have been successful notwithstanding the fact that it was very fanciful and had a lot of bugs in it. But even they say in a scheme like that, if we had only been able to go through with it, it would have been successful. 

That indicates what I say, that most of these places are creditworthy if you were sure there would be followthrough in terms of Government and individual investment until they could actually mature on their development plan. 

Now I believe these things for these three above reasons at least, and there are many others in terms of the fact that the domestic market doesn't have a saturation point but that the exigencies of international affairs are such that there must be ultimately some ceiling on the development of the American standard of living in terms of our own defense needs, in terms of the toughness of our own people and in terms therefore of the development and expansion of industry, the great opportunities abroad. And I think there is a very interesting sidelight on that in the enthusiasm with which our young people have embraced the Peace Corps. There is something instinctive about the fact that the young American boy and girl wants this adventure which the West used to give and which the world now gives, and whether we like it or not this will put us out in the world and will put business out in the world. So, in emphasizing the export trade for business, small and large-and I will come to small business in a minute you are riding with a stream, you are riding with the current of our times.

Now what do we need to do in order to double our export trade in the next 10 years?

First we must be prepared to take our share of imports. This is absolutely indispensable. The dollar may go all over the world, but sometime it has got to come back here, and the same is true of what we export and what we get paid for. Those people have got to find their money coming back to them somehow, sometime, somewhere, and the only way that that can be done is ultimately if we take our fair share of imports just as we develop our export trade. 

Second, we must open up export opportunities to smaller U.S. business. This is the big area, and it still remains to be explored. 

There is a most infinitesimal participation by American small business in export trade, and in my opinion there you come right up to the point of credit and you have just got to be for the export guarantee operations of the Export-Import Bank so that they give a complete program both of political and commercial risks of export credit guarantees which in the first instance represent an underwriting after the credit has been taken by a bank or insurance company. But this is absolutely a very, very grave problem and in very grave need particularly on the part of small business.

The Small Business Administration, I think, is quite ready to help with this particular situation. That has been the nature of the testimony which we have had from them. But essentially this is a matter for the Export-Import Bank.

In other countries, notably in Britain and in West Germany, in France, Italy, and Japan, there is a tremendous program for both small and large business in the underwriting of export credits, and I will say to you gentlemen who are interested in the export trade that I have yet to see a real liveliness on the part of our banks and insurance companies in this regard.

Now our banks do pretty well although not as well as I think they ought to do, but at least I don't think they are nearly as much subject to criticism as are the insurance companies because here are tremendous pools of capital which are very, very useful for the purposes that we are discussing, and, when backed with Government guarantees, would have absolute security and yet make available to the business community tremendous new resources in terms of export trade.

There are other suggestions aside from the straight Export-Import Bank underwriting very much like an FHA underwriting of a mortgage for export credit. For example, one I would like to lay before you is one proposed to us by Francis X. Scafuro, vice president of the 

Bank of America International, of New York, who has been cooperating with the international section of the New York Board of Trade. 

His proposals are incorporated in two bills in the House, of which you may make a note if you like-H.R. 7102 and 7103, one by Mr. Multer of New York and the other by Mr. Widnall of New Jersey.*

He proposes an American Export Credit Guarantee Corporation to be established by Federal charter with the right to borrow Federal funds to make good on its guarantees, but functioning on a premium basis with the efficiency and the independence of a private corporation. 

Some 109 export organizations back this bill, and it deserves, I think, the serious consideration of the Congress, if the Export-Import Bank doesn't come up with a program, although it has got a draft program out and indeed has been conferring with some of our New 

York banks about it.

But the question is of consummation. We must have a very comprehensive program of export credit guarantees.

I might say to you, too, that another legislator, who has been rather active in this field and deserves a good deal of credit, is Senator Engle of California who is a colleague of mine on the Senate Small Business. 

Committee, and is also on the Senate Commerce Committee. He has been very active in this field."

So small business essentially needs the Export-Import Bank backing in terms of guarantees, and needs new pools of capital, that is, of available funds which will back it up in terms of credit extension in its exporting trade. 

H.R. 7102 (Multer), H.R. 7103 (Widnall), American Export Credits Guaranty Corporation Act of 1961, introduced in House May 16, 1961, and referred to House Banking and Currency Committee. 

5 S. 1729, a bill to expand the participation of American small business in exporting, introduced by Senator Engle and cosponsored by Senators Bartlett, Humphrey, Magnuson, McGee, Randolph, Smathers and Gruening, 87th Congress.

Also we had some testimony here in New York which indicated that there are a good many companies starting up which give a comprehensive service to small business and take over all of its problems of export sales in terms of documentation, credit, packaging, advice, selling, designing, advertising, and so forth.

Now these package deals may or may not be too expensive. I just don't know. But, in any case, the testimony before the Small Business Committee of the Senate is available to any of you who wish it, and gives a considerable detailing of those services. If you want a copy of that testimony, write my office and we will be glad to let you have it. 5a

Now the third thing which we have got to do in terms of our activities to increase exports is to enlist the cooperation of Western Europe. 

This is an enormous opportunity. 

In the first place, as some of you may know, I am launching quite a campaign to get the OECD, the new Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development, to back up our alliance for progress and to double it. I think the alliance for progress is just about half enough. That is the $1 to $2 billion a year which Latin America needs. It ought to be $2 to $4 billion a year in order to really do the job that needs to be done. And the Europeans can very well manage it. But beyond that there are some things they have to do themselves. 

For example, you know all trade is not just bilateral. It is multilateral, quadrilateral, quintilateral, and you can go on as far as you like from there.

One of the big bugs which we find now is that the European countries, by a lot of taxes called sumptuary taxes, of which I will be glad to give you a list if you are that interested, keep out some of the very important commodities sold by Latin America, notably coffee and cocoa. They also keep out a good deal of tea. 

We have estimated for example in our office, that as regards one tax, the sumptuary tax on coffee alone in only a few countries, to wit, Germany, France, Italy, if you didn't eliminate it but just reduced it to make it reasonable in relationship to a tax revenue purpose, and took away its penalty features which are now imposed by these three countries-kind of the remains from the past when they had no dollars and were in very difficult financial situations—you would enable Latin America to sell $60 to $100 million more of coffee every year to just these three countries alone. These are called sumptuary taxes, kind of an internal excise tax, sort of a sales tax, if you will.

I mention that only because it is an indication of what we can do to help ourselves, and a very striking indication of how we in the Western World are tying our own hands and making it tougher for ourselves to deal with these major problems. 

I myself have introduced two major measures to expand our exports and to deal with the problems created by our imports. One is called the National Export Policy Act (S. 852), and the other is the National 

Import Policy Act (S. 851). 

Ba "Small Business Exports and The World Market-1960," hearings before the Select 

Committee on Small Business, 86th Cong., 2d sess., Nov. 17, 1960, New York City; Dec. 

9, 1960, New Orleans and Dec. 14, 1960, San Francisco. S. Rept. 89 on Encouragement and Expansion of Exports by Small Business, Mar. 27, 1961, 87th Cong., 1st sess.

In the National Export Policy Act, I had the cooperation of every member of the Senate Small Business Committee. The bill is sponsored by 19 Senators, and is popularly known as the Sparkman-Javits bill. Senator Sparkman has done me the great honor of joining with me. He is the chairman of the Small Business Committee. This measure establishes a Council on Export Promotion in our Government, and proposes to augment very materially the export services, including advertising and the use of other media of that character, rendered both by the State Department and the Department of Commerce. It proposes to expand enormously our trade centers and trade missions. It proposes to run pilot projects which will be especially helpful to small business in respect of warehousing and market research in fields abroad. And, of course, it proposes to beef up tremendously our financial means for accelerating the export drive. 

This is a very important measure. It has an excellent chance for passage. It has rather impressive backing and can be of tremendous aid, especially to small business, and has enlisted the support of the whole Senate Small Business Committee. 

On the import side-and again I emphasize to you as exporters the critical importance of a proper and fair climate for imports into the United States-it seems to us in Washington, who think as I do, that there is no other way than adjustment assistance. Now, what adjustment assistance means, in effect, is that you take the communities or the line of business or even the individual business and certainly the worker who is put at a disadvantage by imports, and you try to do something for him, not by cutting down the imports or eliminating them, although that must stand on an economic bottom if it is a major and serious injury to American business. 

As you know, we have the escape clause, the peril point provisions with respect to negotiations, with the power of the Tariff Commission and the President in those regards, but when you get into a situation where imports are desirable in terms of either the Nation or your world trade position, but nevertheless hurt people, you have got to find some way of helping those people rather than eliminating the imports.

There we think that loans, tax incentives in terms of remachining or retooling or going into another line of business are very important to the businesses themselves, and with Senator Case of New Jersey I have introduced legislation to do these things. 

Also for the worker, we propose supplementary unemployment compensation which I might tell you has an excellent chance for passage. This is a very burning issue, as are early retirement for those who are 60 or over under the social security law, and relocation assistance if the worker wishes to move from place to place, and vocational retraining assistance which may very well find its way into the vocational educational bill which we are now considering. 

The bill S. 1379 (Sparkman-Javits) To encourage and promote the expansion through private enterprise of domestic exports in world markets, is an amended and amplified version of S. 852, a bill introduced by Senator Javits for himself and other Senators. 

Senators sponsoring S. 1379 in addition to Sparkman and Javits, are Senators Long of 

Louisiana, Humphrey, Smathers, Morse, Bible, Randolph, Engle, Bartlett, Williams of 

New Jersey, Moss, Saltonstall, Schoeppel, Cooper, Scott, Prouty, Case of New Jersey, and Keating. Report issued by Senate Select Committee on Small Business, S. Rept. 89, "Small Business and the World Market," Mar. 27, 1961.

That, I think, gives you a fairly rounded picture of what we are doing in Washington in the efforts to accelerate this strong posture by our country in terms of foreign aid, and perhaps will suggest some ideas to you as to how you may fit into it.

Many good men have come up with many ideas, as I mentioned to you, for example, Mr. Scafuro's idea and others which have been presented to us, those ideas I have myself espoused, not original with me either but discussed and put forth by many distinguished economists who appeared before us in the Joint Economic Committee. 

I think a good many of these will be enacted into law. Some may be delayed until the later session of Congress. Some will run afoul of the rivalries in Government agencies, or perhaps be the victims of timidity in industry and reluctance to blaze new trails. But I do think there will be a supporting and helpful body of legislation, and I would urge you in figuring your own export plans to include the idea that the Federal Government will help with the general export picture either in the ways that I have explained or in other ways. 

I might also point out that if any of you have any ideas, let us have them because, after all, they can be made merchandisable where I work, and this is where we can do something about them, and I assure you of our deep and burning interest. And I think I can assure anybody who has had any experience with me or my office that we don't toss any idea out. We examine it and do our utmost to find some way in which we can use all the ideas we get. 

The issue before us is, really, how best can the peoples of the world satisfy their hunger for the world's goods and services. If the answer is through the private economic system, then the private economic system has got to rise to the enormous challenge which the world poses to it. It needs to send its goods to Malaya and Senegal and Brazil and Iraq and Nigeria with the same efficiency and same security with which it supplies eager millions in New York or any other city in the 

United States. And I must say in conclusion that I am very impressed with the fact that an organization like this, which is so seriously engaged in this work, would get you gentlemen together in order to endeavor to give you an education in this field. 

I would also like to point out to you-and I hope very much that you will take this away with you-I think it is very important in terms of government that, much as you like to think of politicians as fellows who run after the next vote or are worrying about the next election or maneuvering for whatever will be their own personal position-I have served in the Congress of the United States now for 8 years, and in the Senate for 5 years. That gives me a total of 13 years of legislative service. And I would tell you, gentlemen, that in the main--not that it is by any means unanimous, but in the main, the men and women who are serving in the House of Representatives and the Senate are just as dedicated as you are to the purposes and the aspirations for which you sent them there. There are a lot of good brains and a lot of good information and a lot of very smart people down there trying to do the Nation's business. 

Francis X. Scafuro, chairman, National Coordinating Committee For Export Credit Guarantees, New York Board of Trade, Inc., 1 Liberty Street, New York 5, N.Y. Pro posals of the National Coordinating Committee are contained in bills, H.R. 7102 and 

H.R. 7103, 87th Congress.

If any of you have ever testified before a Senate or House committee, I think you will go away a lot more respectful of what we know about our subjects than when you got there. 

Also, we are deeply interested in what the American business community proposes to do about the export business and the import business. We know better than anybody else that even with a $5 billion aid bill you don't begin to scratch the surface of what can be done for this world when you figure that foreign trade is $35 billion a year. 

And all the aid we give Brazil, for example, can be swept down the sewer in one afternoon if there is a drastic fall in the price of coffee, and you can duplicate that in every country in Latin America, in Africa and the Middle East. In other words, we are no fools either. 

We understand what we can and what we cannot do. And I will tell you, gentlemen, freedom will not win unless the business community of the United States learns its part in how to operate in respect of our policy all over the world. And if it does, and learns it well, then the victory of freedom is unquestionable. We will pulverize Mr. 

Khrushchev once we learn how to use our resources effectively. 

Our economy is not less than 2½ times his; maybe 3 times. We have a vaunted reputation in the world of knowing how to supply the very things everybody wants. The only point is that they don't trust us to do it as yet, and here is where the American private economic system is very important.

So, while I come up here on a very hard trip-I left Washington this morning and I must go back at once-I assure you that I don't do it because I want to hear myself talk or because you are such nice fellows. I do it because this is a burning question in the public interest and you can, standing where you do, be materially helpful to the future of our country and to the future of freedom. I hope very much that as you go into these things in your day-to-day problems, you will add an additional quotient of adventure and the willingness to take some risk. You can make the analogy with what other men and women have risked in war, and I assure that this is just as much of a war as anything we have ever fought. Thank you very much.

estimates, of course, are at the most a cost of $200 million or $300 million a year for all the things I mentioned. There are not too many people involved, and yet they are scattered enough throughout the country, including my own State of New York. 

Any of you knowing my State here-this is your State, I imagine, for most of you-know what our problem is in Gloversville and Amsterdam and Fulton and Elmira and a number of other places throughout our State, and we have just got to help those people. ~And, yet, if you do help them, it doesn't cost very much and they should be helped.
