Question. Senator Javits, how do you spell sumptuary?

Senator JAVITS. S-u-m-p-t-u-a-r-y. 

Question. And what are the other taxes that you were referring to?" 

Senator JAVITS. Well, they are taxes on coffee, tea, cocoa, and one or two other items of that character. Straight consumer items which in Europe have materially improved their standard of living, which now, because of the vestigial remains of the past in Europe where they are still thinking in terms of personal economy, are robbing the world of this amount of fertilizer really, and we have just got to break it. 

Question. You commented on export and import policy. I wonder if you would care to comment on foreign investment policy of the new administration as it affects particularly the smaller companies. 

Senator JAVITS. Well, I think this tax plan which the new administration has put out for taxing the income of foreign subsidiaries upon their accrual, which is really what it does, has been a serious blow to confidence in foreign investment. I don't find very many takers for this in the Congress, and I hope it will not interfere with foreign investment because I don't think anything is going to happen about this tax program. I don't know why they cooked it up and put it out, but they couldn't have done a more damaging thing to investment abroad.

There are limitations to private investment abroad because they seek profits and they can only go into profit-promising ventures, which are not necessarily basic ventures like ports and roads and education and sanitation which are the business of government. But nonetheless private investments are tremendously vital. They introduce not only new capital; they introduce new tastes and they also introduce a good deal of technical know-how which often goes far beyond the province in which the investments actually are placed in a particular country.

So private investment is critically important, very good, and I am all for it. I believe it should be encouraged, and what I would rather · see than this tax bill, which I think is just as wrong as it can bethere are lots of other ways to raise the few hundred million dollars. involved which are a lot more constructive is the Boggs bill which would give benefits and advantages and inducements and incentives for foreign private investment, especially when devoted to the less developed areas. 

8 H.R. 5 (Boggs) Foreign Investment Incentive Tax Act of 1959 (86th Cong., 1959-60)... 

The bill passed the House during the 2d sess. of the 86th Cong. and was referred to the 

Senate Finance Committee where it died with that Congress.

25. 

My only feeling is that either nothing will happen to that phase of President Kennedy's tax bill, or, if anything does happen, it will have a very big exemption for reinvestment certainly in the less developed areas, or reinvestment even in the developed countries with the benefits that will flow to the less developed areas, and I really I feel that business has no reason to fear the fact of President Kennedy's proposal. I wish he hadn't, but I think this ought not to be a factor because I see so little support for it. 

Question. Senator, just one precaution I would like you to bear in mind, that small business people feel, and that is on this exportimport credit guarantee. Don't overlook the need for permitting small business or any business to call their shots as to what they want to insure. Usually most of the program calls for a blanket credit program there. 

Senator JAVITS. I think that is a very good point, and I never did have in mind anything but an insurance on application. You see, the analogy I made with the FHA is quite an accurate one because the FHA insures about 40 percent of the mortgages placed in the country. About 60 percent is uninsured and represents normal bank savings, savings and loan, and other similar financing. As I see it, the same is true in the export field. Certainly a person shouldn't have to pay a premium unless he feels there is enough risk to warrant it. This is a normal business decision. But the mere fact that he has to back up will, we hope, induce him to extend longer term credit and to extend it more freely, and I think the real spur to expansion of American exports is to begin to note Japanese and German and British competition-and perhaps French, too, although I am not. as well acquainted with their policies-where you begin to have credits from 5 to 10 years. Then you are beginning to talk business, and the businessman should have the right to do that with some part of his production, that part which he wants to, in a sense, speculate with. And these guarantees would help enormously, and in that area, of course, I can see the increase.

Of course, we do an enormous export business now. The question is, What are you going to add on to it? That is what I am thinking about. 

Well, gentlemen, if you are through with me Question. Senator, what is the status of the Boggs bill at the present time? Is that still alive?

Senator JAVITS. The Boggs bill got somewhat bogged down with 

Mr. Kennedy's tax program, and I think if we can eliminate that aspect of Mr. Kennedy's tax program-there are other things that he recommends that I thoroughly agree with, but I think that was a really bad one, and I just wish they had never thought of it-then we will come back again to the Boggs bill concept which is the right concept, an incentive concept. You all know the famous analogy of the carrot and the stick. That is what you have to do with a private enterprise economy, and, as a matter of fact, even the Russians have to do that with the communistic economy. It is just the nature of life, but we can do it a lot better. We are much better experienced and have greater potential for expansion if we use it wisely, and in order to introduce foreign investment of the right kind you have got to give incentive, not pencil it, and that is precisely what President Kennedy did with this proposal.

I don't want to blow that up too much because I don't think it is going to get anywhere, but at the moment it has just blocked people's thinking, and if we can do something to dispel that, I think we will have done a very useful thing in the foreign investment field. 

Question. Just one comment.

There is a lot of feeling that we are pricing ourselves out of the market, in any aspect, not any specific thing. Do you have any comment on that? 

Senator JAVITS. Yes, I do. Our testimony before the Joint Economic Committee would indicate that we are not pricing ourselves out of the market; that we are still priced within the market. There is a tendency to that effect. That tendency is attributable to the fact that wages have run ahead for the last few years of productivity. 

If I had a word of advice to businessmen, I would say that when you deal with your own union, I would take them on on that subject. 

They are becoming very much more conscious of it, specifically in the export field, and you may find a lot more receptivity at least with the major unions which have more or less to lend an ear to the public. 

But, according to the testimony that we have had, this is a tendency which is a bad one, but it is not yet a realized disadvantage in any major way.

Question. Can you shed any light on our Canadian situation which is closer to home? 

Senator JAVITS. Well, when you ask can I shed any light on our 

Canadian situation do you mean the trade problems with Canada ?

Question. Well, the resentment of American investment and so forth. 

Senator JAVITS. Yes. I think the tendency in Canada, according to what we see in Washington, will be toward a greater participation by the Canadian people themselves, perhaps relatively small at the start because of the fact that it is a country with a relatively smaller population and perhaps doesn't have the resources that we have. But we think that there is some hope and some possibility for an accommodation if more and more Canadian investment is welcomed into American enterprise which is carried on in Canada, and I am rather hopeful that political relationships may develop that kind of an opening both for the Canadians and for American business. Certainly 

Prime Minister Diefenbaker is very friendly to the effort to find some way to work out of our present situation. 

Well, gentlemen, I thank you very much and I hope you carry on as effectively as you seem to.

Mr. NORTON. I am W. J. Norton, with Arthur D. Little Co., Cam bridge, Mass. The question I would like to ask you, could you tell us more of the Mexican experience you had which you feel now has reverted to the correct form, having started out on a different basis? 

Mr. HOOD. We formed a joint corporation with another American firm in Mexico, hoping that the two of us together might meet a market situation that perhaps we couldn't meet separately. Again, we didn't get our feet wet enough to realize that we could do it by ourselves. I mean by "ourselves," that we didn't need their product. 

We have a Mexican partner now-but the point is, we didn't need that other firm's product. We were more of a help to them than they were to us. This was a thing that I delegated to somebody and they encouraged the other firm. Our people decided this was what we should do; and with my attention on other problems, I went along 

9 S. C. Selick, vice president (sales), Morrison Steel Export Corp., New Brunswick, N.J. 72811-61--3

with it. It didn't seem to work out as a partnership. As I said, it took us 2 years to get out from under our joint company and finally set up our own.

The primary problem, I feel, was that top management, and I mean myself, did not involve itself enough in looking at the ramifications and implications of what we were doing. We actually did not have a long-range plan. This was an isolate. 

There is a sort of gestation period that management must go through to give it some point of view. We cannot afford to slide into the occasional thing. Just because it is Mexico and because it is close at hand, we cannot operate without some long-range planning. This is what we ran into, and it didn't seem to work.

Question. If I could take a positive approach, we had a situation just recently in England where there was a local manufacturer producing the same kind of equipment and at a lower price than we could possibly sell. Yet, because we were more sophisticated advertisers,

used the American type of distribution and were able to land enough material in England so we could make better delivery than the English manufacturer, we captured the market in about 6 months.

Mr. HOOD. I certainly agree. I don't think it is an either/or situation, and I don't think anyone is suggesting that here. 

I read a story years ago that made a great impression on me. It concerned a Russian scientist who was working on the problem of what happens when we get certain flashbacks in our minds. For example, during the process of drowning, as you pass out of the picture you may get these flashbacks. He was experimenting in putting himself under. After one of these particular experiments, he had a pad of paper and when he was brought back to consciousness, he found that had scrawled something on this pad in handwriting that he didn't recognize as his own. He had written a little phrase of insight: "Think in other categories."

This seems to me especially pertinent here. We can't think about international marketing in the categories or the ways of the past. We have to find new combinations for this new market. 

Any other comments in this area of pricing ourselves out of the market?

I think we pointed out here that some of these are illusions on our part and some are not.

Mr. LAMBERT. I don't think that is true. The Japanese have priced us out of the transistor market right here. I don't think it is any illusion.

Mr. HOOD. They are a more competitive producer. 

Mr. LAMBERT. Right. And as for style, quality, and dependability, I think they have that, also.

Question. Another factor that has some restrictions on exports is obviously the nationalistic approach where in some countries you have to be a local manufacturer in order to get into the market. This is one I don't think many American manufacturers are fully aware of and one that requires a great deal of investigation, particularly in 

Latin America. 

Mr. HOOD. Yes; this certainly has been our experience. We've had to evaluate whether it is worth while to go into Colombia, for example, where there is a restricted market. The nationalistic feeling is very strong in South America in many areas. However, you can start out many times, as some of you gentlemen have indicated, by licensing. Or you can start out by assembling products there as a way of getting started and testing the market. 

Question. Did your company license someone in Japan? 

Mr. HOOD. No; we did not license anybody.

Question. You mean you went home before the negotiations were completed?

Mr. HOOD. No; we are still working on it. We are exchanging prices now.

One of the problems here was that after the war we didn't protect our patents in Japan. We had them in a lot of other places, but we overlooked Japan in the postwar confusion. Somebody missed this one. Consequently, we have a competitor using our name and extinguisher. He wants a license agreement, but we are not especially interested.

We are now working with the steel company in Japan. I think it will gradually work out, but here again, timing is a factor. It takes a long time to go back and forth-back and forth. 

Question. Are you doing this on your own or do you have a representative in Japan who negotiates?

Mr. HOOD. We are doing this on our own with legal advice in Japan. 

Question. You spoke about management, the manufacturer of your product, the 50-50 proposition. What do you do about management control?

Mr. HOOD. Well, this boils down to just facing one another. It is the only experince we had in this. The other person is not interested in giving up his control, and we are not interested in giving up ours. 

However, we are working together toward a common objective. 

There is a balancing factor which you wouldn't find to be a problem if you had 51 percent. There are a number of these 50-50 corporations in the United States, as you know.

Question. Mr. Hood, once again in order to relate your experience and your procedures to what we consider small business, would you give us some idea of the size of your business, by number of employees or some other measurement?

Mr. HOOD. Well, I can try. I am not sure whether all this makes sense. We have about 500 employees. We do about $12 million worth. 

We are in varied fields, as I said, so that we have quite a lot of crossmix products.

Mr. WATTS. I am Ray Watts of the Senate Small Business Committee.

Mr. Hood, how important do you think having more than one product is for entering foreign markets? The impression I have formed in studying this subject for the Senate Small Business Committee, and borne out by the discussion this morning, is that the deversification might be a very important factor. For example, I encountered last December a company that had developed a pretty big thing in a number of foreign markets, including the Philippines, in automobile springs. They are now completely noncompetitive in the Philippines and other oversea markets in springs, but have developed and become competitive in another, different product, electric cars. So what is good this year might not be good next year, and what is good in market A might not be good in market B, and I certainly have formed the impression that if you made more than one thing and watched to see what is selling well and where, that is the road to riches. 

Mr. HOOD. Well, my personal comment on it is, of course, that one of the reasons we are going abroad is for greater flexibility. I think our objective has been not to become inflexible, if we can help it. 

We started in our primary area of fire equipment, but we gradually realized that the chemical opportunities were very great indeed. For example, some time ago we became involved in an investigation of Colombia. We were well along toward agreement except that the

company we were going to distribute through was also handling du Pont chemicals. They said they thought there would be a conflict of interest. We could easily have said that we were not interested in that possibility, only in fire equipment—and talked ourselves right out of that corner.

Fortunately, we didn't go ahead. Now, with our increased interest in chemicals, we're glad we didn't. We have learned here to be careful when we go into an area and not restrict our movements if we can help it. So when we incorporate in a place, if we do, we try to get broad leverage because we don't really know what products we are going to be marketing.

Mr. WATTS. Your watchword is alertness, or sensitivity to the needs of the market, and flexibility, being ready to jump in.

Mr. HOOD. Don't tie your hands if you can help it. There are going to be enough other things to tie them. 

Question. Mr. Watts' point was very interesting because there are a number of American companies that you can name who abroad do much different things than they do in the United States and have actually brought back from abroad a new product, diversifying their 

American manufacturing.

Mr. HOOD. They also bring back a great many new ideas. We have run into some excellent managers in Europe. The way they go about approaching a problem is very refreshing to us. We can use many of their ideas also.

Mr. HOEFS. My name is Richard H. Hoefs. I am with Arthur 

Andersen & Co., Chicago, Ill.

Your comments concerning the time you spent negotiating with the Japanese businessman are quite interesting. We have two or three large clients, not in the small business category but large clients who have gone into oversea manufacture generally, and, through not researching the market and other necessary activities, you could say they have lost their shirt and are now looking for ways to try to salvage or reorganize what they have there and come out with the best result possible.

In addition I talked to one of our clients down in Brazil last year who apparently was sent there under an organization that was set up by his people in the States, and he said he spent the first 2 or 3 months of his time there in the office trying to reorganize what had been done by his legal department and other departments in Chicago because they just didn't have any concept of what was to be encountered in Brazil, and he just couldn't operate under what they had set up for him.

Mr. HOOD. I think you need to have an awareness. I wouldn't underestimate that. In other words, we can't all do it in a nice closeted room like we have here. In most of our negotiations we have always made the effort to go there. We have found you have to build up an understanding between yourselves and your partners or with whomever you are dealing. You don't do that by sitting in the United

States. The shoe is on the other foot now, it seems to me. 

Mr. GREENE.10 We are in the middle of the same problem, too. We have about a $6 million business and 280 employes. This is Standard 

10 Philip Greene, divisional manager, Standard Machinery Co., Division, Franklin Research & Development Corp., Mystic, Conn.

Machinery, and we are in the business of making extruding machines. 

Over the last 2 years the Japanese market has grown tremendously to where we are between a half million and a million dollars a year there, and scared to death. We woke up to the patent situation about 

2 years ago. We have a lot of patents in process right now, new developments and so forth.

In looking at some of these various ways of trying to cover the Japanese market we have heard of this joint venture approach, but it looks as though you would have to settle for less than 50 percent of the stock.

Mr. HOOD. I haven't had any. I am sure this gentleman might know of some. But I think there are some ways to control the spending of capital money. There is a sort of spoiler role that you can play, I think. I haven't had that experience but I have heard people talk about how you can successfully safeguard your interests on less than 50 percent. 

Mr. GREENE. One other question. I appreciate your comments.

You mentioned that this was something the top management has to stay in on, the directors and so forth, that in such a broad decision area you can't delegate the authority too early. You need someone to do the leg work. What is your idea of timing on setting up an international manager?

Mr. HOOD. We have an international company and we have a general manager who is also a vice president. We export to 65 countries now, and this amounts to about $12 million. So he handles that business.

After we get subsidiary companies set up and operating, we have managers. However in the critical period of finding your way around in the dark-getting relationships built up and setting up the operation-top management must be deeply involved. 

In most of the areas, we have used partners, as I mentioned. We have a Mexican partner, a Belgian partner, and a Canadian partner. 

They are the operating managers in those areas. They are controlled by a board of directors of which we are a part. 

So my role has been played in the early stages—in getting things going. 

Mr. BRECK.¹¹ Was your question at what point when you are attempting to get into the international business should you get someone who is an expert in oversea operations?

Mr. GREENE. That is basically it. I have been to Japan three times, and to Australia and Europe a couple of times, and as division manager there are a few other things back home that should have some time spent on them, and yet I don't have the answers and I have got lots of questions about at what stage can you start delegating some of this authority and how is a good way to do it within a small company organization. 

11 John H. Breck, Jr., executive vice president, John H. Breck, Inc., Springfield, Mass.

Mr. BRECK. Well, our experience was that once we decided that we were serious about international business, the best thing we could do was to go out and find someone who was a socalled expert in international business. To attempt to do this with the operating heads of our own company, none of whom had any real experience in international business, but to attempt to get into this field, which is very specialized, is a mistake. So from our own experience I would say that when you make a decision that you are serious about international business, go out and find someone who presumably is an expert in this field.

Mr. HOOD. I would hasten to add he is not going to set your policy alone. You are going to have to do it. What I am saying is, in this early period, I see no way out of getting involved. You will rue the day you did not get involved. We found that out by bitter experience. 

Operations is another thing-you understand that. You set up operations in another way and your role changes to periodic evaluation of the operations.

Question. I think that one of the most important things you said is that you must do it yourself at the start in particular, because I know we would not be in the difficulty we are. It takes time to get it organized, and certainly it takes top management to follow through in the beginning. It cannot be delegated. 

Mr. HOOD. I found that many of us start with the export idea. This is something and I use this in quotes that a high-grade clerk can do because it is a matter of filling out papers. It was certainly a matter of filling out papers during the fifties, until this thing grew to a certain point.

Many companies then find some retread who isn't doing very well in some other area and make him export manager. When it becomes really important, you realize you have to go out and get someone who knows where he is going. As this becomes a vital part of your company's income, you have to put top-grade management on it. 

What I am saying is that we have to concern ourselves more with the problem of good management before we start developing our export division. We must know where we want to go before we can have a good organization. 

Mr. SHEEDY. I am Paul Sheedy with Westwood Pharmaceuticals, Buffalo, N.Y.

What I would like to ask is how can a small company protect trademarks? Your reference to Japan brought up a question that we have, particularly in Latin America, a problem where we see some of our trade names or product names being up for registration, and this is in advance of the time that we anticipate going in there. We had one specific name which we actually manufactured ourselves a few years back; before we tried to go into Cuba it was already registered. Just last week it popped up in Chile. We don't anticipate going in there yet, but how does a small company fight this or protect itself? 

Mr. HOOD. You certainly have to delegate someone to look at the total picture-to know where you are registered and where you are not. 

As I said earlier, if you analyze your market as a whole and where your potentials are, then it is essential to cover yourself in these areas.

I am sure one of the speakers will cover this more specifically. You should register the patent and the copyright. You might have to pay a fee every year.

Mr. SHEEDY. Naturally, we are aiming at the larger ones first, and 

Latin America-wise, we are now aiming for Mexico and some of these countries. We are already in Venezuela, but these countries farther away where we know they just couldn't think of the same name because we have made it from initials of our personnel, for example; things like that.

Mr. HOOD. Well, all I can advise is to cover yourself by paying the fee each year. This is where we missed in Japan. We said we don't need to cover that one, do we? I remember at the time we were plowing $4,000 or $5,000 into these registrations. 

Question. Plus the fact the American Embassy is always very helpful in some of these areas to try to get it removed if it was improperly registered. 

Mr. BRECK. I think you will find where they are registering your name they don't intend in many instances to put out a product under the name. They just register the name and then at such time as you are interested in going into the market they will sell you the name. 

Question. And the price is pretty high, too. 

Mr. WILSON.12 May I comment that this problem is not unique to small business. Even the Ford Motor Co. in Mexico found it was unable to use the name "Falcon" in their merchandising in Mexico for reasons rather similar to what you have outlined.

Question. I would like to second Mr. Breck's comment about when you select an expert. I feel that as soon as you think seriously about entering into the international field you should look for an expert in the particular area that you feel you would be concerned with and consult him before you go overseas. And once you have selected an expert, try and live with what he has to say because we have illustrations periodically where a client will come to us for advice, or will go to our oversea offices for advice and information, and they will go down to the hotel after the day is over and stop at the bar and have a drink or two and have a talk with the fellow next to them, and he knows all the answers to all the questions they have, and he will come back to our office the next day with a contract all drawn up which is almost 100 percent contrary to what they really should have. We find that fellow is a very nice man and seems to know what he is talking about. So they take his advice as opposed to the people they are hiring to help them. 

Mr. HOOD. Another thing your comment triggered off in my mind:

It is amazing how small the industrial and financial communities are in other countries. This gentleman, I am sure, has been in the Imperial Hotel in Japan. Whatever goes on in Japan is known to everybody. Even when you think you are holding secret negotiations, everybody knows about it. So you have to be more careful there than in the United States. 

Question. Earlier you commented about your company having, I believe, an international company arrangement. I wasn't certain whether you said it was a division or company. I wonder if you would comment a little further on the organizational structure and the meth- 

12 Frank Wilson, president, International Division, the Gabriel Co., Cleveland, Ohio.

od of operation, whether outside or inside the country and how you handle it?

Mr. HOOD. We have the Ansul International Corp., a Panamanian company, which handles all our exports and international activities. 

It is a separate organization. We set it up primarily as a tool to use in our efforts to become an international company. We are gradually working toward that goal.

As an example, we have accumulated profits there which we will use to pay for our operation in Belgium. We are proceeding on that. 

We are earning our way in, as it were. 

Mr. PROCOPI. I am John Procopi, with the Milton Roy Co. in Philadelphia. 

Do you have substance in Panama, people working there?

Mr. HOOD. We have a person there on our payroll.

Question. Isn't the time getting a little late for this Panamanian international company system? Is'nt the Government sort of taking a dim view of operating this way? Five years ago it was fine, but is it still something you feel is time to do?

Mr. Hoop. This is what we have been doing for 5 years. If they rule otherwise, we will probably have to change. But I think this relates to our pricing ourselves out of the market. We couldn't possibly, as a small company, bring the money back here and then reinvest it in Europe. We would be more than out of the market that way. 

It is hard enough as it is. So this is our present method. I can't advise you to choose this approach now with all the rumbling that we hear. 

This gentleman in the back.

Mr. STANLEY. I am Alexander Stanley of Dun's Review, New 

York.

Just referring to that problem about trademarks and tradenames, I don't think it is a good idea to wait until you are ready to go into the market. You should do your registering now. In most cases it is necessary. You don't have to manufacture, but if you don't do it now you will find that your trademark and tradename have been picked up. The trademark is the important one of the two. The other thing is that it doesn't matter what the size of the market is; if it has any industrial capacity you will be wise to register because your product under your own tradename will be exposed to others in competition. So you had better jump the gun.

Mr. HOOD. Or you will be competing against yourself. 

Mr. STANLEY. The average cost is $125 a market for all the fees, the legal fees.

Mr. HOOD. Well, this is another thing we didn't comment on-the value of your trademark.

It seems from our experience that it would be more important than it is in the United States. With the interpretation of the laws in the United States and with people in and out of the market, we just compete here. When you are abroad, it seems to be much more important if you come in with a trademarked product. This is what we have found. 

Mr. BRECK. I wonder whether you would be willing to comment on the profitability of your oversea operations in those markets that

have reached maturity as compared with the profitability of your domestic?

Mr. HOOD. Well, the only reason I am hesitating is that we are just starting in such a number of these markets and they haven't reached their full growth. I can talk about Mexico as an example. 

With all the ups and downs that we had, we finally set up this company. Fortunately, we have been in the black right from the beginning. We set up a company in Venezuela about 5 or 6 years ago and we have now paid back all the capital that we invested. 

It appears to us that the return on investment in Belgium is going to be much better than we are able to earn in the United States. 

As directors we are interested in putting money where we can get a return on it. If we brought the money back into the United States, it would cost us about the same as if we earned it here. 

Again, it seems to us that if we are going to survive as an entity, we must be in the free markets of the world. This is going to pose challenges that we haven't encountered before, but I think these can be overcome. 

Mr. BRECK. What about timing, Bob? Have you found any general pattern in terms of how long it takes you to develop a new market to a break-even point or profitability point?

Mr. HOOD. We found that going in with a partner certainly speeds up things. He is already there. He is already doing things. This man is developed as a distributor, and then you create a partnership. 

Or he may have a going business himself. If he has a going business, then you have his past records and you know what he has been doing. 

You can buy into part of the market and there is a potential opportunity. It varies. 

If you are starting in your own wholly owned subsidiary and just feeling your way along, this could take some time. I think that when the markets develop as in Europe, it is probably much better to buy into a particular area than to start from scratch and build it up. 

In the latter case, you wouldn't be ready for the market in time.

In some of the developed areas of Latin America you can start at the other end, I think. 

Mr. PUTNAM.13 Have you reached a point where you have a partner? 

Mr. HOOD. Yes. 

Mr. PUTNAM. Are you now manufacturing in that country? 

Mr. Hood. Yes, indeed. We started out by assembling our fire equipment. Then we put in machinery to make chemicals, and gradually had more and more things made in the country. We did this because of the advantage of the tariff. However, it is never black and white. We still furnish material for export because of the technical involvement. 

I am not sure I answered your question, but again I want to emhasize that most important to us is not whether we manufacture there-but whether we can distribute and have some control over the distribution. As an example, we got caught right in the middle in

Venezuela where we have a wholly owned subsidiary. We had a distributor there who told us the laws indicated they were going to buy Venezulean-made products. If we wanted to protect our market 

13 Harold Putnam, counsel, Senate Small Business Committee.

there, we had to come in. So we came in and assembled fire extinguishers. This fellow said, "I will assemble for you"--which he does. 

Then he sells them to us, and turns around and resells them as our distributor. As a result, we have no control over the market. 

I think you have to put your own people in also. However, this was a long way back in our experience and we wouldn't do it the same way again. We would definitely have some of our people there. 

Question. Finding and selecting partners is a very important consideration, and most of us who are in the consulting business are finding that there are as many potential partners from Latin America or the Far East or Europe who come to us to act as a catalyst to try to locate an American firm who is interested in them, and very frequently you find thousands of us who are in the international area who can be of some great help to somebody who wants to go abroad. 

Our people speak the language and know the customs. 

Mr. HOOD. It is a labyrinth to work in by yourself. I wouldn't recommend that. Banks are another good spot. Underwriting of a bank or recommendation from a bank is very important in every foreign country-more so than here. 

Question. You say you wouldn't go to Venezuela without putting people in. You mean as part of the management?

Mr. HOOD. That is what we learned about Venezuela. We found we are in a captive market-even though it is profitable. Where do we go from here? If we wanted to do something tomorrow the distributor would say the market isn't there. So the interpretation of the market resides in the local distributor. This is a problem we all have in exporting to distributor. If you are going to the extent of putting money in, it seems to me you should follow this with people. The object is not necessarily control but to gain a knowledge of the market.

Mr. Hood. The officers of that corporation are the same as for our other corporations. Our long-range policy is to become an international company. Whether we will be in Panama, Switzerland, or anywhere else in the world, there is gold behind it anyway.

Mr. HOOD. Well, this is very similar to what happens in a board. of directors. Everyone somewhat agrees with what is going on or they find themselves out. You have to depend here on the relationship between the parties. It is not a clear-cut, by-the-numbers arrangement as you can well see. You have to agree that it is in the interest of the operation. If you can't agree-you can't go ahead on the common goals. 

We have not had those problems. 

Mr. SMITHE. I was just wondering if people resort to arbitration or something of that sort.

Mr. HOOD. In making the legal arrangements, there is a manner in which this can be adjudicated. You have to depend on your relationship—one to another. Mr. HOOD. Yes. Title has to pass outside the United States. This is the reason for the Panamanian condition. Title actually passes to

Mr. MITCHELL. Somewhere along the line you establish then that 

Ansul International owns the goods.

Mr. HOOD. Oh, yes. In fact, as I recall, we charge Ansul International 15 percent beyond what our costs of the total operation are. We sell to Ansul International and they take over from there. They have

their own credit problems and so on. If we didn't do that, if we just transferred the goods as our own, the arrangement would not work. 

Question. Could you tell me what the implications and benefits and reasons in general are for your long-range policy, to become an international policy, what this means as opposed to an American company with substantial interest abroad?

Mr. HOOD. I can try. I think I said that our goal is to create economic wealth as an entity. We have certain product lines. When we look at the present market for those product lines in the United States, we begin to see them softening here and there. We have spent a great deal of time and energy in the development of new products. A lot of them have gone down the drain-some of them have worked out. It seems to me the markets of the world are very important to us, and are another opportunity-just like a new product-to take our ideas, our experience and our technical know-how and make a profit on them. 

We found in fire equipment, for example, that in the U.S. market, we began to get a peaking off. We are specialists in dry chemical fire equipment and in Europe, this hasn't quite hit yet. Perhaps there we can follow somewhat the same course as in the United States utilizing the know-how, distribution and manufacturing skills that we have here. While we are doing this, we can help develop our product lines further here. We must look at the world market rather than just the market of the United States.

Let's relate back to our efforts in Japan. 

We know that if we don't enter Japan now eventually Japan will be in the United States. We had better join them rather than fight them at that time. We at least will have some control over our destiny if we work in Japan. The same applies to Europe. Eventually they are going to have more than enough to satisfy their markets-and this is the largest market in the world. They want to get back into the 

U.S. market.

A lot of it is not only outward expansion-into other markets-but inward protection of our own markets.

We also subscribe to the same theory of becoming an international organization. Right at the moment we are purely domestic. But our officers and our members of the board and so forth are all activevice presidents of manufacturing, research and development, marketing and with the domestic organization they are not staffed to operate internationally, and if for no other reason to become an international organization they will be given authorization to staff for that particular purpose. 

Does that answer at least part of your question? In other words, that is one of the reasons you would like to be an international organization. 

I can suggest something else. The Harvard Business Review, the

December issue of it, covers that topic very well. I can't remember the author.

I think it answers it very well, but one item of curiosity-If you create an international headquarters, as you suggest, let us say in

Switzerland, the control of the top-level decision and the bulk of the ownership would, however, still remain here.

Mr. HOOD. Yes, it would as far as we are concerned. I would like to come back to the idea of thinking in other categories. We are doing a lot of pioneering in this area because our markets are forc-

ing us to pioneer. In my lifetime, I certainly think that the major market will be in the United States, however, it may not be as competition is able to come in rather rapidly. 

Mr. MEYNARD. I am E. B. Meynard, Buckeye Tools, Dayton, Ohio. 

It seems to me it is a matter of semantics entering our discussion here when we use the term that we are going international, or words to that effect. Isn't it basically a philosophy or an overall corporate goal when you say go international that you look at the total market rather than the techniques or mechanics of getting to it, whether you actually establish an international headquarters here, there, or elsewhere?

Mr. HOOD. Yes, I think this is very true. We are moving in this general direction from export. Some of us are licensing and some manufacturing in partnerships. We have wholly owned subsidiaries and partially owned subsidiaries. It gets more and more complex, but it seems to me that is what the marketplace is. In some way or other, this is the way we are meeting needs of the market. 

If we come back and see where the market is and what we must do to get into the market, it doesn't always fit these nice little pigeonholes. 

Mr. BERG. I am Quentin Berg from Berg Manufacturing, New 

Cumberland, Pa. 

What is the advantage to your stockholders in foreign operations?

Does this mean to remain out in those operations or does it mean you are coming back to the United States?

Mr. HOOD. This is a good question, and sometimes we are hard put to answer it. You can say that you can bring the profit back and pay a tax on it at any time. Any income that we get from the foreign subsidiaries is so listed on our balance sheet. You gain in assets. 

Obviously this is an asset that we feel we protect to some degree by having a partner. If something happened in another country and we wanted to sell out, at least we have a potential buyer who is a going concern. So that is an asset.

In the long run, that is perhaps the most potent thing-it allows you to grow and survive. You can close your borders, stay right here-not really concern yourself about it-and take what comes. 

This is perhaps meeting the question another way. 

Question. I will volunteer on that one also. I think most organizations would like to broaden their corporate base. They would also like to become, I think for matters of sophistication, if you like, worldwide organizations and to draw on worldwide research and development, and I think the most important part is—and this is in quotation-"not lose any more markets."

Mr. HOOD. I think the markets are one thing. We make a fire extinguisher here. If we don't take it to Europe, someone else will go there. We must ask what this means to us here. Once that market is saturated, they are coming back here. In other words, you are liable to sit here and watch the whole market just fade away. By the time realize what has happened, it may be too late. you

We are trying to make these probing steps to protect our market through growth. This may not work out-but it is a calculated risk. 

Mr. WILSON. May I offer a couple of comments here?

First on the step-by-step procedure in going abroad, an excellent case history is that of Kaiser Corp. which about 10 years ago detailed

a couple of bright boys from Brooklyn to handle exports. Nobody knew quite what they could do. They rapidly proceeded to the organization of a Panamanian company. This was established step by step with establishment of the foreign branches and producing companies of various sorts to the point that today they have 8,000 employees in the United States, but they have 9,000 employees outside of the United States.

There are no figures published, to the best of my knowledge. 

Nevertheless, the probability would appear that more than half of the corporate earnings are made abroad. These are repatriated at the convenience of the company subject to the standard payments of the U.S. taxes as may be applicable at the time they come in. 

In our own case as a manufacturer traditionally in the automotive parts field, our experience in going abroad arose first of all out of exports with the development of the export market in some 60 countries. It proceeded then to the organization of an international division within the domestic company which was expressly for the purpose of setting policy and planning for worldwide expansion. We then proceeded to the organization of a foreign manufacturing plant in one country, and immediately thereafter to the establishment of the Panamanian company for overall market development abroad and for purposes of reinvestment in this third country. 

There were five major reasons why Panama was given the nod over a number of other points that might have been selected. And the first of these was the existence of shipping lines which made it possible to reexport from a Panamanian point with service to almost any world market. Not always at as favorable rates as out of New York but generally the steps were followed step by step. 

The second was the existence of a free zone established by the Republic of Panama within which goods could be stored and reexported without tax penalties. Income derived from reexport from the free zone is subject to 90 percent abatement of the Panamanian taxes thereon. This provided, therefore, a closer and more satisfactory point for service of the Latin American market.

Thirdly was the fact that the ability to under these circumstances provide for solid substance and base of foreign operations inasmuch as this was not a shadow operation at all but a very substantial reexport and worldwide sales operation.

Fourth is the peculiarity of Panamanian law which is, while not unique, not too frequent, that you had a corporate vehicle there which could be used both as a sales company and as a holding company. 

Both Swiss law and some other European points, for example, make a sharp distinction between these. Hence the complexities of organizations are greater where the simplicity of organization within 

Panama lends itself to a dual-purpose corporation.

And, finally, there was the fact that here was a currency area linked to that of the United States on which the likelihood of currency fluctuation was certainly at a minimum, and therefore the parity of the balboa and the dollar was something that one could pretty well count on for the long term.

These factors taken together made it a logical base for expansion. 

This was merely a step then in the further development of the reinvestment of earnings into other manufacturing operations in other

countries abroad, and generally the development of worldwide sales and merchandising activities with a little more freedom, a little more elbowroom than might be permitted under some of our domestic corporate policies. These reasons in the aggregate were rather decisive in influencing us to go into Panama. 

Mr. HOOD. Thank you very much.

I. INTRODUCTION A. QUALIFICATION OF AREA OF DISCUSSION 

Mr. LUCAS. It is extremely difficult to be completely objective in a talk of this kind and assume that some of the situations that we're about to cover would necessarily be the case in some other area due to political overtones, size of the market, the nature of the product under discussion and many other factors. For this reason, the area of discussion that we are about to undertake will be confined to No. 

1, the continent of Europe; No. 2, the Dominion of Canada; No. 3, the Republic of Mexico; and No. 4, the British Isles. We do have specific information about these areas but about the balance of the world in which we operate, while having direct contact through distributor organizations, we still feel that our firsthand knowledge is somewhat limited.

B. COMPANY PROFILE 

The American Sterilizer Co. was founded in Erie, Pa., in 1894 and, as the name implies, was initially confined to the production of sterilizers for hospital consumption. 

At the present time, our annual sales volume is in the area of $23 million. We employ approximately 1,200 people domestically which includes the personnel in 18 branch offices, located in major cities throughout the country. We also have manufacturing facilities for the production of intravenous solutions in Milledgeville, Ga., a plant in Palo Alto, Calif., for the production of sonic energy equipment used for the rapid and efficient cleaning of surgical instruments, and, we've recently acquired a factory in Jamestown, N.Y., for the fabrication of hospital-type cabinets and casework.

Our basic product line in our Erie plant consists of sterilizers in various sizes and shapes designed for specific applications in hospitals and in industry as well as research organizations. We also imanufacture a line of tables for general surgery, obstetrics, orthopedics and urological procedures. Complementing the tables is of course a line of surgical lights designed specifically for these applications. 

So much for a brief description of our organization. 

C. OUR HISTORY IN EXPORTS SINCE 1944 

Quite typical of the ordinary American company, our interest in markets beyond our own borders was almost negligible. We did have some contact in Puerto Rico, Cuba, and Mexico, and our distributors in these areas were personal friends who undertook the marketing of our line on pretty much a windfall basis, and by that I mean—if an order came by, they would write it up and send it in to us. Even domestically during the period prior to 1944, ours was a relatively small market and our greatest volume in any one year up until about 1939 was in the neighborhood of $2 million. After World War II and in fact immediately after, we were approached by an international marketing organization with the proposition offering to sell our equipment throughout the world, and guaranteeing us a volume of $400,000 a year, which to us at the time was quite a phenomenal figure. As it turned out, they turned in sales over $400,000 the first

year and the market seemed to increase as we went along. Even at that time we were quite oblivious to the fact that there existed a seller's market for all U.S. manufacturers in the rest of the world, with the only limiting factor being the availability of U.S. dollars to pay for the material. This went on until about 1955 to 1957, which was the start of serious competition, at least in our field, from many sources. 

This began in the form of local competition in Europe primarily and then it appeared in other countries to whom we previously had exported a considerable volume of equipment. Initially, our European market diminished from about $400,000-$500,000 a year down to $100,000 due to what we found out later to be 35 competitors scattered throughout the countries of Europe. 

Immediately following that the Union of South Africa diminished from a quarter million dollar annual market to almost zero. This again was due to local manufacture of an inferior grade of equipment but acceptable to local authorities because of the local industry. Similarly, the market in Brazil, Argentina, Mexico, India, Japan, and 

Australia followed suit.

Realizing that our markets were rapidly falling away and the only alternative, looking at the situation from a pure export operation, we rapidly realized that the situation called for a complete reappraisal of our position, initially in our home market and secondarily, in the world market. As a result of this introspection, the decision was rendered to- 

1. Defend ourselves and our market, and 

2. Make it a profit opportunity.

There were naturally other considerations, and these would be in the category of- 

1. Broadening the corporate base. 

2. Developing a worldwide research and development organization. 

3. A very important negative-"Not lose any more markets."

D. HISTORY IN INTERNATIONAL OPERATION 

Our first move into the international field was made in 1956 when we established AMSCO Canada. Up until this time we were represented across the Dominion by a very active organization that had generated a fairly acceptable volume of business. However, to adequately defend our northern border, it was felt that a direct marketing operation operating from our own company, would give us firsthand contact with the market as well as give us Canadian identity in the form of a factory utilizing 100-percent Canadian personnel with the exception of one person in the manufacturing department. Almost immediately after the creation of our own organization, we soon doubled and quadrupled our original sales volume with most of the equipment emanating from our Erie plant, being that the Canadian market so closely parallels our own insofar as equipment requirements are concerned.

In this manner, we felt that we could adequately protect ourselves again European or British competitors who might decide to move into Canada and ultimately export into the United States. 

Our second move came when we formed a partnership with a Dutch organization in order to move into the European Common Market which at the time was receiving considerable publicity. As

mentioned previously, our performance in sales was rapidly diminishing due to many local competitors primarily, and equally important, was the fact that our equipment did not meet customer requirements with the exception of a few items in our line. 

Recognizing the fact that the European market constituted a tremendous potential particularly since no hospitals had been built on the Continent or in England since 1939, and each individual government had on its drawing boards plans for scores of hospitals each of which would obviously require equipment of our type. Not long after the creation of the joint venture in Europe, we encountered a rather serious disagreement with our partners which was in connection with the approach to the market. Heretofore, the operation could be called a mail-order type of selling, whereas our approach was on a direct basis utilizing salesmen who would contact potential customers on a firsthand basis. Unfortunately, this difference of opinion could not be resolved with the result that we purchased controlling interest in order to execute our own marketing plan without the necessity of having to consult our partners.

Here again our sales were increased considerably over what we had been realizing in the way of exports, but, one point we do want to make clear, the European market is an extremely difficult one to attack for many many reasons, some of them nationalistic, others political, others are in the nature of the product line-acceptable to one country and not necessarily so in another and with each country having its own federal regulations governing the fabrication and inspection of pressure vessels which is the category in which much of our equipment falls. It has also been necessary for us to revise our entire product line insofar as sterilizers are concerned in order to better meet customer requirements and to exceed the specifications of our competitors.

Although it is common knowledge it bears repeating here that the European market is second only to the United States and we look forward to the development of a very large potential and, correspondingly a relatively good sized company to meet the requirements of this market. (a) AMSCO de Mexico: Our next move in the international area was toward our border to the south, namely Mexico. Here we have been represented by the large international organization, but, for some unknown reason, our market participation remained at a relatively low figure varying from year to year in the neighborhood of eighty to ninety thousand dollars. Again, reviewing the situation from the standpoint of protecting our U.S. market, we entered into a joint venture in Mexico for the marketing and manufacturing of equipment for that particular market. For almost identical reasons, we ultimately found that we could not live together with our partners in Mexico and took off on our own with the creation of a direct sales force adequately trained in our product line and in our selling approach. In our first year as a wholly owned company, we did five times our previous volume of business-almost entirely supplied initially from the parent plant. Our manufacturing operation in Mexico was an extremly humble one to begin with and with our success in the market we have arranged to include the Central American market in with the Mexican potential and on that basis enlarged our

manufacturing facilities in order to develop the product line specifically adapted to the area and as self-supporting as we can make it. (b) AMSCO M.S.A.: Quite recently, in fact only last year, we concluded arrangements in England for our joint venture with controlling interest in our hands to establish a factory and marketing organization in the British Isles. Recent developments in the field of sterilization emanating from England definitely call for a peculiar product line to satisfy this market, and of a type of equipment most. appropriately furnished right in England. Obviously, this organization is still in its infancy but will ultimately follow the pattern established by the others in having a direct sales force contacting hospitals and hospital boards throughout the British Isles. (c) AMSCO Caribbean: Consistent with our long-range plans to more or less divide the world into segments or markets of similar nature under the surveillance of a general manager, whether it be a subsidiary company or purely a marketing company, we established an office in San Juan, P.R., early in 1961 to carry on direct marketing to all of the islands making up the Greater and Lesser Antilles, from Cuba on down to the Guianas. In our opinion, the market potential in this area warrants a direct operation because of the size of the projects as well as their peculiar nature. Hospitals in this area while modern in every sense of the word, nevertheless are usually operated by very few well-trained individuals in positions of authority with the physical operation of equipment relegated to individuals having very little in the way of basic education. Consequently, equipment in these institutions must be of a relatively simple nature due to the extreme lack of trained service and maintenance personnel. This we felt was best undertaken by our own organization in order to better serve our customers. (d) Licensees: Due primarily to the lack of foreign exchange particularly in U.S. dollars, in Australia and New Zealand, we established licensees in both of these countries for the production of equipment to our design.

In the balance of the world, we are in direct contact with some 87 distributors in as many countries, each of whom are in direct touch with the parent company in Erie or with one of our subsidiary companies.

We also have licensees in five countries for the production of intravenous solutions, again for the sole purpose of providing a product from a local source utilizing local labor which, in a product like solutions, is extremely essential being that much of it is ordinary distilled water that is quite expensive to transport with the transportation expense representing in many instances the value of the product itself. 

E. INTEREST IN A PARTICULAR MARKET BEYOND EXPORTING IS LARGELY DETERMINED BY 

(a) Annual sales: Quite contrary to the normal concept of seeing sales volume go up and therefore becoming more interested in a particular market, our experience has been the reverse. Where we had previously had a sizable volume of business in a particular area and then watched it diminish, the question was reduced to a decision of whether or not we wanted to retain a market or write if off as a total

wwww loss. Since no one, at least no one in the marketing echelon of the company, likes the idea of writing a market off, the usual result is to try every means including local manufacture as a last resort, to hold a market almost at all costs. (b) Information from the distributor: We've also been made cognizant by a few of our distributors of the market potential in a given area, coupled with the suggestion from that distributor that a local factory be considered in order to better compete in the market as well as secure shelter as a local industry which affords some protection from the competitor who must import. 

As previously pointed out, our action in the cases cited was predicated pretty much on the idea of a defensive measure against competition first of all in relation to our home market and secondly, in the world market.

Before proceeding on any consideration of local participation we made it a point to check all possible sources of information. A tabulation of those sources is as follows: 

1. American Management Association publications. 

2. Business International. 

3. Common Market (Alexander Poppers). 

4. Comprehensive export schedule and supplementary current export bulletins. 

5. Current Economic Indicator, Statistical Office, United Nations. 

6. World Health Organization.

8. Foreign embassy in the United States. 

9. Customs regulations of the United States. 

10. U.S. embassy in country. 

11. Export buyer.

Review."
