Destination, Groups 6-9.

II. DETERMINE MARKET POTENTIAL 

A. This is the fundamental approach and constitutes a check on the country or areas gross national product, the per capita income and here there can be many misleading factor, but most of the statistical figures can be weighted to reflect the original market. In other words, a country with approximately 50 million people and a per capita income of say $100, as an average, would probably break down into a segment that represented the quite wealthy group and behind them would be a middle income and professional group, leaving about threefifths of the population in the below average category. 

We of course, would in our line of business, check on the number of hospitals as well as the number of doctors and dentists in the particular country or market area.

B. Determine or estimate the annual consumption of the product, in the categories of: 1. Local manufacture versus imports. 

C. Check on the possible existence of government health programs. 

In Latin American countries this would be under the heading of "Seguro social" or "Salubridad."

As mentioned previously most of the countries in Europe have quite ambitious programs in the way of hospital development upon which we are counting heavily for the development of our own organization and profit picture. 

D. We would call on local hospitals to determine : 

1. What equipment they have and whether they are satisfied with it. 

2. Who does the buying? 

3. What techniques of operation they employ in various hospital departments? (a) Central sterilizing facilities; (b) central instrument rooms; (c) utility rooms; (d) milk formula laboratories. 

E. Call on architects and consultants to: 

1. Find out how many hospital projects are under design or contemplated. 

2. The size and type of institution.

F. Visit other American manufacturers in order to trade ideas and learn about local conditions regarding: 

1. Labor; its cost and efficiency. 

2. Finance available. 

3. Political stability. 

G. Check on all competitors (D. & B.): 

1. Their product line whether imported or produced locally and the price. 

2. Size of the company: (a) number of employees; (b) plant facilities; (c) annual sales. 

3. Territory they serve. 

4. Their method of distribution: (a) direct sales; (b) distributor; (c) discounts to distributors; (d) compensation to salesmen; (e) product service facilities; (f) product warrantee. 

5. Their share of the market.

H. Consolidate or "boil down" the individual reports on the foregoing whether they were secured by our own company personnel or from outside sources.

In several instances we have used professional market researchers to determine one or more points listed above. Any organization can contact the American Management Association for the names of several good organizations in this category who are equipped both from the standpoint of experience and staff to render excellent services along these lines.

Once we had gathered all of the information we felt pertinent, we would establish what we call a break-even budget. What this amounts to is a tabulation of likely expenses that would be devoted to property, plant and machinery, the necessary personnel in the marketing, manufacturing and administration echelon, some information on the relative cost of labor and material normally used in the fabrication of our type of equipment and then using percentage figures from our existing subsidiary companies, we could fairly accurately estimate our cost of sales, add on the standard factors for distribution cost, terms of sale and profit factor, which in turn would give us a rough idea of how much we would have to sell in units and dollars to break even. 

If this estimate is in line with the picture we show as a result of our other examinations, then we would feel free to continue our search into the market to find an appropriate partner in the case of a joint venture, or to proceed on our own subject of course, to full approval of our executive committee.

One concluding thought that we would like to impart is that it is certainly heartening to see as many organizations represented by you individuals here at this meeting, who are sincerely interested in the foreign market. Keep in mind that there is nothing really sudden about the threat of foreign competition and that in reality it has been primarily due to the fact that we as manufacturers in a relatively large market have been more or less blind to the potential that exists outside our borders. On the other hand our competitors and especially those from Europe or Japan, who had a long history in foreign trade, in fact ever since commerce began between nations, due to the fact that as far as they are concerned, it was a case of "export or die." Today, the international market represents a challenge to all Americans, not only those of us in the commercial world-not only from the threat of communism but the threat to our individual livelihood represented by foreign competitors here in our own market who are now in a position to offer good products at competitive prices.

It is our belief that the pressure of competition from abroad is likely to be felt by a very large segment of American business to the point where those of us who have the flexibility and at least a cursory knowledge of the international market, will probably survive easily but in the process we will broaden out into truly international enterprises not only as a defensive measure but also to take advantage of the vast opportunities in the expanding markets of the world. 

Mr. HOOD. We have a couple of other fellows here, one of whom has scheduling problems. If you will bear with us, Steve, we will go on to the next two gentlemen and then take questions for all three of you together. If that is all right with you, it will facilitate our planning. 

If you will hold vour questions on Steve's area, I would like to introduce Mr. Frank Wilson who will introduce our next two gentlemen. 

Mr. WILSON. It is a very great pleasure this afternoon to introduce. two distinguished gentlemen who need no introduction to this audi-

ence, Mr. Richard Lurie, who is a longtime editor and now publisher of the American Exporter publications and who has been a guiding father to many of us in the export field for many, many years. When we have been in doubt, we have always been moved to have a weather eye on what it is that he is saying or causing to be published. I think in this light as one of the foremost authorities in this field in the United States, he has a tremendous message for us. 

He will be immediately followed by our other speaker of the afternoon, Mr. Harold E. Mix, general credit manager of S. B. Penick Co. in New York. Mr. Mix likewise has been formerly associated with two of the oldest line companies whose export operations and international operations in total have been worldwide. He was formerly with Parke, Davis & Co. and the Firestone Tire & Rubber Co., and the longtime success of these companies in international operations is too well known for the point to require further elaboration. 

Without further introduction, I am happy to give you Mr. Richard 

G. Lurie and Mr. Harold G. Mix as our next speakers.

A SMALL MANUFACTURER CAN ENTER EXPORT PROFITABLY AND IMMEDIATELY, THROUGH USE OF A COMBINATION EXPORT MAN- AGEMENT FIRM LECTURE BY RICHARD G. LURIE,15 VICE PRESIDENT, AMERICAN EX- PORTER PUBLICATIONS, AND PUBLISHER OF AMERICAN EXPORTER AND AUTOMOTIVE WORLD, NEW YORK, N.Y. 

Mr. LURIE. One of the best ways for a new manufacturer in export and a small manufacturer in export-the fastest, cheapest, and most profitable way I know how, is to make use of the combination export management firm. It is much different than an export merchant who buys or sells any kind of product and is not particularly interested in protecting a brand name overseas.

I define the combination export firm in the following way: 

It acts as the exclusive export sales department for several allied, but noncompeting, manufacturers. It used to, I would say, up until 

1952 or 1953, work on a commission basis. But what is more important today for the small manufacturer, particularly someone who does not have large sources of credit available to him immediately is that, today the majority of them do their own financing. In other words, they pay the manufacturer for the order and assume all credit risks abroad. The manufacturer has no further worries. They protect the brand reputation because in many cases they write on your company letterhead. They are the export department for the XYZ manufacturing company. They extend credit overseas. They advertise in your name. 

15 Richard G. Lurie is vice president of American Exporter Publications and publisher of 

American Exporter and Automotive World, New York, N.Y. He received his A.B. degree from Harvard University.

In Mr. Lurie joined American Exporter in 1947 and was appointed editor in 1950. this capacity, he was in charge of editorial direction and spent 3 months abroad every year interviewing overseas distributors and representatives. He is currently publisher. 

Mr. Lurie has written articles on foreign trade for Dun's Review, Steel, Iron Age. 

Export Trade and Shipper, International Markets Sales Management, Barrons, Distri bution Age, and others.

He is a member of the World Trade Advisory Committee to the 

U.S. Department of Commerce; the U.S. Government Trade Mission to Italy: the U.S. 

Government Trade Mission to France: a past president of the World Trade Writers 

Association: a member of the Harvard Club, National Press Club. and Overseas Press 

Club; and he has spoken before foreign trade clubs and chamber of commerce groups in every major city in the United States on merchandising in export. Mr. Lurie has visited and analyzed markets the world over.

I would say in the United States today there are approximately somewhere between 600 to 1,000 combination export firms. In my personal opinion there are only 300 of them that are worth while. 

The majority of these firms are located in New York, Chicago, Cleveland, and the west coast. Big manufacturing is in the four areas but every manufacturing center or port city in the country has several export management firms. To give you a few: 

United Export in South Bend, Ind.; Gordon Fennell in Cedar 

Rapids, Iowa; P. A. Karl in Utica, N.Y.; and Masten-Wright in New

Haven are examples of export management firms away from the main ports of commerce.

Now, how many different manufacturers does the average combination export firm represent exclusively? On the average about 15. 

Frazier & Hansen, the largest in the country, in San Francisco, probably has between 90 and 100 firms that they represent exclusively for oversea sales. And I know firms that have only five. 16 Last year American Exporter Publications did a survey for the U.S. 

Senate Interstate and Foreign Commerce Committee on future foreign trade legislation " and we surveyed approximately 2,500 to 3,000 firms who are actively in export today, and of the 130 combination export management firms that replied to the survey, the total exports of these 130 firms-this is for the year 1959-totaled $387 million. 

Now, that is, I think, quite a substantial hunk. Each of these firms, their exports ranged between, at the lower end if it was a one-man outfit, $500,000 to $2 million, and the bigger ones in the field were up between the $6 million and $10 million range. 

Now, I estimate that these 130 firms represented the individual manufacturing efforts of 2,300 U.S. manufacturers. These 130 firms represented 2,300 U.S. manufacturers and because these manufacturers had used this type of firm, if there were no combination export management firms in the picture, I would say that very few of these firms, of these manufacturers, would have been in the export business at all. 

Putting it another way, I think that combination export management firms account for about 6 to 8 percent of all U. S. manufactured goods leaving the country. I am not talking about commodities. I am talking about semimanufactured and finished manufactured goods.

Who are some of these firms? I would just like to run down some of them just to show you how varied they are in how many different fields. They are of all sizes and shapes, some specialize: Mercator, Inc., in Reading, Pa., specializes in combustion equipment. OMNÍ 

Products in New York is a specialist in plastic products and plastic forming machinery. Engineering Equipment in Chicago specializes in railroad and construction equipment, as does Bert Darrah in Chicago as well. Henry Jahn in New York on agricultural equipment; 

Overseas Equipment Sales, in New York specializes in all kinds of air conditioning and refrigeration equipment as does Sillcox Refrigeration; Aero Maritime in Washington, D.C., specializes in navigation equipment for both airport and marine navigation equipment. 

There are also export specialists in the marine field. 

16 Interstate and Foreign Commerce Committee, U.S. Senate, 86th Cong., 2d sess.

Hearings on "Measures to Expand U.S. Exports, Present Export Credit Facilities and 

Proposals for New Mechanisms," Apr. 28, 29, 1960. Statement of Richard G. Lurie appears on p. 116.

There are many specialist combination export managers in the au tomotive field: F. R. Schwanke in Chicago, Laurence & Erasquin in 

Toledo, Ohio, Marus & Weimer in Cleveland, and in New York John

Prior, Paul Moss, W. D. Blood and S. C. Prado. 

Also there are eight or nine of these combination export management firms that dominate practically all the electronic components export sales, whether industrial, consumer, or communications. I am talking about small components. They dominate the field as far as exports from the United States are concerned. When you talk about hi-fi abroad, most of it comes the component end now-from firms located in New York and Chicago.

Some combination export management firms are very large. They have several product departments, each having a separate sales manager. They may cover a wide range of industrial and consumer products-A. Torres & Overseas Industries in Chicago; Rock International; Sillcox International; Sabal-Kielmann; American Steel Export; Singer Products in New York; Drake America; Market & 

Hammacher; and AD. Auriema in New York; Frazar and Hansen in San Francisco; Ballagh & Thrall in Philadelphia; and Platka 

International in Fort Wayne, Ind.

There are some combination firms who are more or less specialists in regional areas. Muller & Phipps, which is an outstanding firm who specializes in the Far East and I think one of the best ones of its kind. Getz Bros. also operates in the Far East. Otis McAllister, another firm on the west coast, is a specialist in Latin American sales.

I am going to talk about some of the advantages and disadvantages of using the combination export management firms, particularly for the small manufacturer who has never been in export before. 

First, for all small manufacturers, and for some medium manufacturers, it is an absolute must to use an export management firm because in many cases a lot of these smaller companies require cash payment for the goods they sell. They can't afford to extend credit, and again the oversea importer in many countries has to have credit. 

Anybody who sells on letter of credit today in Venezuela, I take my hat off to him. Anybody who sells on 90-day terms in Venezuela and gets paid in those 90 days, I still take my hat off to him. 

You see, this problem of extended credit is very important, and this is one of the principal reasons many small manufacturers cannot afford to have their own built-in export department. 

There are other reasons, of course, such as the cost of operation. 

Sometimes the combination export manager is an important customer of the manufacturer. He may normally account for 10 percent or more of his sales. This means he can get preferential treatment as far as price is concerned, even better than the local U.S. distributor. 

Of course, whenever an export management firm does a substantial 10 percent, that is, of the U.S. manufacturer's business, he may start shaking in his boots. When a combination export management firm does a really fine job, he may be talking himself out of business, because at a certain point the manufacturer looks at the tremendous job that the combination export management firm has done in building up a line, and he will get to the point where he says: "I can do this with my own built-in export department, my own salaried man-

ager, push my own product, my own brand name, and not worry about six or seven other lines." Then there may be a parting of the ways between the export management firm and the manufacturer. 

On the other hand, because of the close personal relationship that is built up between a manufacturer and the combination export management firm, in many cases these relationships go on and on, and I know of some cases today where an export management firm will do for an individual manufacturer well over a million dollars yearly. 

The manufacturer, of course, is under constant pressure to put in his own export department but because of the personal relationship that has built up through the years with the export management firm, the relationship goes on.

Another advantage the export management firm has is that he can consolidate in one single shipment material from various factories, on one ocean bill of lading. Sometimes the individual small manufacturer will find that he can't afford the cost of operation of a very small order with all the things that have to be done special documentation, and so forth-and export packing alone which might be more than the shipment is worth. With an export management firm a customer can buy from $500 or $5,000 worth of material from three or four different factories. 

Another thing is that the oversea importer gets a better credit treatment in general from the combination firm than he will from the manufacturer when the manufacturer is just beginning in export. 

I mentioned before that whenever the export management firm does too good a job, he may be in danger of losing his principal. Now 

I will take a stab at a figure-I think this varies with the type of products you manufacture-but whenever an export management firm does more than $450,000 for an individual manufacturer-depending on the product that is the danger point for the export management firm because I think it is at this point that the manufacturer may say to him: "I can put in my own built-in export department, hire my own export manager, with a bilingual secretary."

Well, this is just about all I have to say on the combination export management firms and whether you use one or whether you set up your own export department, I can only say one thing, in export be flexible. I think this is most important. Remember there are no rules, and that selling in Valparaiso, Ind., is a lot different from selling in Valparaiso, Chile. 

Thank you very much. [Applause.]

Mr. WILSON. I see that we are changing our order of procedure here, and it has been indicated that we would like to have questions at this time for Mr. Lucas and Mr. Lurie, and Mr. Mix will then continue in a few moments after our question period. 

Are there questions from the floor? Will you indicate to whom you are addressing the question, please?

 Mr. SPANG. I am Bill Spang, manager, Overseas Development Co., 

Boston, Mass.

Mr. Lurie, what is the usual markup that these export managers charge? Is there a usual markup? Does it vary?

Mr. LURIE. The markup, first of all, the sale from the manufacturer to the exporter is usually at a distributor net price. He is selling him at the same terms as he sells to his domestic distributors. Sometimes the export management firm will ask the manufacturer for an extra 10 percent or an extra 15 percent, saying that he is acting as the commissioned representative, and also that he has to split his commission, say, with the commission representative overseas and the distributor overseas.

Now, on his markup overseas, he is selling not to retailers abroadhe may be selling through a commission representative abroad who is in turn selling to a distributor-retailer type of operation. In the majority of the countries outside of Europe, you don't find that differentiation that we have here. The distributor in many cases acts as the retailer in selling wholesale in the country. So the markup to the distributor might be running anywhere from 20 to 40 percent, I would say, and then watch out for the price after that because it is all the traffic will bear. I may be wrong on this 20 or 40 percent. Marty, is that about right?

Mr. SCHWARTZ.17 It sounds a little high. We use about 17 percent. 

Mr. LURIE. After the export manager took the product, he marked it up 17 percent to the oversea distributor?

Mr. SCHWARTZ. No. Actually they extended the American distributor prices to the export prices. 

Mr. LURIE. And he worked on a 17-percent commission under the export price?

Mr. SCHWARTZ. That is right. That was the only way we could protect our prices overseas.

Mr. LURIE. Many manufacturers overseas are not as careful on protecting the prices as they should be. This was the case with an export management firm who got an inside commission under the distributor net price. Sometimes the export management firm does not get that, so he has to mark it up. I would say 20 would be tops on that.

They have to look them up half the time, and maybe will work only four or five. One thing that I am trying to get across here, if you are looking for the specialist, the kind of man you have in this country, in most cases abroad you cannot find him unless you really dig. One of the most interesting kinds of commission representatives overseas is the man-and nobody ever hears about this type-who is usually a retired army officer and specializes only in one thing, not selling to the commercial trade but to his local Government.

Our next speaker will be Mr. Harold E. Mix.

EXPORTING: THE UNIVERSAL ROUTE TO OVERSEA PROFITS FOR THE SMALLER COMPANY LECTURE BY HAROLD E. MIX,21 GENERAL CREDIT MANAGER, S. B. 

PENICK & Co., NEW YORK, N.Y. 

(Outline of lecture distributed in advance by Mr. Mix) 

A. Brief recital of extent of duties, experience and company. 

B. Why consider exporting at all? Why more misery?

1. Utilize excess production capacity, greater stability and efficiency fixed expenses comments. 

2. New and wider markets versus domestic situation. 

3. Inventory overstocked? New product? Dumping? 

4. Profit possibilities. 

5. Credit losses lower with good management. 

C. Requirements for successful export operation: 

1. More capital. 

2. Service. 

3. Knowledge of market, risks, etc. 

4. Time for development.

D. Hazards and problems and points of caution; separation of export department from the credit function 

Export manager responsibilities. 

3 C's domestic versus 5 C's foreign. 

Terms decisions. 

Helping export manager negotiate a sale. 

E. Exchange conditions generally and in Venezuela, Argentina, Dominican

Republic Variable-No stability. 

F. Collection procedure. 

G. Mechanics of investigation.

H. Conclusions. 

Mr. Mix. I am with S. B. Penick & Co. We are in drugs and chemicals, raw materials primarily, for the pharmaceutical industry, and we also have insecticides and essential oils and antibiotics and a few other items which enable us to market our products or be accepted in practically every country of the world. We also import, and my duties are with the domestic and export, and some imports. 

I do not have a parochial viewpoint therefore, and I feel I am sales minded.

I have a competent staff that handles the domestic, so I am mostly involved with exports, being well acquainted with other details than only credit or financial matters. We have a simple but efficient export operation.

In our company there is no special division or subsidiary for this purpose. It is like any other department of my company. 

When I came here today, I didn't expect to find so many of you, so competent in the foreign field. I know there are many here who are just considering entering export, but I was surprised to see so many that are, it seems to me, as able as I to talk on this subject. 

21 Harold E. Mix is general credit manager for S. B. Penick & Co. in New York, N.Y. 

He majored in commerce at New York University.

Mr. Mix has had prior experience with Parke Davis & Co. and the Firestone Tire & Rubber Co. before coming to S. B. Penick. For 16 years he has been engaged there in export, import, and domestic affairs as general credit manager.

Mr. Mix has written articles for Credit Management and is active in many professional organizations. Most notable of these are: the Executive Committee of the Inter national Section of the New York Board of Trade, the Benelux_Committee of the 

National Foreign Trade Council, the Foreign Credit Interchange Bureau Council (of which he is a panel member), and the National Chemical Credit Association. In addition, he is a lecturer for New York University advanced export credit procedure.

Nevertheless, I will give you the benefit of my experience and my company's experience, and, of course, it will be slanted toward drug and chemical and pharmaceutical fields and not machinery, and so forth.

My topic is "Exporting-The Universal Route to Oversea Profits for the Smaller Company." That seems to be an optimistic way of saying that if you export, you are going to be successful and you will make a lot of profit. I would add a warning: you might lose money, too. So you have to be careful. It isn't "universal" by any means; it is not automatic. You have to work and you have to know your markets. You can try to sell in a certain country and find you can't, for one reason or another.

I suggest it is better to say "Exporting-A promising route to add profits for the smaller company." With regard to the aim, which is in your book as "Current Problem Areas-Criteria To Decide Whether Exporting Is Feasible, Credit and Extension and Collection." I 

would like to change one word and put it in this order for my talk: "Criteria To Decide Whether Exporting Is Desirable." Then the 

"Current Problem Areas" follows and the "Credit Extensions, and 

Collections." 

Why consider exporting at all? Why all that misery? We have enough misery in domestic, so if you are going to operate in export, there is going to be more misery. 

You heard this morning the gentleman who was talking about licensing abroad, trademarks, and so forth, and you have heard other troubles and difficulties described with regard to pricing, etc. 

So you must by now appreciate that you are really adding trouble to yourself when you branch into export. However, like anything else, the more difficult the task and the greater the risk, the more reward and profit there is in it.

If you have a product and it is properly priced, and it is in demand or you create the demand, and you have stock for reorders, as well as good service, the probability of success in exporting is good. It is just a more specialized way of selling than domestic, just as Mr. 

Wilson mentioned that this morning. Its special attractions are, that it can add sales volume, absorb more overhead, relieve overstocked inventories, level out sales, and production too. This is predicated on your domestic market being either saturated or you can exploit your domestic market to the full. Domestic sales, of course, are the bread winner of your business. So anything I say is with that condition, that your real money comes from your domestic operation. 

However, I also claim that these days once you are in the export business you figuratively have to export or die. 

Where would America be without substantial exports? We cannot maintain our present standard of living without our exportssimilarly with companies. Mr. Wilson mentioned this this morning; the first line company here in America whose export sales now exceed domestic.

In the case of another giant company, I happen to know there is a rumor from within, that they are looking around for a successor to their present capable president who will soon retire. One who has the inside track is a man who is not only competent domesticwise but he is competent exportwise, and because this firm is approaching the situation I just mentioned of dominance in exports, they are figuring

that maybe this next president that they will elect for their company should be one who has a background of being primarily export minded.

Senator Javits this morning-I though he gave an excellent talkhit things on the head, and I am all for what he said. He cited many indications of the importance of exports and its benefits. Other benefits are production capacity, stability, and efficiency of operation, provided, as I said, the domestic market is under control. 

Surplus capacity if it is available for exporting, would lower your costs, naturally. It stabilizes your production, and therefore you are able to make more money per unit. Going into exporting gives you a broader market. It cushions the ups and downs, all of which is profitable.

Take Cuba, for instance, now all frozen up as to trade. Brazil for our product at least likewise is practically frozen and, we are getting little business compared to formerly. But while those countries are slow, Western Europe right now, and the Common Market are coming along. The slack that we lost in Brazil and Cuba is taken up by the markets of Western Europe where we are enjoying nice business.

Another point about exporting is that when you have a recession here in America, you continue selling exportwise for about 6 months, approximately thereafter. It takes about 6 months for the world to catch up with an American recession if the world ever is going to catch up to an American recession, and by the time that 6 months is up, America is just that nearer the end of the recession. This tends to even out production. 

You fellows know, for I can tell by the caliber of your questions this morning, that fixed expenses are already in domestic production and therefore foreign business is plus business. With greater profit potential, on the foreign business, you can even cut your prices below your domestic in order to promote business because the greater part of the cost of exported goods is already absorbed in your domestic gross. 

One word of caution. You can cut your export price too low so that foreign buyers will undercut prices in your domestic market where you are trying to maintain a price.

A lot of countries like Japan, for instance--a wonderful country, a real threat to us, and yet you must admire them-there is Japan underselling us pricewise and yet she has domestic laws which prohibit the same products which are sold to us, being sold in Japan because the prices at which we buy, are lower than those in Japan. 

Accordingly, you must watch your pricing policy or you defeat your purpose in exporting.

Of course, every dollar of export business as a result of stabilization and fixed expenses, means that it is worth that much more than the domestic dollar. Each dollar of sales, exportwise, because of these savings, this extra capacity, this surplus that you have, is going to mean more to you in your balance sheet and your operations profit at the end of the year, than would be a domestic dollar. That is the attraction to exporting, to try to earn more profit for each individual dollar of sales.

You all know already that an export business, to those who are already exporting, frequently is the difference between a profit and a

deficit for your year's operation. Bear in mind too, that the smaller the company and the larger its share of export business, the greater importance it is to that company. The larger the percentage increase of sales, the greater the potential of profit. For example, to the smaller company, $100,000 worth of business would be important-but it wouldn't mean much to a giant company. The giant company, of course, might feel it is so efficient that it doesn't consider a miscellaneous $100,000 worth of sales as worth the effort. They are not interested in these crumbs, nor are they geared to efficiently scrape all over the export globe for every dollar of business. 

The smaller company, because it freewheels better, is more flexible, would be able to pick up what the giant company would call the crumbs of business, and would find such business profitable. Likewise 

I the possibilities of export expansion is correspondingly more important and proportionately larger for the smaller company. 

There are bigger orders in export. The foreign buyer being farther away from the United States has a time element, even if air express is involved. If it is a steamer shipment, the foreign buyer must allow for even more time between the order and the receipt of the shipment. Therefore he usually gives you a bigger order. We find so anyway. And the bigger the order, usually the greater the profit. 

Maybe it is not tremendous but it all adds up. 

Exporting also is a method of disposing of your oversupply of merchandise.

My final benefit that I have listed is that credit losses can be less in export providing you managed your operation efficiently, and even though there is a higher risk. In my company there are less bad debts in export than there are in domestic, percentagewise, and I i feel that most companies agree such is the case. I add, let me remind you, that it must be a well-managed operation. 

My next topic is requirements for a successful export operation. In the pamphlet before you, it says "more capital," but after thinking it over, that is not the right expression to use. I mean "adequate capital." It might be more capital in certain cases but you must have adequate capital, and in the calculation of the amount of capital that you do need, you must bear in mind the length of time it takes to collect for your merchandise. A sale is never completed until the money is in your till. Therefore if you are giving 90-day terms, you must allow for 90 days at least before you get your money back, and if you grant longer terms and by the way, just as Senator Javits said this morning, export terms are lengthening here in America-you need even more working capital. Suppliers are having to give longer terms these days, in order to meet competition.

Bear in mind, too, that you need capital for cases like Argentina, and other countries that at one time or another have been frozen a year or more. Argentina was frozen 3 years; Brazil was frozen pretty near 3 years; Colombia about as much. Here is merchandise which you have been selling not on letter of credit, but on a draft basis, invested capital, and all of a sudden these countries just don't have the dollars for your drafts and your money is tied up for up to 

3 years.

There are a number of companies, domestic companies, that have gone out of business, or become bankrupt, simply because their working capital was frozen in these countries that I mentioned. They

couldn't discount their bills at the bank any more. They couldn't raise funds because of the length of time it was going to take to get their money. And so they had to sell out or they went bankrupt. 

I mentioned before that you needed inventory for reorders. That is important. Don't just start up an export business and make it a one-shot operation. You need to provide good service. You need to provide the same good service like you give to your domestic customers. 

You can't neglect your export orders and consider them secondary or postponable. There are enough delays in export without adding to them. Allowing them to remain unattended on your desk or letting a secondary domestic clerk handle an export order and letting them get bogged down does not promote a successful export operation. These days you have to give good service to the foreign customers the same as you do to domestic ones. 

Many times service is your best selling point. Your prices might be the same as your competition, but the service, dependable service that the foreign customer looks to you for and thinks of you in that manner, is the determining point for getting the order. A corollary to service is the packaging and conformity and quality of the product. In our industry, at least, we feel that that is very important, and that it overcomes other shortcomings. We pride ourselves on what we feel is ability in that direction, and from the comments we get, we are convinced that we are superior, and it adds to sales for 

S. B. Penick & Co. 

Of course, you need knowledge. That is why you are all here; maybe not all of you, but many. You have got to know your competition and products and the marketing factors which others later in this seminar will speak upon. You must know the practices in each area. The same practices in Colombia are different than in Argentina. And, of course, different from the Far East, et cetera. They are all individual. That is one of the difficulties of exports; learning that. You should try to know all about your customer if you really wish to do a good job, just the same as you try to do a good job domestically. As in domestic you try to visit your customers in export too, if you reach the point where you can afford that kind of effort. 

Customers have idiosyncrasies, certain ways of ordering, certain habits. For example, a buyer from Venezuela will visit my firm and he wants to talk to nobody less than the vice president in charge of our exports. That is an idiosyncrasy. He is only a medium class customer, but we accommodate him. 

Another man might come from another area and be content with seeing a department or area man. Catering to these peculiarities is important and we make it a point to do so. 

You should know Government controls, such as licenses, laws, and decrees. Just because you get a license doesn't mean you get your money. A license could entitle you to only import into the country. You still have to think about that exchange license upon which depends your liquidation.

You must bear in mind exchange clause countries where there is a local deposit first, before the conversion into dollars, which might take a long while. How long that will take affects your working capital.

There are certain countries, too, where you just can't ship on a c.o.d. or documentary sight draft basis. These countries like 

Venezuela and Colombia don't have what is called "to order" bills of lading. Once the bill of lading names that particular customer, he doesn't have to pay first before getting the goods even though you attach the bill of lading to the draft. He can get the merchandise without having to remit. So you must investigate well the caliber of your customer and determine whether to trust him as an open account case, for that is in effect what it is. There is however an alternative course which I will discuss under "Terms." 

My final point here on requirements for a successful export operation is that you need time for development. Some markets dry up over night for one reason or another. Maybe a better product is available from another country. Maybe the country's dollars are drying up. A sound export operation cannot be built quickly. Time is needed. Country acceptance of your name and product. It is a long-range job with ability to weather high and low spots, and average them out. So plan for the long pull-5 years is my idea. The other seminar speakers will take more about that. 

This brings me to hazards and special problems, and points of caution. To be a successful exporter of long standing, you can't afford to sell inferior merchandise or outdated third-rate products. 

Grade A operators today realize that Germany, Italy, France or

England or Japan, all have quality products just the same as we do. 

This is not 20 years ago. They have just as good a price as we have. 

There is no use kidding ourselves, they are every bit as good as we are, generally speaking, maybe a special part here or there, a particular product, or there is no competition and you have the field to yourself. 

But generally speaking, and certainly in my line, there is hardly anything that can't be matched by the wide-awake foreign countries I mentioned. Your mental attitude must be trained to give the customer what he wants. You are investing your capital in export, not as a convenience, but for profit, so build service and product if you want to stay in the foreign field. 

A warning. Recently I myself was at another first grade seminar.

They were telling me about Iran-how suppliers here had shipped merchandise into Iran and it was found inadequate. This had continued for a period of time and to such an extent that this year the customers in Iran apparently banded together and were beginning to sue because of inferior merchandise, causing all sorts of trouble. 

There are other countries that will do the same and there are individual customers who have pride in their operation who will give you a hard time and a bad reputation if you ship them the improper kind of merchandise, and if you continue to be that kind of an operator.

It is well to keep local stocks in the country in which you are selling. If you can afford consignment stocks, fine. See if you can't do that. The reason? A customer in Colombia or Iran might want some of your product and it takes a month or more by steamer, depending on conditions, to get the merchandise. If you had local stocks, the customer could pick up his phone or write a letter. Thus this facility avoids delay of correspondence between continents and it encourages the customer in the foreign country to think along lines

of your ability to fill the order immediately. If he bought from you here in America, he would have to get an import license, apply for exchange, and clear through customs and all that; so it is a service to the customer if you sell from local stocks.

As I said before, the other countries are equal to us in quality. 

They have as good service. And, in addition, they are usually in a low-cost position. That is the reason they are able to meet our prices or better them. They knocked my company out of the market in certain areas because of price. They better us in price and they extend longer credit terms. Better price, longer credit terms, why should a customer order from us if they can get all those things elsewhere nearby if the quality and service is just as good. 

So in my industry we try to overcome some of these difficulties by pioneering in a product. We are in drugs and chemicals. We try to create a combination that will alleviate an illness better or be less toxic, et cetera. We try to use our brains by learning what the other fellow is doing competitively, and then try to make our product superior. Use your ingenuity to get the jump on competition.

Don't take for granted that the foreign customer knows your prod uct. Many suppliers here in America feel that because they mail out what they consider is good literature, and have a good product, and adequate directions are on the package, and so forth, that that is all there is to it. Even when we are already in the market, and we try to be specific as to directions and bibliography, and so forth, there are all sorts of questions which a foreign buyer asks. Therefore, if you can have some agent abroad, someone who can promptly answer such questions and answer them adequately and promptly and fully, you promote your product and the customer will think of you first at the expense of your competitor.

We try not to talk down to the customer. We like to feel that we treat them just like a domestic customer. You will note that I repeat this over and over. Don't be terse. Don't be snappy like the 

American way usually is in answering letters. The foreign customer doesn't mind verbosity, we find that he doesn't mind repetition of thought, as long as the message gets across to him and he is satisfied and he knows your product fully. Then he is sold on it, and naturally you are going to get new orders and earn more profit. Well, so much for the hazards.

Next is the separation of the export department from the credit function. I can tell from the questions this morning that some of you who are in export sales already are fully competent to operate a credit department. Many export managers know all about credit functions. But on the other hand, as they get busier, and maybe as others less qualified would find, they will all of a sudden think only or mostly of the sale and forget about the credit angle, the foreign exchange situation, and whether the dollars are going to be available 6 months from now in such and such a country, or they lose sight of a certain governmental decree and exchange requirement, and boom, all of a sudden they have themselves frozen up and there is a bad debt looming up, for one reason or another. That bad debt might be of such an amount that it could be the equivalent or more of the credit department's entire operation for the year.

So you must determine whether the export manager has too much to do, and whether he can handle the credit function also. 

On the other hand, I know export managers who are so concerned and dedicated to protecting their company's investment, that in my opinion they have lost orders because they have been overcautious with regard to interpreting the credit function. They are afraid that maybe they won't get their money back in 6 months or x time, and so they pass up an order. Or they ask for a letter of credit or cash and lose the order to competitors who give time terms. 

An export manager has plenty to do just to advertise, and watch regulations applicable to his product, without being burdened with more responsibility.

Term is my next point. There are few foreign problems when selling cash on the barrel head in New York. If we are not, then we have to think not only of the character, capacity, and capital as in domestic, but you have to add country conditions and the currency of the foreign country. Consideration of all these matters is necessary before making up your mind what your terms should be.

Letter of credit-well, that is easy. Documentary sight draft-as 

I said, remember what countries will take it and in what countries it doesn't mean anything. You try to ship on documentary sight draft to Venezuela and you have to go through a lot of extra rigmarole in order to arrange for payment before the customer gets the goods and even then you have to contend with carelessness and fear that the goods will be released regardless of the instructions you give. Then all you can do is try to collect or eventually sue if you think you are stuck.

In my line I would say 25 percent of our operation is open account, payable upon receipt of documents or payable on receipt of shipment. 

The documents are sent direct to the customer. Another 25 percent is clean sight draft, which in effect is the same as an open account. 

Again the documents go to the customer. The balance is L/c, or usance drafts.

In some countries it is a poor practice to sell open account. It is advisable to use drafts only so that your draft is registered officially in those countries and not be dependent on the mañana spirit of some customers. If there is a scarcity of dollars, or if there is a past history of difficulty in meeting liquidations promptly, the indebtedness will be officially registered and you will be in line for as prompt payment as is possible.

On some open-account transactions during the freeze in Argentina a number of years ago, all the drafts were paid first, and that took up to 3 years. It took another half year before the open accounts were paid.

The terms on which you sell to individual countries are determined also by the practice of the country or the practice of your industry in that country. We can sell the very same merchandise in one country on a sight-draft basis and yet we are obliged to sell at 90 days or more in another country. Sometimes terms are dependent on stock position. 

When the export department gets the orders you are going to be freer and easier, if necessary, in determining terms in order to expedite sale of the merchandise, while still using good business judgment as to the credit risk.

The cost factor likewise influences your decision. If it is a product that is well accepted and there is no overstocked position and if the margin of profit is tight, why, naturally, I am not going to be as liberal in extending terms as when the cost factor is right. The closer the margin, the closer my terms are going to be. 

As you would suspect, with a new product it is customary to be more liberal as to terms so as to promote market acceptance of the new item. 

There are so many different ways of handling terms. That is one of the interesting points about export, I think. So much is not repetitious. There are so many ways and angles of approaching and solving a problem. Some customer might want long terms which you are unwilling to grant. You negotiate with him and try to minimize exposure as much as possible. My usual practice is to try to reduce the long terms request to shorter or even split terms such as reducing a 180 day request to say 90, or even 90/120/150. Recovering the cost of the merchandise on the shorter terms and accepting the longer terms with regard to the profit percentage of the order is good business when long terms cannot be avoided. This attitude helps the export department clinch an order.

I talked about frozen countries before in connection with exchange conditions. I will add this. The countries that budget their dollars are usually better to deal with in our prime preferential industry. 

The ones that have licenses and allot their exchange keep their affairs in order. They watch their income and outgo better and there is less chance of a backlog of unpaid drafts due to sudden shortages of exchange. 

Colombia was a case of poor management three years ago. They over licensed and did not budget their dollars. A backlog occurred, and we had to take notes for 18 months, after we had already waited 

18 months. 

Collection procedure: I don't want to bore you with details. The bank's responsibility is to automatically follow up periodically for liquidation. They do a good job, but in my operation I like them to do a little better. So I request them to make a closer follow up than is their custom.

I will go into more detail with regard to the mechanics of investigation. One's judgment can only be as good as the facts. If there is an important order-I get all the credit information and data possible. My first source of inquiry is always the banks. All top banks in New York, in my opinion, do a swell job for you in getting informaton. It is a service to you. Why all the banks in New York don't get together in the interest of efficiency, economy and mutual advantage and pool their interests by having one credit service department for all is beyond me. There is so much duplication of effort. My bank will investigate a certain customer in France while another bank is doing the same thing for some other company and a third bank repeats the process for still another competitor. 

Regardless, I think banking institutions here in America do a swell job getting financial information. Indeed, they do such a good job that usually I don't have to get any other credit information than what the banks report. There are of course problems where all possible information is imperative. I use the Dun & Bradstreet rating index and reports. The foreign credit interchange reports on others' experience are valuable, too. These two services are "musts.”

I do not, however, need these two services for every account, only for those which are borderline, or where exposure is strained, et cetera.

The U.S. Department of Commerce reports are helpful, too, but if the report is not recent it takes them too long to update them. 

I always get my agent's opinion. We have agents in each country. 

His opinion on the record is important. I don't necessarily want financial detail from him, although it is welcome, but I do want him. on record as recommending the customer.

You all know that information abroad is scarce, and sparse. The financial statements which may come in may or may not be dependable. You have to be in this profession a long while to recognize whether a financial statement from abroad is dependable. When we get an inquiry or a request for a quotation, et cetera, we immediately apply for all possible credit information within the scope that I just explained, so that if an order does come in, it will receive prompt attention and consideration of whatever special terms that might be requested. We like to feel that this is good service which expedites completing the order. Unless satisfactory credit information is at hand, the order is delayed at risk of customer's disapproval.

Even with a letter of credit, I make it a point to get credit information right away because the letter of credit order today may be an open account or documentary sight draft or term draft order tomor- = row.

In conclusion, I think export offers great opportunities for increased profit, but the process is not universal. And it is not automatic. It 

I requires special knowledge, extra effort to conserve capital. You have got to limit your exposure if you are just starting at export, it seems to me, and watch out for excessive losses because of that danger.

I am ready to answer all the questions you have to the best of my 

1 ability. If in the future you ever have occasion to feel that you want an opinion from me, please be assured of courtesy and all possible help if you call me at my office. 

Shoot away.

We are in a situation right now where I want to know if it should be more capital. I would like to explain what I mean and then ask a question.

We went into operation in Great Britain and at the time we thought that the market there would yield about $50,000 annually in business.

It seems, because of the time that it takes to make shipments, to get payments back to the United States, that you need more of an investment into a foreign marketplace than you would if you do business in the United States.

We are new at this thing and are probably not doing everything right, but to do about a quarter of a million dollars of business, we have inventory over in England plus accounts receivable, about $150,-

Mr. PUTNAM. I am Mr. Putnam of the American Foreign Insur ance Association.

Do you expect that the availability of foreign credit insurance, which we have been hearing about recently, will make a significant difference and stimulate an increase to your company in the export operation?

Mr. Mix. If that credit insurance is available, I doubt that my company will use it at all. You heard me this morning ask Senator

Javits to be reminded to have available in that program a facility where I can ask for insurance on a one-transaction or a one-country basis. I realize if I just ask for one country or one transaction I am going to pay 8 percent, someone said, for that insurance. But if I am interested in just insuring that one particular order, that 8 percent is covered in the order. Otherwise, we are not in a position to pay the regular 3 or 4 or 5 percent, whatever it is going to be, for blanket coverage of all our sales. We don't want it. We think it would be an extra added expense for us. We don't need it. 

What's more, we feel that we are cultivating the market efficiently and screening well our credit risks. Our bad debt losses are so negligible, about one-fifth of 1 percent, that it isn't worth while to add on 2 or 3 or 4 or 5 percent in credit insurance programs. As far as we are concerned, we wouldn't be interested in blanket insurance.

Mr. Mix. I think that is a general situation. They manage their accounts well, they investigate their customers well, and so they have a better line on them. Not only that but I think lower losses also are due to the foreign customer who imports into his own country, being a higher caliber operator and, therefore, more worthwhile and of a 

23 Reidar E. Gundersen, vice president, Morgan Guaranty Trust Co., New York.

better financial status than the average domestic customer here in the States of whom there is a greater percentage of borderline cases and risks.

My domestic losses are very little, due, I feel, to the screening and the credit investigations that we make. But I repeat, and I will stand on this, that generally speaking the credit losses in the foreign area are less than in domestic notwithstanding the greater risk.

Mr. HOOD. If you feel like I do, maybe you would like to stand up while I am making a few announcements and getting on with our next speaker. I will talk while we are standing here a minute.
