LICENSING: CRITERIA FOR LICENSING, SELECTING THE LICENSEE AND TERMS AND CONDITIONS OF THE AGREEMENT LECTURE BY JOHN H. BRECK, JR., SEMINAR COCHAIRMAN, AND EXECU- TIVE VICE PRESIDENT, JOHN H. BRECK, INC., SPRINGFIELD, MASS. 

Mr. BRECK. Before I get started on the subject of licensing perhaps a little orientation regarding our company might be in order. 

We are manufacturers of hair preparations and we do approximately $25 million a year domestic business. We are a privately owned 

24 Alexander Stanley, contributing editor, International Markets-Dun's Review & Modern Industry, New York, N.Y. 

25 Walter Lambert, executive vice president, Thermo Electric Co., Inc., Saddle Brook, N.J.

company. Our export volume is about 5 percent of our domestic volume. So that, in terms of export, we are not big business at all. 

We are very far from it.

We have only been in the export business seriously about 6 years, and I would say that our products are available in about 70 countries, but we have, I think, about 30 licensing agreements, and these licensing agreements represent probably 90 percent of our total export business.

We have attempted to license in most of the countries that have a substantial volume potential. In smaller countries we didn't feel that licensing was practical.

In our export operations I think that we use all the various approaches that are available in that we export finished goods. We have a wholly owned subsidiary. That is John H. Breck, Bermuda, where we manufacture for shipment to the West Indies, and also we ship to England from Bermuda, and also Bermuda happens to be a tax sanctuary.

Also, we operate under a submanufacturing arrangement, and we are contemplating a joint ownership in Guatemala to service the Central American countries. We haven't made any final decision as to whether we will make it a joint ownership or wholly owned ownership.

Our interest in getting into the export field is in the possibility of making profits in exports, but also I think we recognize that in our type of preparations, our type of products, a brand name is extremely important and that we feel that to maintain a name of prestige and quality domestically it is necessary to be international, so that our interest aside from profits in going international is the need for having a name that is international in scope, not just domestic. 

Licensing, of course, is perhaps the middle approach because most companies think that it involves more risk and commitment to a foreign market than exporting from the United States does. But, on the other hand, it is less risk and commitment than owning and managing your own manufacturing and distribution facilities abroad. In other words, it is quite possible through licensing to go abroad without any investment in dollars. 

I guess perhaps I should explain a little bit this term "licensing" so that there isn't any misunderstanding.

We need licenses or our export customers need licenses to import our products from the United States, but we are not referring to licenses in that sense when we talk licensing. It refers to the licensing of foreign patents, trademarks, and other industrial property rights by their private owners.

So this form of licensing that we are discussing is perhaps an intermediary system of going overseas.

Many times you might be just as interested in getting a licensing agreement or trademark agreement in this country as you are overseas, and you can work a cooperative arrangement, and there are trademark agreements. This is the type that our company is primarily in, trademark agreements, because we have trademarked our product in all those countries where it is possible to do so, and we own these, and people who are interested in selling under our trademark must be licensed by us.

There are, however, manufacturer know-how agreements. There are technical assistance and engineering service agreements. There are franchise agreements such as an authorized distributor or user type, and there are engineering service contracts, management contracts such as those used by the Hilton Hotel Corp. There is the franchise agreement under which soft drinks are bottled and distributed in foreign markets. These are all specialized forms of agreements.

In addition to the rights and forms of assistance covered by a patented product or process, a trademark or trade name, there are specialized manufacturing techniques and other proprietary rights generally referred to as company know-how. There are components, material, or equipment essential to a manufacturing process. And there is also the technical advice and services of various sorts. There is marketing advice and assistance of various sorts. These might be of interest in many situations.

Manufacturers of products similar to yours in foreign countries who are in need of marketing advice-and I am thinking of an aerosol filler who has entered into licensing agreements, and all he has provided to the licensor is just some technical know-how and some marketing information. Possibly this could be predicated solely on a financial or management participation in a foreign operation. 

Now the usual parties to a licensing agreement are made between. an independent licensor in one country and an independent licensee in another country. There are, however, different combinations which can modify considerably the working relationships and practical effects of an agreement. In many licensing agreements the licensor is a domestic or foreign subsidiary of a U.S. company, or an autonomous division of the company. In other words, you can have a wholly owned subsidiary overseas but you can still operate a licensing arrangement with them, or you can have a joint venture overseas which is handled through a licensing agreement. And usually the licensee is the one that performs both manufacturing and distributing operations, but that isn't necessarily so.

You can have a licensee who does your manufacturing exclusively, and then you can have licensees who do your distributing, or more than one.

Now no one knows exactly how widespread foreign licensing is today, but various estimates have placed the number of agreements at around 12,000. There was an article in the Wall Street Journal, I believe it was last week, that had some information and they had concluded that there were about 12,000.

In a recent statement that was made to a congressional committee it was suggested that the gross royalty income from foreign licensing is about $500 million a year. There is no explanation of how this estimate is made. So we don't know exactly how authentic this particular figure is. 

Interest in licensing was stimulated by the same postwar factors that led to additional direct investment abroad, and that was increasing trade restraints and regulations. I think that we all find it a lot easier if we could manufacture here and ship overseas rather than getting involved in licensing or manufacturing overseas. But, because of these trade restraints and regulations, it is no longer possible. 72811-61——6

Also, of course, there is the recurrent dollar shortages and exchange difficulties which make it impractical to export. And there is the resumption of more aggressive competition by foreign manufacturers. 

And in that connection I would like to indicate in our own particular field we find that the oversea, the international market is perhaps more competitive than the domestic market. Not only are all the domestic competitors in the oversea markets but all the local, so-called domestic manufacturers in those countries are there. So we find all the same people we have to battle with here are overseas plus some other very good ones. So it is not a very easy situation at best. 

As I am sure you are all aware, there has been a growth of nationalistic sentiment and a desire for economic self-sufficiency on the part of foreign countries, which makes it more imperative that we do business in their country.

The growth of licensing has been part of a general postwar boom in the oversea market development. Until very recently at least it has not led to or been accompanied by a decline in other forms of foreign trade development. In other words, licensing has increased in scope, and while it has increased in scope, also companies have continued to make increased investments, direct investments overseas.

And, in addition to that, we have continued to see a growth in our exports oversea climb.

When a U.S. company arranges for oversea manufacture of its product, either through its own facilities or through licensing, it is usually necessary, if you are going to continue to be competitive in the market, that you do also, and also all of our foreign competitors are licensing or manufacturing in other countries so that if we are going to keep up with them not only in their country but competitive in other countries, it is necessary that we take the easiest route possible. 

As I have indicated, there is a considerable prevalence of licensing activity, and in some groups it is so extensive that it is a subject all in itself, and I think this group includes some of the major oil, chemical, rubber, electrical, and communications companies. They have developed or acquired numerous patent rights of their products or processes, and licensed them widely on a nonexclusive basis primarily for added revenue or for cross-licensing purposes.

Other respondents with numerous agreements have licensed a single important process or a distinctive new product to a licensee in countries throughout the world, and they are trying to extend their licensing to other products or to use it as a means for furthering their own international marketing operations.

Now a few companies such as Westinghouse, American Chemical 

Paint Co., Borg-Warner International, Raytheon, have negotiated foreign manufacturing and technical assistance agreements on a broad scale, both areawise and productwise. Westinghouse, for instance, has 96 agreements with companies in 26 different countries, and these agreements range in coverage from one or two items to extremely broad arrangements for the development of an electric industry from the ground up. So apparently there are many forms of licensing in all degrees. 

The American Chemical Paint Co. has some 150 to 160 active license agreements with manufacturers in 45 different countries. They have as many as 30 licensees in one country.

Cluett-Peabody licenses the use of its "sanforized" process and trademark to hundreds of foreign countries around the world. They don't attempt to have any relative exclusivity. 

And I think you are all aware of what has been going on with Coca Cola and Pepsi-Cola, and also Canada Dry. The franchise agreements that they enter into throughout the world, these number in the hundreds and they are growing very rapidly. 

In an age of contract licensing agreements they are relatively a new form of international business. Prior to World War I there were very few licensing agreements. It did become more popular between World 

War I and World War II, but since World War II the number has in creased substantially and it continues to increase. 

The geographic distribution of licensing is primarily located, of course, in those countries that are industrially developed with Europe and the United Kingdom having about 50 percent of the total licensing agreements in existence. Latin America has 17 percent, Asia has 12, Australia-New Zealand 9 percent; Canada 6 percent; 

Africa 4 percent.

Broadly speaking, licensing is used as a marketing device by the same industries and for the same products that figure prominently in our export shipments and direct investments overseas. That is, machine tools, electrical and industrial equipment, automotive products, household appliances, rubber products, chemicals, drugs, and pharmaceuticals. It is also used for products that do not lend themselves to export either because they are too heavy or bulky in relation to their value for oversea shipment, and also because local production is easy and more economical, or because local servicing and maintenance is essential for customer satisfaction.

I think that is very important. I know it works the other way, that some oversea companies who sell here in the States do relatively well initially, but their service is so poor that ultimately they disappear from the market.

U.S. companies selling products of this sort overseas have usually acquired their own facilities in some of the major foreign markets and tend to use licensing as an interim step, and I again emphasize that, that they use licensing as an interim step, and I think that is the view that we can take of it in our company, that it was impossible for us to make major investments in the major markets in the world simultaneously, so that we feel that through licensing we can establish our brand, we can develop our business. But we know that we can't do it as well through licensing as we can having our own either joint venture or wholly owned subsidiary, so that ultimately we would plan to have our own companies in the major markets. 

In the smaller markets we feel that licensing perhaps is the ultimate, and we continue to operate on that basis. 

The reasons for licensing-there are many of them. The principal ones are as follows:

To obtain extra income from technical know-how and services; to spread the cost of company research and development programs. 

I think all of us today are more and more involved in research and development. We note the amount of money that is required to spend in this direction, so that we can spread this cost over a greater market. It is to our benefit.

To maximize returns from research findings and accumulated know

To retain established markets that have been closed or threatened by trade restrictions. That is what forces a lot of us into making these decisions quickly.

To reach new markets not accessible by export from existing facilities.

To enter or expand foreign markets quickly with minimum effort or risk.

To gain cost or other advantages of local manufacture without committing capital abroad.

To augment limited domestic capacity and management resources for serving foreign markets.

To provide oversea_sources of supply and services for important domestic customers. In other words, you can have a very important customer domestically who is operating overseas, and he would like to use your products or your services overseas, and if they are not available to him, he is going to a competitor. So many times you go to export because one of your customers is very strongly involved in export.

To develop market outlets for raw materials or components made by the domestic company.

To build goodwill and acceptance for other company products or services. 

To develop sources of raw materials or components for other company operations. 

And to conserve the dollar exchange. 

To discourage possible infringement, impairment, or loss of company patents or trademarks. In other words, if-as your friend Mr. 

Sheedy indicated, somebody was picking up his trademarks. So, if you don't get in there and do it first, you are apt to leave it to someone else.

To diversify sources and types of company income. Το pave the way for future investment.

To get the feel of the market through licensing, and you can make your own decision as to whether or not you should make company investments at some future date.

To acquire reciprocal benefits from foreign know-how, research, and technical services. I presume in some instances this is possible, and in our own particular situation we find that the level of technical know-how, the quality in our industry is relatively low in foreign operations, so that we don't look to any possibilities in the immediate future of getting any results from foreign know-how or research. 

To further the economic development of foreign countries. But basically I think that when you are considering going to licensing, it is these three principal reasons, and that is to obtain additional revenue from company-owned patents, trademarks, and accumulated know-how; to gain some technical or strategic advantage in marketing your product overseas; and to acquire reciprocal know-how in research in foreign countries.

Now that is a very imposing list of reasons for going into licensing. 

The reasons for not licensing are very few.

Lack of incentive. In other words, you feel that there is no profit possibility primarily; and also another reason for not licensing is the loss of control and cost considerations. You might have some secret formulas, secret processes which you don't feel you wish to make available to anyone else. Therefore, you are not willing to operate on a license basis. And we have fought this fight in our company. 

Perhaps most of you feel in cosmetics we don't have any secret formulas; we don't have any real know-how in our products and so forth. But that isn't necessarily true. We do have some secrets and we do have some formulations that require considerable research and development, and we find our technical people in research and development are very reluctant to part with this information in terms of sharing it with people overseas. So we have to make some major policy decisions before we make the decision that we would provide our licensees with all the information regarding our products. 

Also, there is a question of reputation. It is quite possible to get tied up with a licensee who can do irreparable damage to your reputation or your brand name. It is essential when considering licensing to make some policy decisions, and if you don't have a policy it may result in lost opportunities or at least a costly delay at some future date.

So even if you are not in licensing now, I think that it is worthwhile to make some decisions regarding licensing so that when the opportunity arises or the question arises, you will know what direction you are going to go in.

Now what impetus do we get to make licensing decisions? 

Well, as indicated, we many times back into this because we receive inquiries from overseas, and we have to make a decision as to whether we wish to follow this route or not. And also you have perhaps seen what your competitors or associate companies in your industry have done in terms of their licensing operations overseas.

I think there are many good examples perhaps in every industry of the success of licensing arrangements. The company factors that you have to consider in licensing are, No. 1, the existing state and status of the foreign market and the objectives and future plans for foreign markets, and the alternative methods by which you could achieve your objectives. The suitability of company products for foreign production and sale through a licensed operation. The adequacy of existing organization and personnel resources to handle a licensing program. 

Now this, I think, is very important. If you are going to go into licensing you want to make sure that you are going to be able to provide your licensee with all the help and assistance that he is going to require. Don't feel that you can say OK, we are going to license you, and send a manual that tells him how your products are made and what they look like and so forth and so on, and then anticipate that he is going to take it from there and do a good job, because it just doesn't happen that way. 

Licensing takes longer. It takes longer to get a license underway, it takes longer than having a baby. In some instances it takes years and you have to be prepared to make available to your licensee all of your administrative operational staff.

We found this to be one of the problems in attempting to get our licensing operations underway in that we had a manager of our international company and it was his problem, and we let him worry about it. But we just couldn't accomplish anything by doing this. 

So, even though the top management of our company was sold on the licensing and the international markets, we found it was necessary to sell the second and third echelons in our company, and we did this by having critiques with them on the part of our international manager when he came back from a trip. We would have a meeting and he would tell all of our people what the problems were overseas, those things that we had to do to accomplish our objective overseas, and then we sent some of our people who were not in the international division overseas to let them have the feel of the problem.

One area was in our research and development because we had to formulate some of our products a little bit differently because of the hard water conditions in some countries. We couldn't get our laboratory too enthusiastic about this particular proposition. They would always have it at the end of the list of their priorities of work to be done.

So, finally, we sent our research and development director overseas, and he came back after about a 3-week's trip, and I guess for a month or so they didn't do any work on our domestic problems. 

They got all fired up on international products. 

So, finally, we got something done. So it is essential that you sell these people on your international operation. And, of course, one of the big considerations is the prospective return in relation to the time, the resources and the risks involved. This always has to be the ultimate consideration before going overseas in any way or licensing.

Now it is essential to organize for licensing. The policy, as I indicated, decisions regarding licensing have to be made at the top level, and then you have to do some market research and analysis as best you possibly can. Certainly the many sources of information that are available to us here in this country regarding markets are not available overseas. So you have to depend upon many of the resources that have already been discussed. And you have to start exploring contacts and how you are going to negotiate agreements, and you consider the transfer of engineering, productive know-how, and technical assistance in launching the licensing venture. You have to give it the administrative supervision and control, and it is extremely essential when organizing for licensing or organizing for oversea operations that you have a centralized direction and control. 

In other words, it is not a part-time job for anyone once you make the decision. 

Before licensing overseas there is one other consideration, and I mention it, that if any of you people are so fortunate to have this situation in your company, that is the antitrust considerations in licensing. The basic antitrust law in the United States is the Sherman Act, and this, of course, was designed primarily for the protection of domestic consumers. Nevertheless it does have some reference in the law to foreign business. So if any of you have a product or a patent that is so exclusive that you are going to be subject to the 

Sherman Act, then perhaps you had better first check with your

attorney to see whether or not you will be in violation of it if you start licensing overseas.

Deciding when and where to license: Well, we have mentioned the need for market research. It is pretty difficult to accomplish this overseas, but at least an effort should be made. Whether or not you actually need it depends on your particular situation. If you have been exporting for years into a market very successfully you know that there is a demand for your product, and research perhaps isn't too essential. Or if you find a strong distributor, even though you haven't done well, but you have a strong distributor who can advise you on the market, perhaps you don't have to do any research. But it is necessary to gage the market potential. 

Also, timing is a very important consideration. You might have a product that there is a highly developed market for in many areas throughout the world, but in some particular country this might not be so. There has been quite a bit said about Japan. 

In our particular industry there are quite a few cosmetics sold in Japan, but not shampoo to any great degree. They are still using the old cake soap. And I think the time is just about ripe to move into 

Japan with a shampoo product.

There are shampoos on the market, but as yet those who are selling them haven't sold women on the idea that they need a shampoo to clean their hair. So we are going to let some of the other people make the market to some extent, and then we hope we can cash in on their efforts.

Well, also there is the competitive situation before going to licensing. 

What is the immediate competitive situation? Maybe someone is so firmly entrenched in the market that it isn't worth while for you to attempt an operation. This might be true immediately, but in terms of the long-term outlook it might be worthwhile for you to go ahead. 

So you have to consider the competitive considerations, both the immediate and the long term.

Also, you have to consider the production facilities and costs and whether the raw materials and components that you require for your product are available. And also the supporting facilities and services that are required for your product and also the labor and management skills. And, again, you always have to consider the prospective properties, what the competitive prices are and competitive costs are. 

And also you must consider the investment climate. What are the national policies? What are the laws? What are the practices?

Look at Cuba. Everybody thought that was a wonderful country to go into even under Batista, and it was, but that is not true any more. So you have to consider the political stability and responsibility of the country, whether it has socialistic or nationalistic aims, the economic and business regulations in the country, the attitude toward foreign investment, the export and import regulations, taxes and tax policies, and the protection of industrial property rights. 

Now you can get this information. It is available through many governmental publications that we have at our disposal. Frankly, I am not too familiar with all of those that are available, and we have had many references to them. So I don't think there is any need to repeat it.

The one thing that I would like to refer to-and it has been referred to before-that is Business International. I think the information they put out is very good and very helpful. 

Now what are the factors influencing the success or failure in licensing?

Well, the only real thing that has to be said on this subject is the choice of a reliable, competent, compatible licensee. After that all other considerations are secondary. And we have had a couple of interesting experiences in this connection. 

In Mexico--when we first went down to Mexico we looked around and touched all the bases that we thought should be touched and so forth, and we chose a company there to license with, and our agreements, our license agreements are usually written for 5 years with a 90-day escape clause if they will hold still for it, and in many instances they will. So we signed up with this Mexican distributor, but he insisted that we have a 2-year escape clause rather than 90 days. But he was a good solid citizen, and we didn't see any objection to it. So we entered into an agreement for 5 years with a 2-year escape clause.

Well, 6 months after we signed the agreement this man sold out. 

And he sold out to a group who had not previously been in business. 

They failed badly and we lost 2 years.

As soon as we saw what direction they were going in, we exercised our escape clause, but we have to wait 2 years before we can get out. 

So in our contracts now we have a clause in there to the effect that it has to be the same ownership; otherwise the agreement is canceled. 

And we had another interesting situation which I don't know how you can protect against. We had a licensee in France with whom we had worked 2 years before we could get started because of all the complications, getting raw materials approved, supplies and so forth and so on, and just as he was about to start the distribution of Breck he decided he would go out of business, close up, which he did.

He had made a lot of money, and decided he was tired, I guess. 

And, so, we were just left hanging there with some nice big inventories sitting in Holland, manufactured for France, and now we are looking for somebody in France. 

So the first consideration is the choice of a reliable, competent, and compatible licensee. Another consideration is the value of your patents and trademarks. Also a thorough, advanced research and understanding of the market. Also confidence in your licensee. Also

I think it is desirable if you do have some ownership or management in the licensee.

There are other factors, but primarily make sure you have got the right person. 

We are running behind time here. 

Locating and screening applicants, licensees: Well, we have discussed to some extent how do you find customers for your products, and a licensee falls into the same category. Many different methods have been discussed. So I won't repeat them.

One method that wasn't mentioned which we have found helpful is that we ask our competitors, for what it is worth, what their opinions are, and we get some pretty good suggestions. Also, we`ask

people who are in our industry but not necessarily our competitors, and they also can be very helpful. 

So if you have a situation that lends itself to that type of inquiry, I would recommend it.

After we decide on the licensee we have to do a little screening. 

After we have got two candidates we have to do some screening. 

And the screening is usually done by our director of our international business and myself, and we usually do it by means of a personal visit. As a matter of fact, that is the only way we do it. We don't attempt to do it by long distance. We always visit the candidates on their home grounds.

So after we have chosen the candidate, then we start negotiating an agreement, and this can be a lot of fun or it can be hard work. 

It depends on how you approach these things. 

In this particular connection, of course, you employ the services of your legal counsel, and again you do the negotiation on a personal basis usually. Usually you have a prepared formal licensing contract all ready which you hand to him, and this is the strategic dream of a contract, and then you have to make the logistic corrections after he has had a crack at it. And it is always necessary, of course, when negotiating an agreement to get clearance from the foreign government so that you licensee has the approval of the government and can pay royalties to you or whatever other basis it might be, and we always leave this up to the licensee. We feel this is his responsibility. 

And in some instances it is necessary, of course, for the licensor to clear with the U.S. Government agencies.

Well, some of the sample contract provisions: I think this subject is one that can best be covered by a legal person, and I am sure that f before entering or even making up any kind of a contract you would refer this to your own legal counsel, and I am sure that he has access to some of the services of his profession that could provide him with the number of do's and don'ts.

But, basically, you have the ordinary introductory remarks, the parties involved, where they are located and so forth and so on, and you make a decision as to whether it is a nonexclusive or an exclusive contract, and in most instances the licensor wants exclusivity, and from your particular point of view, of course, you try to insist that they have no competitive products and that you are not giving them, not assigning to them your trademarks or your patents.

Then the tenure and termination provisions: As I said, we usually write a 5-year contract but with a 90-day escape clause, and I think that in all but about two instances we have gotten away with this 90-day situation. In some instances, of course, the life of your patents would regulate the length of your agreement, and then you do have to make provisions for the termination of your contract in the event you do decide to terminate. That is, how does the licensor dispose of his supply, his inventory of your product. And, also, you try to get him to agree not to compete with you for a specified period of time after you have terminated.

Also, before entering into any contract or making up a contract, you first want to check with the tax laws in the particular country you are going into because in many instances you will find that countries frown on a royalty agreement strictly on the basis of the use

of a trademark, but they don't have the same attitude toward a technical assistance program. So you write this contract even though basically it is just to give them the use of your trademarks. You write it to make it sound as if this is a technical agreement, and in some instances the tax laws are such that the taxes paid to you, the royalties, are taxed at a lesser amount on a technical assistance agreement than they are on a straight trademark or patent royalty agreement. 

In our particular industry, of course, advertising, marketing is our primary problem and it is the problem that we struggle with domestically, and this goes for overseas, too. So in our contracts we spell out some of the things that we insist upon in these particular areas. 

We insist that they spend 20 percent of their sales on our products for advertising. And we insist that they consult with us before establishing their prices. We insist that they consult with us before they make decisions on through what outlets they will sell. 

These all sound good in writing, but in practice it gets pretty sticky because we also insist that they advertise in a way which we approve of, and they take the attitude that if they are spending their money they are not going to listen to us. And, so, we have some pretty good arguments over this situation. 

Naturally, in any contract that you write, quality control provisions are essential if you wish to maintain the quality, the caliber of your product overseas. So you have to insist upon this. And we try to establish a minimum royalty that is to be paid to us, and this varies from country to country depending on the sales potential and the size of the market. Naturally we insist upon reports and auditing provisions, and insist upon secrecy provisions and visitation provisions and arbitration provisions. And in connection with licensing the Business International, which we referred to before, does put out a book which is entitled "Investing and Licensing Conditions in 37

Countries," and this gives generally the economic situation in most of the European countries, Latin America, some of the African and Asian countries, and it is helpful, and I would recommend it for anyone who is considering licensing anywhere in the world, providing that they do have information on that particular country that you are interested in.

So in closing I would like to emphasize again that as far as licensing is concerned, as we view it, it is strictly intermediary. We anticipate ultimately in the more important markets in the world that we will have our own either joint venture or wholly owned companies, but at the moment we find that licensing works and works relatively well. 

The only one bit of advice that I would like to give once again, and this is when you make a decision that you are going to go into the international business-don't try and do it yourself. Hire a competent, capable person—this can be his speciality-and who can work at this full time. 

Thank you. [Applause.] 

Mr. HOOD. Thank you very much, John. 

I don't think man was invented to sit all this time. At least I wasn't--I don't know how you feel. We have had a very interesting program today. It has been most demanding, I am sure, on all of us, because of the extent and the variety of ideas that were presented.

Tomorrow we are going to open by talking about some aspects of manufacturing abroad and the ways you go about doing this. We hope that if you have any comments on the program today, you will jot them down on a piece of paper and drop them off here. This is your last chance to help improve the program that has been planned for you.

I won't attempt to summarize. I think we are all tired and we will see you at 9 o'clock.

AMERICAN MANAGEMENT ASSOCIATION GOING ABROAD: THE PROFIT OPPORTUNITIES OF INTERNATIONAL BUSINESS FOR THE SMALLER COMPANY 

NEW YORK, N.Y., Tuesday, June 27, 1961.

The seminar was reconvened at 9:05 a.m., in AMA headquarters, 10th floor, Hotel Astor, New York, N. Y., the chairman, Mr. Robert C. 

Hood, presiding. 

Mr. HooD. Good morning, gentlemen.

After our long start yesterday we are going to get into more of the meat today-more specific. As I alluded to yesterday, we are going to spend the morning talking about manufacturing abroad, covering three major areas. I am sure they are going to overlap: Contract manufacturing, the elements of joint ventures, and then the wholly owned subsidiaries.

However, before we start we didn't have time, nor did I think it prudent yesterday to have a question period on the fine presentation by John Breck on licensing. I wonder if at this time we wouldn't like to take 10 or 15 minutes and talk with him about some of the aspects of licensing abroad as they relate to a form of manufacturing. 

As John indicated generally, this is a way into manufacturing abroad. 

It is a start. 

John, would you like to begin? 

Mr. BRECK. I don't think there are any questions; are there? 

Mr. HOOD. I think there are.

Question. John, you mentioned yesterday having some stock in

Holland for France. Is that your practice, to manufacture in a limited number of spots and then supply it to others?

Mr. BRECK. Yes, that is what we have attempted to do in the Common Market, and Holland is a good place to manufacture. 

I think actually we can manufacture in Holland and ship to France for less cost than we could manufacture in France. That is a special situation.

Question. Is there any set amount on which you pay royalties, or any figure that you receive?

Mr. BRECK. NO. As I indicated, we insist upon a minimum royalty regardless of what their dollar volume is, and then we don't have a sliding scale. Most of our royalty agreements are 10 percent, although I notice and I was quite surprised-in the article in the Wall Street Journal it discussed royalties, if I remember correctly, that 4 percent was stated as the average royalty, although they vary everywhere from 1 to 40 percent. 

John H. Breck, executive vice president, John H. Breck, Inc., Springfield, Mass. See p. 73. 87

Mr. GUNDERSEN.2 Is that 10 percent of net or 10 percent of gross? 

Mr. BRECK. That is 10 percent of their sales of 

Mr. GUNDERSEN. Gross sales? 

Mr. BRECK. Their gross sales, yes. 

Question. You mentioned yesterday that one of the reasons for going into a license agreement was to get into a market where the export climate wasn't particularly favorable. Can you name any countries where the export climate is hostile but where the repatriation of royalties is no problem at all?

Mr. BRECK. Well, I don't know of any country really where there aren't problems in exporting finished goods or importing finished goods of our type of products. I think Colombia perhaps might be used as an example.

Up until about 3 years ago we could import our finished goods into 

Colombia without too many problems, but today this isn't so. 

As a matter of fact, once we went on to a manufacturing arrangement or a licensing agreement in Colombia we continued to ship to Colombia many of our component parts such as bottles, for instance. Now we find this is no longer practical.

We are going to have to buy glass in Colombia. So really in our particular line I don't know of any country that you can freely and easily ship finished goods into, and I don't think that there is any country that I know of that prohibits these royalty agreements although they do limit the amount of royalty to be paid. But this can be circumvented in many ways. I mean you can sign an official agreement, royalty agreement for, say, 5 percent. But then there are methods by which you can pick up another 5 percent.

For instance, in many countries there is no prohibition upon the importation of essential oils, perfumes. So what we do is mark up the price of our essential oil that we ship from the States to this country, sufficiently, so that we make up the difference between the official royalty and the royalty that we are interested in getting. 

Question. I have another on that. You mentioned that as part of your licensing agreements you insist on the right to approve or at least be notified of how they are going to distribute the Breck product once they get it manufactured in this country. In a place like Venezuela or Colombia or Denmark or Iceland, how can you be familiar with the optimum channels of distribution of your product? It must be entirely different in every country than it is here. How do you know which is the best way to do it?

Mr. BRECK. Well, we don't really. We have to depend upon the advice of our licensée. But at least we would like to have this particular subject discussed with us before any final decisions are made. But we depend pretty much upon their advice as to what the best routes of distribution are. 

One thing I forgot to mention yesterday in terms of qualifications for licensees. You should make sure that your licensee in some of the more unstable countries, politically unstable, make sure that your licensee belongs to the right political party. 

We had a situation in the Dominican Republic, it didn't happen that this was a license agreement, but the party wanted to be a licensee, but we were shipping to him as a regular export customer, and I think he * Reidar Gundersen, vice president, Morgan Guaranty Trust Co., New York, N.Y.

: owed us about $4,000. And then suddenly he disappeared, and his business was dissolved apparently, and we haven't heard from him since. And that was about 4 years ago. 

Any other questions?

Mr. WATTS. Have you ever had a licensee turn up as a competitor after the expiration of the period in your contract?

Mr. BRECK. No, sir; we haven't because our contracts—we have 5year contracts and we have only been in the export business seriously for about 6 years, and none of our agreements have expired. 

Thank you.

CONTRACT MANUFACTURING LECTURE BY NORMAN REDLICH, PRESIDENT, LAFAYETTE BRASS MANUFACTURING CO., INC., NEW YORK, N.Y.5 

Mr. REDLICH. First let me tell you that Lafayette Brass is a comparatively small, closely held corporation engaged in the manufacture and distribution of plumbing supplies, lawn sprinklers, and garden accessories.

I have felt that the field of contract manufacture abroad is particularly suitable for small companies of our size, and I hope to explain in this talk the reason for it. 

First, let me say what I mean by contract manufacture. By contract manufacture I mean the process whereby the American company assumes all the functions of manufacturing except the actual ownership of the plant where the goods are being produced. It means that the American company assumes the responsibility for the design of the product, for the financing of the purchase of raw materials abroad. 

The American company assumes the responsibility for the standards of quality control and inspection abroad. The American company assists the plant which is manufacturing the product financially with purchases of new equipment, machinery, wherever necessary. 

In effect, the fabricating plant abroad acts as the manufacturing subsidiary of the American firm which is importing the product and merchandising and marketing it.

By contract manufacture I also mean subcontract manufacture. 

That is the manufacture abroad of component parts which are incorporated into a completed product in this country. So we are talking about two different kinds of operations, but both running along similar channels, one whereby you contract manufacture a complete product abroad; two, where you contract manufacture a component part which is incorporated into a product over here. 

Contract manufacturing abroad represents, in my opinion, the greatest potential for most medium-sized and small American companies. The reason is that these companies often do not have the financial resources to set up their own manufacturing plants abroad. 

Oftentimes their personnel is so limited that it is just not feasible to send an organization to countries like Japan, Italy, to set up an actual plant. Also these companies don't want merely to purchase an already manufactured product. 

First of all, if they are purchasing an already manufactured product, they don't really have any control over the way the product is designed. They don't really have any control over the quality of the product and over the ultimate marketing of the product. 

5 Norman Redlich is president of the Lafayette Brass Manufacturing Co., Inc., a division of the Durst Manufacturing Co., Inc., in New York, N.Y. He received his B.A. degree, magna cum laude, from Williams College, his LL.B degree, cum laude, from Yale University 

Law School, and his LL.M. degree from New York University Law School. 

Mr. Redlich has been with Lafayette for his entire business career. He was appointed vice president and sales manager in 1950 and assumed his current post as president in 1955.

Secondly, as a practical matter it is generally not feasible just to purchase a product because in many instances these products just aren't being made in foreign countries. The product that I am going to talk to you about, which is lawn sprinklers, is almost exclusively an 

American-made occupation.

We seem to be about the only country that wants to waste its water by pouring it on lawns. Other countries either do this by nature or don't bother at all. So if an American manufacturer of lawn sprinklers wanted to go to Japan and Italy to buy such a product, he would I have no place to go, and this is true of many American consumer items which are exclusively American in origin and American in use. 

So the American manufacturer is really faced with basically one or two alternatives. Either he goes to the foreign country and builds 

Whis own plant in an attempt to manufacture the product himself, which, as I have indicated for medium- and small-sized companies, is just not feasible, or he engages in the process which I will describe to you of contract manufacturing abroad.

How do we, a small U.S. manufacturer, get into the task of contract manufacturing in a country such as Japan, which is where we operate primarily? 

Until 1953 the Lafayette Brass Co. had been engaged primarily in manufacturing small plumbing parts in this country for sale to the plumbing trade. We decided at that time to develop a consumer line of products. In effect, we needed something to help us over the good times because the products we were making were so stable in sales that we weren't getting the benefit of the expanding consumer market in this country. So we decided to go into the manufacture of lawn sprinklers.

We had a limited financial cash reserve available. We are not a publicly owned company, and so it was just not feasible for us to purchase the tools, dies, machinery, equipment, and plant which would be necessary to do the job in this country. We didn't want to go into it piecemeal because, in order to make a serious dent in the American market, we had to offer our customers a complete, full line of products.

Now Lafayette had been importing Japanese plumbing items for about 4 or 5 years, starting in 1949. And we decided that we would expand our Japanese operation to include the manufacture in Japan of a complete line of garden accessories, lawn sprinklers, hose nozzles, and so forth. But, as I have explained to you, we didn't want to just go to Japan and purchase the way many companies do, and even if we had wanted to we couldn't because when we started the Japanese had never heard of a lawn sprinkler. Even if they had we knew that it would have been impossible to obtain quality control and product control by simply sending someone over to Japan and telling them to buy an item.

So what did we do? We formed a company called Lafayette International, which is a U.S. corporation with a branch office in Toyko, which branch office has the legal status of a permanent exchange resident in Japan. A member of our firm lives in Japan as a permanent resident there. 72811-61- -7

We employed and built up a staff of approximately 15 engineers and inspectors. We retained the services of a legal firm and an accounting firm. We started to set up the basic machinery that you would in setting up most any business except we didn't have to make any investment in plant and equipment at the beginning. 

Now the job of this organization in Japan was, in effect to act as the engineering and legal and accounting representative of the parent company in Japan. Through this Japanese branch, contracts were made with a limited number of well-qualified Japanese plants who had the technical facilities to make the products that we were able to sell. 

This would include in our particular case a brass foundry, a diecasting plant, screw machine plant, a plating plant, a stainless steel stamping plant. You had to accumulate the same little family of companies that you would if you were manufacturing here in the United States. 

We investigated these plants, and where they didn't have the machinery which was required to produce the somewhat precision products which we required, we made the investment in machinery in these plants, usually, incidentally, in the form of a loan. 

We found that, at least, the Japanese plants were very reluctant to accept direct investment, and this was usually done in the form of a loan which was to be paid back. 

In some cases our company actually made investments, and in one case acquired a 50 percent interest in a Japanese company. But in the vast majority of instances it was done through loans. 

This office in Japan actually negotiates prices on completed products or component parts with these factories. Our office in Japan sets up standards of quality control, handles all inspection work, handles all the details of financing the manufacture of this product abroad.

To get more specific, I will now just trace with you a typical product through the processes of contract manufacture so you will see what I mean.

A revolving lawn sprinkler generally consists of two parts basically, the top that spins around, and the base on which the top sits. All of you who own homes know what a revolving lawn sprinkler is. In our case we found that it was cheaper to make the base for this lawn sprinkler here in the United States where we had excellent subcontracting facilities, and we continue to make that base here. But we found that it was economical to make the top in Japan because the top had a great deal of screw machine parts, a great deal of chrome plating, had a very high labor component which made it economical to manufacture the product abroad. 

Basically here in this country we designed this product ourselves. 

Our industrial design department designed it just the same as if it was going to be made here. Working models were made of the product. 

The product was thoroughly tested over here. Everything happened just as if it were going to be made over here except in the product design you are careful to maximize the advantages of the country where you are going to make the product. Certain things Japan could do cheaper than the United States can do.

If you are going to make a product in Japan or in Italy or any other foreign country you maximize the advantage that that country has, and in Japan that advantage is cheap labor, with the result that

to you maximize such things as chrome plating, finishing, which would be very expensive over here and which is not expensive in Japan. 

After a working model is made, parts drawings are made, complete assembly drawings are made, and then the model and all these drawings are sent over to our office in Japan, and generally our chief engineer would go out there. He makes about three trips a year. He would go out there with this new product and discuss it with the plants that would make the component parts to this product as well as perform the final assembly, and, just as in this country, it is rare where any one plant manufactures a complete product from beginning to end; the same in Japan. And if you are going to do the job of contract manufacturing properly you have to have the personnel over there so that they can have control not only of the plant where the product is going to be finally assembled but they also have to have access to and an intimate working relationship with the plants that are doing the subcontracting. Otherwise, as you could readily understand, the quality control problems would be substantial. And our staff in Japan would work with the plants that are doing the subcontracting. They would work with the plant that was doing the final assembly and work out a price for the product, iron out any of the redesigning which is always necessary before you go into manufacturing an item. 

Then we would purchase the tools. If it is a diecast product, we 

I would purchase all of the patterns, tools, and dies which are necessary to make the particular product, and they would remain our property. 

With all of these plants we have contracts whereby any product which is made through our design or which falls into the category of products which we are making is to be made for our exclusive use. have in general found that it is not wise to tie up factories on a contract basis whereby they are able to do business with no one else. This involves a responsibility that we have found is generally unwise to undertake. We

We do enforce through very rigid contracts a provision whereby the Japanese producer is unable to make products of this particular category for anybody else. We have found that it is necessary to set up a standard of detailed quality control on the manufacture of such things as a lawn sprinkler whereby the Japanese manufacturer, the workingmen, the plant superintendents, the department heads at the factories, are able to have before them a complete quality control pattern of things to check, things to look for, testing systems, because they are, in essence, dealing with things that they have never made before. 

Once the price is agreed upon, once the basic details of manufacturing are agreed upon, we perform another financing function. 

Through letters of credit or through loans we enable the Japanese manufacturer to purchase the raw materials with which he is going to make the product.

When you are dealing with medium- and small-sized business in Japan or Italy, you find they are generally inadequately financed, and they require the assistance of American prime contractors, so that either through letters of credit or through loans the Japanese manufacturers, the Italian manufacturers, are enabled to buy the raw material to make their product, and it is shipped here where it is incorpotrated with the lawn sprinkler base which is made in this country and marketed as a manufactured product, but clearly indicated on the

product that the top was made in Japan and the base was made in the 

United States.

We have found that it has done us no harm whatsoever to make very clear to the American purchasing public where these products come from.

Now what have been the prime advantages as well as the problems of this type of operation?

In the first place, our Japanese and Italian operations and our Italian operation works substantially the same way-have given us a much lower price on the product than we ever could have obtained by manufacturing the product here. It has given us a lower price for two reasons.

First of all, the manufacturing cost is less. Second, the investment in tools and dies runs anywhere from a third to only 15 percent of what it would have cost in this country. And when you are dealing in diecast products, the cost of tooling has run only 10 percent of what it would cost in this country. 

This lower tooling cost not only is reflected in the lower selling price but it also gives us a much greater flexibility in product design. 

I am sure you are all familiar with the problem of manufacturing a stylized item whereby a large investment in tooling can be disastrous if for some reason the particular style that your designers have selected meets with the wild approval of your own office but no one else. And the problem that this creates in this country is a serious one, but it creates a very small problem in your contract manufacturing abroad because, whereas it costs something like $65,000 to tool up to make an oscillating lawn sprinkler in the United States, which is the kind that goes back and forth instead of spinning around, it only costs something like $10,000 to tool up and make the same sprinkler in Japan. And if the particular model you have put on the market is unsuccessful, the flexibility of switching from one design to another is so much greater operating abroad, and because the tooling cost is so little we have been enabled to develop by means of contract manufacture abroad a complete full line of products in the short space of about 3 or 4 years, which is something that within the resources of our company we never could have done if we would have been operating here.

Those have been our principal advantages. And what have been our principal problems?"

I would say our foremost problem is one of personnel. Contrary to what many people who know nothing about it think, for an American to live abroad in a country like Japan or Italy is not a glamorous occupation. It involves a fantastic amount of work, and it requires a type of person that is very hard to find, and I would say that a firm which is planning to embark on the task of contract manufacture must make certain that it has the proper type of personnel abroad or, else, he is going to have a great deal of difficulty. 

You need a person who not only is technically qualified with regard to the product, but you need a person who is able to merge two completely divergent cultures. I have seen many technically qualified American engineers come to a country like Japan and fall flat on their faces because they are used to dealing in a certain way in the United States, and they are simply unable to adapt themselves to

a completely different culture, to a group of people who look at things in different ways, who approach problems in a different manner. And the American management personnel who think that they can operate abroad the same way they operate here are simply asking for disaster. 

I know of no way to select this personnel in a scientific manner. 

We have made our mistakes and we have had our successes, but it is the foremost problem which I think we have.

The second great problem that we have is one of quality control. 

Quality control is a problem which all manufacturers face, and if you are a manufacturer in New York and subcontracting a part in New Jersey you have problems of quality control if the New Jersey subcontractor has never made this particular component before. If the subcontractor happens to be 8,000 miles away in Japan, speaks a different language, and has never made the product before, and has no idea what it is used for, the problems of quality control become much greater.

The problems are really no different. It is just that it is a much more difficult task to cope with them. You have to establish a system of quality control in minute detail not only at the firm that is making the final product which you are going to import but, as I have indicated, at all the firms along the way who are doing the subcontracting. 

In the United States you can often find, for example, that a firm which is making a product for you or a component for you is qualified to judge the standards of the components which it is buying. Let us say it is making a product that has a plastic component. Generally the American company will have qualified personnel who are able to judge whether the plastic is proper. We found that in Japan you cannot make this assumption. You can't make it in Italy either.

We have also operated in Germany. We can't make it there. So it is necessary to follow through right to the very beginning to make certain that the standards of quality control which you insist upon are carried out.

Another problem which we have overcome is the one of product control. You have all heard a good deal about how products get copied in countries like Japan, and, of course, anyone who wants to do contract manufacturing in Japan is concerned about the problem that the firm he has established to furnish the contract manufacturing for him is going to turn around and sell his product to a variety of other people. This can usually be overcome through proper drafting of contracts.

I have never regarded this as a serious problem, although it is one which a great many American manufacturers are concerned about. 

Another problem that we have had and one that does remain a serious problem is one of inventories. You are dealing with a country, in the case of Japan, which at best is 6 weeks away by boat; Italy 2 or 3 weeks away by boat, and whereas in the United States you can schedule your production reasonably related to your sales, it is impossible to do this, at least we found it impossible to do this in Japan, with the result that we have to make our commitments for raw materials in Japan long in advance of our seasonal requirements because the price which we negotiate with our Japanese subcontractors is based upon a certain price for raw materials. We are putting up the money to buy the raw materials, and it is based upon their being able to buy it in certain quantities, and therefore the raw materials have to be pur-

chased far in advance. Everything has to be set up for the manufacturer of a given quantity of products long before you have any idea of what you are going to sell.

This is very different from the way you operate here. The result is that you have to be prepared to make a fairly substantial investment in inventory. Either you have to hold the inventory in Japan or you have to hold the inventory here, but you cannot expect the Japanese subcontractor to hold it for you.

Inventory investment is nowhere nearly so substantial as the investment which you would make in plant and equipment here in this country, but it is an investment which has to be considered, and the cost of that investment has to be considered in your cost analysis. 

Finally, there is the question of time lag as a problem, and we assume that from the time we send a complete working model with a complete set of parts drawings to a country like Japan, between that time and the time when we will get a finished product shipped to us back in the United States is pretty close to 1 year, and any assumption that it will be done in less is, in my opinion, unreasonable. Sometimes it can be done in less, but this is a matter of luck and not a matter of skill. 

If one assumes that the time lag is a year, you will be on the side of accuracy. Those have been our basic problems. 

An interesting outgrowth of the process of contract manufacturing has been this, and it is the final point that I will leave you with this morning: As a result of the impact that our products made in our particular industry, the lawn sprinkler industry, other American houseware manufacturers came to us and said: "Well, if you can make a lawn sprinkler top in Japan why can't you make a part for an outdoor barbecue or a particular part for a toaster?" And, so, gradually we have been expanding our operation in Japan to perform the task of contract manufacturing for other companies besides ourselves. And in talking to one or two others who are in a similar position I find that we are not unique in the expansion of our Japanese and Italian operations in this direction because if our office in Japan is able to perform a certain service for us, it is also able to perform a certain service for others, and we have found that in the past 2 years this aspect of contract manufacturing, which we never counted on in the first place and never thought of, has been expanding at a rapid pace because American companies faced with the problem of foreign competition, American companies who subcontract certain components in this country, feel that with proper personnel and engineering talent there is no reason why the whole world cannot be used as a basis for subcontracting.

The American manufacturer idles no equipment if he subcontracts a part in Japan which he is already subcontracting in the United States, and to any firm which starts a program of contract manufacturing in Japan as we have, I would say that the possibility of expanding this to include contract manufacturing for other American manufacturers is a bonus which perhaps you don't count on at first but which makes the entire operation an extremely valuable one. 

[Applause.] 

Mr. HOOD. Thank you very much, Norm. This is certainly a fascinating approach and it looks like one that will grow as our needs grow.

Now may we have some questions directed to Norm at this time?

Question. Mr. Redlich, I didn't quite understand as far as your sales are concerned, your products that are subcontracted overseas and that come back here for local sale. Now, in addition, are they also sold in other areas of the world from the foreign manufacturing point?

Mr. REDLICH. Until recently the products we are concerned with had very little market in the rest of the world. We are engaged primarily in lawn sprinklers, American-type plumbing items, and there was very little market in the rest of the world. 

We have within the past year entered into arrangements with a German and English company, and we have also established a Canadian subsidiary, and basically the operation there is that the products come from Japan to the German firm with whom we have a licensing arrangement, to the Canadian subsidiary which is our wholly owned subsidiary, and are marketed in those countries through the sales organizations established by those companies. In Canada it is our own company. In Germany it happens to be a hardware manufacturer and firm that is actually assembling and distributing our products. Here in this country, of course, we have our own sales organization which handles our distribution.

Mr. HOEFS. Mr. Redlich, I was wondering why you elected to set up a separate organization to do this subcontracting since you are selling back into the United States at that point, and it would be subject to U.S. tax. I presume taxes were not the particular consideration. 

Mr. REDLICH. No; taxes were not the problem. The main reason was that this company was entering into certain contractual arrangements and financial arrangements with Japanese subcontractors, and for that reason we wanted to insulate the American company from these contractual arrangements.

Secondly, taxes were a problem in this respect: there was no question that this branch in Japan would, in the opinion of Japanese tax authorities, be earning income in Japan. Therefore, all of the books and records of this company would be subject to audit. 

Now for that reason we didn't want this company to be merely a branch of our parent company in America because we didn't want to subject the parent company to all of the harassment and problems which would occur from such auditing. Therefore, we set up a separate American company, Lafayette International, which had a branch in Japan, and all this separate American company does is act as a purchasing and engineering organization.

Question. You mentioned that it takes about a year to go from the final design to the end product. Can you explain why? The basis for my question is with this contractual service that you offer can you give more prompt deliveries? 

Mr. REDLICH. Oh, yes. The reason for the delay at the beginning, tools and dies have to be made, prices have to be negotiated, and this is a time-consuming process. It has to be negotiated not only with one firm but with subcontracts all down the line. A variety of technical problems have to be solved in getting a product into the manufacturing process. Dies have to be redesigned after you find that in an assembly operation things don't work properly.

Once you get the product rolling, then of course the delivery is a much more rapid process. We figure about 90 days from the time of placing of any new order for an existing product-between 60 and 90 days the time it would actually be shipped. 

Question. Can you estimate how long it would take you to go from a finished product, a finished design, to a product in America? 

Mr. REDLICH. It takes us, to go from a finished design to a product here in the United States, anywhere from 6 to 9 months. It is roughly 

4 or 5 months longer in Japan.

Question. Mr. Redlich, in the early part of your talk you made some interesting comments about tooling costs which were a little bit at variance with some of my experiences and observations. 

I would like to ask for further clarification: you mentioned something like-perhaps in odd or infrequent cases-10 or 15 percent of the cost over here. When I was in Japan I looked at some of these jobbing tool shops. Their methods were maybe 10 or 15 or 20 years behind ours, and the equipment wasn't quite comparable. The quality of the tooling which I observed wasn't in some cases up to our standards, and the costs-perhaps they were below our costs but they were perhaps not anywhere near those figures. 

Mr. REDLICH. You were looking in the wrong place. You see there really is no tool or die industry in Japan. 

Question. There is. There is an industry association of 500. 

Mr. REDLICH. I know you can formally say there is one, but most of the tooling which is done in the die cast industry or the plastic injection molding industry is done by the molders themselves. You don't have as built-up an industry there as you have here, and you don't have a separate class of highly skilled labor working at this task in Japan to the extent that you have it here, with the result that with a little tool department over in a small die casting plant we find as a customary practice that the tooling costs will run from 15 to 30 percent of what they will here, and we have found that there is very little quality problem with regard to the tools for our particular use. 

Perhaps we are not engaged in the type of precision work that other people might be. We have not been very successful in attempting to develop the separate sale of Japanese molds and tools in this country. People have said to us, well, if the tooling is so cheap why can't we buy our tools in Japan. This just doesn't work because if somebody is tooling for a product that he is not going to make, the price is not nearly so low as when he is tooling up for a product that he is going to make. We have not really been able to come in with any great cost advantages, and certainly no cheaper than Germany. 

Question. Thank you for that last expression. That is what I was leading up to.

It would seem to follow from what you said originally that I haven't found in my experience that there have been too many brought in. A few from Japan but relatively few. And our company itself has been able to sell tooling components in Japan competitively. 

Mr. REDLICH. That wouldn't surprise me.

Question. Mr. Redlich, how much of a dollar volume is necessary before you could consider contract manufacturing in order to cover some of your personnel staff expenses?

ར. Mr. REDLICH. I would say that you should have an operation which is importing from Japan at an f.o.b. value of no less than about $500,000 or $600,000. This is the level that we regarded as our breakeven point to cover our expenses over there. That, of course, is f.o.b. 

Japan values; not including duty, freight.

Mr. REDLICH. It depends on the product. Our particular products generally carry a 19-percent duty. Electric motors, which we have been making in Japan for manufacturers, have been coming in at a 1212or 13-percent duty. It depends entirely on the product.

MANUFACTURING ABROAD: THE CASE FOR THE JOINT VENTURE LECTURE BY J. R. SWIHART, VICE PRESIDENT, INTERNATIONAL OPERA- TIONS, THE DIVERSEY CORP., CHICAGO, ILL. 

OUTLINE 1. INTRODUCTION A. Diversey's international experience: 

1. Contract manufacturing. 

2. Joint venture. 

3. Wholly owned subsidiaries. 

B. Description of Diversey's business: 

1. Producer of industrial chemical specialities: 

(a) Products for the food processing industries. (b) Products for the metal processing industries. 

2. Limited producer of raw materials for own consumption. 

C. Basis for Diversey's decision to manufacture abroad: 

1. Minimize economic fluctuations. 

2. Overcome local trade barriers. (a) Tariffs. 

(b) Nationalism. 

3. Retain control of know-how.

D. Current considerations in manufacturing abroad: 

1. Effect of U.S. prestige. 

2. Effect of U.S. tax laws. 

A. Positive values:

II. THE VALUE OF THE JOINT VENTURE 

1. Provides knowledge of local conditions. 

2. Provides local staff. 

3. Requires less investment. 

4. Can provide existing business. 

B. Questionable values: 

1. Satisfy nationalistic desires. 

2. May provide tax advantages.

C. Negative values : 

1. Shareholder dissention. 

2. Must share profits.

III. 

ORGANIZING THE JOINT VENTURE 

A. Survey the market: 

1. Knowledge acquired from export: (a) Location and requirements of customers. (b) Tariff barriers. (c) Local competition. 

2. On-the-spot survey team.

J. R. Swihart is vice president, International Operations of the Diversey Corp. in 

Chicago, Ill. He holds a B.S. degree with majors in chemistry and mathematics, attended college in South Dakota and continued with advance courses in chemistry at the Illinois 

Institute of Technology. 

Prior to his present association with Diversey, Mr. Swihart was with the Jordan Co. of Chicago as chemist and chemist-production manager, successively, and later with the Victor Chemical Works (now merged with Stauffer) as research analyst. After serving with the U.S. Armed Forces during World War II, he joined Diversey's Laboratory. He served successively as group leader of Metal Industries Product Research; founding general manager of Diversey (A/Asia) in Sydney, Australia; and acting general manager of Diversey-France in Paris. In 1956 he was appointed director of Diversey's international department. Concurrently, he serves as vice president of the Diversey Overseas 

Corp.; director of Diversey (A/Asia) Pty. Ltd. in Sydney; director of Diversey (UK) Ltd. in London; and director of Deosan Ltd., London. 

Mr. Swihart holds three U.S. patents. He is a member of the American Chemical 

Society and the Canadian Club of Chicago.

B. Select the partner(s): 

1. Related business. 

2. Responsibility. 

C. Select the legal adviser. 

D. Draft the letter of intent: 

1. Limits of ownership. 

2. Jurisdiction of shareholders. 

3. Obligations of shareholders. 

E. Select the bank (s).

IV. THE REWARD OF THE JOINT VENTURE 

A. The return on the investment. 

B. Intangible benefits to all partners. 

C. Intangible benefits to the countries.

Mr. SWIHART. Gentlemen, I won't say how very wise these remarks are to be, but I will make certain observations. They are probably very similar to observations you have made yourselves.

For those of you who haven't ventured outside the United States in business, maybe you will hear about something new.

Going abroad, especially for the first time, is a fascinating experience, whether it is on a personal level or whether it is on a business level. I think reactions of all of us are similar. We like to get together and we like to compare notes. We like to talk to someone who has been there. Many of you have had experience outside the 

United States and others haven't. Those who haven't undoubtedly are looking forward to their first venture or adventure, as the case may be. To help you, I will give you some of my observations and describe some of Diversey's experiences as they relate to the joint venture.

At this point, I feel it is necessary to inject a note of "corn" into the discussion. In speaking of a joint venture I am speaking in a broad sense-partner or partners, corporation or partnership-but I am not speaking of a tour of the night clubs of Paris. 

In discussing manufacturing abroad, the case for the joint venture, there are certain advantages and there are also disadvantages to be considered. The various speakers that you have heard so far have presented cases for one type of arrangement or for another type of arrangement for doing business abroad.

Diversey has been abroad for going on 25 years. We have tried most arrangements; and so, again just as an observation, I should like to say that the disadvantages experienced in the joint venture may very well appear as advantages in some other type of arrangement. 

By the same token, the advantage of the joint venture may appear as a disadvantage in some other type of arrangement. 

For example, entering a joint venture implies a free exchange of knowledge between the partners. If your business is based on secrets, obviously this isn't a very advantageous type of arrangement to make.

As a further preface I should like to give you a little information about Diversey which will enable you to evaluate your own plans or your own experiences in the light of our experience. 

We first went abroad in 1937 when our Canadian subsidiary was established. Diversey Corp., Canada, Ltd., is a wholly owned subsidiary. It was started from scratch, and it was the experience in 

Canada which prompted the company to go abroad again, but not on a wholly owned basis. It took a number of years for the Canadian organization to become profitable, and I think one of the advantages

of a joint venture perhaps being able to reach a profit level faster than might be the case in a new wholly owned venture. 

Bear in mind, whichever type of arrangement a company might decide upon would depend upon the company's products. 

The speaker this morning spoke of oversea trade in a different light than I had thought about, perhaps because our business doesn't lend itself to this type of operation. It would be very difficult for us to manufacture products abroad or components abroad and bring them into the United States. It would even be difficult in most cases for us to obtain raw materials in the chemical field abroad and bring them into the United States and be able to end up with a finished product which would be more profitable than the one we would manufacture here.

So, when I am speaking of going abroad and a joint venture abroad, I am thinking of manufacturing abroad for sale abroad, as opposed to manufacturing abroad for export to the United States.

Our second voyage abroad was to Australia, with operations started with Australian partners as a joint venture in 1948. The company was 

Diversey Australasia Proprietary, Ltd., and its market area was to include the Fiji Islands, New Zealand, and other regions within the trade sphere known as Australasia.
