During the 10 years following the establishment of the Australian company, Diversey established subsidiaries in five additional countries.

Of these new companies, four are still joint ventures, and one is wholly owned. The locations are Brazil, France, Italy, United Kingdom, and Venezuela.

Diversey's other international activities include the operation of a Chicago-based export company which distributes products of U.S. origin to markets not served by foreign subsidiaries. Operations of a semi-international character are conducted in the Hawaiian Islands and in Puerto Rico.

I think, in the case of many U.S. companies, if there is merchandising over water or the management goes over water, it is referred to as an international operation. At least, in our case, it is. 

Classifying the experiences I will describe on a wholly owned or joint-venture basis, is really a little misleading. Only one of our companies, as I have said, was started from scratch as a wholly owned operation. In some instances we have purchased control, or we have purchased 100 percent ownership in existing businesses.

I think for the purpose of the present discussion, subsidiaries acquired in the latter manner, could also be classified as joint ventures, since from the practical point of view, the same advantages are obtained as are from a joint venture which starts from a fresh beginning. In other words, you don't go into a market cold and you don't start your own organization from scratch.

We have experimented with other types of arrangements for doing business abroad. We have tried contract manufacturing to some extent. We have a few licensing arrangements. But these things are not done through the parent company in the United States. The only contract manufacturing that we have attempted was in France, and this was a means to an end. Our partners there are in the heavy chemical business; and in assisting the new company to get started,

they made the products for us on a cost-plus basis until we had progressed to a point where we could establish our own factories. 

This we dedicated last year.

This arrangement was quite satisfactory during the early days of the company, and one of the reasons we abandoned it was that we felt we had greater flexibility in our products and better control if we had our own manufacturing facilities. In addition to this, we found that the French associates didn't always go at the problem the same way that we might. Understandably, we often preferred our way to their way. The only way that we could have our way, was to do it ourselves. There was some variation in product appearance. There sometimes was variation in product performance. This, of course, is a problem of quality control, as was mentioned by the previous speaker.

Also, we found that our products might require certain raw materials which our associates normally didn't stock. We found ourselves carrying inventories of such raw materials in their factories. 

We had to supply the production schedules, of course; and with each succeeding product which we introduced, we found ourselves more and more involved in the manufacturing process. We then decided we would be better served with our own factory. 

Our licensing arrangements are negotiated by our wholly owned subsidiaries. These subsidiaries, holding companies I am speaking of primarily, license our other subsidiaries, but generally the subsidiaries which receive licenses are 51 percent or more owned by Diversey. In most cases, rather than have a licensing arrangement, we sell our trademarks, patents, and know-how to the oversea subsidiaries, so that it becomes the property of those companies. This is another reason our company's management insists that we retain control of the business. 

We usually don't want joint ventures in which we have less than 51percent ownership. This gives you some brief idea of where we operate and the arrangements we have for operation. 

Now perhaps just a word about our product would help further to lay the foundation for what is to be said later. Diversey Corp. is a chemical-specialty company. We make specialized chemicals, but primarily for industry. We don't get into the consumer market except in very special instances.

We also manufacture a limited line of equipment. We make spraycleaning equipment; we make electronic solution-control units. Our products find application in the food-processing industries where we provide cleaners and sterilizers for their equipment. In the metal processing industry we provide cleaners and surface-preparation chemicals. We make some raw material for our own internal consumption, but this is not really a significant factor in our business. 

As a secondary producer in the chemical industry, we secure most of our raw materials from the larger producers. The investment for plant and processing equipment in our type of business is much less than that required for heavy chemicals, say sulfuric acid, caustic soda, or oil refining; but the application of our products covers a very wide field. They are used by dairies, breweries, beverage companies, meatpackers, canning and preserving companies, wineries, hospitals, hotels, schools, restaurants, automobile manufacturers, aircraft manufacturers, and metal-specialty manufacturers.

Our customers' operations range in size from the corner hotdog stand to the largest meatpackers, from the Podunk plating shop to the automotive and aircraft industries.

Our interest in going abroad was acquired early in the history of the company, as I have said. Establishment of the Canadian subsidiary was decided upon by top Diversey management as a direct result of the company's experience during the 1930 depression years. 

The extension of the program, however, didn't really get underway, as you probably noticed, until after the period of chemical shortages was over, at the end of World War II. One of the prime considerations in the decision to manufacture abroad was the desire to minimize the effect of economic fluctuations within the United States on our business.

Now, here we have another little switch. In speaking of the plumbing industry, the previous speaker said that their market was too stable. In our case, we felt that the market fluctuated too much, and we wanted to try to stabilize it. So this was one of the reasons why we went abroad. Top management thought that the company should be in a position to operate without the serious hindrance presented by major depressions or recessions within the United States. 

It felt that if there was an economic crisis in the United States, there would be a timelag before that same crisis appeared in foreign markets. This would permit recovery to start in the home markets, and the effect of a U.S recession on the earnings and profits of the company as a whole on a worldwide basis would be minimized. 

The idea was to attempt to develop a recession-proof business. I think present day communications and interdependence in the Western World tend to reduce the timelag between economic situations existing in this country and those developing abroad but, nonetheless, this sort of thing still exists. Now we are said to be emerging from a recession period. Last year several of our oversea companies were really riding the crest of a boom. I returned from Australia a few weeks ago. 

There they are starting in on a recession.

So, it is true that distributing one's markets over the world helps to minimize the fluctuation in the total. As Diversey subsidiaries abroad grow, it is felt they will provide a strong buffer against any decline in the economy of the United States which might affect our domestic business and our domestic business is still the principal part of the business.

A second reason in our case for manufacturing abroad is the nature of the product. Our experience in the United States has shown us that it is not feasible to export our complete range of products. 

There are certain products that we export with no difficulty, but maybe only 5 or 10 percent of the total. Most nations have high tariffs on finished chemical specialties, but low tariffs on chemical raw materials. 

Prices to the trade become prohibitive after the application of such tariffs, especially in industrial chemicals.

It is possible, of course, to declare this merchandise incorrectly, that is, import it as raw material and have it enter a country with a lower duty; but sooner or later, one is found out, and such means do not justify the end, if the company is sincere in its wish to build for the future as well as the present. en

I think a third reason for our company's considering manufacturing abroad is growing nationalism in many of the markets of today. It can mean considerable to the sales effort if the company can claim, "This product is made in Graustark, using locally available raw material, and by local labor."

We hear a great deal about the corporate image, and if local nationalism and patriotism can be incorporated in that image, it makes our selling job a lot easier. Many of the international friends of the United States are perhaps disappointed with our failure to have been first with some of the spectacular achievements we have read about recently; but in my opinion, there is no loss of respect or loss of admiration for American technology, particularly in the practical application of technology.

Speaking from a sales point of view, a product made abroad with U.S. know-how is often a very formidable opponent to the local competition. And, to me, the admiration for U.S. technology has been rather subtly demonstrated on several occasions. When we started our Australian company, I felt that there might be some antagonism developed toward our product, because the image of the big American corporation going in and freezing out all the small local competitors might develop.

While we aren't a big corporation in that sense, nonetheless that doesn't alter the impression that is obtained from the movies. So when we started marketing, we sort of emphasized our Canadian subsidiary and our Canadian association in the initial sales pitch. 

This approach recognized the local nationalism, and recognized Empire preference. But the first customers were very quick to point out to us that they weren't in the least interested in what happened in Canada. They wanted to know what happens in the States so much so, in fact, that after the first few years of our operations in this market, one of our major local competitors allied himself through a licensing arrangement with one of our U.S. competitors in an effort to regain some of the business we had taken. As of now, I am happy to report that he is still trying to regain the business. 

In France when we started our operations in 1956 I suggested putting the statement-and forgive my French-"Fabriqué en France" on our labels. This, I discovered, was just not done. One always uses "Made in France." So our labels may carry volumes of instructions in the French language, but they always end up at the bottom in English-"Made in France."

In Italy we were deciding upon a design of the letterheads to be used by Diversey Italiana. We had agreed to include our "D-man" emblem, which is a trademark in the United States as well as abroad. 

This is a capital "D" with arms and legs and a hat, and it represents the Diversey field-service man. In a circle around the "D-man" is the legend "Protecting Quality, Assuring Purity."

I asked our Italian associates how this legend would read in Italian, and a principal partner said, "Oh, no, no, don't translate it." 

I said, "Why not?"

He said, "Keep it in English. It shows that we have foreign association. It has good snob value." 

The fourth reason for manufacturing abroad through the medium of a subsidiary is retention and control of know-how. To us, this

is one of the more important reasons. The know-how in a company contributes its full share to the success of the venture. Unless one can direct the use of such know-how into the most fruitful channels, the value of the know-how may be lost. And, in my opinion, licensing minority ownership, or contract manufacturing will not permit control of know-how or permit direction of its proper use. 

Looking at the other side of the coin, there are some reasons to consider which are against a decision to manufacture abroad. One of these is brought out by all the talk that has been going on recently of legislation being considered by the U.S. Federal Government to tax the undistributed earnings of U.S. subsidiaries abroad. I am sure that most of you are familiar with the arguments for the plan and those against it; and while such legislation, it appears, won't be passed this year, I am sure that it is going to come up again in the future. 

To comment briefly, it would appear, as one commentator put it, that the Treasury's purpose may be to destroy foreign-based U.S. companies. Enactment of such legislation would put U.S. companies already operating abroad in a precarious position. This is our concern. We have no idea what the reaction of our subsidiaries' minority shareholders would be. They are already quite perplexed at some of the laws the United States has, and to put in a law like this will undoubtedly perplex them further.

From a purely business point of view, they would look with less favor, I am sure, upon partnership with a U.S. company. Even when subsidiaries are 100 percent owned by the U.S. interests, I am sure that the control of these subsidiaries would become more difficult, because I am sure that the local management would resist payment of such taxes, and in this resistance I am afraid that automatically the profit would go down.

Payment of such taxes by the subsidiary would seriously limit its growth or make necessary further financing in the form of loans from the parent company. I say this because our philosophy is to start a subsidiary with a minimum of capital investment and put the earnings back over a period of several years. I am sure that foreign governments would retaliate against such tax measures as they have in the past.

Our taxes abroad would go up in most cases where they aren't already 50 percent. The U.S. Treasury appears to revel in its role of teacher of modern methods of taxation, and foreign governments are very quick to learn.

If this type of legislation is passed by the U.S. Congress, we, as a smaller company, must critically reexamine our own position and we certainly wouldn't want to advise anyone to organize subsidiaries abroad under such circumstances.

Now that you know something of our experience, let us consider the case for the joint venture. Obviously the management of Diversey feel that the joint venture is the best way to start a business abroad, or manufacturing operation abroad. Again, by definition, I include, in "joint venture," the purchase of operating businesses.

The positive value of partners abroad is obvious to all of us; but, at the risk of repetition, I will mention some of the points which a foreigner-which is what we become when we go abroad-must consider.

Among our questions are: What are the local conditions as they apply to the market? How many potential customers are there? 

How much product do they use? Whose product do they use?

Answers to these questions and many others can be secured from a partner in the joint venture. 

Oversea labor markets have a great shortage of trained technicians. 

A partner can often supply keymen who can head departments and assist in recruiting the balance of the staff. When we entered business in France several years ago, one of our partners gave us a chemist with over 20 years' experience in the business. It would have been impossible for us, on short notice, to supply this sort of man from the United States.

This man helped us to hire and train salesmen, establish production, and establish our technical service department, assisted in our sales department, and now is general manager of the company. 

A partner obviously reduces the amount of capital the U.S. company must provide. This may be a disadvantage or an advantage. The disadvantage, but justly so, is that the U.S. company receives only a proportionate share of the profits. On the other hand, a company can be started which is twice as big and twice as strong. 

In addition to providing capital, the partner may have a certain amount of existing business which can be given to the new company. 

This will provide income. It will permit the commercial and manufacturing part of the organization to begin functioning, and very often can help iron out difficulties before marketing of the U.S. products are undertaken. And, conversely, the good ideas don't all come from this side. The partner can often supply good ideas to the parent company for use in the United States.

I mentioned that local partners help satisfy the nationalism of a country. Now, a company can become nationalistic without local partners. In our case, it would be impossible for us to staff a subsidiary with 100 percent personnel from the United States, even if we so desired. Thus, a foreign company becomes more national as it acquires stature and as each local employee is hired. In addition, there are laws in some countries which require a certain number of nationals on local companies' payrolls. The existence of local shareholders further emphasizes this national character. 

As you know, in Canada there is agitation to get the U.S. owners of Canadian subsidiaries to sell shares to the Canadians. This is perhaps more a point of pride, in my opinion, than an attempt to create a Canadian identity. I am sure that our Canadian customers are not concerned about this problem, for they are dealing directly with Canadians.

Except for the president and secretary, who are U.S. personnel, all others are Canadians.

In France we have no U.S. personnel. In Australia we have no 

U.S. personnel. In England we have one person from the United 

States, and in Italy we have one. 

Another aspect of going abroad, of course, may be the tax advantage but I know of no instance where there are tax advantages derived because of the nationality of the partners in the venture. In our case, at least in the instances that have come to my attention, it doesn't make any difference whether we are wholly owned or whether 72811-61--8

we are in a joint venture. We get the same tax treatment as anyone else. A joint venture by virtue of being a joint venture would offer no tax advantage.

It is true that the choice of the type of corporation that a company decides upon abroad may have a bearing. You have your closed corporations. You have your public corporations. A private-type corporation sometimes has certain advantages. In Australia, for example, the income tax for a private corporation is less than for a public corporation, but what the right hand doesn't take, the left hand does, because there is there a high tax on undistributed earnings of private corporations. 

The choice of the type of corporation will depend upon the nature of the product and the aims of the partners. 

As far as tax advantages are concerned in foreign countries, I prefer to look upon them as fringe benefits to be enjoyed if they are available. I wouldn't base a decision on the local tax situation unless the taxes were absolutely prohibitive, in which case the decision would be negative. Taxes can be changed tomorrow. Markets can't. Markets change over a period of years.

When we started in business in Italy, we evaluated the tax advantages of locating in the southern part of the country. We decided to pay the extra taxes and locate in the north. 

When we started business in France in 1956, as I have said, the corporate tax rate was, I think, around 30 percent. In the ensuing years, it has gone up until now it is around 50 percent. 

Well, this gives you a little idea of some of the positive advantages of the joint venture. You get information from your partners; you get assistance; you get business, and in some cases, you get established companies.

Now let us look at the negative side of joint ventures. Perhaps the most important negative point is dissension. Whenever two people get together, there are two different ideas, and this can lead to dissension. The biggest single factor which may introduce dissension, in our experience, is the one of management control. This is why one's partners should be carefully chosen. The magnitude of the problem of management control will depend upon the circumstances under which one enters the joint venture. 

It is our policy to go in with nothing less than 50 percent and preferably 51 or more. When we buy into an existing organization we attempt to retain the existing management. However, frequently, there is a difference in business philosophy, and this often results in a high management-casualty rate.

Often these companies are small, and the founders fail to accept the status which 49 percent of the ownership of the enterprise dic tates. This is quite understandable, although it should be recognized as a prime danger for the new partner when he obtains controlling interest in an existing business. 

Finally, managers who sell a controlling interest in their business frequently, I am sure, look upon the new owners of the business as a source of finance and not as a source of management ideas. Since usually these people are well experienced in their local markets, their confidence in their ability to make the business successful is boundless. 

All they ever need is more money. However, once the honeymoon ends and beg ma are 

Sul res th ma ге Co el

and the money is cut off, then the dissension over operation policies begins. 

Another source of dissension is U.S. management personnel who may be imposed upon local minority shareholders. If such personnel are inept or unqualified, they create all sorts of problems, and I am sure this is not anything that is new to you. 

Oftentimes the minority shareholders will attempt to woo the resident U.S. manager in order to promote their own policies. If these policies are contrary to those of the parent, and the resident manager adopts them, he has to be relieved of his command. Conversely, when U.S. personnel remain completely loyal to the parent company they are frequently looked upon as troublemakers or spies, and subtle but effective methods are employed either to effect their removal or to cast them in a role of negligible influence. This is something a U.S. company must recognize in a joint venture abroad, and to counteract, must develop tactics of its own which are also subtle and effective.

Sometimes these problems can't be resolved. So one side sells out to the other.

Sharing profits is not the disadvantage it may seem in the beginning since, as I said, one expects the business to be substantially larger. 

However, local shareholders sometimes adopt the policy that capital must be served. This, in the beginning of a foreign operation, is contrary to our own philosophy, since we like to put the profits back into the business. Occasionally there will be dissension on this point. The reasons for dissension are endless; so there is no point in bringing up more of them here.

The establishment of a joint venture in our case is approached, we think, logically, with the view of anticipating as many of the advantages as possible while, at the same time, laying a foundation to avoid many of the disadvantages. One way to do this is to be sure that you have everything you are going to do down in writing at the beginning.

When one starts negotiating with partners for a joint venture, there are conversations, there are letters back and forth, and each side somewhere in the negotiations says: "Well, all right, I will do this if you will do that." It is wise to get all these things down on paper in some final form before the money is paid in. We do this by means of a "letter of intent."

In the course of negotiations, which may run in some instances as long as 3 or 4 years, we have a tentative partner, and then the venture falls through. We then try somebody else and the cycle repeats itself. Maybe later we get back to the group with whom discussions were originally started. So there are changes in each side's ideas as time elapses, and before the venture gets started, it is good to have the broad intent, as well as some specific items, down in writing. 

Your choice of partners abroad is important. In our case, we attempt to choose people who are in a related line of business-other suppliers, for example, to our type of customer. These people have an understanding of our market. We have common customers and are able to get along better with each other. Where possible, it may be good to include a raw materials manufacturer, if your product lends itself of this type of arrangement. Raw material manufacturers can

often tell one about one's competitors' activities. By the same token, in areas where one is buying raw material from people who aren't partners in the venture, I am sure the competitors find out something, too. 

Be sure that your partner has a good standing in the business community. If you get off on the wrong foot in the very beginning, it will take a long time to recover.

I have mentioned several steps in getting started abroad in a certain sequence, but this doesn't mean that this order would always be followed.

Consider the choice of a foreign legal adviser. By the time you are ready to incorporate the new company, you have to hire a lawyer. It may be better to have one early in the negotiations, say, along about the time the letter of intent is to be written. We prefer to select our own firm of attorneys. We don't rely upon our tentative partners for advice in this matter. We feel that our way of proceeding is more in the interest of our U.S. shareholders.

In selecting attorneys, we rely upon the advice of our own attorneys here in the United States, on banks, on other U.S. people in business abroad.

Care in selecting a partner, getting good legal counsel and making out a letter of intent, is of great importance in the attempt to eliminate dissension, once your operation starts functioning. These companies usually in the beginning at least in our case—are very small. They are composed of just a handful of people, who react like people in a small town. They know all the gossip about everybody else's business, and they are very, very quick to sense when there is a disagreement among the shareholders. This has a bad effect on morale. They begin to think: "Well, is this thing going to fold up?" or "Am I going to be out of a job tomorrow?"

The more of these things that can be anticipated, the better understanding with the partner, the better the chance is of getting off to a smooth start, once the actual business is begun. 

In our letter of intent we cover certain things. One is distribution of shares. Another is the fact that we will manufacture such-andsuch type of products. We may be limited in this respect, depending upon the business of the other shareholders. We will receive a certain number of shares for know-how, patents, trademarks. The partner will receive a certain number of shares for his present business or for his know-how, if he has something to contribute. 

There will be a board of directors, of whom so many will be ours. 

The new company will enter into technical assistance agreements with our company in the United States, and for these services they will pay management fees of a certain amount. 

Other points covered are: where the head office of the company will be, what the allocation of expenses will be at the beginning of the company. Any of these things, if you come along later and start to bring them up, might cause dissension. 

Another thing that we request is the right to an annual audit of the firm's books by an outside firm of auditors. This is a difficult thing for some countries to understand because there are statutory requirements for auditors, and for us to come in and want an outside audit, in a sense casts reflection upon the nationals of the country where we are doing business. This is something else that has to be

understood before the business is ever started. We will require an outside audit. We will have our U.S. firm of public auditors come in and look at the company's books. We will pay for it if we can't arrange for the subsidiary to pay for it. 

Then we describe the trading areas. And here again, in our case, it is necessary to designate trading areas to a degree; but this has to be carefully handled, because trading areas can't be designated specifically under the U.S. antitrust laws, which apply to subsidiaries abroad as well as companies here.

While companies can be established without a letter of intent, we have found it to be an invaluable assistance in preventing future troubles.

The final step in establishing your company is to select the bank to handle the share transaction and keep the paid-in cash on deposit for the new company. Usually we rely upon our partners for information on which to base the choice of a bank. The reason for this is that we are unknown in the foreign land. They are known and they can give us banking contacts which may be advantageous to the new company.

On occasion, we accomplish transfer of the funds through foreign branches of U.S. banks; and in some instances, we retain these banks as depositories for the subsidary company, So the joint venture have been established, the shareholders can now sit back and enjoy the fruits of their efforts. 

We expect, on the average, that a subsidiary company will start paying dividends to the parent company by the end of the fifth year of operation. This time will vary from business to business, I am sure. This is sort of an empirical rule and it seems that in our business the subsidiary reaches sufficient stature to start sending back money by the end of 5 years.

Probably the best illustration of the reward of the joint venture would be to cite the success of our Australian company. This company was, as I said, incorporated in 1948 with 50 percent ownership. 

I think everything happened in this company that could happen in the establishment of a joint venture. Originally it was only going to be a selling company; it was going to import products from the United 

States and market them in Australia. Import controls prevented this; and in 1953, it was decided to start manufacturing operations in Sydney.

The first products were manufactured in August of that year, and by the year's end, there was a small operational profit. In 1954 the operation was again profitable; and at the insistence of the 

Australian shareholders, from whom I borrowed the phrase "capital must be served,” a small dividend was paid.

The North American management was withdrawn in 1955, and the year ended with a profit. In 1956 a sizable loss was accrued. This resulted in the Diversey Corp.'s acquiring complete ownership from the Australian interests later in that year. Australian general management was replaced by North American general management. The operations of 1957 recovered the 1956 loss, and ended profitably, and the company has shown a growing profit every year since. 

We stopped payment of dividends in 1956, 1957, and 1958, and kept the profits in the business. We resumed dividends in 1959 and 

1960, and have declared a dividend for 1961.

In 1960 the earned surplus retained in the business reached the point of being double that of the initial capitalization, including the shares for know-how. Profits after taxes as a percentage of invested capital since 1957 have consistently run over 20 percent. 

Now, I don't know what your businesses require in the way of return on invested capital. I think it will vary from business to business. In our case we feel that 20 percent after 5 years is satisfactory. All Diversey subsidiaries abroad that have been in operation for 5 years have been able to approach this sort of record. 

There is a lesson-and this was touched on earlier today-to be drawn from our experience in Diversey-Australasia as reflected in the balance sheet; but it can be lost in the evaluation of the statistics. 

The lesson is that the products and services being acceptable to the trade, the success of the company abroad is in direct proportion to the ability of the manager to operate the business. In no case do we have successful subsidiaries operating under managers who could be classed as unsuccessful. Diversey-Australasia didn't really start to be a successful company until the tenure of its third general manager, and this number seems to be about par for the course. 

The fact that a company has had three general managers doesn't mean that we have to fire the first two before we get a good one. 

Sometimes we do, but not always. Often we move managers from one company to another, depending upon our need. 

What are the intangible rewards of a joint venture? I think one reward is getting to know and understand our foreign neighbors better-events abroad cease to be just headlines in the newspapers. 

I am sure that the United States is much closer to the employees of our French company than it was before our entrance into the business. When I say intangible benefits, I refer to things which may have monetary value in some sense, but which don't show up directly to the shareholder in a dividend check or in taxes to the local government. I am also sure that in all the markets where we operate abroad, Diversey's presence in the dairy and other food industries has resulted in improved quality of the products these industries produce. We don't take credit for this, but certainly do take credit for stimulating the activity which results in such improvements. 

Part of the business cycle, when we enter a market, is that all of our competitors immediately improve their products, or they ally themselves with other U.S. companies. This is stimulating to the industry, and it is to the benefit of the country. 

The same thing applies, I am sure, in the case of an equipment manufacturer. If a company goes in to sell farm machinery, immediately attention is focused on agricultural methods and there is an improvement. 

Well, I hope I haven't spoken to you too long, and I hope that you won't be scared away by some of the disadvantages of the joint venture. By the same token, I hope that you aren't oversold perhaps on the advantages. The joint venture is something which should be approached with an open mind as well as open eyes; and if you select the right partner it can be a most rewarding experience. 

Thank you. [Applause.] 

Mr. HOOD. Thank you very much, Roger.

Mr. HOOD. May we have questions now on the area of joint ventures? 

Certainly I think it becomes evident that flexibility, which was stressed yesterday, is important because there is no one answer in any area. Any of us going abroad can go with a general idea of what we would like to do, but certainly have to modify it to fit the particular situation in which we find ourselves. 

Question. Does your organization structure overseas correspond with that in this country?

Mr. SWIHART. Generally speaking, yes. We have the same departmental arrangement. We don't always have the same officer arrangement. The directors, the president, the officers of the company may be different abroad than they are in this country, but basically we ignore this part of the structure from an operational point of view. We comply with the statutes for officers as they exist in the foreign country, but operationally we work with a general manager with essentially the same departments that we have in the United States. 

Question. We have heard that many foreign countries operate with a lot less staff than American companies, and some are able to compete on a cost basis. Is this true?

Mr. SWIHART. Well, in our case I wouldn't be able to generalize. I will say that in probably 80 percent of our operations, we have difficulty getting the same productivity out of the individual that we do in the United States.

Now it is true that foreign labor may be cheaper, but our experience is that such labor doesn't do as much work as labor in the United States. So we end up in about the same place. 

Our products roughly cost about the same to manufacture abroad as they do in this country. They sell for about the same amount as they do in this country. We pay the foreign salesmen less, because we pay the standard which is prevalent in a given country. By the same token many of these salesmen bring in less. So I don't know that in our business we can draw any conclusions about the comparative size of staff of a foreign and a U.S. company. 

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Does your operation in Great Britain have arrangements with companies on the Continent?

Mr. SWIHART. No. We acquired ownership in a business in Britain that was an existing business. It does a certain amount of export business; but, as for business on the Continent, there is little. We have manufacturing operations in Britain, Italy, and France. Our product doesn't for the most part, lend itself, as I said, to export whether it is from Britain or someplace else. In the United States, as is the case with many companies, we are decentralized. We manufacture in five different places in the United States. The European market lends itself to the same decentralization. 

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Who handles the chemical sales in the British Commonwealth countries?

Mr. SWIHART. Well, the more important Commonwealth countries are handled by resident companies. We have one in Australia, and we have one in Canada. We have never been active in South Africa. We do a certain amount of export business from the United Kingdom in English products to South Africa. We don't export any United

States developed products from England to South Africa. Inquiries from this region are handled by our export company in Chicago. 

Question. Do you make any product adaptations in any areas or try to utilize research facilities that exist in various countries? 

Mr. SWIHART. Yes. I think that it is almost impossible to manufacture abroad without making some adaptation of the product. For one thing, the market, in our experience, is different than the market in the United States. By that I mean sometimes you can go abroad and sell a product that maybe was used in this country extensively 5 or 10 years ago, quite successfully, because your market might be 5 or 10 years behind that of the United States.

In such a case, to go out and sell the most modern, up-to-date thing, you would find yourself with a limited market because you might have only a very few modern, up-to-date customers. In such instances it is necessary to start out with a simpler product, one which is again this is in reference to chemical specialties one which is more easily understood by the customer, and which can be used to improve his desire for a better product over a period of time. 

So where we introduce a new product in the United States this year, it may be 4 or 5 years before it will appear in some of the oversea markets. For example, in Australia one of our best items is a material which has almost disappeared from the U.S. market. 

Question. This is a difficult question to generalize, but could you give some idea as to how valuable your know-how and patent portion of your contribution to this joint venture could be? Do you find foreign manufacturers are eager enough for it to put up most of the cash, you put in the know-how, and still keep control of the company? 

Mr. SWIHART. Well, I think this would depend upon the area, but the value of your know-how depends upon how good a salesman you are and how much you can get for it. In our case we make the best arrangement we can. We are more successful some places than others. We did have an inquiry from Pakistan not too long ago which touches upon what you say.

Here is a company that wants just know-how and no money. Well, I don't know what the angle is, but I am sure there is money involved in it some place.

Question. Two questions, please. One, for a matter of clarification on the return on investment; I understood you said you anticipate a 

20 percent return at the end of 5 years. 

Mr. SWIHART. That is right.

Question. And that is a continuing rate; that is what you expect in that period, expect to reach that rate at 5 years? 

Mr. SWIHART. That is what we strive for.

We may reach the rate before that time actually, but when we put the money back in, of course the investment increases. So the amount of money represented by the 20 percent goes up. 

Question. My second question is relative to the markets surveyed and how you determine where and when you are going into a joint venture? I think you skipped over that pretty fast. I didn't hear too much about it. Would you mind elaborating a little bit?

Mr. SWIHART. In our particular type of industry we have conducted market surveys in the larger countries. We know from experience that there is business for us almost any place. Whether or not there

is enough business to justify a manufacturing operation is then the decision that has to be made. We will base this decision largely upon what we learn from our export sales if we are active in a particular market, or we will send in a market survey team.

Now when we send in a market survey team, we like to have somebody in mind as a partner because the partner again is of great assistance in opening doors and providing transportation and all of the little things that are important in a successful market survey. We have employed professional consulting organizations to make surveys for us. But our experience with these people has not been very satisfactory. They don't understand our business. While it isn't a unique business, it doesn't represent a large segment of the chemical industry. It is different from soda ash or steel or something like that, and we end up having to supply the questionnaire, the technique, all of the information on our company, and then the consultant goes around to ask the questions. Well, very often we can ask the questions better ourselves. 

Does that answer your question? 

Answer. Yes.

Question. I would like to ask a question with regard to the organization again.

In your letter of intent you specify the trading area in an effort to eliminate competition, I guess, among the subsidiaries or the partners. Is that right?

Mr. SWIHART. No, we don't do it to lessen the competition. Our experience has been that there is just too much business available for a new company beginning its operation, and unless there is some restriction placed upon where they are going to work, they will go off in all directions at once with the result that a lot of time will be wasted and a lot of fruitless effort will be spent. 

All of our companies are free to compete with each other any place in the world where there is no conflict of Diversey, trademark or patent ownership.

Question. Wouldn't there be a situation, for example, where the 

French manufacturer would ship into Italy or England where it would be a more favorable arrangement for the product to be shipped out of France rather than, let us say, Italy or England? Does this occur often?

Mr. SWIHART. Well, in our case, no. If it were to occur, in the interests of being practical I am sure we would try to work out an arrangement for the English company to buy it from the French company and act as a distributor for the French company. 

Question. You were in England, France, and Italy. Is there any basic reason that you would like to give as to why you were not in 

West Germany other than no partner?

Mr. SWIHART. Well, that perhaps is a principal reason. We have felt that the West German market, while it is a very vast one, is not one that we wish to tackle all by ourselves. We are quite familiar with our competition in Germany. We have the same competition in France, the same competition in Brazil, the same competition in certain of the export markets, and in the case of Germany I think it is our management's feeling that certainly a joint venture would be the most preferable way to approach that market.

Question. You made a very strong point about the control, 50 percent or more. I wonder, in view of the conditions in the market or the nationalistic feeling, or Government regulations, would you ever go in under 50 percent?

Mr. SWIHART. Well, I can't say we would never go in under 50 percent, but we never have.

Question. I talked with one large chemical firm not too long ago, and they take the opposite point of view. They prefer to go in for less than the 50, and I am kind of curious as to why they have that other policy.

Mr. SWIHART. Well, I think for one thing, as a segment of the chemical industry, our business is a little different from the heavy chemical industry as such. In heavy chemicals you are dealing with a big volume of relatively low-margin material. In our case the volume is much lower and the margin is higher. These are specialty products. They are formulated to the needs of the customers in many cases.

One reason we require 51 percent is because many potential partners perhaps aren't interested in our type of business as such anyway, and as long as we are willing to go in and carry the ball they are quite happy to ride along. If it works out well for us, we prefer to carry them along.

Mr. SCHWARTZ. I would imagine that a number of your partners were in a business at the time they joined you. When you form a separate company do you talk in terms of 51 percent? Aren't you now investing in the other man's business?

Mr. SWIHART. Well, it will vary. In France, for example, we started out with a new company; Diversey France did not take over an existing company. It is a combination of Diversey Corp. in the 

United States and six of the major raw material producers in France.

This was a coup, if you wish, on the part of one of the manufacturers. He wanted to bring all of the phosphate producers together in one company, and he achieved this in Diversey France. So 50 percent of the shares in that company are owned by six phosphate companies. We own the other 50 percent.

In Brazil we purchased into an existing business, and we changed the name.

Mr. SCHWARTZ. In Brazil then you invested into that company? 

Mr. SWIHART. That is right. 

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Was their business the same as yours?

Mr. SWIHART. Yes. These were people that were attempting to get a license from us.

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Were they selling to the same distribution at the time, same product and distribution, or did you find yourself with another business?

Mr. SWIHART. No. Similar products to the same customers.

Question. You mentioned that a joint venture might not be too good for sales to the United States. Can you expand on that a little, the fact that you found costs were not equal?

Mr. SWIHART. In our business as in any other, it is a matter of economics.

Question. Is that the sole reason that you feel it is not practical? 

Mr. SWIHART. No, but in the case of our product we feel that it isn't practical. In Puerto Rico, for example, we have a joint venture. 

We would very much like to use this company as a source of product for our export company to supply the Latin American region. The reason we can't is because there are no trade routes between Puerto Rico and any place else. All the trade routes come out of New York or New Orleans. So, to try to ship quickly to Trinidad or someplace else from Puerto Rico is very difficult.

There are a lot of factors that have to be considered, but one other thing that occurs to me that would limit one's view toward supplying products from a joint venture to a U.S. parent company, is that you split your profit with your partners as far as export items are concerned.

Question. How do you plan to handle the rest of Latin America other than Brazil?

Mr. SWIHART. We handle it through agents supervised from Chicago. Are there any other questions? None? Thank you.

Mr. HOOD. I would like to call your attention to the table outside. 

We have some additional folders there from the Lafayette Brass Manufacturing Co. There also is a text of a talk given by Frank 

Wilson while in India. He will speak to us tomorrow on some special marketing problems in selling abroad. He suggests that if you are interested in this phase, it might be helpful to read. 

Mr. HOOD. We have been looking at various aspects of manufacturing abroad. Yesterday we saw that we could start by licensing, by joint venture, or by contract manufacturing. Many times you might start out being a wholly owned subsidiary or, as Roger, our last speaker, mentioned, sometimes you wind up being one because of the nonperformance of a partner. 

So starting abroad as a wholly owned subsidiary is often the first step, primarily because we are not sure about the control aspect. 

Or it might wind up that way because of the failure of another venture.

This morning we have the pleasure of having with us Mr. Kermit 

Fischer, president of the Fischer & Porter Co. He will explore with us the possibility of wholly owned subsidiaries abroad-the problems and also some of the advantages of this type of manufacturing. 

Mr. Fischer. 

8 See p. 138 for Mr. Wilson's lecture.

MANUFACTURING ABROAD: THE CASE FOR THE WHOLLY OWNED SUBSIDIARY LECTURE BY KERMIT FISCHER, PRESIDENT, FISCHER & PORTER Co., WARMINSTER, PA.9 

Mr. FISCHER. Good morning, gentlemen.

In starting let me remind you of the sad occurrence that you must have heard about sometime during your life when Pat was sitting in church one Sunday morning, mass had just concluded, and the priest came down to say, "Pat, I am so glad to see you here as usual, but your brother Mike is not with you. I suppose poor Mike's lame legs have been hurting him so much that he just could not make it to church today." "No," ," said Pat. "Quite the contrary, Father. When Mike got up this morning he was filled with religious fervor and zeal. He came to the church with me on his crutches, stopped in the vestibule, dipped his hand in the holy water, put some on his left shoulder, and promptly threw his left crutch away." "How wonderful," exclaimed the priest. "Then what happened?"

Pat answered, "Then he put his hand in the holy water again and touched it to his right shoulder and threw away his right crutch.” "A miracle," exploded the priest, "and where is he now?" 

Said Pat: "Out in the vestibule, flat on his face."

Well, I have fallen flat on my face so much in trying to undertake oversea ventures that I hesitate to come before you and express my opinions. But I have found that, out of all the mistakes I have made, each time in the end there has come some measure of usefulness and success. So if I discuss with you occurrences that must be classified as mistakes, still it may be of some use. 

Our principal difficulty in trying to work with others has indeed been that of finding the right people. We started going overseas immediately after the war, in 1946, and we had the feeling that, since in our country we had been able to build up our manufacturing facilities and our technical knowledge while the Europeans were out fighting instead of learning and were having their establishments ruined, we owed them something as it were, and we started out with the proposition that we would offer our technical knowledge for between 33 and 40 percent interest in joint ventures, and we would look to "natives" to supply the capital, the sales effort, and the manufacturing facilities. 

The most astonishing result occurred in a country where we found that a manufacturer of a very precise and important instrument was willing to work with us.

We make industrial process control instruments. This firm X was noncompeting but worked in a parallel field. It had everything that Pa. 

Kermit Fischer is president of the Fischer & Porter Co. at Warminster, Bucks County,

He was trained in mechanical engineering at the University of Pennsylvania and at 

Drexel Institute of Technology. 

Mr. Fischer's early experience includes a year in the sales department of an elevator manufacturer and 10 years in the sales, engineering, and development departments of a process industry equipment manufacturer. He organized Fischer & Porter Co. in March of 1937 and has been president since its inception. 

He is a member of the American Association for the Advancement of Science, the 

American Institute of Chemical Engineers, the American Society of Mechanical Engineers, and of the Instrument Society of America. Mr. Fischer is also president of Welcome 

Home, a child adoption agency.

was needed, and from everyone to whom we spoke about X we received the answer, "Of course, this company X, and its product, is known the world over. It is the finest of its kind. You couldn't be with better people."

The president, the majority stockholder of company X, visited my house and stayed for a week. This occurred in the summertime and we went swimming and on picnics, got to know each other very well, I thought. He was a cultured, educated, considerate gentleman and everything seemed fine. But once the venture was undertaken things went very badly. This president was a third generation manager.

He was not an engineer in an engineering business, and it proved that he did not have the ability to grasp technical matters as presented to him by his technical associates. He was a snob. He considered himself better than everybody else in the company, and he treated his employees pretty much like dirt. He was a petty tyrant and he wanted to make all decisions by himself. 

This sort of thing we didn't find out in the beginning. There seems to be a sort of unwritten rule that, no matter how much you hate your boss, as long as you are employed by him you always put on a good act when a customer or client is around. We never saw the other side of the coin until much too late.

At the end of a year and a half all the money that was invested was gone, and we had to start over again by ourselves, in this case the lucky break being that one man employed as a draftsman wrote us saying that one of our products interested him so much he wished he could have a chance to try marketing it. He began working as a one-man sales operation 3 years ago, and now we have a factory employing over 90 people in this country, and business is almost doubling itself each year.

In another country we associated ourselves with people of quite a different background, two men who had started out young on their own. One of them had worked for a U.S. company involved in instrumentation and had gotten a license arrangement from them for his company when he decided to go back home. By pain and struggle, these men had built a successful business. Again everything was fine on the surface. But these men had gotten older. The spirit of venture was out of them. One had put aside his wife and had taken unto himself a mistress who happened to be a rather repulsive person. But he insisted on taking her everywhere with him anyway. 

Thus he was in the process of becoming socially ostracized-but not a word of this did we find out or did we suspect while we were establishing our relationship.

So in that case, too, we ended up buying our initial partners out and owning the firm-lock, stock, and barrel.

How to avoid this sort of thing I don't exactly know. After all, when you come into a country the people who are most apt to jump at you are the very ones who, while perhaps not exactly in trouble, are nevertheless not doing too well and need some help from somewhere.

Now, of course, if your product is a metal that can't be corroded by anything, has infinite strength, and infinite flexibility, you have patent coverage all over the world and you are earning a profit, fine. 

No doubt Imperial Chemicals in England or Montecatini in Italy, or

somebody like that will jump at you, though I think you are still not apt to get majority control. But if you are trying to come in early with a relatively new product, with a product that has perhaps not yet attained dominance or shown itself clearly ahead of the market, but you would like to get into the foreign country on the ground floor, then I think you are going to have much more trouble attracting the kind of partners you want.

Possibly one could go through full-scale detective investigations and have people followed to learn all about their habits; put some operative into their plants and see how they treat employed people, but we simply didn't have the time and facilities and money to do such things. 

And now we have become very frightened about starting with local associates in any country new to us. 

I am inclined to feel that at least in our business the ideal way to start is to find some properly qualified individual inhabitant of the country where we want to establish ourselves, bring him here to the United States if he is not here already, have him work in our plant for 2 years to become fully familiar with our products and our people, and then have him go to his own home country and start a venture there in a very small way.

In our particular instrument-manufacturing business it is possible for us to select one instrument, a very simple one, one that we see has an obvious market in the country in question, and with as little as five men we can start operating and then gradually expand. Obviously, when you start with as little as five people you are not doing any manufacturing. You are relying on subcontractors. We, however, have also found difficulty with subcontractors.

I agree certainly that if we did as the Lafayette Brass Co. does, and drew up very tight contracts, we could possibly avoid troubles. But I have tended to find that local subcontractors shy away from contracts fearing to be somehow swallowed up or done in by the foreigner. 

On the other hand, we have had the experience that hardly did they see that our product was starting to do well than they tried to hold us up, and indeed in some cases investigated if there wasn't some competitive situation out of which they could earn additional profit. 

So we have, generally speaking, also shied away from subcontracting, or at least have tried to get some basic little machine shop of our own in each country as soon as possible, so that we could be doing part of the manufacturing ourselves, and had the means for checking costs and prices being charged us by subcontractors. 

My views would become almost identical to those of Mr. Swihart 10 if we defined an operation where the American firm has 51 percent of the voting stock not as a joint venture, but as an American-controlled venture, and indeed I am perfectly satisfied that inhabitants of any country where we are going should have as much financial interest as they may want, just so we have 51 percent of the voting stock and don't have to go through eternal arguments when we finally come down to any one of those cases where a divergence of management opinion occurs.

In our Canadian company there was a time when local Canadians owned 65 percent of the total shares, but we always held more than 

10 J. Roger Swihart, vice president, International Operations, the Diversey Corp., Chicago, Ill. See p. 100 for his lecture.

50 percent of the voting stock. In that case there were two categories of stock.

We do make it a practice to use entirely indigenous people. None of our affiliated companies-and we have them in England, Holland, Germany, France, Australia, Canada, and Mexico-have a U.S. manager except in the one case of Mexico. He is the only U.S. citizen employed by us there. Every other employee in every other country is either indigenous or from some other country than U.S.A. 

We certainly have had the same difficulty that Mr. Swihart mentioned, trying to distill a proper management team out of the applicants, and we have also found that sometimes we have to go into the third generation of management before we have people who will do a real job. I feel that if you start your operation on a very small scale the losses you incur in what I regard as the inevitable mistakes that you are going to make, will be small. Once you have got your feet on the ground, once you understand the special local conditions, then I think you can expand rapidly in a sure-footed manner and you can also start acquiring top-grade local men who have seen your initial success and come to believe that here is an opportunity to advance themselves. Then you retire the American manager or send him on to some other country where you also wish to establish yourself. 

Of course, we have found that among Europeans and Asians there seems to be a much broader viewpoint taken towards each other than we Americans take. I found that in our French company they are glad to have German engineers, and in our Dutch company they are glad to have French people. It seems to make no difference. More or less, they all seem to be of one group, except we Americans, who are the outsiders.

I do think it is very unfortunate that Americans, to such a great extent, have the attitude they have. A comment was made about adapting to different cultures. My impression is that any American can get along in any country and any culture if he does have a sincere interest in the geography, history, and language of the country to which he is going. No matter how little he knows of those three things, if he shows he has a sincere interest the people will love him and take him to their hearts, and anything that he does incorrectly because he is American and doesn't know local customs, will be cheerfully forgiven.

It happens that I am just "fresh out of the oven," inasmuch as I returned from India on Friday. My wife, who was with me, got herself a sari to wear at an Indian dinner party. She didn't try to get away with it but came to the dinner smiling sheepishly, and remarked: "Well, I hope I can keep this thing together until its time to go home." All the Indians burst out laughing, but it was all a good-natured laugh, and they showed they appreciated her gesture of temporarily trying to make herself a part of their country. 

The matter of shipping behind the Iron Curtain was also mentioned. 

Our experience has been that we just can't stop our oversea affiliates from being involved in Iron Curtain projects, and I am inclined to think that we here in America-and most particularly our Government—are taking a rather unrealistic attitude about these matters. 

In Egypt and India, particularly, I could find no feeling of difference as to whether they might be dealing with Czechoslovakia or

Germany or whether they might be dealing with Austria or Yugoslavia. They didn't seem to think in terms of one country being on one side of the curtain and another on the other side of the curtain. 

This curtain seems to be much more of a psychological problem in America than I found over there; and I have the feeling we ought to get this sharp division out of our minds. 

Czechoslovakian and Hungarian machine tools are all over the place, high quality stuff, whole rows of them, cranking out parts just as fast as any American machine tools. Salesmen from these countries, and servicemen, were in there wheeling and dealing, behaving just as ours. Frankly, I think we are better off trading behind the 

Iron Curtain to whatever extent we can. Anything we try to hide is going to be found out anyway, and we might as well be making some profit on it and might as well be gaining a certain amount of good will, and friendship and acquaintance. That, at least, we can gain by dealing with the people "on the other side." 

I cannot take a stand in regard to the first talk about contract manufacturing, as based upon the particular plan of reimporting goods into the United States and satisfying a U.S. market rather than an oversea market. In our particular business we have not yet found it possible to manufacture overseas on a basis which would make it profitable to import into the United States, and our oversea activities have been based wholly on satisfying oversea markets. Therefore, I think the contract manufacturing idea would be much less useful to us, but, as I have already indicated, I fear it from the standpoint that you have to tie the people up very tightly as you are apt to find that avarice grows as they see your success. 

There was mentioned the matter of taxes and repatriation of capital. 

We also have paid no attention to taxes, feeling that we were establishing ourselves permanently to serve a local market. Just as we didn't invest our money in our company here with the expectation of taking the capital out again, so we have not figured on taking the capital out of any oversea ventures.

Our feeling is that the dividends should be plowed back into the oversea companies and should go to create product research and development in those countries. I am inclined to feel that an organization which is involved with new products but has an affiliate that is doing nothing about research and development is building a sort of a monster that may have a very big body but has no head. 

So we have been plowing profits back, first to get the expansion necessary to take care of the local markets, and then to build local development teams. I feel that this is the way American firms can gain most from oversea ventures, in spite of our bad international situation.

I refer to the 3 to 1 wage scale ratio respective to Europe and that we are, by and large, priced out of the oversea markets unless the oversea competitors get so busy (which at the moment in our industry

they are) with Russian and Asian orders that there is a residue of business that can't be handled in Europe, and therefore we get it even at our high prices.

So being out in left field for the nonce, as far as competing from our U.S.A. operations is concerned, I harbor the hope that our oversea affiliations will, in due course, be contributing original research; be giving us new products which will help us here on the American market, and will tide us over those approximately 10 years which, I think, it will take to bring about a modicum of balance on wages and manufacturing efficiency throughout most of the world.

I should also like to make some remarks about timing. This is another entirely different matter on which I have stubbed my toes. 

Back in 1946 I thought that England and France were going to make up for what they had missed in previous decades and that they would become the large manufacturing countries of Europe. thought that industrially Germany would be flat on its face for decades to come.

Well, everything turned out differently as you know, and had we I waited a bit we would have actually been in a less confused situation than we find ourselves now.

Furthermore, had we waited a bit we wouldn't have wasted money promoting the sale of instruments which at that time had very little market even in the United Kingdom and France. They had too much to do rebuilding their basic industries, rebuilding their railroads and improving their highway systems, to worry very much as to whether their chemical processes were operating efficiently. And I have the feeling that had we come in in 1952, we would have done just as well as starting in 1946 to set up affiliated companies. 

So I am inclined to suggest, even though generally I take the attitude "get there fustest with the mostest," that in establishing overseas companies it might be well to be patient. I think of this particularly as I contemplate the emergence of the new nations in Africa which I believe will all industrialize, and which offer great opportunities for us, even though they look to Europe as their first source of supply. 

They will fill up Europe with orders, I think, and keep it solid full of orders so there will be a great deal of business for us even though our prices are high and even though we don't learn their languages and behave in a great percentage of the cases very much as boors and ignoramuses. Still we can do a lot in Africa in the next 15 years, and 

I think there will be great opportunities to establish manufacturing affiliates there.

But I am certainly going to be careful to wait and try to see when the time is ripe. I have the feeling that right now-and please understand I am talking from my own industry's point of view; it might be quite different in other industries-places like Israel, Egypt, and India are becoming logical places to move in. For basic indusstries possibly the time is already late. For certain other types of things that are even more sophisticated than ours, it may be too early. 72811-61--9

Thank you. [Applause.] 

Mr. HOOD. May we address some questions?

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Mr. Fischer, early in your speech you had mentioned that one of the advantages of going abroad as a wholly owned company was that if you are going into the market early there might not be a demand for your product, and therefore you might not be able to find an interested partner. That means then, that you assume there is a demand for the product at that time and move in rather small and start developing your market. 

Now if you piddle into the marketplace that way aren't you running the risk that a local manufacturer will take the initiative away from you? I mean we have found, our approach has been when we move in we move in fully prepared to try to take that market and then hold it against competition. 

I am just trying to measure our own thinking against yours. 

Mr. FISCHER. I suspect that our specific business conditions are quite different. Whenever we have gone into a country we have had a situation where our product seemed to be distinctly superior to what was available there, and, in effect, we were not fighting competition, at least at the start.

Mr. SCHWARTZ. The question now is, are you going to create competition? 

Mr. FISCHER. Well- 

Mr. LAMBERT. Sure. You do it every time you go there.

Mr. FISCHER. I was going to say that. In the end every successful venture creates competition. 

Mr. LAMBERT. Why are we all in this room?

Mr. SCHWARTZ. No, no. It is a question of your philosophy here. 

The question here is this: If you are going in in a small way will you be able to react fast enough and be prepared fast enough so that if you do get local competition you will be able to meet it? 

Mr. FISCHER. Well, when I said "going in in a small way" I was thinking of being small for possibly a year or a year and a-half. By that time we expect to have some products introduced. We have expectation of finding out the pitfalls in that local market in that time, and then we propose always to move ahead just as fast as we can. 

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Another question. 

Is yours a public company? 

Mr. FISCHER. Yes, ours is a public company.

Mr. SCHWARTZ. What is the attitude of your stockholders toward moving into a country to establish yourselves there? I think you used the words "you are there to stay." It is not reinvesting money into that company.

Mr. FISCHER. Well, I can beg that question by pointing out that in our company, even though I don't hold the majority of the stock, I personally hold the majority of the voting stock. So, if I want to, I am still in a position to shrug my shoulders at the stockholders. However, they have been kept informed of our ventures, and I would say that we have had no recognizable opposition.

In our early days some of the banks felt we were frittering away our money, and asked what was the use of having an affiliate if it doesn't bring money back here. But certainly in the last 3 years I would say the banking attitudes have changed enormously and are much more broad. 

Mr. HOOD. Are there other questions?

Question. Carrying along with the point you just made, Mr. 

1 Fischer, if I understood you correctly, your philosophy is to retain the capital invested in the company, in the local company, the oversea company, as well as plow back the dividends or the earnings there. Then I take it your expected benefits are to be derived from development, primarily. What other benefits do you get?

Mr. FISCHER. That is the one long-range benefit we do expect. 

Meanwhile we ask them to pay us a fee based on our guess as to what it costs to service them. We have engineers and draftsmen and stenographers doing work specifically for them. We try to evaluate that at the end of the year, and we send them a bill for what it costs, which may be very small or rather large. In addition, we tell them that soon they are going to have to make a contribution to research and development. Now, our expenditures for research and development here amount to about 7 percent of our gross sales. So we say 

I "We expect you to build up until you are spending something like that same percentage in your country, and if you don't do it we expect you to make a contribution to help our R. & D. here. If you are going to do no research and development and we are going to spend 7 percent, please pay us 7 percent of your gross to help us do research and development."

This, of course, is stimulating them to start development of their own. 11 

Mr. PROCOPI.¹¹ Mr. Fischer, when you first start this small-scale venture how do you take care of the discrepancy in the price which you would be faced with? In other words, you say that we are pricing ourselves out of the market, so that when you start initially you have got to somehow make your pricing competitive with the local area pricing. Is that right? Then do you start by assembling units or do you immediately start to manufacture, or just how do you enter that?

Mr. FISCHER. In every market which we have entered as manufacturers we were selling some equipment out of the United States. No matter what the cost disadvantages there might have been in those countries, there was somebody who found enough use for our equipment that he was willing to pay the U.S. prices. So, having, shall I say, a decent volume of business out of that country, then we started manufacturing there, and indeed, as the first step, we started sending parts for assembly and thus reduced the assembly costs. This enabled us to reduce our prices somewhat, enlarging the market a little bit. 

Gradually, almost on a piece-by-piece basis, we then started to manufacture the components out of which the assembly occurred. 

So gradually the prices went down, gradually the volume of business went up, and the number of employes grew. 

John Procopi, vice president, Internal Operations, Milton Roy Co., Philadelphia, Pa.

Mr. PROCOPI. This is a multipart question: 

You then had your own assembly facility right from the very begin. ning, I assume. You did not subcontract or have somebody else do it. 

Mr. FISCHER. That is true. 

Mr. PROCOPI. You had your own test equipment and so forth. 

Mr. FISCHER. Yes. 

Mr. PROCOPI. One other question. 

How long in your experience does the U.S. manager have to stay before you relieve him?

Mr. FISCHER. If he has luck, of course, he might find a local man of quality in as little as 6 months. I think a proper time to figure is 2 years. 

Mr. PROCOPI. Thank you. 

Question. Mr. Fischer, you mentioned that you had a very difficult time finding out about the partners that you go in with. How do you find out about these managers so that you are sure of them, where you are not sure of the partners, as to what they are going to do afterwards?

Mr. FISCHER. Well, first of all, the initial man we try to employ right here in the United States. I mentioned as long as 2 years. So we know him, we hope, before he goes overseas. Then we try to find the additional men to become leaders in the local country by again employing them with us in the United States and seeing how they function. 

However, evaluating individuals is very much easier, I thinkdifficult as it still is-than evaluating a whole company. You can, after all, get a trained psychologist and his tests; you can meet the guy for dinner a number of times; you can ask him questions; you can give him trials of various types. You can weed out individuals, but if you are dealing with the president of a prospective partnership company who is employing maybe 1,000 people, I don't quite know how to shove a psychological test under his nose and say "Please take this so I can evaluate you." I think selecting an individual is much easier than selecting a partner company. That doesn't mean I underestimate the difficulty of selecting individuals. 

Question. You stated some enthusiasm about the Orient, Europe, and other places as I recall. How do you feel about Latin America? 

Mr. FISCHER. Here you are running into a prejudice. I was only in South America once, in 1947, and at that time I was rather unpleasantly affected by what seemed to me was a lot of callousness, even cruelty toward themselves and toward their animals. As far as I could see, lack of interest in basic progress for all the people. So I don't happen to have been a personal devotee of South America. Perhaps we stayed out too long through improper prejudice on my part, but, in view of all the various devaluations and political upsets that have occurred, I am inclined to think if I made mistakes they were not too bad. We are at the present time actively trying to move into South America.

Question. Mr. Fischer, did you say you are operating in India at the present time, or just beginning to?

Mr. FISCHER. No; we are not. We have just come to the conclusion that for our industry it is time to get in there.

Mr. FISCHER. I am afraid I am just innocent enough that this thought never occurred to me. I hope it is not so. I just don't know. 

Question. Can't you find enough local research and development facilities to do some of the things that you are insisting that your subsidiaries do?

Mr. FISCHER. Finding good research and development men here in the United States in our field, I find enormously difficult. Exaggerating a bit, all the men we want are working on some Government missile project. Contrarywise, a place like Israel seems to be loaded with good scientists who went to Israel out of conviction, out of loyalty to their nation, and who are now sitting in places like the Weizmann Institute working on totally theoretical problems while wishing they could have their hands in something practical. 

I would say Israel is the outstanding example. From there it tapers on down through India where there seem to be many men who don't find their proper place in the economic organization; who are perhaps ahead in their education and outlook of the industrial situation in 

India. 

Eventually we come to the United States, where R. & D. men cost the most and are hardest to find.

Question. Mr. Fischer, in India do you expect to have an organization in which you own more than half? 

Mr. FISCHER. Are you a representative of the Government of India?

Question. Not at all. It is a problem with them. They are very jealous, of course, of foreign ownership, and I wondered if that entered at all into your preliminary investigations.

Mr. FISCHER. I am aware of the fact that, due to their previous history, they are very worried about foreigners having control. I do hope that it will be possible to work it out, yes.
