AFTERNOON SESSION 

Mr. HOOD. Working on the particular problems of going abroad, I feel we must not overlook the fact that we make products or sell services to earn a profit-the basic reason for our going abroad is to extend this opportunity.

Consequently, we get all involved with the laws of our land, the laws of other lands, and other particulars. This afternoon we are going to discuss some of these problems in more detail: The problem of the holding company which we mentioned earlier, the problem of financing and taxation, and the other one that we have frequently brought up-patents and trademarks. Because we are now crossing

borders and governmental jurisdiction areas, this can be very confusing. Our job is to work through this labyrinth of problems. 

Speaking for myself, I know it would be almost impossible to consider going abroad successfully without the possibility of retaining our earnings cutside the United States. If we had to bring them back in and be taxed again at 52 percent, and then go out and reinvest, we could never make it. We could never realize a return on the amount invested. So it seems appropriate at this time that we consider the holding company operation in more detail. 

It gives me a great deal of pleasure to introduce to you Mr. Bill 

Vaughn, president of the Overseas Management Service, Inc. He has intimate knowledge on how one goes about doing this, and certainly will explain to us in more detail the possibilities of foreign holding companies. 

Mr. VAUGHN. Thank you very much.

THE HOLDING COMPANY LECTURE BY WILLIAM D. VAUGHN, PRESIDENT, OVERSEAS MANAGEMENT SERVICES, INC., NEW YORK, N. Y.12 

Mr. VAUGHN. Gentlemen, the tax haven, or foreign base corporation is very much in the headlines at this time. This is fortunate for small businessmen because this circumstance has made available to the public a great deal of information about the activities of these companies which were heretofore regarded as no one else's business but the owners. Since the administration through the Treasury Department has attached the concept of foreign business paying only taxes where it does business, American owners of such business have revealed some significant information. Much of it is readily available in the recordings of the hearings of the Ways and Means Committee on the Boggs bill 13 last year, and on the proposed tax legislation 2 weeks ago. 

I highly recommend reading these as postgraduate homework for this briefing session.

Certainly the small businessman, such as yourself, should have the same opportunities in foreign market participation as the larger company. Many of the larger companies were small a few years ago and grew; at least in part, as a result of proper utilization of a foreign base company.

As a basis of this discussion I would like to quote from testimony which was made to the Ways and Means Committee on the 8th of this month. It highlights my point, the small company which made good through a foreign base company:

A LITTLE AMERICAN COMPANY VENTURES ABROAD 

I became president of Joy in 1940. It was a little company. Its total sales for that fiscal year ending September 30 were $5 million all in the United States. 

12 William D. Vaughn is president of Overseas Management Services, Inc., with offices in New York, N.Y. He has a bachelor's degree in economics and business administration from Calvin College.

Mr. Vaughn has been manager of the International Division of Talon, Inc., and director of corporate planning at Bruce Payne & Associates, international management consultants, before entering Overseas Management Services. His present scope of activities as president includes the overall direction of the establishment and administering of foreign base corporations as well as consulting work in the area of international operations. 

13 H.R. 5 (Boggs), Foreign Investment Incentive Tax Act of 1960, H. Rept. 1282, 86th 

Cong. 

14 President's 1961 tax recommendations, hearings in 4 volumes: vol. 1, May 3-11; vol. 2, May 12-19; vol. 3, May 22–31; and vol. 4, June 5-9, 1961 (87th Cong.).

די I [] For fiscal 1957, the year I retired as chairman of the board, the company's sales outside the United States were $36 million--$10 million of exports and $26 million of foreign manufacture. Joy exemplifies what a small company can do in developing oversea business under present tax deferral provisions. 

By the end of World War II, we knew there were promising markets for our mining machinery abroad. Those markets were short of dollars, but had pounds and francs. This required the equipment to be manufactured in Great 

Britain and France. But Joy was small, poor, and in debt. Nevertheless, studying the corporate tax structures of different countries, we boldly went ahead. 

The British exports that we could generate persuaded the British Government to build and lease us a factory. We borrowed $400,000 of working capital in 

London, with Bank of England approval, and without a parent company guarantee.

In France we employed a capable, profit-nosed Frenchman on a salary and bonus based on the profits he would make, but told him he would have to finance the operation. He did so by collecting 25 percent cash in advance on the machines he sold. Using these advance payments in lieu of the profits he would have later, he employed a staff, rented and equipped offices, and was quickly in business. For a few years, we subcontracted the manufacture of Joy machines in France, until we could get our own factory. 

Presently, we began exporting equipment to South Africa and Australia. 

Mining machines, subject to rough usage, require a constant, dependable supply of maintenance and repair parts. A factory 10,000 to 12,000 miles away would not suffice. So, we had to manufacture in Sydney and Johannesburg. The business grew so rapidly, the plants had to be enlarged again and again and more money borrowed.

All our oversea business was managed by our International Division. Studying further the help that tax deferral could provide, we transformed that division into Joy International S.A., a Panama corporation, that pays no U.S. corporate income tax until it remits dividends. We made it responsible for managing our foreign subsidiaries for a fee, for the exports of all Joy products from foreign and domestic sources, and for financing our oversea companies. 

In the latter capacity, Joy International has loaned our foreign subsidiaries a total of $4,200,000.

Here is an example of how a small company, without sufficient capital of its own, through tax deferral, has built up a splendid business abroad, that benefits both the United States and the entire free world. 

That is the end of the quotation.

There is substantially more to the presentation, however, and interesting statistical data is provided. Further, it is pointed out that these companies' activities did not over the years or at any time adversely influence this country's gold flow, its international activities have developed additional domestic employment rather than curtail it as has been suggested by proponents of the administration tax proposals.

It is possible for small companies to use a foreign base corporation, and to do so with the same objective and advantage as a large company, providing they establish their objectives, determine the most suitable type of corporate structure and relationship, select the right country, operate the company in a fashion which is consistent with its purpose and good business practice, and providing there is a sufficient volume of business to justify the whole effort. 

The establishment of the objectives is certainly most necessary for a sound beginning to any business activity, and this is no exception. 

What is of interest, however, in this case, is that many companies after operating for a period of time find themselves engaging in new and different activities. This happens because they have the company and its operating benefits, including accumulated funds. They are looking for opportunities to use the company and they find them. 

Hence, draft your objective, but leave room for expanded and diversi-

fied activity. Experience shows you may well be able to use the flexibility a foreign company provides. 

There are many types of corporate structures used and many variations on the relationship between the parent and subsidiary. The basic concept is to establish a company best suited to engage in a real business activity in some area outside the United States. 

For a smaller company probably the most workable procedure is the one apparently used by Joy Manufacturing, initially the sales subsidiary with development into technical service and ultimate ownership of production facilities. Of significance in a smaller company is the determination of whether the foreign company should be owned by the stockholders of the U.S. company or by the company itself. 

In following the latter course taxes can be saved in some cases, but along with the advantage goes the responsibility of making the foreign company a completely separate entity capable of profitably operating at a clear-cut arms length distance from the U.S. company. Once started, a foreign company can frequently develop activities which are completely unrelated to the U.S. company because of its inherent advantages not available to it. The flexibility provides opportunity. 

Selection of the right country is dependent upon several factors, including the existing and potential markets or sources for the products involved. The company's product may lend itself to free zone warehouse distribution and a country which has the facilities available is desirable. The alternate purpose of the company can also influence the country to be used.

There are quite a number of countries which have been used for base purposes but for consideration by this group, I will cover only the four most commonly used.

The criteria used in evaluating a country are: geographical location, flexibility of operation allowed corporations, economic and political stability, monetary controls, existing and potential, and ease of incorporation.

Switzerland is favored by companies with principal markets in Europe and by those who contemplate holding other foreign companies in the base company. There are taxes applicable to the Swiss company, levied by the Swiss Federal Government, the canton, and the municipality. The rates can be negotiated depending on various circumstances with a resulting total of around 12 percent or 13 percent minimum. The city of Geneva being the most popular and most conveniently located is the most expensive in operating costs, taxes, and speed of incorporation at the present time. The country is obviously stable and its monetary position is good. Incorporation in 

Switzerland is not as easy as in other, and it usually takes considerably longer to become operational.

The Bahamas provide a convenient geographical location to the United States and, to some extent, to Latin America. Business communication facilities have not kept pace with the growth of activity. 

It is not the center of any trading areas as are some of the other countries. Incorporation is not as costly or time-consuming activity. 

It will cost less than $1,000 to incorporate. The Bahamas Freeport is going to become a port in which products can be held for distribution into the United States, but it has by no means reached its effectiveness.

CO to th Πέ ag or rec pa

The political stability has historically been good; however, there are some who look to the future with skepticism regarding the imminent takeover of the Government by the Negro population at the next general election. 

Monetary controls do exist in Nassau, but are very easy to comply with and not a problem to a typical small corporate activity. 

In Bermuda taxes are levied on a negotiated basis, but are very small. The location is convenient en route to Europe, but is not convenient to any single market area. It does not lend itself to warehouse distribution. Incorporation is not difficult and the Government is stable as any country available. 

In Panama the geographical location lends itself to marketing activity in practically the whole of the Western Hemisphere, certainly the southern part of the Western Hemisphere. 

A company organized in Panama is as flexible as any, can do just about anything once you are incorporated, and the Government doesn't ask too many questions or seek to know too much about what you are doing so long as you don't do business in the Republic of Panama. 

The stability of the Government and the economic situation in Panama are somewhat beclouded today by virtue of the shadow of Castro. It is a little bit difficult to evaluate what is going to happen in Panama any more than it is possible to evaluate it in some of the other Latin American countries.

Currency restrictions as far as Panama is concerned are nonexistent. 

Panama uses the U.S. dollar as its own medium of exchange. It does not have its own currency. There are no taxes levied as foreign source income.

In summary, picking the right corporate structure and the right country can have a substantial influence on the means of foreign investment capital accumulation. The less the tax the more you are going to accumulate; the more flexibility, the greater the chances are that you are going to be able to maneuver your activity to utilize these funds.

There are many different activities and procedures which companies use to accumulate funds. There is the sales agency. The 

Panama company or Nassau or Bermuda or Swiss company acts as an international sales agency for the products of the parent company in the United States. It is paid a commission for its activities. The sales agency performs the typical functions of the sales agency, direct selling, use of distributors and so forth; in return for this the company is paid a commission.

The foreign corporation can act as a management service, and this was referred to in this presentation which I just quoted. It can manage the activities of international companies belonging to the parent or it can manage the activities of companies belonging to itself and receive a fee from these companies which flows into the Panama corporation, and it remains there free of taxes for subsequent use elsewhere.

The foreign company can act as an export distributor. It can buy from the parent company and resell outside the United States. In this respect it buys at a reasonable arm's-length price from the company in the United States and marks it up, sometimes under certain conditions to a lesser degree than the parent might do a regular export

sale. Because the foreign company can take into consideration that it doesn't have to pay the same tax as the parent it can more readily compete in international markets with a lower price offered by a foreign firm.

The foreign subsidiary can act as an import buyer. Some companies have a combination of exports and raw material imports. The international company can do the buying for the parent and perform certain traditional and necessary buying functions, such as resident buyers on its staff located at various places around the world. The company gets its profit as a fee or as a markup. 

The foreign company can act in a technical service capacity. Companies which have a product which requires a supporting technical service frequently have a problem of making available to foreign operations a technical man who has to come off the local staff somewhere and be sent outside the country for 6 months at a time. 

This company can also be used to finance foreign customers. Those of you who have activities outside the United States are quite aware of the fact that one of the competitive disadvantages that U.S. companies have is that non-U.S. sellers are quite willing to extend far more liberal credit terms than is customary in this country. This means tying up funds of the parent company. After funds accumulate in the subsidiary corporation they can be used to finance customer purchases under conditions which are comparable to competitors outside the country.

Another function for this foreign corporation is that of distribution from advanced warehousing. This lends itself more to those products which have a relatively high ratio of value to weight. From the Colon free zone, which is an advance warehouse distribution point in Panama, last year the average products which went out by sea were worth $3.50 a pound. The average product which went out by air—and this is 50 percent of the dollar volume, incidentallyhad a value of about $7.50 a pound.

If you have got a product of that nature, free zone warehouses is a decidedly advantageous activity.

Small companies today have the facilities, the opportunities, and really the obligation to participate in international markets. The 

Government on one hand through the Secretary of Commerce is today encouraging businesses to get into international activity. I believe that the Secretary recently said that only 4 percent of the U.S. companies that are in existence in the United States are engaged in international activity.

On the other hand, I think the Government has some facilities, some opportunities, and also an obligation to encourage this type of activity outside the United States, particularly on the part of small business in the United States. Indicative of that is the fact that you are here. People are here from small business, and, fortunately, there are people here from the Government. You are both trying to get into this situation and take a more active part. 

Many of the larger firms today were small companies a few years ago who utilized a foreign-based corporation, accumulated the funds, invested into selling, marketing, and extra production facilities. 

They are now successful and large. They don't particularly need this foreign-base activity. It is advantageous, but they have got the

עד manufacturing plants in these various places, and I think the representatives of the Government who are seeking to help small business should try to get this point across to whomever they have to work with, that without that same approach the small businessman today is going to be at a substantial disadvantage when he tries to take advantage of opportunities which are in the international field. 

One of the advantages which a small firm has in competing with large companies is its inherent flexibility, and it would be most regrettable if the small businessman's opportunities outside of this country were to be hampered by regulatory restrictions which don't permit him to do the same things that his competitors can do. 

That concludes what I have to say. We have a few minutes left for any questions.

Question. I was curious about the banking situation in Panama. 

Do you keep-do any Panamanian corporations keep the funds there in Panama? 

Mr. VAUGHN. Yes, you can keep the funds there. Two American banks have branches in Panama. Other banks have corresponding branches in Panama. You keep your funds in dollars. And this question is usually asked with respect to the stability of the currency and the Government. It is generally felt that if you accumulate $1 million down in Panama and you don't wish to keep $1 million in the bank in Panama, that you could very well move it into some other type of revenue-producing investment, buy short-term paper out of Canada or English paper. It is possible to take these accumulated earnings of the Panamanian corporation-let us say they have got $1 million; this is then equivalent in the parent company of $480,000, and they can frequently, if they watch their short-term paper market rate, put this to work, starting with twice as much money as they have in the parent, and sometimes get as much as 4 to 5 percent on their money, which is then equivalent to 8 or 10 percent back here. 

This is sometimes a greater rate than the parent is earning on the basis of its own investment.

Mr. GUNDERSEN. 

.15 Would you care to indicate what some of those abuses are of the foreign-based companies to which you alluded? Not for edification; just for information.

Mr. VAUGHN. There are many variations. The basic abuse is that a company will create a situation whereby it has a foreign corporation which is a foreign entity. And, by keeping the books of that entity in a bottom drawer, of the parent, if you will, it can make entries into that corporation and back out of the corporation again, and go through a mere paperwork process. That is the basic abuse. The basic concept of this is that the parent corporation pays all the expenses and all the profits flow into the foreign corporation. 

You can avoid this whole concept if you make an honest effort to pay all the expenses of the international corporation from the international corporation and do the work in the international corporation and then attribute to it the profits which it makes. 

Question. I have another question on this subject. 

Reidar E. Gundersen, vice president, Morgan Guaranty Trust Co. of New York.

There is a firm I am familiar with that just set up a Venezuelan corporation. I want to know first the advantages or disadvantages of other Venezuelan corporations from a tax standpoint. They are also setting up an office in Puerto Rico. They don't maintain any office.

Is it customary in these tax situations that you must have a resident office with resident personnel?

Mr. VAUGHN. From the standpoint of the foreign government who grants you the charter for the corporation, most of the countries do not require that you have an office per se, although most of them require that you have a local address and an identifiable person as your registered representative.

In Panama this costs you $100. In Nassau this would cost you about-I am talking per year-$500; in Switzerland about the same. 

But there is no requirement beyond that. 

From the U.S. Government standpoint, when the Internal Revenue 

Service comes in and says do you have substance in this foreign corporation, it is advantageous to say yes, I do have personnel over there actively engaged. In other words, there are people who are drawing expenses from foreign companies.

With respect to the use of the Venezuelan corporation and the office in Puerto Rico, this is a procedure which is quite common, more extensively used a few years ago than at the moment. One reason is the lack of stability of the Venezuelan Government. Therefore, if you have a Venezuelan corporation you domicile it in Puerto Rico where you can use U.S. funds. Incidentally you could have a

Panamanian corporation in Puerto Rico or a Swiss or Nassau cor poration in Puerto Rico and accomplish the same thing.

Mr. VAUGHN. The portion of the hearings I heard did not concern themselves with the foreign tax credit.

Now the question of developed countries versus undeveloped countries and the utilization of a holding company, there is a current opinion holding companies in Switzerland would be taboo as the President expressed it, but that holding companies in Panama would

But Mr. Dillon, when he made his remarks in his testimony, suggested the criteria for a tax-haven corporation be that if more than 20 percent of its activity and earnings came from outside the country it would be considered a tax-haven company and then would be completely disallowed, whether it was Switzerland, a developed country, or

Question. The current discussions as you see it, have they had a significant impact on American business planning? That is, are 

Americans now holding back on future plans to go abroad? It is difficult to say, of course.

Mr. VAUGHN. It has had an influence on it. My direct observation is this: Those companies who have had this in the mill and who were emotionally if not actually prepared to go into this thing have continued down this course. Based upon direct experience, the ratio is 

11 out of 12 continue. One says he will hold out and await action by Congress.

Now those companies who are just approaching this question of going into this are probably going to hold back because they have only another 60 or 90 days to wait to find out what is happening. However, those are the people who are going into this as a new venture. 

There are other situations whereby larger companies with existing facilities who are contemplating the establishment of a manufacturing facility here or a new distribution procedure there, who are faced with the consideration of whether they are going to be forced into financing this for 48-cent dollars from a parent versus 100-cent dollars of one of their subsidiary companies, are saying, "We will wait and find out what happens."

Mr. SCHWARTZ.17 Tax havens seem wonderful, but when does the minority stockholder benefit?

Mr. VAUGHN. Generally speaking when a company gets into it the way Joy Manufacturing did, they bring back at some point in the future a far greater amount of dividends and revenue than they could possibly have created with the same amount of funds if they had been retained here and accumulated each year after 52 percent had been deducted. 

Am I getting across? Do you understand what I mean? 

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Yes, I understand what you are saying, but I was just wondering if the corporation would be better off yielding dividends to their stockholders and getting greater benefit from their stocks.

Mr. VAUGHN. This is a question which a company and its management has to decide, in my opinion, before they go into a foreign corporation.

Earlier in my discussion I pointed out that the purpose of the foreign-based corporation is to accumulate funds. 

This utilization of foreign-based corporations is like having a savings account. You put the money in the bank, and as long as you leave it there you have twice as much as if you drew it out. Now, if you need the money for groceries, you take it out. If you leave it there, it is there; it is yours; it is an asset of the corporation, and 

16 Hon. C. Douglas Dillon, the Secretary of the Treasury. 

17 Martin Schwartz, vice president (sales), Amprobe Instrument Corp., Lynbrook, N.Y.

it can work for you at an ever-increasing rate similar to the example which I recited earlier in terms of investments outside the United States, and presumably those, sooner or later, are put to use for direct benefit, the spreading of brands or know-how or products around the world, and it has to come back sooner or later. 

There are many companies who, when they face this decision, say: "We don't want to tie our money up overseas. We feel it is an obligation to pay our stockholders." When that occurs my suggestion is forget the foreign corporation; just bring the money back for dividends. Then you are more interested in accumulating dividends than you are in accumulating funds outside the United States with which to build up your business outside the United States. 

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Thank you.

Question. Is it considered an abuse to have a wholly owned subsidiary, let us say, in Europe owned by the holding company in Panama, owned in turn by the American company in which all the profits are accumulated in Panama for purposes of reinvestment either in the European subsidiary or other foreign operation?

Mr. VAUGHN. This is a holding company function. The parent owns the subsidiary in Panama; the Panamanian subsidiary owns an operating company in Europe. Is that right?

Question. Yes, but is it considered an abuse to avoid the taxes in both countries, this country and the European country? 

Mr. VAUGHN. You can't avoid the tax in the European country under those conditions because your manufacturing subsidiary will pay local taxes.

Question. Supposing they sell through the holding company, the markup is all on the holding company side?

Mr. VAUGHN. You mean it would manufacture in Germany but not sell any products in Germany?

Question. With the exception of Germany, but they would probably have to sell their own product in Germany, but those which were sold to other countries pass through the holding company. 

Mr. VAUGHN. It is the same thing as operating outside the United 

States. You have to convince the foreign government that the intercompany pricing situation is equitable.

Mr. SCHWARTZ. It is policed. The government will police the arrangement between the subsidiary and the holding company. 

Mr. VAUGHN. Which company?

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Assuming we are manufacturing in England and the English. firm sold at a no-profit basis the material to the holding company, you would then find yourself in a position of paying very low taxes to England and earning very high profits in the holding company.

Mr. VAUGHN. I just went through that with one of the people we work with, and they have manufacturing facilities here. The parent is here. They have manufacturing facility in England, which they have had for 25 years. The company for the last 25 years has had dual distributorships all around the world.

Quite recently they changed all this, merged the whole situation. 

They had a majority interest in the English company. They bought the whole thing up. Now they have appointed one distributor in each country. They went to the English Government and said we would like to have our foreign distribution subsidiary handle all of the non-United Kingdom sales in this situation, and we would like to do it on what we consider is an arm's-length, intercompany pricing situation, and we will be doing the same thing for the parent from the United States.

It took the English Government less than 30 days to approve the whole thing. This incidentally is something the U.S. Treasury will not do. They just say go ahead and take your chances; if we don't like what you have done, then later you pay the piper. 

Mr. LAMBERT.18 What is the situation in Ireland? They are making overtures now.

Mr. VAUGHN. If they are I am not aware of it. I think they are seeking to have production facilities put in over there, but I am not aware of the fact that they are seeking companies to operate out of Ireland as a foreign-based corporation. 

Mr. LAMBERT. They are starting. 

Mr. VAUGHN. If they are, it is news to me.

Question. Could a holding company put its profits into stocks and bonds of the parent company?

Mr. VAUGHN. This is a matter of interpretation. I don't think it is a good idea. A variation of this is that the sub accumulates funds and lends it back to the parent. This has been tried, too. This is not considered a good idea if you want to keep Uncle Sam happy with what you are doing. [Applause.] 

Mr. HOOD. Thank you, Bill.

Frank will talk to us about special marketing problems in selling abroad. These will, I am sure, rather directly involve some of Bill's statements. I think we are hoping that after Frank is finished, we can have a joint panel with these gentlemen. 

So, Frank, will you take over at this time, please.

SPECIAL PROBLEMS IN MARKETING WHEN SELLING ABROAD: WHAT HAPPENS BEYOND C.I.F.—A CHALLENGE TO AMERICAN MERCHAN- DISING ABROAD LECTURE BY FRANK S. WILSON,19 PRESIDENT, INTERNATIONAL DIVISION, THE GABRIEL Co., CLEVELAND, OHIO 

Mr. WILSON. I will take the liberty at the outset of referring to a few points of reference and directing a few matters to your attention which are outside the subject matter of my talk this afternoon. 

First, our chairman very kindly has laid out on the table an address of mine which was delivered to the Press Guild of India in Bombay the 29th of last month; this is of possible interest to some of the members today simply from the viewpoint that it relates most closely to our subject matter, the general problem of the establishment of a smaller industry abroad, with elements of a recent case history in a particular country, India. Hence, if you care to pick up copies of that, they are available afterward.

I am also advised that the copies of my address this afternoon will be available for picking up later, either at the close of this meeting at 5 o'clock or whenever processing is completed in the excellent me chanics of the American Management Association here. 

Now, may I further call attention to some matters which will be referred to in the course of my address. I know that in the back of your kits in the envelope there is a foreign investment checklist which has been prepared by one of the banks, and it is a lengthy document, some 12 to 14 pages. Without knowing that this was going to be there, I took the liberty a few days ago of asking Mr. Van Blarcom, who is here among our number today and who is with the Department of Commerce in Washington, if he would be so kind as to arrange to supply copies of the U.S. Department of Commerce foreign investment checklist. That is this little leaflet here, which is a shorter but very concise document to the point of exactly what one should look out for with respect to foreign investments.20

Believe me, although this is only seven pages in length, an extraordinary amount of staff time has gone into its development, into its crosschecking with businessmen, and generally into making certain that the necessary meat was there. Copies of this leaflet are available on the table over here at your left when the meeting closes. 

19 Frank S. Wilson is president of the International Division of the Gabriel Co. at Cleveland, Ohio. He received his education at the University of Redlands, Lingnan University in China, the University of Hawaii (A.B. degree, 1936), and, for graduate study, the 

University of Michigan, Stanford University, and the University of Southern California.

Following World War II during which he held positions with Lockheed Aircraft Corp. and the War Production Board and as manager of foreign and development engineering for Van Tuyl Engineering Corp. at Los Angeles (1943-46), Mr. Wilson assumed the post of managing partner of Watumul Wilson & Co. of Los Angeles and Bombay (1946-49). 

He was a consulting engineer during the 1949-52 period, served as Assistant Chief of South Asian Affairs for the Department of Commerce in Washington, D.C., in 1952-53, and was principal adviser to the Government of India at New Delhi (on transportation and private investment) attached to the U.S. Technical Cooperation Mission during 195357. For shorter periods Mr. Wilson served as an alternate delegate to ECAFE, as adviser on machine tools to the Government of India, and as adviser on industrial development to the Government of Jordan at Amman. He was Director of Foreign Investment Development for the Department of Commerce in Washington in 1959, was named to his present post as president of the International Division of the Gabriel Co., Cleveland, in 1959, and was elected to the additional posts of president of Gabriel International, Inc., Panama, in 1960, and chairman of Gabriel India Ltd., Bombay, in 1961. 

Mr. Wilson is president and a director of the Cleveland World Trade Association, a founding member of the Indian Management Association, and a member of AMA. *Retained in committee files. 

20 See app. IV, p. 215, for copy of checklist referred to.

A further point that I should like to refer to-thanks to the cooperation of the Department of Commerce-is a series of booklets which unfortunately are not giveaway booklets but are samples which can be obtained from any Department of Commerce field office. This "Investment In" series is available for your inspection over here at the side of the room. The latest of these publications is the one I have in my hand, "Investment In India," this being the second edition of "Investment in India" and published the 4th of May 1961. So this is hot off the press. [Copies of documents above referred to retained in committee files.]

Necessarily, with any documents of this kind, particularly those that are 2 or 3 years old, there have been changes in regulations, lapses of time, and other economic changes which to some extent dilute their value. Nevertheless I would say that as a whole, these, for the 19 countries for which they are available, are far and away the best and most concise, comprehensive source of background information in regard to both investment and marketing and sales conditions in the countries with which they are concerned. We may only hope that more of these publications for additional countries may be coming out before too long.

For instance, in the central African area one publication on investment in Nigeria, which was prepared primarily under the direction of Bernard Blankenheimer of the Department of Commerce, is far and away the most penetrating marketing guide to that territory. 

Nigeria, with one-third of the population of Africa, has a tremendous sales potential. At the time that Mr. Blankenheimer was preparing the background information on this work he was getting into towns and villages where literally not even the British residents had penetrated more than once or twice in the last 20 years. So this was based on intimate knowledge in the Department of the market. 

Similarly, we may find that this series of publications is based on an extraordinary amount of work in depth by the U.S. Foreign Service and by representatives of the Bureau of Foreign Commerce, U.S. 

Department of Commerce. I think this is sufficient by way of background referrals for the moment.

Turning now to the subject of my topic today, it is entitled "What 

Happens Beyond c.i.f.21-A Challenge to American Merchandising Abroad."

If one were to ask a reasonably well-informed citizen in almost any foreign country in the world today to give his opinion as to the reasons for success of the American economic system and the high standard of living enjoyed in America, he might answer in two words: "mass production." Before his eyes would be whirring machines and endless assembly lines turning out neatly packaged merchandise at some phenomenal rate. Moreover, viewing the American market as practically limitless, he might visualize these endless lines of packages as automatically distributed and sold, and profits as automatically accrued. I am sure we could all wish to live in as happy a world.

When faced with this viewpoint I have sometimes been moved to inquire whether the speaker was aware as to which actually did come first historically in America, mass merchandising or mass production? 

21 C.i.f. cost, insurance and freight. 72811-61——10

The pioneer merchandising vision of John Wanamaker in Philadelphia, out of which steadily stepped-up volume placed growing demands on manufacturers, resulting, in turn, in steady pressure on them to refine their production methods and reduce costs, is too well known for the point to require elaboration here. It does, however, underline the starting point of my remarks today. 

Perhaps the most important American export today is neither our high-quality merchandise and machinery nor our widely recognized technology. It is, above all, our salesmanship, our know-how in the field of mass merchandising, and our scientific and nontraditional approach to the problems of marketing. For this is the fuse to ignite an expansion of exports and a growth in our oversea investment. 

To the local businessman in many parts of the world who is perfectly certain that he can drive a harder bargain than any American, that he can haggle a price more successfully and with greater advantage to himself than even the traditional shrewd Yankee trader of whom he has heard little in recent years, this is a paradox. The idea that 

American merchandising methods may be the best key to development of his market and the increase of his profits is greeted with polite skepticism.

Unless he has traveled widely in the United States and has also had the good fortune to observe and study precise merchandising situations which he himself recognizes as clearly parallel to situations in his own home market, his skepticism is likely to remain. Nor is his skepticism entirely unjustified. As Secretary of Commerce Luther 

H. Hodges has observed:

If you really want to find what's wrong with this country, then you ought to see how little we are trying to sell ***

With notable exceptions, including many old-line international trading and manufacturing companies, American exports in recent years have all too frequently been the result of one or the other of two alternate approaches to export. One we may call the come-and-get-it plan, under which a manufacturer produces an excellent product for domestic sale, merchandises vigorously within the United States and perhaps Canada, and if home demand is not inconveniently great, agrees that the foreigner is welcome to come and get it-c.o.d. of course. 

The second approach has the merit of sophistication and tradition and, accordingly, delivers more predictable degrees of success. This is the use of traditional techniques of export service and foreign market development which served us well during the interwar period and helped sustain a satisfactorily high level of U.S. exports for many, many years. These techniques are, of course, basic and still necessary today. The trouble is they stop short. 

After the American exporter is satisfied with the reliability and creditworthiness of the foreign importer, he quits thinking about the transaction upon arrival of his shipment at foreign port-c.i.f. This we might call the quit-at-c.i.f. plan.

I do not for a moment wish to minimize the substantial success achieved nor the excellent work accomplished by these traditional techniques. However, two factors weaken their applicability today.

The first is the emergence of a wide variety of new governments around the world engaged in developing their own ground rules for the operation of their economies. This is particularly observable in

1. former colonial areas. The second is simply the fact, particularly applicable in areas where change has not been so drastic, that our customers and competitors abroad have had ample time to study these traditional techniques of ours, adapt them to their own use and sometimes improve on them.

It is singular that neither of these current or traditional methods of American export salesmanship, or, in the former case, lack of salesmanship, adequately reflects the advanced level of vigorous merchandising thought which a successful American company brings to the operation and improvement of its own domestic position. 

May I suggest that the urgent challenge of today, from which substantial long-term results may be expected in our own national aims of export expansion, is the progressive application of American scientific marketing methods within the major foreign markets themselves. Happily, moreover, these aims and methods are consistent with the best efforts of most foreign countries to expand and accelerate their own industrial development in their own interest. 

The problems and techniques presented by this challenge may be considered on two planes. First, the development within the foreign market of distribution channels directly for American exports. The second, of parallel and progressively rising importance, the merchandising abroad of products of oversea American affiliates, themselves fed by an increasing stream not only of local supplies but of American materials and semimanufactures. Parenthetically, with respect to the latter, we do not subscribe to the view that the establishment of American plants abroad necessarily either "exports American jobs" or reduces American exports. Rather, as industrial horizons expand and standards of living rise elsewhere in the world, the volume of American exports can increase, albeit their character may progressively change to best serve the needs of the foreign markets concerned. 

Of significance to our thesis today, however, the establishment of an American plant abroad necessarily forces an immediate reevaluation of the adequacy of established merchandising methods. Thus, the threat of mass production forces both general and sales managements to develop some pretty clear answers as to precisely how this new flood of merchandise is to be sold. Let me mention three major signs of constructive action overseas in the naturalization of new merchandising techniques.

While already before the last war, notably in Japan, China, and certain countries of Western Europe, excellent progress had been made in adaptation of the American concept of the department store to local conditions, the most notable postwar development in this field has been the outstanding achievement of Sears, Roebuck in Latin 

America and elsewhere.

With local supply problems to be solved which would drive a stateside buyer to distraction, Sears' buyers, often with a knowledge of product extending through to its design and industrial production methods, have succeeded in extracting production out of the grassroots where literally nothing grew before.

The application of standard price and markup policies has exerted tremendous effects both on primary importers and competitive retail establishments. It has stepped up the overall volume of merchandise moved and often lowered prices to the consumer, thus tending to broaden the local market base.

I recall visiting the manager of the first Sears store in Mexico City some years ago on the evening of opening day. Standing there with a dazed but pleased expression on his face, he waved his arm across empty shelves and a nearly bare floor and said simply: "Nothing left. 

Its all sold. Now what do I sell tomorrow?"

The second sign is the rise of American advertising overseas, even in such sophisticated markets as Western Europe. Europeans may be alternately repelled or intrigued by traditional American flamboyance in this field, but hardheaded businessmen among them have commenced to recognize that American advertising influences their own conservative consumer just as surely as it sells to the American public. 

True, the cultural shock has been considerable, and the end is not yet. Nevertheless, the achievement of American advertising abroad is gaining a widening circle of recognition among local businessmen and not alone among affiliates of American companies. 

Every so often American advertising for foreign companies rings a bell and develops some such charming totem as the little Maharaja of Air India International. It is no detraction from the Maharaja's success to observe that he may be more widely appreciated outside 

India than within it.

The third sign we have already referred to the development of concrete merchandising plans for the movement of their own products by American manufacturing affiliates abroad.

I wish here merely to underscore one special facet of the problem confronting such enterprises: It is frequently the case that such an oversea affiliate will be established to manufacture a far more limited line than the home corporation. Thus, a manufacturer of electrical equipment cataloging 3,000 items in his general export catalog may start manufacture abroad with less than 100 items appropriately selected for the market.

The problem, for instance, of price lining of domestic versus American-made end products may require immediate evaluation as well as other special problems of handling merchandise originating in two or more countries through a single distribution system.

Certainly it is safe to say in merchandising terms that when a new manufacturing plant is established abroad, the strain on an established distribution system is likely to mean new wine in old bottles with a vengeance. As one surprised dealer put it to me recently, "By Heaven, you mean now we have to sell!" Naturally, the effect on traditional customs in some countries is disquieting to many. 

Considering, then, that the impact of American merchandising abroad increasingly must be on the two planes of the selling of U.S. imports and the merchandising of local production, let us touch on certain practical considerations of market analysis and market development abroad. While, obviously, it is impossible to generalize about as diverse and rapidly changing a world as we live in today, certain guidelines may have relatively broad application.

In the first place, to restate the obvious, the viewpoint of primary importance is that of the customer. By this we mean not alone the primary distributor and his jobbers or dealers but the man who buys our end product.

This problem is at a minimum for industrial products enjoying a relatively few large customers, but it becomes acute for a widely dis-

tributed products. This is a field in which in the less developed parts of the world factual data is either entirely lacking or peculiarly difficult to gather.

As a practical matter, most of us will rely on the best opinions available among our established distributors and dealers or on our own local personnel if we already have a sales organization in being. Nevertheless it would appear that increasing importance must be attached to ascertaining what the end user really thinks as distinguished from dealer opinion, concerning his desires. In many areas of the world today, objective analysis of this type is hard to come by. 

More easily ascertained from available published data and from a minimum of field survey work is a basic estimate of the size of one's market. If professional counsel in this field is unavailable, the home office executive unfamiliar with the territory may nevertheless do a good preliminary job if he asks the right questions often enough from the right people. For him particularly there are, however, pitfalls for which there is no easy remedy except experience. 

Happily, there are an increasing number of competent American and 

European agencies able to conduct satisfactory and reliable market surveys. While such competence is for the moment most heavily concentrated on Western Europe, some firms are able to offer satisfactory service in other areas as well. 

At this point may I call particular attention to the voluminous and authoritative information contained in published and unpublished reports of the U.S. Department of Commerce and the Foreign Service. 

While specific market research data on a particular industry in a particular country may or may not be readily available, a wealth of information exists as to general conditions in the country, matters affecting the investment climate, and trade policies and practices generally.

The number of foreign businessmen and foreign government officials who draw information about their own countries from these same publications is perhaps one of the strongest tributes to their excellence. 

Particular attention is called to the "Investment In" series which has now been published for 19 countries, the latest being the 1961 edition of "Investment in India."

Of particular importance also to the American businessman checking out a new market is the opportunity to call on the U.S. commercial attaché or commercial officer abroad in the cities of his concern. How often, particularly in Europe, is a schedule arranged with no room left for such private and perhaps critically important conversations. Such calls can be made the more effective by advance communication to any Department of Commerce office in the United States setting out the purpose and approximate dates of the businessman's calls abroad. 

Frequently these consultations bring to light authoritative information at variance with or supplementary to that available to one's local representatives.

If one talks with Foreign Service officers in the commercial field abroad and is happily able to get them to let their hair down a little hit, so to speak, the number of case histories of American businessmen (unidentified, of course, even to a friend) who didn't come in or came in at the last minute by the way, and the amount of research that had

been done on their behalf and wasn't passed on and wasn't obtained by the American businessman, is extraordinary. 

I am struck by the repeated uniformity of this story among our responsible Foreign Service officers abroad, the extent to which the American businessman who is paying their salaries is omitting to take advantage of what he is paying for. And, hang it, gentlemen, this is the finest information that is obtainable on any market in any country and the most authoritative because there are sources available to these men and women which are out of the official as well as the unofficial area of their contacts. They know what they are talking about. 

This is not to minimize the fact that some may say, "Well, I really didn't get excellent treatment by So-and-So. Nevertheless, if we take them by and large I would submit-and without yielding a moment to the need or any cadre for continued improvement-I would submit that by and large they constitute the team on which the American businessman can most heavily lean abroad if he realizes that this is the important thing for him to do, and that it is folly to try to proceed without so doing. 

Excuse my homily.

A further source of reliable information abroad is the excellent network of leading American banks which have been established in many countries. Here realistic and competent advice is available to the visiting American businessman for the asking.

After determination of the size of the market, its regions and other characteristics, an examination is, of course, essential of the channels of distribution already in existence. Here in most countries we find radical differences from American practices. For example, rarely does the retailer receive anything like the margins available to his American counterpart. Whether the local theory be-"He is a small man and can live on little" or that "Since I have to finance him, I'll give him only what he deserves"-the end result is a relatively thinner margin for the retailer. In general it is probably true that the lower the general standard of living of the country the lower the margin of the retailer.

At the opposite end of the spectrum is the primary importer or distributor. An American of comparable position in the distributive mechanism in the United States may well envy the self-assurance and handsome margins of some of his foreign counterparts. In between may be one or two levels of middlemen, but probably the overall chain of distribution will be shorter than in the United States. 

At first glance this might suggest lower cost distribution abroad.

Such is, however, not necessarily the case. There is abundant evidence that some merchandising functions simply don't get performed in many traditional foreign distribution systems. And the most important of these is selling. The transformation from order taker to salesman is not an easy one, and indeed in many cases new and younger merchandising people may have to be sought.

It is precisely at this point that the importance of American merchandising organization appropriate to the specific objectives to be achieved comes to the fore. At the risk of dismaying some of our foreign friends, it appears evident that in many cases where the operation is large enough to stand the cost, the placement of an Amer-

WE ican sales manager in direct charge of local merchandising may be justified, provided an American can be found possessing the necessary linguistic competence, cultural sympathy, and merchandising know

As Americans, we may as well admit that here we come up hard against the national weakness of our own thin margin of linguistic capability. In sales above all else the importance of knowledge of local language and custom is paramount. This, be it noted, was a point well understood also by an earlier generation of American businessmen living abroad, particularly in Latin America, during the interwar period.

In proportion to need, the availability today of competent multilingual Americans trained in merchandising appears thin. In many cases a satisfactory alternate solution can be found by use of local personnel thoroughly trained in American merchandising methods. 

Increasingly this approach can indeed yield solid results. However, a weakness of this course of action may be the greater susceptibility of such local personnel to social and economic pressures of their local areas to which they may be more exposed than the American. One does not wish to see a sales punch diluted out of deference to local susceptibilities.

No one abroad really expects an American to act as anything but an American. Hence once accepted as a person, the fact that he is different is also accepted. He is then free to exert his charm and persuasion to introduce new ideas of merchandising which everyone expects will, of course, be different-and, if not better, at least worth looking at. We need urgently to spread the idea of the charming American.

This receptiveness of local attitude may in some cases be denied to a local citizen. A prophet is not without honor save in his own country. Nevertheless many old-line companies have for long made use of local personnel in top positions. Goodyear's use of Luxembourgers in key sales and management positions even in third countries is a notable example which is working well for all concerned. 

In either event, however, it is obvious that what is here outlined is not imposition of American merchandising methods and institutions but, rather, introduction of scientific marketing principles, adaptation to local merchandising practices and institutions of such scientific principles and American methods as may be applicable, and then letting the chips fall where they may. The result is guaranteed to be both lively and interesting. Rightly conceived, it will also be profitable.

For we do not propose change in merchandising methods for the sake of change. Change is for the purpose of lowering prices, broadening the market and increasing profits to all concerned. As the inexorable logic of a well-conceived plan adapted to foreign conditions sinks in, initial resistance will yield to constructive action, and it appears inevitable that the new patterns of merchandising will owe most to American concepts of scientific marketing. 

It is no exaggeration to observe that Europe is on the brink of a merchandising revolution. With the institution of the Common

Market and the enormous growth in sales potential for many companies, only new merchandising methods freed from past habits and

restrictions are capable of coping with this expansion. While one must give the fact of the Common Market itself primary credit for this forced merchandising revolution in Europe, the fact remains that only American institutions and principles largely developed in America are adequate to cope with it. Europe offers a tremendous challenge to the development of new concepts of merchandising. 

Elsewhere in the world, particularly in its less developed parts, the challenge is no less but different. On the one hand, there are small markets in shaky economies capable of being served directly by the American only with difficulty. On the other hand, there are major and rapidly growing markets, of which India is the foremost example. These call for application of the same principles, but with many differences in institutional arrangements from those in either the United States or Europe. 

Both in Europe and the rest of the world one might fervently wish for guidelines. Unfortunately, for the most part they simply do not exist. The nearest approach to guidelines is the experience of old-line companies with sales or manufacturing establishments abroad since long before the war. Few even of these appear satisfied with the adequacy of their merchandising answers to changed and changing conditions in major oversea markets. Shifts are taking place, from caution experimentation to radical change. Few will care to predict their oversea sales structure 5 years hence. 

With even this limited know-how, the possession of only a few dozen American companies and a scattering of competent individuals, the lack of guidelines for the smaller company expanding abroad is the more evident. There is no royal road to this knowledge. The answers are largely yet to be developed. But this is precisely the arena of challenge-what happens beyond c.i.f

The tools are at hand if we will use them. Our merchandising know-how based on principles of scientific marketing is here to use. 

Only our will to sell is in doubt.

To the extent that we decide to sell and to organize in the light of our own best American principles in cooperation with men of like mind overseas, we can meet this challenge. Where we succeed we may expect the results to be of profit not only to ourselves but equally to the citizens of the countries which, as our trading partners, extend us their hospitality. Are we indeed in the best American tradition willing to sell? 

I will be very happy to receive questions. 

I should like to ask Mr. Bill Vaughn if he would be kind enough to backstop me on these out of his very broad experience. 

Perhaps before questions, let me take this opportunity to pass on to this audience, by permission of senior officials of the Government of India, an indication of receptivity to specific industrial opportunities which I am authorized to give to this group today. This is from the Ministry of Commerce and Industry in New Delhi, 3 weeks ago. 

In the smaller size and medium-size plants and operations, in some cases spilling over into larger type operations, I shall enumerate a series of fields given to me as being of particular interest to the Government of India and those in which they will be particularly receptive to proposals and approaches. re

1 Chemical plants, fertilizers; both of these, be it known, can be small or large, but by and large, they tend to large size. 

Chip board, hard board: By this is meant the type of building material processed all the way from bagasse to any other suitable cellulose base material; if you have a formula in this field, a process by which you can get into production at reasonable cost, it is of interest to the 

Government of India. 

In some categories, flour mill machinery: In some categories, what are referred to there as automotive ancillary industries, that is to say, broadly, what we call automotive parts. Some fragments of this are already well occupied but others are wide open. 

To a limited extent, automotive headlamps and garage equipment of all types, fuel injection equipment, transmission gears-gears keep recurring in this connection for a variety of applications, not alone automotive. 

Stub axles and half-shafts, hangers for springs, possibly mufflers, wire harness, that is, the automotive-type harness for some of which raw materials and some types of suitable wire may be available already within the country.

A variety of rubber items for automotive and other applications. 

Repair kits for cylinders, bundy-type tubing, repair kits for water pumps-it is the problem of keeping vehicles rolling. 

Marine diesel gears-don't ask me offhand to say how large the market is. I can't tell you, but, as far as receptivity to the field is concerned, here it is. 

Flywheel rings, automotive distributor system repair kits, fuel pump repair kits: Note this recurring motif of repair kits. 

The basic question is how to get more out of equipment that is already on the road.

Diecastings, a field extremely broad, clutch bearings, clutches, forging: This can be all the way from forgings for limited applications to those of very broad range. Very little forging capacity in the country as yet.

Industrial instruments of a wide variety of types: Pressure gages for tires, feeler gages, specialized automotive tools of all types, armatures and field coils, hydraulic control gear for dumpers and tractors. 

I would emphasize that this is an unofficial and informal list, even though I sought and obtained express official permission to bring it to this audience today. It means simply this, that these are not static matters. If you undertake to investigate a particular item here, it is possible that somebody else may have gotten there before your application comes in.

On the other hand, this is for the moment the best available current indication short of an official Government list which I think nobody wishes to put out in either our Government or theirs for good and sufficient reasons. It gives the kind of indication of the type of thing in specific terms which is concerning the licensing and industrial developmental authorities of one major country. 

I think you can take this type of list, expanding it out a bit, as pretty well bracketing what is called for in a larger number of less industrialized countries of Asia. It is indicative of the type of product without regard to the specific reception of the local government to this or that individual product. In other words, if you have a

product that is fundamental, capable of being produced in some fashion to American specifications but in modest volume, the door is wide open in a considerable number of countries for its reception as a potential item of local manufacture. 

Now I wonder, Mr. Chairman, if you would be so kind as to handle the questions and channel those where they should go. [Applause.] 

Mr. HOOD. Thank you very much, Frank.

We have about 15 minutes for questions here. 

Question. Mr. Wilson mentioned supply of partly finished goods to India vis-a-vis wholly finished goods. Would the partly finished goods be supplied through his own subsidiary company for completion of manufacture?

Mr. WILSON. That is correct, sir. In other words, in that particular case we have agreed with the Government of India on what is known as a phased program of manufacture. In other words, steadily declining but not vanishing-and this is important-proportions of American-manufactured parts. I would estimate over an initial 5-year period that our sales into that market will run two and probably three times present averages over the 5-year period, based on parts supplemented by a limited amount of finished goods, than they have on the present basis where we are shipping in finished goods only. In other words, a net doubling or tripling over a 5-year period of a market for American-made merchandise directly as a result of the establishment of an affiliated foreign manufacturing operation.

Question. Let me ask another question. This will probably seem pretty naive.

Would it be possible for you to supply partly finished goods to an intermediary in India? Not your own subsidiary; an intermediary who would complete manufacture, supply to a distributor who could then remit to you in dollars? It

Mr. WILSON. Conceivably something could be worked out. would be a little difficult. The Government of India as a practical matter has prided itself on very scrupulously protecting the rights of foreign investors to remit foreign exchange even when the country has been squeezed on foreign exchange. This is in contradistinction to some of the Latin American countries where you might have to whistle for 2 or 3 years to get your foreign exchange. But not so in

India. 

The question would be whether your remittances came as a result of your normal equity holdings in whatever the company is that was proposing to remit to you. If that was an approved program and right down the groove, fine. However, I know of no instance in which it has been set up through such a distribution company as you mentioned. Rather, it has been through some type of majority or minority arrangements, as the case might be, in a directly manufacturing and sales operation.

Mr. SELICK.22 Mr. Wilson, who is the contact point for all these products that India is looking for? 

22 S. C. Selick, vice president (sales), Morrison Steel Export Corp., New Brunswick, N.J.

Mr. WILSON. In the case of India there has been the recent establishment of an investment center in Delhi sponsored by the Government of India and assisted by the ICA mission. The director of the investment center at New Delhi would be one point. However, the easiest first point to which to address inquiries for development of an idea as far as American businessmen are concerned, and to get it kicked off, would be to the U.S. commercial attaché, directly or through the 

Department of Commerce in Washington, and get started in those channels.

The function of the investment center can be very useful in seeking to introduce potential partners. They don't guarantee their integrity or reliability, but they try to act as honest matchmakers in terms of getting like people of likeminded interests together on both sides, and in this way they can perform a useful function. This is merely a starting point, however, and there are at least five different steps which were outlined in a recent issue of Business International which have to be gone through in terms of actually getting overall approval. 

Mr. SELICK. But the most important thing they are looking for is the establishment of manufacturing facilities for all of these products. 

Is that correct?

Mr. WILSON. I suppose that would be correct from their point of view, yes. But, more fundamentally, their viewpoint is based on, "How can we get more merchandise for our people"?

Obviously, in a shortage situation on foreign exchange in India, as in the case of Brazil and various other countries, if you can't afford to buy it from abroad you have got to produce it at home. 

Mr. SELICK. But they buy now

Mr. WILSON. They buy now or go without. The point is to buy an equivalent volume and larger volume, or your buying now is only to a very limited extent, with vehicles, for instance, necessarily going to rack and ruin simply for lack of spare parts. It is as simple as that.

If you can't afford to buy and you can't afford to import, what do you do? There it is. 

Mr. SELICK. Are you talking about the Birla group and the Tata group? 

Mr. WILSON. No, sir. Where we are talking in terms of smaller and medium-size enterprises, this would specifically exclude the very distinguished and able gentlemen, the Birla and Tata groups and a half dozen others, all of whom in their respective fields and in cooperation with major American companies as well as European are engaged in a series of very large scale industrial moves indeed. But the very fact that these major moves are taking place as, for instance, the introduction of basic rubber-synthetic rubber production which is now getting underway-these moves open the door for a host of secondary manufacturers in our size who can come in and say, "All right, fine; here is a whole category of rubber or synthetic rubber goods that we can now undertake to manufacture where we couldn't have considered it before merely for lack of the material, not for lack of market but lack of material." 

Mr. SELICK. Thank you. 

Question. One final question, sir.

There is an Indian Chamber of Commerce in New York. I was unsuccessful in finding them on my last trip up here because they have

moved. Would they be of any assistance in providing new first steps. in this direction? 

Mr. WILSON. I am sure they would be. And not to be overlooked is the Indian consulate general in New York. However, my recommendation for the preliminary informational approach, as far as the U.S. businessman is concerned, would still be the U.S. Department of Commerce and the commercial attachés abroad. 

Question. Mr. Wilson, I assume from your remarks that you have already received authorization from the Indian Government and have gone through all the negotiation steps. I wonder if you would comment a little more completely on how long a period of time was involved, whether or not the Government cooperated. 

Mr. WILSON. You may discount my remarks as being slightly prejudiced, but we have just succeeded in hanging up a record, so the Government of India informs me, in terms of getting into production in less than 1 year after the first preliminary approval from the Government of India which was received on July 27, 1960. 

I would say this is a practical illustration of the timetable of approval-I won't say this is as fast as it can be done. I hope this will be merely an indication of how it can be done more and more rapidly-but to give you precisely our own timetable: We moved first in terms of formal application to the Government of India the 

10th of May 1960. We received approval without a word of our application being changed on the 27th of July. This is in part due to the fact that I think we had excellent counsel as to what not to ask for as well as what to ask for. One has to have a certain sense of direction there.

The subsidiary approvals of other types came through, the last of them about the 28th of January 1961, by which time the ordering process for machinery was already well advanced and there was sufficient confidence that, barring major procedural changes, there would be no major holdup. We commenced shipping equipment in April of 

1961. Meantime, the plant had been completed. So there was a roof to put it under and matters have moved within 3 weeks of schedule established 14 months ago. 

Question. Do you have your own personnel in India following up with the Government or do you work strictly with your partner or legal counsel ?

Mr. WILSON. We have worked strictly with our partners and legal counsel over there. 

Question. Thank you.

We also call on these men more and more as we go abroad to make sure we are meeting the legal requirements of our Government and of foreign governments. FOREIGN PATENTS LECTURE BY S. DELVALLE GOLDSMITH,23 SENIOR PARTNER, LANGNER, PARRY, CARD & LANGNER, NEW YORK, N.Y.

23 S. Delvalle Goldsmith is senior partner in the firm of Langner, Parry, Card & Langner with offices in Chicago, Ill., and in New York City. He received an A.B. degree from

Mr. Goldsmith has been partner with Langner, Parry, Card & Langner from 1944 to the present.

OUTLINE OF LECTURE DISTRIBUTED IN ADVANCE BY MR. GOLDSMITH-Continued IX. Purposes of patenting abroad: 

A. Protecting exports. 

B. Manufacture abroad through branch, subsidiary, licensee or joint venture.

C. Basis for marketing agreements.
