Mr. GOLDSMITH. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

If I may before I get definitely into the field of foreign patents, I would like to say just a few things about patents generally because then I think this will lead into the field in which we are most interested, and will enable us to have a comparison between the U.S. patent system, with which I think we are all fairly familiar, and the foreign patents and foreign patent systems with which there may not be such great familiarity.

I doubt if either I or my colleague will be able to cover the whole ground in the time at our disposal, but afterward in the questions we will be able to go into the points which are of particular interest. 

First of all about patents in general, of course, one of the features, perhaps the main feature of any patent, is that it is a reward to the particular inventor of the invention concerned, and also an incentive to other inventors to invent and reap a similar reward. It is a particular type of reward, namely, a self-adjusting one. 

The value of the reward is determined more or less automatically by the value of the invention which is achieved by the inventor. In other words, there might be an entirely different system of rewards for inventions more in the nature of awards, and this indeed is contemplated in some of the countries, particularly in the Soviet bloc where there are various systems of awards to inventors in addition to patents.

However, in our system the patent is given to the inventor and then it is up to him to do what he can with it. He can manufacture the invention himself. He can license others to manufacture it. And he has exclusivity, which is, of course, the characteristic of the patent grant. The Government provides that he alone will be able to use this invention and that they will aid him through the courts in stopping others from using the invention.

In return for this guarantee of exclusivity, the inventor must in his patent make complete disclosure of the invention so it immediately becomes part of the general knowledge of the relevant art and, at the expiration of the patent, anyone is entitled to use the invention to the extent that it was covered by the patent. 

In addition to this basic characteristic of patents, there is another characteristic which is particularly important from the foreign viewpoint, and that is the existence of patents with their feature of exclusivity serves to attract capital, the capital that is so needed in these modern, rather complex times, not only for the commercializing

of an invention but also for the research and development that goes into making an invention. And it is particularly true in some fields like the pharmaceutical field, where there may be a tremendous amount of money spent in developing and going along certain lines of development and research, perhaps with no results at all, and then finally there will be an invention which has great commercial success.

Well, the money that is realized from that successful invention. must, of course, be used to pay off the development not only for it but for all the other unsuccessful lines of development that have been pursued. And without patents to provide the exclusivity for the successful invention, it is doutbful whether the capital would be attracted to the same extent. 

Now so much for patents in general. 

You might ask as the next step, well, this will explain the grant of a patent in the inventor's home country. It is to reward him and encourage other inventors and so forth, but why should foreign countries also grant patents to foreigners? Certainly they haven't got any great interest in rewarding the inventor. He has already been rewarded in his own country, and they don't feel particularly akin to him anyway. But the point is, the feature of exclusivity will attract capital to the development of the invention and to the establishment of an industry in the foreign country. 

Now supposing the foreign patents were not granted after the U.S. patent was issued. The description of the invention would be available. There would not be any protection abroad, and any foreign manufacturer, theoretically, could just start up and operate the process or produce the product covered by the U.S. patent, without any difficulty because the U.S. patent, of course, is not extraterritorial. However, what manufacturer in the foreign country wants to do thisassuming he can do it successfully-when his colleague down the block will do the same thing so that he will have competitors all over the place? And, of course, if he can't do it successfully, he doesn't want to get into the field at all. So the net result is that the foreign manufacturer wants a patent. He is interested in dealing with the American patentee on the basis of a corresponding foreign patent because he knows in that way he can not only get an exclusive license but, another important factor, he can get the active cooperation of the American patentee, the man who has developed the invention, who has manufactured it over here, and he can get the know-how; in other words, he does not start in cold to set up his manufacturing operations, but he does it with his partner in the United States through a license or other arrangement based upon the foreign patent. 

Furthermore, he may even get more than that. He may get the advantages of the U.S. manufacturer's reputation. Perhaps even licensed use of his trademark, as the next speaker will go into, to a greater extent.

So that, just because the foreign manufacturer has to pay a royalty under this patent, that doesn't mean that he doesn't like it. In many instances he would much rather pay the royalty and operate under a patent than to try to operate without a patent and pay no royalty at all. 

Then, of course, there is the other feature of the very human characteristic of reciprocity. If, say, the Germans didn't grant patents to

the U.S. nationals, it is not likely that the United States would grant patents to German nationals, and the U.S. patent is an extremely valuable document. So, even apart from other consideration, on the basis of reciprocity, certainly there is a good reason for the grant of patents outside of your home country.

The next point that comes up, assuming for the moment that these foreign patents are valuable, is how to get them. 

Well, I think one of the most important factors in getting foreign patents is to operate through your domestic patent lawyer, the patent lawyer who has obtained or is obtaining your U.S. patent. 

There are a number of reasons for this. In the first place, your 

U.S. patent lawyer is familiar with the technology involved. He has had conferences with the inventor or inventors. He has probably made a search. He knows the particular field of technology involved and the relation of the invention to it. 

All of this is going to be the same in any country. I mean there are lots of differences in form and substance as to foreign patents and how you prepare and draft and claim them, and when you have to file them, which I will try to get to later, but basically the technology of an invention is the same anywhere in the world, and if you have the foreign patent program directed by the U.S. patent lawyer you have the benefit of his knowledge of the technology applying to the countries all over the world, and, moreover, you have the coordinating effect that what is done in one cuntry can be adjusted and coordinated with what is being done in another country. And, additionally, there is always the danger in any legal proceeding, of which patents are one, that possibly something may be said in connection with the proceeding in one country which might react on the situation in the United States. 

You might say something in an argument in England or in Germany or in litigation which might be damaging to the U.S. patent, and, as usually the U.S. patent is much more important than the foreign patent, you should let the U.S. lawyer have the final say in controlling foreign filing, prosecution, and litigation.

Now from that point on there are various ways of operating. The 

U.S. patent lawyer could go directly to the patent attorneys in the different countries, or he can go to a specialized firm in the United States that is familiar with foreign practices. Of course, I may be prejudiced, but I feel that that latter course has advantages, first, because of coordination; second, because there is somebody right in the 

United States who knows the foreign practices, who can confer with the U.S. lawyer or the inventors, if necessary, and all the responsibility for foreign patents in any part of the world can be centralized in one spot. Also the specialized firm is able to maintain a close check on the foreign attorneys.

Coming now to the selection of foreign countries for filing and patenting, the first difficulty, of course, is that there are considerable costs involved.

If for no other reason than because translation is necessary, the cost of foreign filing is bound to be rather expensive. I mean some patent specifications, as you know, may run to 20, 30, 40 pages, and it does cost a great deal to translate these properly, and even after the case is filed there is further translation of official objections, amendments, and so forth, so that the costs involved make it necessary to carefully select,

first of all, what inventions you are going to file abroad, and, second, the countries in which you are going to file these inventions. In other words, you can't just say: "Whatever I file in the United States I will file abroad," because, in the first place, there are about 120 different jurisdictions that grant foreign patents of one kind or another, and it would just be unfeasible or impossible to file in all these jurisdictions. 

It wouldn't be economical or logical.

So you must make a selection on the basis of the countries in which the patent is likely to have value, present or potential, and then, of course, the determination must also be based on the value of the invention. A very important invention should receive wide protection abroad. Relatively unimportant inventions might only be filed in the principal countries or not at all.

In this connection you should also bear in mind that after the foreign patent is granted this does not end the cost because in most of the foreign countries yearly taxes are payable to keep the patent alive. This is something which we don't have in the United States. 

Here, when you pay your final fee, that is the end. The annuities are not quite as bad as they sound for the reason that they are graduated and in the early years of the patent term they are quite small. In fact, in many countries they won't be payable for the first 3 years or possibly 4 or 5 years. So they are small in the beginning and, if the invention proves of value and it is used as the basis for licensing or manufacturing agreements, then the annuitiesthe higher annuities that are payable in later years are not very much of a burden. If, on the other hand, the invention turns out not to be important, the patent can always be dropped and no further annuities will be payable. Even if the foreign patent is dropped, very often considerable of value has been accomplished by way of the filing of the application, issuing it, securing publication in the country. This may keep the field open for you and prevent a third party getting a patent even though your patent is not kept in force by the payment of annuities.

The next question that arises is when to apply for foreign patents, when to do the filing.

Well, the nicest answer to that would be, of course, to file them when you need them. Put off the evil moment of going to this expensive chore until you are sure that you are going to manufacture abroad, that you are going to sell there, that you will have a license.

But this is not often possible because it may not be possible to get a valid patent at a later date due to publication or use of the invention. Therefore, you have to consider foreign filing at a fairly early date. However, there can be some lapse of time after the

U.S. filing date, principally because of a multilateral agreement or treaty which is usually called the Bern Convention or the International Convention. This provides a period of a year from the 

U.S. filing date within which you can file abroad and get the benefit of the U.S. filing date. Consequently, any use or publication of the invention after the U.S. filing date would not be a bar if you file within this year and go back to the U.S. filing date.

Most of the European countries as well as the United States are members of this International Convention. Russia is not, but that 72811-61-11

is not too popular a spot for filing anyway at the moment. Very few of the Latin American countries are members. Brazil is, also 

Cuba, and the Dominican Republic. None of the others are members of the convention.

Where the country is not a convention country, then, of course, it is desirable to consider foreign filing at an earlier date because you don't get this priority privilege of going back to the U.S. filing date. 

However, in some of the nonconvention countries, particularly in Latin America, it is possible to obtain so-called confirmation patents. 

That is, after your U.S. patent is issued and, of course, there has been publication of the invention, it may still be possible to get a valid patent provided there has been no use in the country. And this is another indication of the theory that foreign patents will be granted even after publication and patenting abroad because they feel that by the grant of the patent they can bring industry to their own country.

Then, in addition to the confirmation patents, in many of the British colonies there may be a registration system where the British patent can be registered some years after grant.

Just talking for a moment about the convention again, I mentioned the priority provision of this International Convention treaty, and that is the most practical day-by-day value of the convention to us. 

However, it also has a very important basic provision of national treatment. That is, the convention provides that, if the U.S. patentee or applicant goes over, say, to Germany, he gets the same treatment in the German Patent Office as a German national gets, and that, of course, is very valuable. And also the International

Convention has resulted in considerable harmonization of laws, particularly in the field of working of inventions, compulsory licensing, and so forth.

Differences between the United States and foreign patents: In the United States we have quite a rigorous examination. This is true in some of the foreign countries like Germany and Holland. In other countries, like France and Belgium and Italy, the patent is registered without any examination at all. The annuities I mentioned beforethey have these yearly payments in the foreign patents. 

Then there are differences as regards the protection you can obtain. 

In the United States you can obtain product protection for chemicals and pharmaceuticals. In many of the foreign countries it is only possible to patent the process of making a chemical or pharmaceutical product, but the process usually extends to the product. You cannot however, in these countries get protection for the product regardless of the manner in which it was made. 

In many foreign countries there are provisions for opposition. After examination in the patent office the patent is published, and then third parties can come in and oppose it. 

Then, an important difference is "working." The U.S. patent does not have to be put into effect or worked, in order to maintain it alive. 20

In most foreign countries, because of this idea that the patent is basically for the purpose of developing industry in the countries, they have working provisions. At one time there were many countries where, if patents were not worked, they would be subject to revocation, and largely as a result of the International Convention this has changed so that, instead of revocation being the penalty for nonworking, compulsory licensing is the penalty, and usually only after 

3 years from the grant of the patent.

So for 3 years the patentee has the opportunity to work the patent himself without any interference. If he hasn't worked after 3 years, then theoretically third parties can apply for compulsory license. As a matter of fact, wherever there is compulsory licensing on the books there is very little actual grant of compulsory licenses because the third party much prefers to deal with the patentee directly, and in this way he will get the cooperation of the patentee and the know-how whereas, of course, if he get the compulsory license under protest he won't have that active cooperation.

So, as a matter of fact, there is little grant of compulsory licenses, but it does take away the specter of revocation of the patent for nonworking.

One thing I ought to mention briefly is that there is considerable activity now in the field of providing regional patents, particularly in the Common Market group. This may still be some years away, but they are now actively considering how to have a single patent that will apply to all the countries of the Common Market. There has also been discussion of a single patent for the Scandinavian countries. However, information has always been a very difficult problem because of the differences of the various patent systems, some of which I mentioned briefly before, various questions of nationalism, sovereignty, the fact that you have enormous presently existing organizations like the 

German Patent Office and Dutch Patent Office. All of this makes it very difficult. But they are at the moment thinking seriously of some sort of Common Market patent, and then a problem is going to arisewill the United States be able to take advantage of such a single patent, not being in the Common Market?

These plans change around from month to month. At the moment they are thinking of a system with one Common Market patent, that is, a supranational patent, together with individual patents and provision that anybody outside the Common Market will still have to apply for individual patents. That doesn't seem too realistic. So possibly it will be changed. But everything is in a state of flux. But there is this possibility, and, of course, it will be valuable if we can have such a patent or a series of patents and U.S. firms can take advantage of it.

Well, I think I will stop at this point and let Frank Sullivan take over on trademarks, and then anything that I haven't covered, which is a great deal, we can go into in the questions later. [Applause.]

INTERNATIONAL TRADEMARK PROTECTION LECTURE BY FRANCIS J. SULLIVAN,24 PARTNER, LIDDY, SULLIVAN, HART, DANIELS & STEMPLE, NEW YORK, N.Y. 

OUTLINE OF LECTURE DISTRIBUTED IN ADVANCE BY MR. SULLIVAN 

The proper management and protection of trademarks necessarily involves a consideration of the manifold problems which confront U.S. trademark owners in foreign countries. While no attempt will be made in the time alloted to discuss in detail the specific problems involved in obtaining protection in foreign countries or of the peculiarities of certain foreign statutes, this discussion will consider the following questions:

I. Is it necessary and/or advisable to have a search made before commencing use of your trademark in a foreign country? 

II. Is prior use a prerequisite for registration in foreign countries?

III. Is ownership of a corresponding U.S. registration a prerequisite for registration in foreign countries?

IV. May you use your trademark in foreign countries as an unregistered trademark?

V. Do you have trademark protection in foreign countries without the benefit of registration?

VI. Should you defer the expense of foreign trademark registrations until your foreign business justifies such an expense? 

VII. How do you handle an inquiry from a prospective foreign distributor for your product? 

VIII. Should you permit a distributor to register your trademark in his name?

IX. May you take advantage of the Bern Bureau international registration program as provided for by the Madrid Arrangement of 1891? 

X. Is it necessary to have a trademark license agreement with a distributor? 

XI. With a foreign manufacturer of your product?

XII. Is it necessary to record such a license agreement? 

XIII. Should you permit a licensee to use your trade name and/or trademark as part of his corporate name?

XIV. May one license cover the Sterling Area; the Inner Six, and the Outer 

Seven markets? 

XV. Will one trademark registration suffice for each of these markets? 

XVI. What provisions should you include in your license agreement?

XVII. Should you permit the licensee to use his own trademark in association with your mark?

XVIII. May trademarks be transferred and assigned in foreign countries? 

Mr. SULLIVAN. At the present time there are approximately 140 countries where there are provisions for the protection of trademarks. While no attempt will be made in the time allotted to discuss in detail the specific problems involved in obtaining protection in foreign countries, I would like to discuss the questions that are generally asked by companies going abroad. 

1. The most common question is whether you must first use your trademark in foreign countries before you may apply for registration. Undoubtedly, many of you are familiar with the requirement in 

24 Francis J. Sullivan is a partner in the law firm of Liddy, Sullivan, Hart, Daniels & 

Stemple in New York, N.Y. He is a graduate of Manhattan College and Fordham Law 

School. He obtained a master of law degree in trade regulation from New York University 

Law School. 

Mr. Sullivan has specialized in patents and trademarks since 1945. He served in the 

U.S. Navy during World War II and was recalled to active service during the Korean war. He served as legal officer of the Atlantic Reserve Fleet with the rank of commander. 

Mr. Sullivan is the author of "International Trademark Protection" which appeared in 

The Trademark Reporter. He has discussed trademarks at meetings of the New York 

Patent Law Association, the New Jersey Patent Law Association, the Practising Law In stitute, and the United States Trademark Association among others. He is a member of the American Bar Association, the American Patent Law Association, the New York

Patent Law Association, the United States Trademark Association, the New York County 

Bar Association, the Richmond County Bar Association, and other professional organizations.

PL 

E 

the United States that you must first use your trademark in interstate commerce before you can qualify for a Federal registration.

Prior use of a trademark, with two exceptions, is not a prerequisite abroad for registration.

Apart from the Philippines and Burma (and also excluding those countries that require a basic U.S. registration), one can select a trademark today and immediately apply to have the mark registered throughout the world without having made an actual use of the mark. 

However, for our purposes we can divide the countries throughout the world that have provisions for trademark protection into two categories; namely, the so-called first-user countries and the so-called first-registrant countries. In the first-user countries you do not require prior use of the trademark as a prerequisite for registration. 

Your application may be based upon a proposed use. However, a person who has previously used his mark in that country has the right to oppose your application. Furthermore, in most of these countries there is a requirement that use of the mark be commenced within a specified period of time or your registration is subject to cancellation on the basis of nonuse. 

In the first-registrant countries, and this includes most of Latin America, the first to apply for registration obtains exclusive rights to the trademark and this is true even as against a prior user. 

2. The second most common question is whether ownership of a corresponding U.S. registration is a prerequisite for registration in foreign countries.

The number of foreign countries that require the ownership of a basic home registration as a prerequisite for filing has been substantially reduced since the end of World War II.

The countries that still require ownership of a basic U.S. registration are Belgium, Bulgaria, Czechoslovakia, Cuba, Finland, Guatemala, Honduras, Hungary, Iceland, Panama, the Philippines, Poland, Portugal, Turkey, Uruguay, and the U.S.S.R. However, some of these countries offer an alternative method of applying for registration in the absence of a home registration. 

A lower court in Belgium recently ruled that a Belgian registration was valid even though the registrant did not own a corresponding home registration. No appeal was taken from this decision and presumably a home registration is no longer required in order to obtain a valid registration in Belgium. Incidentally, the cost of maintaining trademark protection in Belgium is quite nominal as a Belgian registration is issued for an unlimited period of time. 

3. Is it necessary and/or advisable to have a search made before commencing use of your trademark in a foreign country?

The answer depends on several factors. In the event the product is in the pharmaceutical or chemical field it is definitely advisable to have searches made regardless of the number of countries in which you propose to file applications. If the filing program includes a large number of countries and the product is other than a pharmaceutical or chemical product, one might file the applications without incurring the expense of trademark searches provided that it is not proposed to commence actual distribution of the product abroad in the immediate future. It has been my experience that if a mark is registrable in the United States it is generally available for registration abroad, a possible exception being a trademark used with a

pharmaceutical or chemical product. In the event distribution of the product abroad is about to begin, it is recommended that searches be made regardless of the expense involved in order to avoid a possible infringement of an existing registered trademark and the inconvenience of being compelled to withdraw the trademarked product from the particular market until a new trademark can be substituted. 

In connection with registrability one should also determine whether the proposed trademark lends itself to an obscene or unfavorable connotation in the language of any of the foreign countries where one intends to use the mark. 

4. May you use your trademark in foreign countries as an unregistered trademark? The answer is "Yes," but I would not recommend your use abroad of your trademark as an unregistered trademark.

Presumably you are aware that in the United States a trademark infringement suit may be successfully brought in the absence of a registration of the trademark involved. However, the general rule in foreign countries is that an unregistered trademark has no legal status. 

In the absence of a foreign registration the remedy, if any, involves either an action for unfair competition or for "passing off." In addition, the lack of a foreign registration may result in an inadvertent infringement of a registered trademark of a third party, especially in those countries where the first registrant acquires exclusive rights superior to those of a prior user. For example, if you are using your trademark in a Latin American country where the first registrant acquires ownership, you may be charged with infringement despite your prior use of the mark. 

5. What about priority and international registrations?

The United States is a member of the International Convention and also the Pan American Convention. Priority of the U.S. filing date may be claimed if the application in the other member countries is filed within 6 months from the filing date of the U.S. application. 

The United States is not a member of the Arrangement of Madrid (1891) which set up the Berne Bureau for the international registration of trademarks. A trademark to be registrable in the International 

Bureau must previously be registered in applicant's home country. An application for the international registration of a trademark should be filed in the trademark office of applicant's home country. When the local registration is granted the trademark office of applicant's home country remits the documents to the International Bureau at Berne, Switzerland. If there are any formal deficiencies the documents may be regularized within 6 months. Apart therefrom, the mark is registered at once without examination and the trademark offices of the 22 contracting countries are notified. This method of registering a trademark is less expensive than obtaining separate registrations in each of the contracting countries. 

Since the United States is not a member of the Arrangement of Madrid, U.S. applicants cannot file foreign applications through the 

Berne Bureau-with one possible exception. If an American company has a branch office which constitutes a commercial enterprise in a country which is in one of the 22 countries that are members of the Arrangement of Madrid, it may obtain international registrations through the Berne Bureau. You first obtain a national registration in the name of your branch office, for example, in France,

and then file the application for the international registration of the trademark. Such an international registration certificate would read somewhat as follows: "XYZ, a New York corporation, having a branch office at 00 Rue de Provence, Paris, France." It is presently believed that such international registrations are valid even though the United States is not a member of the Arrangement of Madrid. 

I am frequently asked if it is possible to protect a trademark by a single registration in the European Common Market. The answer is "No." However, France and Italy recently concluded a treaty and it is now possible to cover both countries by a single registration. 

The application for registration may be filed in either country and a nominal additional filing fee is charged for extending the registration to the second country. 

6. Should you permit a foreign distributor to register your trademark in his name? The answer is "No." Many American manufacturers enjoy very friendly relationships with their foreign distributors and from time to time will permit a distributor to register their trademark in his own name. I think this is a serious mistake for distributorships, like marriages, are sometimes terminated. You are then confronted with the problem of reacquiring ownership of your trademark in that particular country. Unless the distributor will voluntarily assign the registered trademark to you, he is in a position to preclude you from continuing the distribution of your trademarked product in that country. 

7. Is it necessary to have a trademark license agreement with a distributor? The answer is generally "No." We must make a distinction between a foreign manufacturer and a foreign distributor. If the distributor is merely acting as your agent for the distribution of your trademarked product in a particular country there is no necessity of entering into a trademark license agreement with the distributor. Your arrangement with a foreign manufacturer should be governed by a trademark license if the manufacturer is manufacturing your product for his own account and selling it under your trademark. 

8. Is it permissible to license trademarks in foreign countries?

It is my understanding that a previous speaker has already discussed the question of foreign licensing in general, and I shall therefore limit my comments to the licensing of trademarks.

The licensing of trademarks is generally permissible and for the purpose of this discussion foreign countries may be classified into three groups, namely, the "registered user" countries; the countries where licensing is permissible with little or no official formality; and the countries where licensing of trademarks is ordinarily prohibited. 

Prior to 1938 the licensing of registered trademarks was prohibited in Great Britain and in countries such as Australia, Canada, Hong Kong, Jamaica, New Zealand, India, Singapore, South Africa, etc., that follow the British practice. In 1938, Great Britain adopted a new trademark law which includes provisions for the recordation of a "registered user." A formal application to record the licensee is filed with the Registrar and, if he is satisfied that there has been compliance with the statutory requirements-particularly as to the provision governing the standard of quality of the products to be sold

under the registered trademark, he will accept the application and record the licensee as a registered user. If the owner permits his registered trademark to be used by a person other than a registered user, the registration is subject to cancellation. This might occur where an alleged infringer petitions for cancellation on the grounds that the mark has been used by a licensee without benefit of the registered user recordation.

In recent years most of the British dominions, colonies, possessions, and protectorates have amended their laws so as to include the registered user provisions.

The group of countries where licensing of trademarks is permitted with little or no formality would include the Latin American countries and the continental European countries. In most of these countries the trademark laws make no specific reference to licensing and a trademark is generally considered in the same category as any other property right and may be freely transferred or licensed. To my knowledge, there is no country in this group where it is compulsory to record the license agreement. However, in some countries the 

Trademark Office will record a license agreement even though the Trademark Act makes no reference to such recordals. Whether or not it is intended to record a license agreement, an appropriate license agreement should be executed by the parties. In those countries where a registration is subject to cancellation on the grounds of nonuse, be certain to verify that use by the licensee inures to the licensor's benefit. If it does not, there should be a reservation of the licensor's right to make at least a token use periodically to avoid a possible cancellation of its registration on the ground of nonuse. 

The third group of countries, those where the licensing of trademarks is not ordinarily permitted, includes Egypt, Germany, Iraq, Iceland, Israel, Korea, Finland, and those few remaining British colonies that have yet to adopt the registered user provision. The licensing of trademarks in such countries is not uncommon and this is particularly true of Germany. It is generally believed that there is only a minimum risk if strict control over the licensee's use of the registered trademark is exercised and deception on the part of the purchasing public as to the ownership of the mark is avoided. 

In the event, for business purposes, it is deemed necessary to permit a licensee in any foreign country to use your trade name or trademark as part of its corporate name or trade name, be sure to clearly spell out that such use is covered by the license agreement and is to be discontinued immediately upon termination of the agreement. In my opinion, it is a mistake to permit a licensee, other than a wholly owned or controlled subsidiary, to include either your trade name or trademark in its business title. If permission is granted to an unrelated licensee to incorporate the trade name or trademark in its business title, the license agreement should provide that any trademark subsequently adopted by the licensee is to be registered in the licensor's name. Where the licensee does not use the licensor's trade name or trademark in its business title, the agreement should prohibit the licensee from using another trademark in association with the licensed trademark unless the second mark is also registered in the licensor's name.

Today, perhaps more than ever before, many of us are very much aware of the antitrust laws of the United States. Many attorneys will advise their clients not to include a provision in a foreign license agreement wherein the licensee agrees he will not sell the product bearing the licensor's trademark in the United States. The theory is that such a provision constitutes a division of territory and may be illegal under our antitrust laws. Personally, I am not convinced that such a division of territory constitutes an antitrust violation. However, to be on the safe side, I would not include such a provision in a license agreement. You have an adequate safeguard by reason of the fact that you may record your U.S. trademark registration with the

U.S. Bureau of Customs so as to preclude the importation into the 

United States of products bearing your registered trademark whether they be goods of your own manufacture, of the licensee, or of a third party. 

9. May trademarks be transferred and assigned in foreign countries?

Where the assignee has acquired all of the assets, including the good will of the assignor's business, you have no problem in effecting a transfer of registered trademarks. However, in quite a few countries there can be no valid assignment without a transfer of the assignor's business and the good will attached to the marks. Therefore, I suggest careful consideration be given to the assignment requirements of the particular country involved before committing yourself to the transfer of the registered trademarks.

One important point to keep in mind is that the execution of assignment documents is not necessary where there has been a corporate merger. If corporation A merges into corporation B, you can effect the transfer of record title by the recordation of the certificate of merger. Let me repeat-it is not necessary to prepare and record assignment documents where there has been a statutory merger. When recording a certificate of merger in foreign countries the foreign translation charges may be reduced by requesting the Secretary of State of the state in which the surviving corporation is domiciled to issue a short form certificate in lieu of the certified copy of the actual certificate of merger. The short form certificate is normally limited to a certification that on the blank day of blank month and year, corporaiton A merged into corporation B. The same procedure can be followed when you are recording a change of corporate name. 

In Latin American countries the usual practice for recordation is to file a separate petition for each registration included in the assignment document. Frequently when an assignee has acquired all of the assets of the assignor, the assignee may have some reservations as to whether it wishes to incur the expense of recording the transfer of all of the registered trademarks. In the Latin American countries it is generally possible to effect the recordation of the assignment document in respect of one or more of the registrations included therein and then, at a subsequent date, again record the assignment document in respect of one or more of the remaining registrations.

Now, according to the program I am to suggest how you can go about selecting a trademark counsel at home or abroad. Unless you have a very large operation, it would not be economical for you to attempt to set up your own staff to handle your foreign trademark program. In fact, many law firms rely upon firms that specialize in

international trademarks and patents. Mr. Goldsmith's firm, my own firm, and several other firms throughout the United States do specialize in international trademarks and patents, and it would be my suggestion that you communicate or contact the general counsel and ask him to help you to select the firm of trademark and patent attorneys which he believes is best suited to your requirements. 

Thank you. [Applause.] 

Mr. HOOD. Thank you very much, Mr. Sullivan.

Mr. HOOD. We are now open for discussion in this important technical area of patents and trademarks.

Question. Mr. Sullivan, is Canada one of those 22 nations in that 

Madrid agreement? 

Mr. SULLIVAN. I'm sorry. No, Canada is not. In Canada you can apply for registration of a trademark on four different bases. 

One, if you have acutally used your mark in Canada.

Two, if the mark has been made known in Canada through advertisements, let us say in U.S. publications that are circulated throughout Canada. 

Three, if you have a U.S. registration you can base your Canadian application on your U.S. registration.

Four, you can base your application in Canada upon a proposed use in Canada.

The application will be prosecuted through opposition. In other words, it will be published through opposition, but the certificate of registration will not be issued until you file an affidavit stating that you have commenced use of the mark in Canada.

Mr. GUNDERSEN. A very elementary question to Mr. Goldsmith and 

Mr. Sullivan. What is the life of a patent and what is the life of a trademark in the United States and in foreign countries? 

Mr. GOLDSMITH. Well, shall I start off with the patents? The life varies in the different countries. I mean, there is not uniformity on that. In the United States it is 17 years from grants. In foreign countries it usually runs from 15 to 20 years from grant, sometimes from allowance, sometimes from filing. Where it runs from filing, of course, then any time that it takes to prosecute the application is more or less removed from your patent term. It varies from country to country.

Mr. SULLIVAN. The same is true of trademark registrations. In the 

United States the trademark registration is for a period of 20 years and may be renewed. In foreign countries, the longest period, with the exception of Belgium, where I mentioned the earlier registrations are issued for an unlimited period of time, registrations are issued for 20 years and less. It varies according to the country. But the maximum would be 20 years.

Mr. GOLDSMITH. I would like to add, of course, that an important difference between a patent and a trademark is that a trademark generally can last forever and grow progressively more valuable, whereas a patent, of course, will run for a fixed term and will not be renewable. 

Mr. HOOD. Mr. Sullivan, would you explain further what a trademark is? Is it a name? Is it a sign the way you describe it or can it change? Can you amplify that a bit?

Mr. SULLIVAN. Sometimes we use the terms "brand name" and "trademark" as being interchangeable terms, and that is correct. Other times a trade name and a trademark might be one and the same thing. 

For example, we have the General Tire & Rubber Co., "General," of course, is their trade name, and also happens to be a trademark. 

Another illustration would be the Underwood Typewriter Co. or 

Royal Typewriter Co. where "Royal" is a trade name and is also a trademark.

Now, it depends on how it is used. Where the name "Royal" appears on a typewriter, it is, of course, being used as a trademark to indicate the source of origin. It is believed that in time the purchasing public will associate a particular trademark with a particular manufacturer, or at least the purchasing public will accept a trademarked product as indicating that he is again purchasing a product of the same quality which he had previously purchased under the same trademark. 

Have I answered the question?

Mr. HOOD. Not quite. Let us assume that I have a mark and I change it. Now what do I do?

Mr. SULLIVAN. Well, it depends on how you change it. If it is merely an alteration of mark and the original mark has not lost its full identity, you basically have the same trademark. In recent years there has been quite a trend on the part of different companies to modernize their trademarks but you will always find that the trademark maintains the basic features of the original trademark, so for all practical purposes it is merely a revision of the earlier trademark. 

Now, of course, if you have a trademark which you find over the years hasn't created good reception on the part of the purchasing public, you may conclude to drop that trademark and introduce a new trademark. That is something else again. 

Mr. HOOD. At that time I reregister. 

Mr. SULLIVAN. Yes. You must apply for registration of a trademark. As a matter of fact, some of our bigger corporations may have as many as 300 or 400 trademarks registered in the United States alone.

Question. In the absence of a clear-cut patent position, can you couple together a trademark and technical agreement and get a royalty on the basis of that from the foreign subsidiary? 

Mr. GOLDSMITH. I don't know to whom that is directed but I think you can.

I think these days there are quite a number of agreements, perhaps even know-how and technical assistance, know-how alone and technical assistance and trademark rights without patents. 

On the other hand, sometimes it is very valuable to have patents referred to in the agreement not only to provide for exclusivity in proceeding against infringements but also sometimes I believe it facilitates the transmission of royalties from the country to have patents involved.

Mr. SULLIVAN. I concur. 

Question. Is this preempting of trademarks in first registrant countries fairly prevalent, a fairly prevalent occurrence, and if so, where does it occur? 

Mr. SULLIVAN. Well, the answer is "Yes." You may go for a long period of time without encountering any trouble, but all of a sudden you find that you run into a great deal of trouble. For example, you

It may be familiar with the trademark "Heathkit." If you are, if you do know anything about hi-fi, well, that mark has been preempted in Argentina and at the present time there is litigation going on. happens quite frequently. I wouldn't say it is actually on a large scale but we do know for a fact there are individuals in Latin American countries that closely peruse American publications and when they see a new trademarked product being advertised, they may make a gamble that sooner or later that particular product will be introduced into their particular country, and on that basis they may apply for registration.

Now, keep in mind, as I have already stated, with two exceptions, the Philippines and Burma, any of us could go into any country tomorrow and apply for registration of a trademark, and in those countries where the first applicant or registrant gets exclusive rights, we can preclude others from coming into that country using our trademark with a similar product. 

Have I answered your question? 

Mr. GOLDSMITH. Frank, wasn't a great example of that the old trademark "Vasoline" that was used in some of the South American countries? 

Mr. SULLIVAN. Yes. That goes back a good many years. I assume he is talking about the current trend. It isn't as prevalent as it was years ago but it is still prevalent. 

Mr. LAMBERT. You answered part of my question.

Question. In countries where patents are registered without examination, what is the patent worth?

Mr. GOLDSMITH. Well, that is a good question. It may be worth a lot or worth very little if it is registered without examination, and also to a great extent there are no claims such as there are in the other examination countries where you have to indicate what you consider the features of the invention to be. 

Now, in France, for example, there are no claims, so in other words not even the patentee has to indicate the claims of the patent. It is merely up to the court if the patent is ever litigated to decide what is there in this patent description which is novel and patentable and it may be worth nothing at all.

For example, if you file an application in France today and I go in tomorrow and file an application for the same thing, we will both have our patents. My patent will be worth zero. What yours will be worth will depend on the novelty of the invention at the time that you filed the application, and so really the difficulty with a system of that type is that there is this enormous mass of patents on the books, some of which may be absolutely valueless and some of which have very little value. 

I think it is not a particularly good system.

Of course, even in the strict examination countries, take the United States, for example, the court still has the final say. I mean the fact that you have gotten your patent after an examination in the Patent Office doesn't mean that it has guaranteed validity, and certainly particularly in past years the U.S. courts have struck down many patents but it does have much more of a prima facie evidence of validity where there has been an examination than a patent in a registration. tr CO 01

1: Question. In such a country which registers without examination, is there a possibility of prior disclosure in advance of the U.S. patent being issued, because certainly they don't take the time if they don't examine. 

Mr. GOLDSMITH. I am not quite sure I understand the question. I mean take France, for example, which is a registration country, and when you file there, you have the benefit of your filing date, or if you file under convention, you have the benefit of your U.S. filing date.

So any subsequent publication or use would not be a bar. 

I should take the opportunity of pointing out that although_the convention provides means for getting back to the U.S. filing date, one thing that should be watched carefully is that there is no publication or use of the invention before the U.S. filing date,

Now, under U.S. law, with which I might mention I am not too currently familiar because I only operate in foreign countries, I don't work in the U.S. Patent Office but I do know that under the Ú.S. law publication or use of the invention for a year prior to the U.S. filing date is not a bar to validity.

However, if such use has occurred and then you go abroad, even under the convention, that convention priority is only getting back to the U.S. filing date and the use of the publication prior to the U.S. filing date may be a bar, and that is something that you have to watch. 

Question. I didn't make my question very clear. It may take several years for a patent to issue in the United States. 

Mr. GOLDSMITH. Yes, because of the examination.

Question. In the meantime you filed it in France where they issue it at once without examination. Then a patent is disclosed to anyone who may be interested even though it has not yet issued in the 

United States. 

Mr. GOLDSMITH. Yes. I mean that is not going to cause any difficulty because it has already been filed in the United States. Of course, if you filed it in France and it issued before you filed in the United States, you might get into trouble, but not if you filed in the United 

States first.

For example, in Belgium you can't delay issuance for more than 6 months, so 6 months after you file in Belgium, the Belgian patent will issue and be published, but this does not have any effect on the United States or on other applications that have been filed beforehand. 

Mr. SCHWARTZ.25 Mr. Sullivan, will you please review first user and first registrant, this time just a little slower? 

Mr. SULLIVAN. Well, generally speaking, you can divide the countries throughout the world into two categories. The so-called first user countries and the so-called first applicant or first registrant countries. 

Now, the term "first user" is really misleading because it does not imply or it is not intended to imply that you must first have made use of your trademark in that particular country before applying for registration. The first user countries as I am using the term include all countries where you can apply for registration without having made use of your trademark but where the first user of a mark actually has rights to oppose a registration. 

25 Martin Schwartz, vice president (sales), Amprobe Instrument Corp., Lynbrook, N.Y.

Now, let us assume corporation A has been using its trademark in a first user country, for example, we will take Great Britain, since as early as 1950, and corporation B comes in today to apply for registration on the basis of a proposed use. 

When the application of corporation B is published for opposition purposes, corporation A can come in and oppose that application on the basis of its earlier use. But keep in mind that corporation A could not charge corporation B with trademark infringement because corporation A does not have a registered trademark and an unregistered trademark has no legal status in Great Britain. 

Now, also in the first user countries the law usually provides that following registration within a specified period of time-it varies according to the individual country but let us take as a general average a 5-year period-you must commence use of your trademark. If not, a third party, even though a subsequent user, subsequent to your registration, can come in and petition for the cancellation of your registration on the basis of nonuse. 

Now, we go to those countries where the first registrant acquires exclusive trademark rights. I can register my trademark in such a country without using the mark and assuming that the term of regis tration is 10 years, at the end of the 10-year period I can come in and renew my registration for another period of 10 years even though

I have not actually commenced use of that mark in that particular country.

Also, in the first registrant country, if corporation A has been using its mark since 1950, and I come in today as corporation B and obtain a registration for my mark, I can charge corporation A with infringement on the basis of my registration. 

Have I answered your question? 

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Yes. Just one more question. Assuming that you have a distributor in a foreign country who is purchasing your material and advertising it under their name, if it was one of the first user countries, could he then possibly have a claim on you? 

Mr. SULLIVAN. I really don't follow your question. As I understood the question, the distributor is advertising the product under his name and selling it under his name. Then you come in and you want to use his name? 

Mr. SCHWARTZ. No. You want to use your own name in a foreign country after he has been selling your product under your name using your mark. 

Mr. SULLIVAN. He has been using your mark? 

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Using your mark in his advertising literature.

Mr. SULLIVAN. Well, there you have a question that it is more of a contractual relationship between the parties than it is a trademark question. Let us assume if you go into a country, a first user country, and you pick a distributor and you commence selling your product under trademark X and he advertises the product as trademark X and he sells it under the X trademark, but he doesn't apply for registration. He is using that mark as your agent if he is your distributor, and therefore I think the courts would uphold the fact or recognize the fact that you are the lawful owner of the mark and it is being used by the distributor merely as your agent.

Now, you recall earlier I attempted to make a distinction between a distributor and a foreign manufacturer who manufactures the product for his own account but uses your trademark. There, of course, you have to have a license agreement. 

Mr. SCHWARTZ. Thank you.

Mr. WILSON. I think there has been one common denominator in our discussions this afternoon, namely, the intimate relationship of our Governments, both the United States and foreign. It reminds me of a little story I ran across in the Middle East a few years ago. 

It seems on the occasion of the opening of the Kaiser-Frazier operation in Israel, the management thought it would be a splendid idea to present the first car off the line to the Prime Minister, and so as a surprise to him in connection with the opening ceremonies, the great man was up there and his cabinet was ranged alongside and a very nice address of welcome was given to the general manager and he responded in kind, and at the close of his remarks, he turned and said, "Now, Mr. Ben-Gurion, I should like on behalf of our company, recognition of all the kindnesses and courtesies we have received here, to present you with the first car off the line." There was a pause for a moment, and the great man got up and he said, "Unfortunately, sir,

I have a strict rule for myself and my cabinet that we never accept favors of any kind, and I am afraid therefore it would be quite impossible for me to accept any kind of gift." in The general manager rose to the occasion and said, "Well, sir, we wouldn't for a moment want you to violate official policy, and we quite understand how this must be. Nevertheless, perhaps the niceties of the occasion would be preserved if you permitted us to sell you the first car off the line for a shilling."

Well, there was a moment's hesitation and it seemed as though this was going to be acceptable, and the Prime Minister fumbled for a shilling and the smallest he could come up with was a two-shilling piece, and then the cabinet fumbled around for some coins and most of them came up with paper. Nobody had the necessary coin, the coin of the necessary size.

So after this fumbling had gone on for a moment, Mrs. Ben-Gurion leaned forward and tapped her husband on the shoulder and said in a loud stage whisper, "David, give the man 2 shillings and take two cars." [Laughter.] 

Mr. HOOD. We have had more than 2 shillings worth today, I am sure.

Again may I say if you have any suggestions that might help us, we would be more than happy to entertain them.

STATEMENT BY WALTER A. SLOWINSKI 3 

Mr. SLOWINSKI. The smaller company going abroad to sell, license, or manufacture is faced with domestic and foreign tax problems. identical to those encountered by larger corporations. To bring these problems within focus and to minimize the time and expense of tax planning, it is most important that the executive of the smaller company take a keen personal interest in foreign and domestic taxation. 

Frankly, the direct and indirect taxes imposed on business operations abroad make it clear to businessmen that taxes become as much a part. of the cost of goods sold as direct labor or material. To overlook tax costs is to lose a portion of the ultimate profit available for reinvestment.

With this consideration in mind, the foregoing recommended reading list has been prepared to give the executive of a small company a summary of the tax provisions which may apply to his company's. situation as it prepares to go abroad. 

Much more tax planning must be done, however, in terms of the foreign tax costs in each country in which selling, licensing, or manufacturing operations are to be conducted. In each country the local laws on corporations, taxation, customs, tariffs, labor, social security, foreign exchange, etc., must be carefully analyzed to be certain the private investment will be welcomed and will have a real opportunity to be profitable.

Each of the countries' laws on taxation will differ, since in many instances the rules of tax jurisdiction will differ. Commonwealth countries, such as the United Kingdom and Canada, use "management and control" as the test for tax jurisdiction; the United States uses "citizenship or residence"; other countries such as Venezuela and 

Panama use "territoriality," while India and Pakistan employ the test of "economic connection." Space here does not permit a full analysis of the differences in these concepts of taxation, but for an explanation the reader may be interested in a new legal textbook entitled, “Taxation of Foreign Income" by Bittker and Ebb, published by the international legal studies program at the Stanford Law 

School, Stanford University, Stanford, Calif.

On each specific project the most effective international tax planning can take place only where lawyers carefully schooled in the tax systems of the United States and the particular foreign countries join to mesh the tax systems of these countries and plan a program of minimum tax cost to the investor. The foreign lawyers now also trained and practicing in the United States as well as the U.S. lawyers now practicing abroad bring this kind of expertise to the problems of the private investor. The U.S. Commerce Department and American 

Embassies abroad are prepared to offer a list of such lawyers to U.S. businessmen. 

3 Mr. Slowinski's lecture to the seminar was delivered off-the-record. statement was furnished to the committee by Mr. Slowinski subsequently. 

This summary™

FINANCING FOR OPERATIONS ABROAD LECTURE BY MICHAEL A. LUCKEY, INTERNATIONAL DIVISION, CHEMICAL BANK NEW YORK TRUST Co., NEW YORK, N.Y.ª OUTLINE I. TYPES OF FINANCING AVAILABLE 

A. Local financing through correspondent banks: 

1. Ordinary financing. 

2. Guaranteed by Time Deposit in an American bank with interest payments and transfer of funds in event of default arrangements. 

3. Long term lenders such as insurance companies, housing trusts, etc. 

B. Financing from the United States direct to subsidiary or joint venture with 

U.S. parent guarantee.

C. Use of governmental agencies: ICA, investment guarantee program; eximbank; Cooley plan loans; development loan fund, IBRD affiliates offering terms somewhat more liberal than world bank, i.e. International Development Association, International Financing Corp. 

D. Public offering of shares.

E. Direct Government help; foreign government loans, tax concessions, plant and site offerings, accelerated depreciation.

II. PRIVATE SOURCES OF FINANCING 

A. Large U.S. banks-direct financing, usually with parent guarantee. 

B. International finance companies: Advantage chiefly in contacts, prestige and assistance in obtaining loans in local market. Disadvantages are equity participation features when enterprise becomes profitable. Edge Act corporations such as CIF, IBEC, CIIC, DELTEC, etc. 

III. METHODS OF ENTRY IN FOREIGN MARKETS 

A. Short term licensing; export sales to exclusive agent with a view to participation on joint venture basis leading to eventual acquisition; outright purchase; plant building.

IV. CASE HISTORIES 

A. Financing in United Kingdom under board of trade development scheme. 

B. Financing in India through use of the International Finance Corporation.

C. Financing in Australia through local borrowing based on U.S. deposit. 

D. Others. 

V. FOREIGN EXCHANGE RISK 

A. Local participation as a means of minimizing exchange risks and political problems.

B. Medium- and long-term hedging. 

C. Capital repatriation, interest, and dividend payments.

BIBLIOGRAPHY 

"Investing and Licensing Abroad,” a Business International publication ($60 to nonmembers).

Mr. LUCKEY. According to Tom, I have got about 5 minutes. I 

have two approaches to this, the short approach and the long approach. 

I will use the short approach. This is a checklist, just a quick going over of things that anyone approaching any of the Government or private international finance organizations should think of. 

4 Michael A. Luckey is representative, International Division, Chemical Bank New York

Trust Company and assistant secretary and assistant treasurer, Chemical International 

Banking Corporation in New York, N.Y.

Mr. Luckey received a B.S. in economics from LaSalle College in 1941. He took special studies in international relations at Yale University from 1947 to 1948. From 1951 to 1955 he was an officer in the U.S. Air Force. He received a fellowship in international trade law, at Frankfurt University, Frankfurt/Main, Germany in 1957-1958.

The reason I speak of this checklist: Our people in Chemical Bank and some other people I have talked with tell me that there are many, quite often even rather large, corporations that come on some international financing project and have overlooked some of the simplest things, not the least of which is having someone go to the area where they expect to expand, develop, or start anew. 

Of course, the very simplest approach is to make certain that the right country and the right area are chosen for the type of product that you have.

A preliminary checking saves a lot of more detailed inquiry later. 

We had one instance-I forget exactly what the situation was; something like a plan to sell-in an area where in effect, I think they had something like under 1,000 miles of paved roads a type of truck these people expected to first export and then, later, manufacture in that particular area, which just couldn't be economically used for the next 10 or 15 years.

The Department of Commerce, of course, has a terrific amount of information on market conditions, loans available locally, by Government banks and so forth, labor situations and attitudes in the area that you are going into.

The State Department, of course, is excellent for what are called country reports, dealing with such things as climatic conditions, attitude toward foreign participation and control, and so forth. 

The investment guarantees of the International Cooperation Ad ministration, these guarantees run to convertibility risk, expropriation risk, and war, should be examined. At the present time approximately 35 underdeveloped areas and dependencies of industrialized countries are covered by these risks. In all of the 35 countries you can get convertibility, exchange convertibility coverage, and in almost all of them, expropriation can be covered by an ICA guarantee. And about a third of them, I would say, cover war risks. 

One of the big problems, of course, is the type of penetration you expect to make. There are three major types. The first one, of course, is licensing and in many instances it is much better than having your own plant, subsidiary, branch, whatever it is, in a particular area. It is sometimes more profitable to license. 

Philco in South America is an excellent example of the advantages of licensing. After study they found it was much more profitable for them to license to a recognized radio manufacturer down there and send their own people to assist in the manufacture than it would be to build a plant.

Joint venture has certain problems. The first one, of course, is control. In any joint venture you must always find a workable partner. Another is nationalistic attitudes. 

Mexico is an excellent example. Now any American concern going into Mexico should be prepared to have 51 percent of the equity portion of the investment locally held. 

Du Pont arranged this very beautifully. When they moved into 

Mexico they very early got the Banco de Commercio as their 51-percent partner. The Banco de Commercio, in turn, furnished a portion of the financing of this particular Du Pont penetration into Mexico.

Another problem of joint venture is that it is normal for American concerns in the early part of their development to retain a large share of their earnings. When you have joint venture, you may run into the problem of local people wanting dividends fast. I think one of the best stories about that: A friend of mine years ago was all lined up for investment in China, before it became Communist, in a soft drink line.

This fellow had lived in China and got everything fixed up with the New York principal. They sent some people out to do a market survey, and everything went well until at the end of the negotiations. 

The Chinese who were putting up most of the money asked him what return they could realize, and he said he figured the return would probably be about 8 or 10 percent for the first year or two and then possibly it would be a bit better.

The Chinese were quite happy. This was their attitude: They were happy at this 8 or 10 percent, and he noticed they were figuring something on a piece of paper. They wanted to know how much of that money they could get back to the United States. They mentioned some astronomical figure. It turned out throughout all these negotiations that the Chinese were figuring on an 8 to 10 percent per month return on their money, and my friend was thinking of 8 to 10 percent a year. 

The whole question just dropped right there. They removed their offer to finance. Simple things but it is surprising how many people coming to our international financial organization, Chemical International Finance and I am sure the other banks and international finance outfits, run into the same thing; lack of awareness of problems such as local and U.S. taxes which are involved. A quick look into that is a necessity.

Another aspect of dealing with the business of having a subsidiary or a joint venture is any possible antitrust violation. I suppose one of the leading instances of that is the famous Timken Roller Bearing case where their subsidiary abroad-where Timken in the United States and the subsidiary abroad made an agreement on price, it later being considered a violation of antitrust. As a matter of fact, I think 

Timken, since then, has circumvented this by having branches instead of subsidiaries. The theory, of course, being that if you have a branch, it is definitely a part of the parent corporation. If you are going to have a violation of the antitrust laws you have got to have two companies. When you have a branch, of course, you don't have two individual entities.

An understanding of the local personnel problems, of course, includes, naturally, the ability to get competent management. We have a number of instances of people going abroad-as a matter of fact, I can even tell you some stories about an old bank-that even up to the very last minute, do nothing about getting competent management. Usually a citizen from the United States or some industrialized developed country is required in these new situations, particularly in underdeveloped areas.

Naturally, it is often difficult to get people. Everybody wants to go to France or to England, but very few people seem to want to go to Africa or some of the southeast Asian countries.

The American agencies abroad on the scene, in some areas the American Chamber of Commerce, American business groups, are often very helpful. I can't think of one instance where we have had occasion to write these people where we haven't gotten very satisfactory replies. 

One of the problems that I think should be avoided, and people in our International Financial Corp. look for this all the timesome countries, of course, the United Kingdom particularly, will warn you of it in advance and that is the business of going abroad with large debt; in other words, with the equity low and your debt with fixed debt charges extremely high.

The International Finance Corp.-as a matter of fact, in a publication just put out about a year or so ago called attention to the fact that they will not make a loan to anyone where the fixed carrying charges on debt exceed 50 percent of the equity. We had a case in the United Kingdom a short time ago where everybody thought they had the approval of the Bank of England to move in, and the Bank of England, in looking at the structure, suddenly came to the conclusion that there was too much local financing. They insisted upon more equity.

At this point the thing had been going on for approximately a year or a year and a half. The plant was just about ready to be built. 

Management was hired. As a matter of fact, in this case management was on the scene.

The Bank of England said no, you are doing too much local financing; the point there being that they are afraid of having something get started and if it doesn't succeed, then the local people are stuck and the outside people get out.

The credit information department of your local banks, either themselves if you happen to be with a rather large bank, or through their New York correspondents, are very helpful in getting information on a proposed partner or a proposed licensee. That is naturally very important. Quite often we have people come to us knowing very little about the people they are dealing with. They will go some place, either in Europe, in Latin America, or some place in southeast Asia, and we ask what they know about their contact, and a good bit of it is just hearsay; even in cases of people who have traveled through the area. Several good credit reports on anybody is a necessity in something like this. 

Incidentally, I meant to start out with this. Business International, an organization which you are all familiar with, I am sure, publishes every year-the last one was published in December 1960an excellent booklet entitled "Investing and Licensing Abroad." It costs $60 if you are not a subscriber to Business International. What it costs if you do subscribe, I don't know. Possibly you get it free.

But it covers about 38 or 40 countries and is broken down into such topics as incentives, labor supply, transfer, repatriation of capital, transfer of dividends, taxation, capital sources, etc. I think it is a must for anybody intending to go abroad, particularly any of the areas covered by this publication. [Applause.] 

Mr. WILSON. I am sure there will be many questions for both of our distinguished speakers, and at this point the floor is open for questions.

Question. A question for Mr. Slowinski, sir.

All during this session I have been discussing with one of the accountants here whether or not a copyright is a capital asset and, as such, would the leasing of a copyright to a foreign publisher be possibly subject to capital gains treatment? 

Mr. SLOWINSKI. Copyrights are a little different from trademarks, as you know, and they are carved out of the definition of "capital asset" by section 1221 (3) of the Internal Revenue Code. 

Copyrights are therefore different in terms of selling or licensing than trademarks, patents, or technological data. Incidentally, I want to say this man to my right is no stranger. This is Col. Mike Luckey. 

He used to be the pilot for Chiang Kai-shek. He has had a lot of experience which you people would do well to ask him about at coffee. time. He is a West Point graduate and Air Force alumnus. 

Question. Mr. Luckey, you mentioned that in going into Mexico in particular that you have to have a joint venture. Does this mean you cannot get licensing in Mexico? 

Mr. LUCKEY. No. The thing is if you want to open a subsidiary in

Mexico, the way it stands now, more than half of the ownership should be Mexican. In some product lines it is a legal requirement. 

Many countries have that. As a matter of fact, my personal guess is that is going to be in underdeveloped countries the trend more and more. As a matter of fact, many large American corporations are anticipating it.

The Philippines is an excellent example. For nationalistic reasons they are insisting more and more on local participation. 

I am not familiar with the latest laws of the Philippines on this situation, but my guess is that eventually they are going to have a law that will specify that so much of the participation must be local. 

Question. Mr. Slowinski, you said there were 23,000 American corporations operating overseas. Do you know if a list is available, and, if so, where?

Mr. SLOWINSKI. The list is not in existence. However, we understand the Internal Revenue Service estimates that 23,000 U.S. businesses have some foreign ties such as exports, licensing, manufacturing, or equity investment.

Mr. WILSON. I have one question to address to our panel considering the delightful simplicity of the Spanish law in terms of tax exposure under Spanish theory.

Is there appearing by any means a satisfactory guideline by which one can determine where the sovereignty of the United States may end as far as the long reach of the Commissioner of Internal Revenue is concerned?

Mr. SLOWINSKI. No.

Mr. WILSON.

for many years his name has become almost synonyous with the regulatory activities of the Departent of Commerce in relation to foreign commerce.

The diversity of the operation is something that he is best qualified to speak on himself. I shall not attempt to enumerate it now, but it gives me very great pleasure to introduce Mr. John C. Borton, Acting
