HOW THE BUREAU OF FOREIGN COMMERCE CAN HELP DEVELOP PROFIT OPPORTUNITIES FOR YOU LECTURE BY JOHN C. BORTON, ACTING DIRECTOR, BUREAU OF FOREIGN COMMERCE, U.S. DEPARTMENT OF COMMERCE, WASHINGTON, D.C.5 OUTLINE OF LECTURE DISTRIBUTED IN ADVANCE BY MR. BORTON 

An exchange of helpful ideas and information concerning international trade between business and Government is helping new firms to enter the export business.

Improved exchange position in Japan and major Western European countries has opened new profit opportunities for U.S. exporters. Discriminatory restrictions against dollar purchases have almost been eliminated throughout Europe. 

Emerging African countries offer new trading opportunities. 

The Department of Commerce offers new exporters the following specialized services either through the head office in Washington or through the 33 Commerce field offices located in principal trading cities: 

1. Statistics on exports and imports by commodity and by destination. 

2. Evaluation of market potential through review of specific export opportunities. 

3. Current, practical information regarding new developments in international trade through the publication, Foreign Commerce Weekly. 

4. Published lists of established firms, country by country, handling specific lines of merchandise. 

5. A specialized service to identify agents abroad especially qualified to handle the individual exporter's line. 

6. "World Trade Directory Reports," a brief analysis of the past business experience, financial backing, and reliability of individual foreign firms. 

7. A series of booklets on how to operate an export business.

5 John C. Borton is Acting Director of the Bureau of Foreign Commerce, U.S. Depart ment of Commerce. He graduated from Haverford College with a B.S. degree. 

After graduating from college, Mr. Borton spent a year of travel and work in Europe and returned to the United States to enter a large firm of manufacturers and wholesale distributors of building supplies and industrial equipment. In 7 years he rose from stock clerk to branch manager. For 3 years, he was a partner in private business in 

Washington, D.C., acting as international trade consultant. In his 23 years of service with the Federal Government he has served with the Federal Alcohol Administration, Bureau of Internal Revenue, War Production Board, and the Office of Lend-Lease Admin istration. For the last 10 years he has been devoting full time to the administration of the Export Control Act. After serving in the capacity of Director of the Office of Export 

Supply, Bureau of Foreign_Commerce, in the U.S. Department of Commerce, he rose to his present position of Acting Director of the Bureau of Foreign Commerce. 

Mr. Borton is the author of numerous articles, and is coauthor of the book, "Private 

Trade in Foreign Trade." He was coleader of a team of Government experts on inter. national trade which visited the United Kingdom in 1953; he was leader of the U.S. trade mission to Italy in 1957; and during the last 2 years he has attended three international conferences on problems of effective multilateral strategic trade controls.

8. The facilities of trade missions to present to foreign businessmen the "business proposals" of U.S. exporters. 

9. Opportunity for discussion with returned trade mission members of the profit opportunities in the country visited by the mission. 

10. Opportunity for the presentation of American products to a foreign marget at minimum expense through the new technique of the U.S. trade center. 

11. Opportunities for selling overseas by participation in U.S. trade exhibitions at international trade fairs. 

12. "World Trade Information Service," a continuing service in three parts designed to inform international traders on basic economic developments affecting international trade on a country-by-country basis. 

13. A "one-stop service" in Washington, D.C., through which advance appointments can be made with the proper officials anywhere in Government regarding an exporter's individual problems. 

14. The cooperation of experienced international traders working through 

33 regional export expansion committees.

In addition to the Government services, trade associations and service organizations, as well as established export firms, are ready and willing to help any firm interested in entering the export market.

Mr. BORTON. I want to make it clear that this morning we are going to talk about export promotion rather than export control. I am leaving my export control hat in Washington, although if you have any questions on that later I will be glad to answer them. But we want to continue this morning with an attempt to solve some of these problems concerning the promotion of exports. I know that you have had helpful suggestions on many of them during the course of these sessions, and I think they illustrate better than anything else the way in which this fraternity of people interested in international trade works together for their mutual benefit, and we like to feel that there is a similar closeworking relationship between the business community and the Department of Commerce.

We have for many years had a program of helping international traders both through our main office in Washington and through our 33 field offices which are located in the principal trading centers of the United States, and when I refer to the Department of Commerce from now on, just bear in mind we are also talking about our field offices and the information is available there just as it is in the headquarters in Washington.

Our office here in New York is on the 61st floor of the Empire State Building, and you will find a list of the addresses and telephone numbers in the literature which appears in a little envelope at the back of your book there.

Now, although we have always had a national policy of encouraging international trade, there is greater emphasis being placed now on the proportion of exports than heretofore, primarily because of our serious position with respect to international payments and the bright hope that increased exports might go a long way toward correcting that imbalance.

So we now have, in addition to the regular routine Department of Commerce interest in promoting trade, a specialized interest in doing what we can to improve our international balance-of-payments position. And, so, we have inaugurated some new methods of approach, some new services about which I want to speak today, and these have been oriented primarily with the objective of encouraging new firms to get into the export business and particularly the smaller enterprises

so we hope that if you have a small business you will call on our services when the occasion warrants.

Well, it may very well be the national policy to encourage exports, but the thing that you are interested in is whether or not this is going to be a profitable venture, and not only profitable for the next year or two but over the long term of the foreseeable future. And, so, I would like to review very briefly some of the factors which make the foreign markets more attractive, and hope they are more profitable right now than they have been for many years.

One of these is the improved exchange position of many of the foreign countries, particularly throughout Western Europe and also in Japan, and as a result of the improvement in their exchange position, and by constant hammering through our Foreign Service and through our international negotiations, there have been really some remarkable reductions in the lists of items with respect to which there were formerly quantitative restrictions when those items originated from the dollar area.

Now no one action has been really dramatic, but there has been a whole series of these over the period of the last year and a half or 2 years, and the cumulative total has made available to us tremendous markets which were formerly very limited if not entirely cut off. 

It is impossible to add these up together and come out with a precise figure, but we have what we believe is a good estimate, that in 1960 about $750 million of our total exports could be attributed to the items on which foreign restrictions were removed during the last year or year and a half, and this year the figures indicate on a commodity-bycommodity and country-by-country basis that these same items are again accounting for a very substantial share of our increase in trade. 

We are still continuing through our negotiators at the State Department to eliminate the final discriminations against dollar imports abroad, and we believe that within the next few years, unless there is a complete change in our international position, these specialized restrictions will have been almost entirely removed. 

But, in any event, they will no longer be a major factor among all the problems of international trade.

Now there is another area that has proven very profitable to some firms, and those are the newly emerging nations, particularly in 

Africa. I think there were 16 just last year alone that broke away from their former ties, and when they break asunder from France or Britain or whoever it is, there is always an upsurge of nationalism and an attempt to seek new sources of supply and new markets. 

There is a golden opportunity to move in, and just because a place like the Congo has been in the public press it does not mean that all of those African areas are chaotic.

On the other hand, we believe that many of them may turn out to be very profitable markets over the long term. And, incidentally, we have a man over there right this minute to look at all those markets, to make a careful survey, who is the kind of fellow who will be available to you back in Washington when you have inquiry on a specific commodity or country in the African area. This is part of our attempt to be informed on the market potential, and that information is for you. 72811-61-- 13

Assuming then, after all of these sessions and with the attractiveness of these foreign markets who want to plunge in, how specifically can the Department of Commerce help you? 

Well, first of all, you may want to start off looking at the statistics to see whether or not firms in your field are already doing a substantial volume of business in a given area, in a given commodity, and those statistics are available at our headquarters or field offices. They are published by the Census Bureau. They are identified by the number FT 410. They come in two parts, 1 and 2. The first part is $5 a year, and the second part is $6 a year. You can get them by writing to the Government Printing Office, Washington 25, D.C., and sending your check. However, you may, of course, consult them free at any of our locations. They will give you a fairly accurate idea of what the market has been, current and historic. 

Then there is another step you might take if you are still undecided even after seeing that the statistics look attractive, and that is to glance for a period of a few weeks at the export opportunities which are published in our publication, Foreign Commerce Weekly. 

There you will see them listed under an appropriate heading and coded so that you can quickly identify the items in which you are interested, and you may get a feel of whether or not there are people in the area which you are exploring indicating an interest in acting as sales agent, and people for prospective licensing agreements or joint ventures.

All of these may not be fruitful but it is worth taking a look to get the feel of what the activity in the market may be.

I want to mention that these opportunities, as we call them, are screened before they are published. They come to us from trade missions, which I am going to talk to in greater detail a little later on. These missions are composed of trained commercial officers of the Foreign Service or experienced businessmen, and during the course of the mission activities they develop a lot of these opportunities which are sent back to us in Washington for publication. Other leads come from our regular established Foreign Service officers. 

They are all screened before they are published, and, although we can't make any guarantee that they are all bona fide, everything has been done that is reasonably possible to assure that before they are published they are bona fide opportunities for somebody to make a profit.

Just to get the feel of how many of these opportunities are coming in, in 1960 from all over the world we published 6,400. These are just the export opportunities, and that represented a 50-percent increase over the number that we published in 1959. That doesn't necessarily mean that business increased by 50 percent, but it is a measure of the increased interest in U.S. supply. 

Six thousand four hundred export opportunities alone in 1960, and so far this year the rate is running a little bit ahead of 1960. So they are flowing through at a very encouraging and steady rate, and somebody is going to make a profit out of them.

These opportunities are listed under what we call the standard industrial classification with which you may be familiar. You can pick out the classification number and tell right away whether or not in any given issue there happens to be an opportunity for the commodity or commodities in which you are interested.

The next step then, assuming your interest has been piqued by this list of published opportunities, would be to find an agent. And here we have what we call a trade list, which was mentioned a little bit earlier this morning. This is a listing of the established firms in a given line of business, country by country, and the list is headed by a brief analysis of the economy of the country, an appraisal of the kind of competition you will get from other countries, and the briefest kind of outline so you can get some appraisal of whether or not this kind of a line will sell in this particular country. 

Now those lists at the present time cost only $1, and it may very well be that after you look through that list and after you correspond with some of the names, you may find that there is nobody on the list who is exactly qualified for your particular needs, and in that event we are in a position to undertake for you what we call a trade contact survey. That is a hand-tailored appraisal of the market for you, done by our Foreign Service. The charge is a nominal $10 although it obviously costs many times that.

You tell us of the nature and scope of the commodities you handle, the kind of information the Foreign Service officer would need to sell your company to a prospective agent. We then beat the bushes until we find two or three firms who are interested prospects, turn the names and banking information over to you, and from there on you take over.

This can very well result in your getting the names of two or three firms. You can have correspondence which may sharpen up one as being the most advantageous, and then, when you make a trip abroad, which is usually the best thing before you finally close, you may have saved yourself a month of time searching around for the proper firm to handle your line.

This is a service that has been used primarily by smaller companies, and, of course, as a time and expense saving operation. 

On the other hand, we cannot promise you very speedy work on these. You have to count on at least 2 months and sometimes a little bit more, because the same officers who are working on this have dozens of other assignments. But if you are thinking of a long-term investment, a matter of 60 days to get a line on the right company affiliations over a period of years is only a minor consideration. I would like to give you an idea of the way in which this service has been utilized, again primarily by business and in recent years. In 

1959 we requested 205 on behalf of U.S. exporters from the Foreign 

Service-205 requests from business in the United States for the Foreign Service to seek out these agents for them. In 1960 the figure jumped to 988, approximately 500 percent, and from January up until today the figure has been 868. In other words, it will almost double the figure of last year.

Now this is a tremendous burden on the Foreign Service, but it is also a tremendous bargain and it is because of this tremendous increase and still no increase in the total appropriation for commercial officers abroad, that a delay inevitably results. Incidentally, the State 

Department has asked for additional funds for the next fiscal year beginning July 1, and we are hoping that they will get sufficient amounts to be able to speed up on this particular service. 

Then we have another series called the World Trade Directory Reports. These are roughly the equivalent of a Dun & Bradstreet report

on a foreign firm. We have half a million of them in our files. One buck each is what they cost, and if we haven't got the name of the firm in which you are interested, we will ask for a report from the Foreign 

Service. 

Again there is some delay, but there are half a million immediately available for you. So you can get a line on the credit of established firms, and we hope you will find them very useful to you. 

Well, let us assume then that you have an agent that is satisfactory; the credit report rating looks good. You still have problems of finance, how to pack and so forth. We have what we call a "how-todo series. How you go about it. And again those are available at headquarters or our field offices. I won't identify them, but they are listed in the little, I think it is green, folder in your kit there which lists not only the Buureau of Foreign Commerce publications but also publications of the Government. 

But they are there and we won't have time to identify them this morning. If you have any questions on them we will be glad to cover them in greater detail at our field offices or later this morning. 

Now there is another technique available to you that has proven very successful, and that is a business proposal really sold for you by one of our trade missions. What you do is to outline what your company is interested in; identify the country to which you want to sell; enclose your catalog, your price sheets, send them in to the

Director of the trade mission, and he and his colleagues offer that proposal to the businessmen in the country to which the trade mission is accredited. 

There is a lot of advance publicity in which the specific proposals are identified very briefly by number, and if, as the trade mission travels around the country (I was in France, for instance, last fall and we got to a city like Lyons where there were 70 people who came in who had seen these proposals in France and wanted to pull the file and look at them) it gets the name of a firm who is interested, it is sent to the firm in the United States which has sent us its business proposal, and then they get together with the man in France, let us say.

Well, again, just some quick figures. We have had 13 trade missions that have used these business proposals in 1960 and 1961 in 26 foreign countries, and we had over 2,000 of these proposals. This is the cheapest way of getting your proposition abroad, and really the trade mission man is acting as your supersalesman; 2,000 of them, of which more than half were used with individual firms. Some of these turned out to be duds, but over half of them were presented and a contact was made. And many of these, of course, had several different firms who were interested. A total of 1,500 different firms showed an interest in these proposals.

Our Foreign Commerce Weekly contains advance notices of these trade missions, and the deadline dates for filing your business proposals.

There is another way in which you can utilize these trade missions. 

I wish I had a little more time to tell you about these because they are a wonderful tool for going into business. 

When these men come back we have debriefing sessions; we've had maybe half a dozen in New York. Unfortunately, these meetings are

not well attended. We draw these mission members together from all over the country. We spend a day in which each fellow puts on a little song and dance about his findings of his particular field in the country which he has been visiting, and then there is an opportunity for individual consultation afterward so that you have a chance to get a firsthand report from an experienced businessman on the lines of goods in which he has specialized training. And, of course, there is no charge for that kind of service at all. You can make an appointment in advance for one or more of these experienced men when they come back. They are glad to help.

Again it is a wonderful illustration of the way the experienced men are willing and able to help the other firms in the business. 

One other activity that is just blossoming this week, and that is the trade center. We opened up our first one on Monday of this week in 

London, and the first reports we have had in the last 2 days are that it was a smash hit. I am a little leery about some of the figures we are getting back of business being booked, but one firm reported it already had signed up $70,000 of business. Another man, a little optimistic, I think, said he thought he already had an agent lined up for business that should get him $1 million a year in the United Kingdom.

I would like to wait a little while and winnow these things out, but our man who is in charge of the center cabled us yesterday that in an hour he counted 60 buyers coming in there to look at this display. 

The United States leases the building, makes no charge to the exhibitors. You, as an exhibitor, would pay the freight and insurance to get your goods there. We set up the display for you. You have your own salesmen on the job. It is worked through your local U.S. trade association, and if a firm doesn't have its own salesman on the spot, the trade association will book the order.

It looks as though we hit on a wonderful scheme, particularly for new firms, because we will get the agent for you if you want, or put you in line with prospective agents as I have indicated on this trade contact survey, and this is not selling to the consumer; this is selling to buyers to help establish an agent for the long-term pull. 

There were 61 exhibitors at this fair that opened in London on Monday; 33 were firms not previously in business with the United Kingdom, and 43 out of the 61 were small firms. 

We are going to continue this program. We have already negotiated a contract for one in Bangkok, and we are hoping to open another one during the current fiscal year in Europe, one in Latin America, and one in Africa. We have high hopes because we think it is the most effective way, judging from the way things are going at London, in helping new firms get started with a minimum of expense.

There is another opportunity through our fairs. This is not going to be as practical save for larger firms. So I will pass this over quickly.

Just as an illustration, at the recent fourth Tokyo International 

Trade Fair 20 American exhibitors booked $3,079,000 in new sales. 

That is a nice piece of business. .of.

There is one thing about international trade that you can be sure That is, that it is going to change always. There are always new 72811-61--14

problems, new challenges, and here we try to help you by having a service which we call our World Trade Information Service. 

There is a sample again in your kit there. It is in three parts. 

There is one part on economic reports, one on operations reports, and one on statistics. They are $6 per annum per part. These contain the basic background information; not current hot trade news, but it ought to give you the kind of information that you need to appraise a market and understand how to operate profitably in it. 

One other final thing. We have what we call a one-shot service in Washington. If you send a note to our friend Mr. Herbert Van

Blarcom, your fellow colleague here, he will arrange in advance a meeting or series of meetings not only in the Department of Commerce but anywhere else in Government, with the right person who can answer your particular problem so you don't have to go floundering around through the bureaucracy for several days to try to search out the right man. We will search him out for you if you tell us in advance what your problem is. No charge.

Well, this is an exciting business, and there are lots of people who are ready, willing, and able to help. Not only people in industry but people in the Government. And we just hope you will use our services the way the established firms do. We hope they will be profitable to you, and as taxpayers they are designed for you. I hope you get your money's worth. 

Thank you very much. [Applause.] 

Mr. WILSON. Thank you, Mr. Borton.

Export-Import Bank, at the conclusion of the presentation by the gentlemen from the Export-Import Bank.

It now gives me very much pleasure to introduce Mr. Charles M.

He is here with us this morning in association with Mr. Charles E. Houston who has for a considerably longer period been a member of the staff of the Export-Import Bank in Washington.

At this time a review by perhaps our leading foreign trade lending institution in the United States, by these distinguished gentlemen, will be most timely to us as they give us their particular views with respect to the applicability of the Export-Import Bank and its facilities, not only to large business but to the problems of smaller American business. 

Gentlemen, Mr. Meriwether.

THE EXPORT-IMPORT BANK OF WASHINGTON REMARKS OF CHARLES M. MERIWETHER, MEMBER, BOARD OF DIRECTORS, EXPORT-IMPORT BANK, WASHINGTON, D.C. 

Mr. MERIWETHER. Gentlemen, the President of the Export-Import 

Bank and the other three Directors have asked me to express to you their appreciation and the Bank's appreciation for the invitation you have extended to myself and Mr. Houston to be here today. 

I have been a member of the Board of Directors for approximately 3 months now. My background has been in municipal finance and not in international banking.

I thought in dealing with a group of such widely varied and astute gentleman in commerce that it would be appropriate for me to have accompany me today, Mr. Houston, who is quite experienced in this field. He is a senior economist at the Export-Import Bank with varied and wide experience. Mr. Houston graduated from Drury:

College in Springfield, Mo., and got his master's degree at Clark 

University. He also attended New York University. He was in. 

World War II in the Mediterranean and Pacific theaters in mine sweeping.

He has been a senior economist at our Bank for some 5 or 6 years: now. He has traveled extensively in both India and the Philippines, and is well acquainted with both business and government personalities in these countries.

He has given a great deal of attention in particular to the progress and problems involved in the massive development programs now being undertaken in India. He was a member of the Bank group that negotiated the first significant credit to India of $150 million. 

That was back in 1958. He was a member of the Bank team which went to India to get the credit set up and operating at that time. He works closely with representatives of the Government of India and ErIndian and American businessmen in implementing the above-mentitioned credits and other loans to India, all having a bearing on the export business of the United States. He has dealt with matters in the Philippines as well in this capacity. He has been serving as a liaison officer between Export-Import Bank and the Development Loan Fund. He attends meetings of the Development Loan Fund and keeps the officers of our Bank generally informed regarding the activ ities of the Development Loan Fund and other lending agencies of the Government. 

He represents the Export-Import Bank at our meetings with the National Advisory Council, which coordinates Government activities in foreign aid.

Charles M. Meriwether is a member of the Board of Directors of the Export-Import 

Bank of Washington in Washington, D.C. He received his education at the University of 

Tennessee and at Cumberland University College of Law, where he was awarded the 

LL.B. degree in 1933. 

Mr. Meriwether worked in the field of insurance law and insurance management in 

Memphis, Tenn., during the 1933-42 period, served in the U.S. merchant marine during 

1942-43, and in 1944 entered the retail and wholesale drug business as part owner of the 

Dewberry Drug Co., in Birmingham, Ala. In 1958 he become director of finance for the

State of Alabama. Other State of Alabama offices he has held are those of president of the educational authority, president of the highway authority, chairman of the investment committee for the teachers' retirement fund and employees' retirement fund, and director of the State board of adjustment. He was named member of the Board of 

Directors of the Export-Import Bank of Washington in March 1961. 

Mr. Meriwether has been active in civic and fraternal organizations in Birmingham.

Mr. Houston, in the opinion of those who have been in our Bank longer than I, as well as myself, is perhaps the best qualified gentleman available in the United States today to discuss the subjects that we have at hand this morning. For that reason I brought him here, and I now introduce him, gentlemen. 

Thank you. [Applause.]

LECTURE BY CHARLES E. HOUSTON, ECONOMIST, EXPORT-IMPORT BANK, WASHINGTON, D.C.' 

Mr. HOUSTON. Well, that was quite a remarkable person Mr. 

Meriwether described. I hope I have an opportunity to meet him someday and get a little better acquainted with him. 

I have always thought that when someone walks into a room with a group of people and stands up before them and begins to make a talk or speech of some kind, that he owes some accounting or explanation of how it is that he got there, to excuse or justify the position in which he finds himself. And, first, I would like briefly to do that.

Down in the hill country where I grew up, at the time I grew up there, we did not have a lot of radios and televisions and fast automobiles and paved highways, but, rather, made our own amusements and extracurricular activities from our own resources. 

A very favorite thing at this time and place was to organize little groups around the local schoolhouses to have debates and make speeches and put on plays, and people often got very much interested in that kind of activity.

There appeared in my community one day a kind of itinerant teacher, what they called in those days a teacher of elocution, and he signed up several students who were interested in the kind of things I have described. He used the teaching technique which was drawn, I believe, from ancient history, from the old teacher who had his students put pebbles in their mouths until they could learn to speak clearly. He gave a slightly more modern touch to that same idea, and he issued each one of his students a handful of small marbles, and from day to day as they learned to speak with those marbles he would allow them to remove one. And when the student had finally lost all his marbles he was considered to be qualified to appear before groups and speak in public. [Laughter.] 

So I did feel that some kind of explanation or excuse was indicated. 

More seriously, in speaking about the Bank and its activities, realizing that surely all or the great majority of this group is at least reasonably well acquainted with the Bank and its activities, I would seek to do two things: one, to make a few very brief comments to restate in summary fashion the nature of the Bank as an institution but not labor the point at all since, as I say, I am sure that you are generally acquainted with it. But 2 or 3 minutes about it nevertheless.

The first point I mention-and I mention it because I do encounter some misunderstanding on the point-the Bank is wholly an agency Charles E. Houston is associated with the Export-Import Bank of Washington, Washington, D.C. He is a graduate of Drury College, Springfield, Mo., and did additional study at Clark University in Worcester, Mass., and at New York University.

Mr. Houston served in the U.S. Navy during World War II and has held positions of 

Chief Project Analyst and Chief of the Investment Guaranty Division of the International 

Cooperation Administration. His association with the Export-Import Bank began in 1956.

ea of the U.S. Government. Since it engages in what we may generally call commercial-type activities, I not infrequently meet people who believe that it is in some way a quasi-private or mixed entity. 

It is not. It is wholly an agency of the U.S. Government, organized in a corporate form of organization, having a board of directors as its governing body, of which Mr. Meriwether is, of course, a member. 

Second, as to the purpose of the Bank, again to restate it for the sake of emphasis, the statute says that the aims and purposes of the Bank shall be to aid in financing and to facilitate exports and imports of the United States.

Two things flow from that fundamental statutory statement, I think. One, it helps to explain why the Bank has clung so closely throughout its activities to a policy of what has come to be called tied procurement, that is, in the United States with the funds it makes available. And the other statement is more or less a corollary or outgrowth of the first one-the Export-Import Bank is not in its purpose and its day-to-day goals an aid agency. People do sometimes tend to think of us as part of that complex of operations of the Government whose aim, purpose, and goal is to provide assistance to foreign countries, and, while we would argue very strongly that our activities certainly serve an extremely useful purpose in that direction, we do try to draw the distinction that we are not doing it for the fundamental purpose of aiding the recipient country. 

We are doing these things to facilitate and assist the foreign trade of the United States, either immediately and directly, or indirectly and, more subtly, over a longer term.

The Bank's resources consist of $1 billion of stock issued and held by the U.S. Treasury, and an authority to borrow from the Treasury an additional $6 billion, so that the Bank's total resources are $7 billion. Even in this day and age it is a respectable sum of money. 

Now, having, I hope, discreetly and not too obtrusively boasted a - little bit about the Bank and the scale of its operations, let me, as it were, immediately turn the page and point out that the fact that we do dispose of rather substantial resources absolutely does not mean that we are only interested in big deals and in dealing with large companies who have large transactions.

I have here, for example, a copy of a little report which the Bank prepares monthly, and this is the most recent one, recent transactions which the Bank has in one way or another assisted or participated in, and going down the line of some of those there is a sale in Lebanon by the Brock Desk Co., of Pomona, Calif., of $56,500. Here is a sale in Thailand by Henry R. Johnson & Son, of New York, $27,500; and ef the Abbot Machinery Co., Argentina, $15,000; Caterpillar Tractor, Argentina, $12,500. am: 86. add Positi term an ir It is not a little company but it is a small transaction. 

Here is Voltarc Tubes, quite a small company, indeed, up in Norwalk, Conn., $26,500. And, without laboring the point, I see a number here as far down the line as $9,000, $11,000, $13,000. 

So, as I say, let me reiterate and emphasize as strongly as I possibly can that the Bank does not focus only upon the General Electrics and 

Westinghouses and Armcos.

Well, so much, briefly, to describe what kind of institution we are. 

I want to proceed then for the balance of the time to describe the spe-

cific programs which the Bank has available to carry out the stated purpose of aiding exports and imports. 

Actually, of course, we do substantially nothing in the import field. 

Let us talk about exports. This tends to get not really complicated perhaps, but with a variety of approaches or variety of programs one needs to take them rather carefully in some sequence or the thing does get confused. So let me state first that the form of assistance which the Bank provides may be either in the form of guarantees of one kind or another, or, secondly, it may be in the form of financing specifically.

So there are those two basic roads that one may travel in reaching the goal of providing assistance_guarantees or financing specifically.

So with those two basic tools I turn next to the categories of transactions which one may deal with, and essentially there are three. 

First, the category of short-term transactions, and by short term we mean up to 180 days. Secondly, the medium-term category which means from 180 days to 5 years, with a little footnote that in the case of jet aircraft we go up to 7 years.

I take it that there are probably no representatives of Boeing or Douglas or Convair present. So we don't need to labor the exceptional case of the jet aircraft.

And, thirdly, what we identify as project credits where the terms may range very widely, indeed.

So, having established the three basic categories of transactions, short term, medium term, and project cases, let me, if I may, turn then to the short-term situation in somewhat more detail. This, by the way, is a rather new area for the Bank. We have until quite recently not engaged in this kind of thing to any extent at all. 

What we go back to here primarily is the mandate of the President of the United States a few months ago that the Bank bestir itself and undertake to provide facilities for American exporters which at least equal those available to exporters in other countries. 

What we have available at the moment then in the 180-day category is a political risk guarantee arrangement. 

I bear down on the words "available at this moment" because we expect quite soon to come out with a further announcement which will describe a program which will offer credit coverage, or, taken all together, of course, a comprehensive coverage package for transactions of this kind. That has proved to be an exceedingly intricate thing to get completely worked out, to get the private insurance companies and banks lined up with us on it, but I would hope in the next few weeks that a further announcement will appear. Let us focus then on what is available at the moment, still in the 180-day category; a political risk guarantee. 

Now I am going to have to speed up here and talk a little faster or I am not going to get finished. So I am going to have to run through some of these points fairly speedily.

There are five categories of what we identify as political risk which can be covered under this policy. The first of those is the transfer or convertibility risk which is, of course, a familiar one. The buyer has the local currency in hand. He is able to deposit it. The problem is not his credit problem at all but the problem of conversion of that local currency into dollars.

So that is No. 1, transfer risk.

Secondly, laws, regulations, decrees, interventions, whatever you want to call it, which would have the effect of preventing the import of the purchased goods into the buyer's country. That could be almost any kind of situation.

Thirdly, and this ties in with the preceding point, the cancellation of a previously valid import license. A man may have a valid import license but something may happen in between. 

Fourthly, loss resulting from war, rebellion, revolution, civil commotion, all that kind of thing, to the extent that that kind of risk or loss is not insurable through commercial channels. There is, of course, some coverage of that kind available through private companies, and we do not intervene in their area. We pick up at the point they leave off.

And, finally, of the five categories, the possibility of the expropriation of the goods after they have been moved out. 

So, as you see, what we are aiming at is essentially the kind of noncommercial, noncredit kind of thing which is quite beyond the control of either the buyer or seller. 

The second question-what kind of goods can one cover through this program?

You can cover anything that can be legally exported from the 

United States. 

Third, to what countries can you export and obtain this coverage? 

At the moment-again notice the caveat at the moment to any free world country except Cuba, the Dominican Republic, and the 

Congo. The reasons there, I think, require no comment.

And as world events move it is, of course, quite possible that some of these will disappear from the list while others may appear on it. 

Also, to some countries where we are open for business in a general sense, we are open for business only on a letter-of-credit basis, which is to say the door is not exactly closed and not exactly wide open. It is sort of half open, and one can operate under our program on a letterof-credit basis. 

For example, Bolivia and Haiti. 

How much coverage?

Ninety percent of the value of the order. The value can be the c.i.f. value. But, however the value is established, up to 90 percent of it. 

What are the general requirements that we have in the way of operating here?

Well, the first one I mention is one that has been the source of a great deal of conversation and some difficulty. The phrase has come to be kind of invidious phrase, but it is a handy one, so I use it. It is this business about whole turnover, taking the position if one is going to operate a quasi-insurance kind of thing where he wants to spread his risk and he doesn't have anything very much in the way of actuarial experience to guide him, he cannot operate if he is going to allow others to pick and choose the risk that is assumed.

So we start out with the statement that an exporter who seeks to use this facility would be expected to cover through this program the whole of his business, or, as the phrase has it, whole turnover, excepting-as I it gets kind of intricate-excepting shipments to Canada, which always occupy a little bit of a special spot, and excepting whatever say,

business is done on a letter-of-credit basis. Aside from those two exceptions and a somewhat different one I am about to mention, we would expect the exporter to put under our guarantee program the whole of his export business.

The next exception is the proviso that if the exporter has quite different and clearly separable and clearly distinguishable product lines, then we will undertake to deal with a product line or product category, that one category being on a whole turnover basis, but will allow the exporter to put aside from the coverage of the program some quite different line of goods which he also exports. I think it is quite clear what we are aiming at here, and I will not bear down on it. 

Next the question of cost of coverage of this kind: 25 cents per $100 for the first 30 days or less, 5 cents per hundred for each additional 30 days. It follows, therefore, that $100 coverage for the full 180 days would cost a premium of 50 cents.

Also we have a program or plan by which the exporter when he enters or registers or joins, whatever word you want, this program makes a deposit of 35 cents per $100 on the estimated volume of his coming year's business. He joins, as it were, for a year. 

He can, of course, continue to renew that, and the deposit is refundable if one later draws out of the program and wants his money back. 

Where do you get this coverage?

Let me put it negatively first. You do not get it from the Export- 

Import Bank directly. You get it from your commercial bank. 

Any commercial bank with a foreign department can operate this program directly with the exporter for reasons that I think are probably clear.

We have tried to avoid getting into a tremendous concentration of detailed handling and detailed paperwork in Washington, and tried to establish the program so that the exporter does not have to come to us or deal with us but deals with his bank, and we are standing in the background. However, if the worst should come to pass and the occasion arises when you would file a claim, in that circumstance you would file the claim with the Export-Import Bank, not with the commercial bank that you dealt with in the first place. 

The question probably in the minds of some of you is, well, this is all very well, but does anybody actually have occasion to get any good out of it?

We are at the present time paying off on some guarantees issued in connection with sales in Venezuela and Brazil. The difficulties of those countries over recent months are well known. 

If one is describing not exactly selling, let us say describing anything, a reasonable and proper question is, has anybody else been buying the product?

I have a little tabulation here which comes almost up to the very day that we are here, which indicates that from the time this program took its first faltering and tentative steps a bit more than a year ago, until the last few days, we had issued 267 guarantees of this category covering $176 million of exports, and have 159 banks that are engaged in dealing with this kind of transaction. 

Naturally enough, if one saw some kind of curve or chart he would find a considerable share of that total business concentrated in recent

months as the program has become more widely known. This has been rather well accepted and is receiving fairly wide use. Trouble situations in so many spots in the world would seem to suggest that business might get better.

Well, trying to move along here so that I don't overrun too much the time allotted, I go to the medium-term situation, which is to say the 180-day to 5-year (airplanes at 7 years). There we depart markedly from most of the things I have been saying about the 180-day situation. We go at it on an individual case-by-case basis. 

We have none of this matter of whole turnover and so forth. One can shift from one of these next facilities to the other. It is a very wide open, flexible situation, we think.

First of all in connection with exports in this time category, we offer the same political risk guarantees that I have been describing, and will therefore not repeat that. The terms that we expect to see on an export sale to cover it are the following, that the exporter obtain at least 20 percent cash payment of the transaction; that the remainder be payable in installments semiannually or more frequently than semiannually; and that there be a rate of interest on the outstanding balance of at least 6 percent. Parenthetically, interest up to 

6 percent is covered equally with the principal amount that we are dealing with. We do not cover interest above the 6 percent rate. 

The cost of this particular coverage is three-quarters of 1 percent per annum on the declining balance. 

Where does one go for this kind of coverage? He goes directly to the Export-Import Bank. We do not deal through the commercial banks in this particular category.

Secondly, still in this category of medium term, comprehensive guarantees where we cover not merely the political risks that have been talked about hitherto but the credit risk as well.

Here we are dealing with an amount equal to 85 percent of the financed portion. That is a somewhat slippery statement, but if one takes the typical case where there is a 20-percent downpayment, what it means is that there is 20 percent cash, the exporter is carrying 12 percent of the deal, and the Export-Import Bank is carrying 68 percent on a nonrecourse basis.

The Bank in this kind of case does wish to explore into and be satisfied with the credit standing of the purchaser since we are including the credit risk as a part of the coverage. This costs 12 percent per annum on the declining balance of the amount. 

Next, still in the medium-term category, the so-called standard or old-fashioned exporter credit which is purely and simply a financing arrangement. It isn't tied up in this web of guarantees of one sort or another. The Bank simply says find a credit-worthy purchaser, get a 20-percent downpayment, and we will take 68 percent of the total, and you the supplier take 12 percent, and there we are.

It is, you see, essentially the same thing as the last preceding case except that in one instance we are dealing with a guarantee. In the other we are dealing with the outright purchase of a major part of the notes of a buyer of the goods. 

There is no fee or cost as such here. The Bank is paid in this kind of arrangement by the fact that it owns a fair share of the importer's notes and is receiving the interest that is borne by those notes.

Fourth, still in the medium-term category, is a scheme that was introduced some while back squarely with the aim of trying to speed up, facilitate, and make matters easier and quicker in the moving along of some of these export transactions. Here we are operating through the commercial banks. We are asking them, in effect, to become partners with us in the financing of the exporter transaction kind of cases. And we say there that we will participate in the transaction on the sole reliance of the credit judgment of the commercial bank if the commercial bank will agree to carry for its own risk and account, without recourse on the exporter, the first three semiannual installments if the credit is up to 3 years or the first four semiannual installments when maturity is 3 to 5 years. 

Now clearly two things are being aimed at: one, to speed up and make easy and rapid the whole transaction by removing the Export- Import Bank from the field of examining the credit worthiness of the foreign buyer; and to say "Yes, we will rely on the judgment of the commercial bank if the commercial bank is prepared to back its judgment by taking a significant part of the deal for its own account on a nonrecourse basis."

As I am sure you gentlemen all know very well, indeed, this is a little bit of a departure from the usual commercial bank practice, but it has found pretty good acceptance.

I have been speaking in this first plan of the situation where the exporter would not be carrying any of the credit himself. Cash payment would be made and all of the financed part would be divided between Export-Import and the commercial bank. 

Now there is another plan where, if the exporter and the bank wish themselves to join together and divide a part of it, the exporter will take 15 percent, the bank 10 percent without recourse, and then we will take the balance. But in every case the thrust and force is to speed up the whole thing by getting the judgment of the commercial banks into the picture and suggesting that they back their judgment by a nonrecourse participation.

So, to recap very rapidly indeed the first part of my comments, we have the 180-day political risk guarantee which we hope to expand. 

We have quite a number of windows to deal with the medium-term exporter situation, either political risk guarantee, comprehensive guarantee, the plain old-fashioned exporter credit where it is cash, supplier participation, Export-Import Bank participation, and the arrangement whereby we get the commercial banks in with us. 

On the matter of project credit I have very little time really to go into it and would not really elaborate if I had a lot of time. Those are the situations, of course, where the Bank is dealing directly with a foreign sponsor of some major project, power plant, fertilizer plant, cement plant, what have you, and where the basic notion is the extension of credit by the Bank to the foreign enterprise in order to put it in funds to carry out a program of U.S. procurement. There is not then the direct dealing between the Bank and the American supplier that we have in the previous arrangements. 

I mention it partly to round out a little bit the scope of the kind of things the Bank does and to suggest that, even though you can't always see it, there is a very great deal of business done by U.S. suppliers through that project kind of financing.

Somewhere here I have got just one or two numbers. I am not going to give you a whole lot of numbers. A tabulation here shows that calendar years 1951 to 1960, inclusive, the Bank authorized credits totaling $612 million, that the number of individual allocations or authorizations was 1,654 and we disbursed $4¼4 billion during that I period. One more number. We have an opportunity to brag a little bit.

During calendar year 1960 the Bank did 712 transactions aggregating $897 million. It is estimated-one cannot prove this kind of thing with accounting accuracy that 73,000 export sales were assisted, financed or, as the statute says, facilitated and encouraged through that activity.

So if you have export financing problems it may be that we don't have exactly the answer that you are seeking at the moment, but we certainly are trying to round out a program that tries to pretty well touch all the bases, and we will certainly be most happy to have any of you come in and see us, call us up or write to us and either see if we can help out with the immediate problem or, if we cannot, see if we can help devise means that will face the problem in the future. 

Thank you very much indeed. [Applause.]

A man has to go in all the way in every country or you won't insure one transaction.

Question. Mr. Borton, are the trade fairs that you are just starting publicized in nearby countries as well as in the one in which they are being held?

Mr. BORTON. I think we ought to distinguish between the trade center and the trade fair. You are talking about the trade center, the one we have in London?

It is our plan that at least for the moment the London center shall serve all the Continent, and we are making a definite organized effort to draw in buyers from the Continent to the London center. 

It may very well be that later on if this program succeeds and is expanded that there will be a center somewhere on the Continent, but for the moment the London one will serve that whole area of not only the United Kingdom but Western Europe as well.

Mr. SPANG. Mr. Houston, on the immediate term financing commercial banks, is there any prospect of having that paper be a recourse paper rather than nonrecourse paper? 

Mr. HOUSTON. I lost the point.

Mr. SPANG. For the time being the banks will take the paper without recourse, and are there any prospects for a change that they will have recourse which, of course, will make it much more attractive for them to participate in that field? Mr. HOUSTON. I know of no discussions or plans or ideas in that direction. As I remarked, we feel that there is a reasonable balancing of concepts here. You say to a man, I am willing to put my money on your credit judgment of some third party. It is not unreasonable, I think, to ask him to back his judgment with some of his own resources. 

Mr. WILSON. Mr. SLOWINSKI. Mr. Chairman, I would have in mind in one minute perhaps offering an explanation of a pessimistic statement regarding 

9 William F. Spang, president, Overseas Development Co., Boston, Mass.

the possible loss of U.S. tax deferral over the next few years. This may occur if the attacks continue in certain congressional circles. 

However, it shouldn't for one moment deter a U.S. company in planning its foreign operations so that it pays the minimum taxes worldwide. of When I say worldwide you must realize that taxes become as much your cost of product sold as direct labor and direct material. If your company is not careful, it can walk quickly into situations in Germany and in France and Belgium and the Netherlands where it pays transmission taxes, turnover taxes, sales taxes, excise taxes, most of which are not creditable against the U.S. corporate tax. 

So when we hear the Secretary of the Treasury saying that our income tax rate is comparable to the European income tax rate, you must then determine what are the excise taxes, transmission, capital, turnover taxes which your company will pay and for which your company is not to receive U.S. tax credit. 

Secondly, in terms of the new legislation, I must tell you very frankly that the Commerce Department did a wonderful job in supporting the corporate community. It was probably overruled at the 

White House but it took the campaign that far. 

Thirdly, if the corporate community works closely together on the Senate side as it did before the Ways and Means Committee and presents the true picture to the Senate Finance Committee and to the Senate as a whole, it should receive the full cooperation of those Senators who understand and support our foreign trade policy. 

One last comment about Mr. John Borton and the Commerce

Department. 

These men are most cooperative when you go in for help. For example, if you are considering a joint venture in Nigeria (this is not unlikely) not only are all of the materials he mentioned available to you, but the full resources of the Commerce Department. For example, the complete corporate law of Nigeria plus the nonclassified dispatches are available to you under certain conditions. 

Lastly, Mr. Houston, Mr. Meriwether, and Mr. Borton may agree that lawyers are not always necessary on Washington transactions such as a routine exporter credit at the Export-Import Bank. Nor is it necessary for a small business to retain a lawyer to obtain information from the Commerce Department. In many cases this can be done by the small businessman himself at a saving in time and costs. 

Thank you.

Mr. WILSON. Mr. Luckey, would you care to add a comment at this point?

Mr. LUCKEY. Well, I have 1 minute. 

When I first discussed this thing I spoke with the vice president of our International Finance Corporation, and I asked him what advice

I could give to small businesses thinking of going abroad, and he said: "The first thing I would do is go down to Washington and investigate the public and quasi-public financing organizations and see what I could do with them."

The second part deals with the fact that in many of the situations we have, it is a two-way proposition, that is, public and private financing. I am thinking of something that Chemical International does in

Brazil. The major portion, the loan aspect of it came from a government organization or a quasi-government organization, and some of it came from our own finance corporation.

The third thing, more and more governments today I think I mentioned this when I spoke before-more and more foreign governments today are making local funds available. I am thinking particularly of Europe, and even now we have some South American governments that are making loans in local currency, certainly a big help to a new concern going abroad. 

Mr. WILSON. Thank you, Mr. Luckey. 

May I ask Mr. Van Blarcom now if he has some words of wisdom for us from the Department of Commerce. 

Mr. VAN BLARCOM. I wouldn't say, "ship Santa Fe," but I would say to be very careful in exploring the situation before you take your final step. As Mr. Slowinski says, we are there in Washington to help you, and through our field offices. 

Mr. Slowinski made one comment that I think should be enlarged upon. For several years we have been listing the Foreign Service reports that have come in to us, and they are now averaging somewhere in the neighborhood of 7,000 a year that we list, and the final lists are in the field offices for people who can borrow these reports if they will indicate the titles of those that they wish. 

This has gotten to the point where we are now moving over into a complete indexing operation and we hope by next January to be able to provide you, if for example, you ask for information on advertising electric refrigerators in Australia, we can give you information or give you the reports that bear on that particular item. 

We have taken the marketing mix and we are putting it on punchcards so we can sort out the reports that might be of interest.

Mr. Watts, would you care to add your comments at this point? 

Mr. WATTS.10 Just to remind you that you can speak to the reporter over here who is with Ward & Paul in Washington, or write to Ward & Paul in Washington, if you want part of the transcript of this seminar―those parts of it that are not off the record. She can give you the company address.

There are a number of extremely important questions bearing on world trade before the Congress in this session and the next. One is the tax question which Mr. Slowinski has alluded to, and then there are the questions of better export credit programs.

The Senate Banking and Currency Committee has before it now S. 1379, our commitee's bill which would, among other things, encourage the activities in the area of export credit expansion within 

Export-Import Bank. 

Very probably the Senate Banking and Currency Committee will have before it, before the current session is over, a bill which is already 

10 Raymond D. Watts, counsel, Senate Small Business Committee.

before the House Banking and Currency Committee to set up an 

American Export Credits Guaranty Corporation. This is the National 

Coordinating Committee proposal which Mr. Francis Scafuro is principally identified with. It is my hope that the Senate Banking and 

Currency Committee will have hearings this session or early in the next session on S. 1379 and the National Coordinating Committee proposal, at which the relative merits of these two alternate approaches can be compared, discussed, and debated. 

There are differences of opinion as to which of those is the better way to get the job done. You people who use export credit guarantees, or would like to use them, are the ones whose views will be most welcome, I think, by the committee in answering that question. We don't anticipate that the Export-Import Bank will agree completely with Mr. Scafuro on this, but it is a field that is very hard for Senators who are not experts in this field to make an informed decision on, and I hope, if hearings on the relative merits of the two approaches to the same problem are set up, that you will make yourselves aware of them and contribute whatever knowledge and advice you can. 

Another extremely important question before the Congress now, and one which involves considerable controversey, is whether or not there should be created a foreign professional lifetime career service in the Department of Commerce, a Foreign Commerce Corps, as proposed by the Engle bill, S. 1729. Hearings have already been held on that bill by the Senate Commerce Committee. Those of you who feel that the commercial attachés in the Foreign Service of the State Department are doing an excellent job might usefully make that view known to your Senator. Those who feel that a professional career service in the Commerce Department would be a better way of getting that job done might again usefully make that view known to your Senator, and specifically you could do it by indicating your support for S. 1729, the Engle bill. 11

Our own Senate Small Business Committee is sponsoring a World 

Trade Ship Act. My colleague, Harold Putman, was a principal architect of the bill. This is a proposal to have the United States outfit one or more ships, perhaps the first nuclear merchant ship, to tour the world with exhibits. It would be a sort of traveling U.S. trade center.

Finally, there is S. 610 12 which has already passed Congress and gone to the White House, a proposal to set up an International Travel Division in the Department of Commerce. I think that bill is going to be signed by the President, perhaps today, without any more help from any of us; but if anyone has particular interest in increasing the foreign tourist trade in the United States you certainly ought to put your shoulder to the wheel and push for S. 610, and for a good program in the new Commerce Department office, when it is set up. 

If the Senate Small Business Committee can be of any help to yoù, we would like to hear from you. 

Mr. WILSON. Thank you very much.

Mr. Borton? 

Mr. BORTON. First, I would like to thank Mr. Slowinski for his kind remarks about the Department of Commerce. 

Second, I would like to amplify a little on his remarks about the specialists we have who are available to you, country specialists, men who have lived and traveled abroad in the countries about which they have learned the key things that we think would be most helpful to the prospective businessman interested in trading with that country, and those men are in Washington available to anybody who is interested.

And, finally, there is no such thing as a foolish question. The only thing that is foolish is not to ask a question. And I notice very often at meetings even when people come down to see us, they are afraid to ask a question because they think it might be naive; it might indicate they are not sophisticated in a particular area.

Even the old established firms run into new problems of trade in new areas with which they are not familiar, and I can assure you, no matter what the question may be, we will do the best we can to give you a prompt and intelligent answer, and if we haven't got the answer ourselves we will try to put you in touch with the proper source and authoritative source of the information. 

Mr. WILSON. Thank you, Mr. Borton. 

Mr. Meriwether? 

Mr. MERIWETHER. Thank you.

The really one pertinent point that I am interested in making here is this: Financing of exporter credit through the Eximbank may seem rather complicated in discussions of this type, but in reality it is simple, and if you would convey to your people at the operating level that this financing is available, and if they will inquire from the right party, they will be very pleased to find that in many cases it will be taken from there by the interested parties. 

You have your commercial bank which, if it does not have a foreign department, has a corresponding bank in New York that can get right to the bottom of the subject. You have your Department of Commerce people for advice. You have your transportation people for advice. 

You have us at the Eximbank who are as close as your telephone, and, believe me, that is true; we are just that close and we will give consideration to taking your problem and handling it for you wherever you may have run into a stumbling block. Simplicity is really the order of this thing, and it is our hope that we can overcome some of the complicated methods involved. 

Mr. WILSON. Thank you, Mr. Meriwether. 

Mr. Houston?

Mr. HOUSTON. I have been trying to formulate a couple of sentences and discover that Mr. Meriwether has apparently been formulating the same sentences. So he kind of stole my thunder there.

Let me reiterate what I said just for the sake of emphasis once more, that we are not remote or hard to find or hard to talk to about any problem or proposal or proposition you have. Please call us up or drop in or write or whatever means of communication you might want to use.

This brings me back to Mr. Slowinski's comment that I should have made myself. You don't need a lawyer to talk to the Export-Import

Bank. Certainly it is your privilege to retain one to represent you if you wish to do so, but please do not start on the assumption that that is in some way required or necessary. It simply is not. 

The fact of the matter is that what usually happens when a lawyer comes in, is we immediately begin to ask him questions about the proposal, and he doesn't know the answers and he has to go back, and everything will go a lot more smoothly if the principals are talking directly to each other in the first instance. 

Mr. WILSON. Thank you, Mr. Houston. 

Mr. Breck?

Mr. BRECK. Our title for this seminar has been "Going Abroad-The 

Profit Opportunities of International Business for the Smaller 

Company.

I am sure the presence of all of you here was motivated by the fact that we felt there were opportunities for our companies to make additional profits by going into international trade. And I think there are any number of instances that would indicate to us that this is true. But, in addition to money profit, there is something called psychic income or psychic profit, and I think it would give all you who are not international at the moment a tremendous amount of satisfaction to see your company and your product distributed overseas to points outside the United States. of We have become conscious of more worldwide competition, and we have become conscious of what our competitors are doing overseas, which has proved extremely helpful to us, and I think this is a fringe benefit which might prove helpful to you, too.

All of you who are representatives of manufacturing corporations, or are at the general management level, as a result of being exposed to the many varied topics, the technical subjects and complicated subjects that we have been exposed to in the last few days, I think you all realize that this is a very specialized subject, and the best advice I can give you, something I learned painfully and expensively, is that when you decide that you are seriously going to enter the international market, find yourself someone who is a specialist, who has made this a specialty, and put this in his hands with you retaining, of course, policy decisions. 

Mr. WILSON. Thank you, Mr. Breck.

Before returning the meeting to our chairman Mr. Hood, I take the liberty of adding my own comment. 

We are

We have listened to a great deal of good advice, I am sure. a little bit overwhelmed by the mass of detail which affects our transactions on the governmental side. We worry in regard to whether we are making the right decisions taxwise. 

The question inevitably arises: Is it worth going abroad at all?

This there is no one answer to. But let me point to one small measure of possibility.

In a recent year the average profits of U.S. domestic corporations were listed in that year as 132 percent. In the same year the average profits of U.S. foreign corporations were listed as 152 percent. 

Now does this 2-percent differential represent the opportunity for small business abroad?

In my opinion it does not. The opportunities that arise for the small business considering the possibility of going abroad are those 72811-61-15

of investigating carefully a specialty situation, working out a tailormade program which can be a program of extremely rapid growth. 

It is no exaggeration to say that in case after case after case the smaller business, rightly analyzing a particular foreign situation, can certainly see the prospect of a full return of capital after taxes within 5 years from date of commencing operations, and, in the case of return of capital, within 2 years of the date of commencing operations is not exceptional. This, in a word, is the incentive for going abroad. 

Mr. HOOD. Thank you, Frank. I would like to add a few closing remarks. 

I believe those of us who have the responsibility for the growth and survival of our companies must consciously look to the free markets of the world. Despite the many hurdles we have heard about here, we must find a way through.

It seems to me this is one of the New Frontiers for the businessman. 

This is an area where, because of its newness and complexity, it is almost impossible for the top man to delegate authority. Our experience indicates that a key factor is building a team that can work with you and learn with you-particularly in the investigating area.

Although the financial movement may be high, we caution you not to overlook the cultural aspects of your dealing abroad. These aspects are much more critical there and can make many of the fine financial ideas you had go for naught. 

Is there anyone in the audience who would like to make a comment? 

Mr. ROSEMOND.13 I would like to make this comment: This is my first meeting and I would like to express myself publicly as to the way you gentlemen have contributed your time and help that you have given, and I am most appreciative. [Applause.]

Mr. SNYDER.¹ The various figures of this session have with great ability and care described the services available to the manufacturer who is interested in a foreign operation. However, none of this will be to any avail if action with enthusiasm is not actually taken. Compared to countries like England or Germany, we are not an exporting Nation, and we have to face this. But now we must become a nation of exhibitors if we are to win the pending economic war, and that could affect our survival. 

Mr. HOOD. Very much so.

Well, speaking on behalf of those of us who have been involved, we appreciate your comments, and this has been really worth the time of all of us concerned.

I will turn the meeting over to Tom Cogan here who wants to wind up with us.

APPENDIX II RESULTS OF AN AMERICAN MANAGEMENT ASSOCIATION SURVEY OF SMALLER COMPANY CHIEF EXECUTIVES, APRIL 1961 THE BACKGROUND 

During the last year or two we have seen increased interest on the part of smaller companies in exploring the profit opportunities in foreign business. Possibly, this results in part from emphasis being given export promotion over the last few years. It may also be accounted for by word-of-mouth sharing of experiences of smaller company top management people who have successfully developed international business with significant profit margins. 

Accordingly, during April 1961, AMA's International Management Division conducted a survey among 700 smaller companies' chief executives on the extent of their foreign business. Excepting for the fact that these chief executives had previously attended an AMA seminar, the selection was entirely random.

For example, questionnaires were sent to chief executives of both manufacturing and services companies in wide variety of trades located in almost every State. 

The size of the companies varied from those with less than 100 employees doing less than $1 million in annual sales up to companies with around 1,000 employees with sales in the neighborhood of $15 million a year. No attempt was made to select only companies which might be expected to be engaged in foreign business. In fact, all companies on the list were kept in this random sample to preserve the statistical integrity of the survey, although many were known to have no foreign business.

THE RESULTS 

Out of 700 chief executives, 327 replied to the survey. Of the 327 replying, 81 indicated a consumer goods product line and 174 an industrial goods product line; 208 of the 327 responding to the survey indicated that their companies were engaged in some form of foreign business. Of the companies reporting foreign business 72 percent reported an industrial goods product line and 19 percent reported a consumer goods product line and 9 percent had both. 

As might be expected, practically all companies reporting foreign business were doing some exporting-201 out of 208. 

Seventy of the companies reporting indicated that they had licensing arrangements abroad and several pointed out that they had cross licensing arrangements. Thirty-five companies reported at least one wholly owned subsidiary abroad; eight reported they were engaged in contract manufacturing and twentyeight reported at least one or more joint ventures. 

Of those companies reporting joint ventures, six had a private partner and one a public partner. The balance did not specify. Eighteen of the companies specifying joint ventures indicated the percent of ownership. Seven of the eighteen indicating percent of ownership reported a minority position. This varied from 10 percent ownership up to 49 percent ownership; the median appeared to be about 30 percent ownership. Five of the eighteen reporting share of ownership indicated 50-50 partnerships. Six reported a majority position varying from 51 percent ownership up to 90 percent with the median about 65 percent. 

Of the total of 208 companies reporting foreign business, only 4 reported having a holding company abroad.

Finally the importance of the volume of foreign business as a percent of total sales varies quite widely, ranging in several cases from less than 1 percent of total sales to more than 20 percent of gross annual sales. It appears that the median for the reporting companies is in the neighborhood of 3 to 5 percent of annual sales.

SOME CONCLUSIONS 

The number of companies replying to the survey and doing foreign business compares favorably with a national estimate of only 12,000 out of the 300,000 U.S. manufacturing companies doing some foreign business. However, it should be pointed out that an estimated 20 percent of the companies polled reported nominal foreign business of less than 1 percent of annual sales. 

Many of the companies reporting no foreign business were in nonmanufacturing enterprises such as retailing, banking and wholesaling. 

On the other hand, several chief executives pointed out that foreign business was an important part of their profit picture. Said one chief executive: "Foreign business in my industry during 1960 made the difference between a fair profit and showing a loss for many companies." Several presidents reported they were aggressively pursuing new opportunities abroad; many planned on-the-spot surveys this summer in search of distributors and licensees. 

While profitability of foreign business as compared to domestic business is not always easy to measure, those who volunteered comments said they believed their foreign business either more profitable, or offering added profits through licensing royalties, not otherwise available.

Another comment by a few chief executives was that they found cross licensing offering new opportunities for business in the United States. Thus, they not only profit from licensing fees and royalties abroad, but by manufacture of their foreign licensee's products in the United States. In this connection, two chief executives reported that they used their licensing fees and royalties to buy equity in the foreign licensee's company. This gives them a voice in the licensee's management which they consider important "for quality control and product service" and also “avoids setting up a future competitor when the licensing agreement expires."

Two presidents also noted that licensing involved "little investment" and protected their "competitive position." (One president reported he would not consider licensing unless he could have equity participation.)

On investing abroad, two presidents reported that while they preferred wholly owned subsidiaries, joint ventures were satisfactory and had the advantage of "a smaller investment” and a “minimum political risk."

In conclusion, it appears that while many smaller companies have sought out profit opportunities abroad, many more are just beginning, or are exporting only at this time, but are planning further steps. Nonmanufacturing companies also are looking into foreign business, especially where the company has "knowhow."

Generally speaking, it is probably worth any company's time to investigate profit opportunities abroad where the company has patents, trademarks or other special "know-how" whether in manufacture, process, or management skills.
